The Face You Bear

A Riarhin novel

Part I — Her Mother’s Country

Chapter One — Three Nights

The wheat had been cut on one side of the road and stood on the other.

Riarhin rode between the fields with her cloak rolled behind the saddle and the dust of the southern road in the crease of her left elbow. Ahead, above the wagons, Valora showed a pale wall and a confusion of roofs. A woman in the standing wheat straightened to watch them pass, her sickle held away from her skirt, and then bent again. Beside the road a little boy was gathering the heads the carts had shaken loose.

Na’ar stopped his horse.

The boy had stepped beneath the pole of a stationary wagon. Its driver, who was talking to the man ahead, put his shoulder against a wheel and shouted to the team. Na’ar caught the bridle of the nearer horse. The other horse pulled against it, the wagon shifted, and the driver turned with a ready objection which went away when the boy came out between the wheels.

“Keep him where you can see him,” Na’ar said.

“He isn’t mine.”

“Then he is doing very badly for supervision.”

The boy ran to the field with his handful of wheat. The woman came out far enough to strike his shoulder, looked at the horses, and drew him against her skirt.

Na’ar let the bridle go. The driver thanked him after a moment, in the reluctant way of a man who had very nearly done something he would have to remember.

Riarhin had ridden a little beyond. She waited for him.

“You could have shouted.”

“He was shouting already.”

At the gate they joined the wagons. A guard wanted to know what was in the long bundle behind her saddle; she loosened the wrapping sufficiently for him to see the bow and the sword together, and he stepped back before asking her name. His fellow had begun writing it when somebody inside the gate called for the flour carts to be brought through first.

The guard held up his hand. “Wait.”

A carter behind them objected that he carried flour.

“Then you may go.”

“He carries bran,” said the next carter.

Riarhin moved her horse aside and retied the bundle. The guard looked from one wagon to the other, then at the full page on his board, as though her name might have become a different problem while he was attending to the sacks.

“Where are you lodging?”

“I don’t know yet.”

He had a place for that answer. She watched him fill it.

***

Lyra found them outside a baker’s.

Riarhin had dismounted to let an old woman with a basket pass between her horse and the shop wall. The woman caught a sleeve on a nail, stopped to swear at it, and was still swearing when Lyra came out of the lane with a folded paper in her hand.

For a moment Lyra only looked. She wore a plain dark gown, good at the seams, and had her hair fastened higher than Riarhin remembered. One of the pins was slipping. She caught it without looking away.

“You’re late.”

“I didn’t give you a day.”

“Your letter said before the end of summer.”

“It’s summer.”

“It is almost supper.”

Lyra embraced her before Riarhin had decided what to do with the reins. The mare, finding herself released, put her nose into the baker’s open shutter. Na’ar rescued a loaf and gave the animal a reproachful look.

Riarhin held Lyra a little longer than she had meant to. There was a narrowness at the back of her shoulders that the gown concealed, and a smell of warm linen. She had expected to find some change she could name. Instead she was pleased by the inconvenient fact of a person being there.

Lyra stepped away and looked at Na’ar.

“You can walk.”

“Very well.”

“Your letter said—”

“Her letter,” he said, pointing at Riarhin. “I walked when she wrote it. She made a great deal of noise about the manner.”

“He fell over a saddle.”

“It was on the ground.”

“That was why.”

Lyra laughed. The baker leaned through the shutter and asked who intended to pay for the loaf. Na’ar held it up.

“This one?”

“Yes, that one.”

“It has been very nearly eaten.”

“Then you will find it fresh.”

Riarhin paid while Lyra took a corner of the folded paper out of her own sleeve and checked the address written there.

“I must leave this first,” she said. “I can take you to Mara afterwards.”

“We can find her.”

“You can wait here for ten minutes.”

There was something too deliberate in that. Riarhin looked at the paper again, at the way Lyra folded the written side inward when she saw the glance.

“Your work?”

“Yes.”

“What sort?”

“The sort I must deliver before the office closes.”

She tucked it away. Riarhin almost asked whose office. Then the old woman, having freed her sleeve, caught Lyra by the arm to complain about the nail, and Lyra listened as though the matter had a place among the things she had come to do.

“Ten minutes,” Riarhin said.

“Please don’t go north in them.”

***

Mara was kneeling beside a chest when they arrived.

“No,” she said to the woman standing over her. “These go back. Those can stay until he comes.”

“He’s come.”

“Then he can take them.”

“He has nowhere to take them.”

Riarhin stopped in the doorway. The room beyond was full of folded blankets, two stools, a table laid for four, and an older man sitting on the only chair with arms. His right boot had been cut open and laced with a strip of cloth. Mara looked over the chest, saw Riarhin, and remained on her knees.

“Oh,” she said.

Then she got up too quickly, struck her shin, and swore.

Riarhin crossed the room.

Mara’s embrace was hard enough to hurt. She smelled faintly of vinegar, and her hands, when she took Riarhin’s face between them, had small cracks beside the nails.

“Let me look.”

“I’m here.”

“I can see that.”

She turned Riarhin’s face towards the light. The old man in the chair made an interested sound and was checked by the woman beside the chest.

“You look well,” Mara said at last.

“So do you.”

“I look like I’ve been moving blankets.”

“You’re very accomplished at it.”

“You haven’t seen me.”

“I heard.”

Mara let go, smiling now. “You can help.”

The blankets belonged to a station that had closed three weeks earlier. Their keeper’s son had promised to take the remainder away, then accepted work carrying stone, and the older man was his father, who had come to collect the blankets in a handcart which could hold perhaps a quarter of them. He needed the cart home before dark. The woman standing beside the chest needed the room empty by morning. She had promised it to her sister.

Lyra put down the loaf. “Where is the other key?”

“Marten has it.”

“Where is Marten?”

“Carrying stone.”

Na’ar went outside to look at the cart. Riarhin lifted a blanket. It was good wool, patched at one edge with a different weave. Whoever had washed it had left soap in the hem.

“Dry upstairs,” she said. “Damp along the bottom.”

“I know. I wanted them opened.”

“Then we open them.”

Mara looked round at the crowded room. Riarhin put the first blanket over the rail beneath the window and the second across the chest. Na’ar came back with a length of cord from their baggage and asked permission to put it between two wall hooks. The older man objected that his son had told him to fetch everything.

“You’ll fetch what will fit,” Mara said. “You’ll tell him the rest is here. He can come after work.”

“He’ll be tired.”

“So will I.”

The man looked at her, then at the room. “I could come with him.”

“Good.”

By the time Elyas arrived, they had a table under a low tent of damp wool and no room for his sword on the wall.

He stopped at the threshold.

Riarhin saw the recognition begin in his face. It made him look younger, then something in his mouth changed and he came in carefully, moving a stool out of his way before he reached her.

She had thought he would say her name. He put both hands on her shoulders instead.

“You took your time.”

“You’ve married.”

“That took less.”

“Elyas,” Mara said.

He turned to her, still holding Riarhin, and smiled. The turn was enough: the ease of it, the expectation that Mara would be there to answer. Riarhin had seen his father turn that way, years before, to someone who had not remained beside him. She let the resemblance pass without keeping it.

“I congratulate you,” she said.

“Both of us?” Mara asked.

“I’m considering.”

Elyas kissed Riarhin’s cheek and let her go. There was a chair to be found, then another plate, then the question of whether the older man should stay to eat. He said he would not trouble them. Mara told him his plate was already on the table.

They sat beneath the blankets.

***

The stew had caught on the bottom. Mara said so before anybody tasted it, and Na’ar, who had been given a spoon but no authority over the pot, said he had eaten worse. This failed to reassure her until he named two occasions in detail and Elyas asked him to stop.

Lyra wanted to know about the sea.

“It moves,” Na’ar told her.

“All of it?”

“All of it.”

Riarhin told them about Varek’s bird and the sailor who had tried to convince Na’ar that one side of the ship moved less than the other. She left out the first night of sickness until Na’ar supplied it himself, accurately and with an objection to the cook’s interpretation of broth. Mara leaned back, laughing, and a drop of water from a blanket struck Elyas on the collar.

He moved his chair.

It was a very small movement. Riarhin watched him make it and suddenly wanted the evening to be longer than it could be. Lyra had already looked twice at the light in the window. The older man had finished and was waiting for a chance to leave without interrupting. Outside, somebody called the name of the woman who needed the room, and she answered that she would come when she could.

Riarhin cut another piece of bread.

“Where next?” Elyas asked.

“North.”

“Ubral?”

She nodded.

“Your mother’s people?”

Mara glanced at him. Riarhin could have asked when they had discussed it. She could have made the question sound idle. Instead she said, “Rocke. A place called Glenruan, if the man who told me was sober enough to remember it.”

“Was he?”

“He remembered what I owed him.”

Elyas offered to write. She shook her head before he had named a recipient.

“I’m going to see them.”

“A letter would only—”

“I’d rather arrive.”

He considered that, then passed her the dish she could have reached herself. A scar crossed the knuckle of his thumb. It was new to her.

“There is a thing to do here first,” she said.

She took the folded release from her coat and laid it beside her plate. The Council’s seal had travelled inside two layers of leather and still acquired a chip. Elyas turned the paper so that the writing faced him but kept his hands clear of the seal.

“A gift?”

“For the queen. I’m to put it into her keeping and send an acknowledgement south.”

“What is it?” Mara asked.

“A small thing.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s the answer that fits it.”

Elyas read the opening, then looked at Riarhin. “I can ask for an audience.”

“Tomorrow?”

“I can ask.”

She folded the paper again. Lyra’s gaze followed it back into her coat.

“Have you told anyone else you’re here?”

“A guard. He wrote it down.”

“Then we may be late already.”

***

The answer reached them before the blankets were dry.

A young man in the queen’s service stood outside the lodging with an expression that improved when Elyas came to the door. Behind him the older man was loading his cart, refusing Na’ar’s help with increasing irritation. The messenger held out a folded instruction and said that the queen would receive the bearer of the Taharkan gift after the morning petitions.

Elyas read farther.

“What?” Riarhin asked.

He handed it to her.

Three evenings had been marked for her attendance. The words at the queen’s request stood above the clerk’s careful account of where she should present herself and to whom. There was provision for a companion, and a line concerning rooms.

“I have a room,” Riarhin said.

“No,” said Mara. “You have blankets.”

The messenger smiled before he remembered to keep his face still.

Riarhin looked down at the paper. Three nights would not change the north. She had taken longer to cross a flooded stream and made less objection. It was the shape of the request that troubled her, the way it had already become something other people were arranging.

“What does she want for three nights?”

Elyas looked at the dust on her coat and the worn leather wrapping the bow.

“I imagine she wants to hear where you’ve been.”

Na’ar set the last bundle into the cart. “That takes longer.”

Riarhin refolded the instruction along its existing creases.

“Tell her I’ll come.”

Chapter Two — A Little Prize

The clerk wanted the outer wrapping.

Riarhin had put the case on the table and the Council’s release beside it. The linen lay in her hand. It had been washed since Tigist gave it to her, and a corner mended, and it was no longer worth the attention the man was giving it.

“For the description,” he explained.

“The description is there.”

“It says delivered wrapped.”

“It was.”

He looked at her hand again. She let him look. On the other side of the door a woman was describing a boundary which appeared to have been moved by men with authority to move it and insufficient authority to pay for what had stood on it. The queen asked where the displaced family was living now.

The clerk lowered his voice. “I have to retain the things received.”

“You can retain the case.”

“And the wrapping.”

“No.”

He sat back.

His pen waited above the paper. Riarhin smoothed the corner of the linen with her thumb. Tigist had torn it from a longer piece with both hands, bracing a foot against the table. The tear had run crookedly. Riarhin could see the whole act, the impatience before the woman reached for her shears.

She did not intend to tell him that.

“Write that I took it back.”

The clerk considered, dipped his pen, and wrote.

***

They had examined it in Adur-Gah.

She could see Meret at the chancery table with the archive’s books open on either side, refusing a councillor’s suggestion that she call the object an ornament and be done. Riarhin had sat through the refusal, and through the next examination, and through a man who wanted it carried to a northern storehouse until Dejen’s reply arrived and disposed of that proposal in a single paragraph.

The last meeting had been hotter than the others. A shutter would not stay open. Sada had wedged it with her sandal and conducted half her evidence with one bare foot beneath the table.

“You have found nothing,” a councillor said to Meret.

“I have found nothing I can identify as a working.”

“Then we may send it.”

“You may decide to send it.”

Riarhin had liked her very much at that moment. She liked her less when Meret required the qualification to appear in the final paper, because the clerk had already sanded it and they all had to wait while he made another.

At the word Viterra, Sada had looked across the table.

Riarhin remembered wishing she had discussed the destination with her before the councillor asked. It had seemed a convenient answer: a court she could reach, a recipient who would acknowledge the gift, and no further reason for a Taharkan watch captain to be sent into the ruins because someone had decided a thing ought to remain where it had already been stolen twice.

She was going north anyway.

They had asked whether she would undertake delivery. She had said yes.

Afterwards Sada had walked with her into the shade of the outer wall. The councillors were still behind them, discussing the letter in which a cautious disposal had become a gesture of friendship between peoples.

“You needn’t leave tomorrow,” Sada had said.

“I haven’t said tomorrow.”

“You have packed.”

Riarhin had been annoyed by the accuracy of this. She remembered it now while the Valoran clerk turned the Council’s final paper to compare its seal with the copy made for the queen.

“Did they examine it?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“And find—”

“What’s written.”

He read farther. She put Tigist’s linen in her pocket.

***

Veralyn was standing when they entered.

The petitioners had gone, but their bench was still occupied by a small girl fastening the ties on her mother’s bundle. A clerk waited with them. The girl looked up at Na’ar and forgot the knot; her mother touched her wrist and brought her attention back.

Riarhin bowed.

The queen wore red over black, the red darkened where a window cast the frame’s shadow across her sleeve. Her hair had been arranged with more skill than patience. A pin stood at a slightly wrong angle above one ear. Tylan sat near the window with a folded paper across his knee and his sword upright against the chair.

“Riarhin,” Veralyn said. “You have been very difficult to invite.”

“I’ve been away.”

“So I’m told.”

The queen’s gaze went to Na’ar. He gave his name and bowed less deeply than Riarhin, with perfect seriousness. Veralyn received it in the same spirit. The clerk came forward with the case on a folded piece of household cloth.

Riarhin had seen it in dust, lamplight, the grey light of a ship’s cabin. Here the brass took colour from the queen’s sleeve. The leaves worked around the glass were fine enough to disappear when she looked at the whole thing and return when she looked at one edge.

“The Council sends this with its friendship,” she said. “And asks acknowledgement that you have received it into your keeping.”

“Into my keeping.”

“That is the wording.”

Veralyn looked at the paper. Her finger moved to the qualification Meret had required.

“Do you advise me to keep it?”

Riarhin had expected a polite acceptance, perhaps a question about the journey. The direct question required her to look at the little case again.

“They found no working in it. Meret would not say there could be none.”

“And you?”

“I carried it here.”

“That is what you did.”

Riarhin met her eyes. “I don’t know what it was for.”

The queen nodded, as though that answer had completed a necessary part of the presentation. She lifted the case from the cloth.

It was small in her hand.

Riarhin felt the unreasonable impulse to tell her how to hold it, though there was no way of holding it that she herself had established as proper. Veralyn turned it once and placed her thumb beside the narrow mark in the glass. The green deepened towards the centre. The window behind her showed through it badly, broken into a colour without a shape.

“All that way,” the queen said.

“We went to a great deal of trouble for it.”

“So I shall hear.”

“It looks less trouble now.”

Veralyn smiled. “Does it?”

She lifted it closer. Riarhin could see the brass reflected in her eyes. Tylan set his paper down and watched her examine it, smiling a little at the concentration.

“May I?” Veralyn asked, touching the loop.

“It’s yours.”

A woman attending the queen found a length of black cord. Veralyn bent her head to let her fasten it, then caught the case against her breast before it had quite stopped moving. She adjusted it by the width of a finger.

The girl on the bench had finished her knot. Her mother stood, made an awkward reverence to the queen, and thanked her for something Riarhin had not heard granted. Veralyn looked over at once.

“The clerk will go with you,” she said. “Take him to the house, not to the officer who sent you here.”

The woman began another thanks. Veralyn stopped it gently enough, with a question about whether they had eaten.

Riarhin stepped aside to let them pass. The girl looked back at the green glass. Then the clerk took the bundle from her mother, and they went out together.

***

Na’ar declined a second fitting.

The woman charged with making him presentable had found a coat which closed across his chest only when he stood very still. He had done so long enough to prove the difficulty, then opened the fastening and laid the coat over her arm.

“I have clothes.”

“For the queen’s supper.”

“Yes.”

She looked at Riarhin for support. Riarhin, who was standing on a low stool while another woman put a pin into her hem, would have offered it if the coat had fitted.

“He’ll be clean.”

“I am clean,” Na’ar said.

The woman considered his own coat. “The stitching here—”

“I did that.”

“I can see.”

“You could do it better.”

“Yes.”

He removed the coat and gave it to her. The exchange pleased him. Riarhin saw that the woman was pleased too, though she concealed it by turning the seam inside out.

Her own gown had been borrowed from a woman who had left court in haste and sent instructions that nobody was to put it away damp. It was brown, with a small red pattern at the cuffs, and required two fastenings Riarhin could not reach.

“You’ll need someone tonight,” the woman at her hem said.

“I can manage.”

“You can pull, certainly. Then I’ll have to mend it.”

Riarhin looked down at her.

“Ask,” the woman said, and shifted the pin.

***

They ate in a room that had once been intended for fewer people.

A table had been extended near the window. The added section sat a little lower, and Tylan placed his cup on it, watched the wine settle towards one side, then changed seats without comment. Jaennis arrived while a servant was still laying spoons. She greeted Riarhin with a warmth that seemed to include having expected her for several days, although the messenger had come only yesterday.

“You should have written sooner.”

“I wrote to Lyra.”

“Yes. You should have written sooner.”

Veralyn came in behind her wearing the amulet.

The cord had been replaced. A narrow black ribbon held it higher against her throat. Jaennis touched the edge of the brass and asked where it had been made; Riarhin said she could tell her where it had been found. The answer earned her the place beside the queen.

For a while supper prevented the story. The fish had been brought farther than the cook liked, and someone wanted to know whether the new road would improve that. Tylan asked how much farther. Jaennis asked whether the cook objected to the journey or to being told what to serve. A woman farther down the table said he had objected to both at considerable length.

Riarhin listened. She had sat at tables where every interruption concealed a contest, and at others where men spoke because the silence troubled them. Here a young woman tried twice to begin a story about a horse, then found the opening and told it badly, and Veralyn waited while she got to the part she thought funny.

Riarhin laughed at the wrong point. The young woman looked at her in surprise, then laughed herself and finished with less effort.

When the dishes had been moved, the queen turned.

“Now,” she said. “Where did you find my gift?”

“In a city.”

“Many things are found in cities.”

“This one was difficult to reach.”

“How difficult?”

Na’ar leaned back. “Tell her about the floor.”

“I was going to start with the ship.”

“We can have the ship tomorrow.”

The queen looked between them. “There was a floor?”

“There was meant to be,” Riarhin said.

She began with Meret insisting that a line in a damaged inscription contained a negative. The queen wanted to know what the negative changed. Riarhin explained, and had to describe the door, then the position of the hinge, and by the time she reached the floor Na’ar had objected to her account of how much warning he had given.

“You said my name.”

“Loudly.”

“You often say it loudly.”

“Because you are often doing a thing you should stop.”

Tylan laughed into his cup. Veralyn’s fingers rested on the green glass while she listened.

Riarhin found herself describing Kebede’s face at the culvert, the complete offence of a man who had brought them so far by understanding the ground and now had to crawl into a place from which the ground intended to leave. She had disliked him once. It was difficult to remember the dislike while making Jaennis laugh at his demand for an answer nobody could give.

She did not tell them everything.

The drowned passages remained passages. The thing behind the next door remained a reason to find another way. When she reached the injured leg, she looked at Na’ar before she described it, and he moved his hand in permission.

The queen noticed.

Later, when the servants had taken the cups and Tylan had gone to fetch a man who had been waiting too long outside, Veralyn asked, “Did you think you would lose him?”

“Yes.”

“How did you get him out?”

“With help.”

“Whose?”

Riarhin named them. She named Mulu, although Mulu had been waiting beyond the escape itself, and Hailu, and the people who had been persuaded to turn back towards danger for somebody they scarcely knew. Veralyn listened more closely to that than she had to the floor.

“And the woman who wrote this?” She touched the release on the small table beside her.

“Sada.”

“She trusted you.”

Riarhin looked at the paper.

“She required me to come back.”

“Did you?”

“Yes.”

The queen waited, but did not ask the next question. Riarhin was grateful for that and irritated by the gratitude. She stood when Veralyn rose, and remembered the borrowed gown before she stepped backwards onto its hem.

***

The woman who had fitted it was waiting in the passage.

“I said you’d need someone.”

“You did.”

Riarhin let her undo the fastenings. In the small room beyond, her own coat lay across the bed with the clean linen showing from its pocket. She reached for it as soon as the gown was free.

“Did she like it?” the woman asked.

“The gift?”

“She was wearing it when they brought the plates out.”

“Yes. She liked it.”

The woman shook the gown once before folding it. “Good.”

Riarhin sat on the bed. For months she had checked the same pocket before sleeping, sometimes before waking fully, and her hand went there now with the certainty of a habitual task. There was only the linen.

She took it out and folded it smaller.

A knock came from the passage. Na’ar asked through the door whether she had eaten enough fish. He had found bread somewhere.

“Come in,” she said.

Chapter Three — The Last Evening

Mara had cut the split branch.

She told Riarhin this while trying to persuade a shopkeeper that a length of cloth with a hole in it ought to cost less than the same length without one. The shopkeeper agreed in principle. He disagreed about the size of the hole, the amount of useful cloth remaining, and whether Mara’s proposed price accounted for the quality of the weave.

“We thought it was dead when we took the house,” Mara said, holding the cloth to the light. “Then it put out leaves. Elyas was delighted.”

“Why cut it?”

“It split in a storm and dropped half across the path.”

The shopkeeper, uncertain whether the remark concerned his cloth, said the damage would be lost in the cutting.

“Then you cut it,” Mara said. “Give me this length clear of it, at the price we agreed.”

He considered the position of the hole again. Riarhin stood beside the door, watching a dog sleep with one ear inside and the other in the street.

“You’re enjoying this,” Mara said to her.

“Yes.”

“Help.”

“The weave is good.”

“I know the weave is good.”

“That’s why I won’t take less,” said the shopkeeper.

Mara gave Riarhin a look and returned to the cloth. The dog opened an eye. It had a grey muzzle and the composure of an animal that had been stepped over for years.

They left with the shorter piece at a price neither buyer nor seller proposed to call satisfactory. Mara carried it beneath her arm. Riarhin had offered, and been given a basket instead.

“What are you making?”

“Curtains.”

“For the house?”

“No, for Elyas. He’s tired of clothes.”

Riarhin looked at her. Mara laughed and took her arm, navigating a pair of boys carrying a ladder through the crowd.

“You must come,” she said. “When you return from the north.”

“I don’t know when that will be.”

“The house will still be there.”

Riarhin nearly said something about houses. She had several remarks available, each dry enough to end the subject without seeming to refuse the invitation. Mara’s hand was warm through her sleeve. She let them pass.

“Tell me where.”

Mara did, carefully, naming the road and the turn that travellers missed because the larger gate belonged to another holding. Then she described the kitchen, the small room above it, and the pear tree whose remaining branches were alive if not productive.

At the next stall Mara stopped for onions.

***

They ate behind the station, sitting on a bench that had been repaired with wood too new for the rest of it.

Mara had brought the food for a woman replacing her at the afternoon work. The woman was delayed. A boy came to say that the patient in the corner room would drink from the blue cup and no other, and the blue cup had been broken that morning. Mara gave him a small jug and told him to try that, because arguing about colour had not made anyone less thirsty.

“Does he understand?” Riarhin asked when the boy had gone.

“He understands some things. More when he’s tired of us than when we’re tired of him.”

Mara broke the bread. There was a little dirt caught beneath one nail, which she picked out with the point of a pin before putting the pin safely into her sleeve.

“Elyas says you’re well.”

“You saw me.”

“Yes.”

Riarhin took the smaller piece. The bread was better than yesterday’s.

“He means something else,” she said.

“I know. I wondered whether you did.”

From inside came the sound of a man objecting to the jug. The boy answered with a patience so elaborate that he must have copied it from an adult. Mara closed her eyes briefly.

“Would you like me to go?” Riarhin asked.

“He’ll drink. If you go in, he’ll begin again for you.”

“I meant north.”

Mara turned towards her.

The question had come out more plainly than Riarhin intended. She could have pretended it referred to the timing, to the audience she had accepted and the borrowed room. Mara would allow her that if she asked it properly. She did not ask.

“I’d like you to come back,” Mara said.

It was an answer she could bear. She nodded and ate her bread.

After a while Mara asked about Sada.

Riarhin had named her the first night. She had described her work, the obligations of an expedition, the people whose water and animals they needed before they could go anywhere. She had omitted the room in Adur-Gah where Sada kept a spare pair of sandals beside the door, and the fact that one of the straps had been mended with red thread, and the way Sada sometimes spoke while washing her face, requiring Riarhin to wait for the rest of a sentence.

“She’s occupied,” she said.

“So am I.”

“She has people depending on her.”

“So have I.”

Riarhin looked over. Mara’s face held no invitation to be teased.

“I came north.”

“Yes.”

“I told her I was going.”

“Did she ask you to stay?”

A woman passed them carrying clean bowls, and Mara reached for the top one before it could slide. They redistributed the stack between the woman’s arms. By the time she had gone, the question had become more difficult.

“She asked when I would know what I was doing after.”

“And?”

“I said I’d tell her when I knew.”

Mara picked a crumb from her skirt. “Write before then.”

Riarhin disliked being told so simple a thing when she had already considered it and found several reasons to delay. She looked towards the station door. The boy emerged carrying the jug, triumphant and trying not to show it.

“He drank.”

“Good,” Mara said. “Did you?”

The boy looked inside the empty jug.

“Get yourself water.”

When he had gone, Riarhin said, “Have you always done that?”

“What?”

“Asked the next thing.”

Mara smiled. “You make it difficult to stop at the first.”

***

That evening Riarhin told the queen about the sea.

Na’ar was elsewhere. A man he had met in the yard had promised to show him a horse whose owner claimed it could bear anybody, and Na’ar had taken the claim personally. Riarhin was left to describe the ship without correction.

She did not improve his performance aboard it. She described his careful study of the first wave breaking across the bow and the length of time he had spent deciding which part of the vessel was least deceitful. Veralyn laughed at the exchange with Varek, then asked about the captain’s knotted braid.

“His own business,” Riarhin said.

“You didn’t ask?”

“I did.”

“And?”

“He answered enough.”

Jaennis, sitting farther along the table, tilted her cup towards her mouth. Riarhin caught the amusement before she drank.

“You must know many people’s business,” Veralyn said.

“Less than people imagine.”

“Do they tell you?”

“Sometimes.”

“Because you’re leaving?”

Riarhin looked at the queen.

The green case lay against the black ribbon. Veralyn had pushed her sleeve back to get it away from a dish, and the edge of the cuff had left a mark on her wrist. She was young enough for the question to have been merely curious. Riarhin could not quite make herself receive it that way.

“Sometimes because I’m listening.”

Veralyn accepted the correction. “Then I should listen.”

She did. Riarhin found her attention disconcertingly pleasant, particularly when the queen asked a question that proved she had kept something small from the previous evening. By the time the candles were shortened, Riarhin was telling her about the cook at the wells and Na’ar’s objection to the onions. The story had no danger in it. She told it to the end.

On the way out, she saw a woman carrying the borrowed brown gown towards the rooms assigned to her.

“Tomorrow as well?” the woman asked.

“One more.”

“I’ll press the cuff.”

Riarhin held the door for her.

***

Elyas brought his horse round the following afternoon and asked whether she wanted to ride out.

She did. They took a road between harvested fields, leaving the escort he had not brought and the questions he had not yet asked behind them. Riarhin’s mare put her ears forward at the open ground. Elyas loosened his reins and let the two animals find the same pace.

For a while they talked about horses.

It was easy work, being with him when neither of them tried to supply the intervening years. He had a new habit of turning his right shoulder before reaching across his body; when she asked about it, he said an old strain had found an occasion to become troublesome again. She told him how he was making it worse. He told her Mara had said the same.

“Then listen to your wife.”

“I was hoping for another opinion.”

“You have it.”

They stopped where a farm track met the road. Two men were lifting a wheel onto an axle. Elyas watched until the hub seated properly, then turned his horse towards a strip of shade.

“I went to see him,” he said.

She knew which man he meant. There was no need for him to say Braedyn’s name. The field before her had stubble in straight rows and a little green growth beside the ditch, where the scythes had missed it.

“Did you?”

“Yes.”

She waited for the account. Elyas rubbed his horse’s neck.

“I can tell you where.”

“I know where.”

“All right.”

He had made a place for her to ask. She could feel its exact shape between them, and hated him briefly for the care with which he left it empty. Braedyn’s voice was readily available. So was the last argument, and a joke from a campaign neither of them had wanted to remember accurately, and the movement of his hand when he was pretending not to be tired.

She looked down at the mare’s mane.

“The north first,” she said.

Elyas nodded. He asked nothing about what might come after it.

On their return, the wheel was turning. The men had loaded the cart and were trying to persuade a child to ride on it. She wanted to walk beside the horse. Riarhin watched her father give up and take her hand.

***

On the third evening Veralyn asked for something Riarhin had not told anyone.

“That would be a difficulty,” Riarhin said.

“Surely there is something.”

“There are several things.”

The queen smiled, then saw that the answer was complete. She rested her chin on her hand.

“Something you choose, then.”

Riarhin could have returned to the expedition. She had enough of it left to fill the evening, and enough skill to arrange the danger so that nobody felt the absence of what she withheld. Across the room Tylan was trying to mend the fastening on a small case someone had brought him. Jaennis had gone to speak to a woman at the door. The room was briefly less attentive than it had been.

“My mother had a blue shoe,” Riarhin said.

Veralyn waited.

“Only one?” Tylan asked.

“Two. She disliked one of them.”

“Why?”

“It hurt.”

The queen laughed softly. “That seems sufficient.”

“She kept it because the pair had cost too much to give away. She would wear them when she wanted to appear very pleased to see someone.”

“Was she?”

“Sometimes.”

Riarhin could see Bea sitting on the bed, one knee crossed over the other, rubbing the base of her toes with both thumbs. She had been talking about a man Riarhin was too young to understand and quite old enough to dislike. Then she had caught her daughter watching and made a face at the shoe.

“She told me,” Riarhin said, “that you should never let a man know he had been less comfortable than your clothes.”

Tylan put down the case.

“Sound advice.”

“You have uncomfortable clothes?” Veralyn asked him.

“I have met uncomfortable men.”

The queen turned to answer him. The little brass case slipped against her collar, and she caught it with a finger, still smiling. Riarhin had given them enough. She felt oddly bare for having said so little.

Before the evening ended, Veralyn asked how long she meant to be in Ubral.

“I don’t know.”

“You will return through Valora?”

“If I come south.”

“Then you will come here.”

Riarhin bowed her head.

***

She wrote to Sada before dawn.

The first page began with delivery and described the queen’s acknowledgement. She set it aside for the packet that would go south through the carriers. On a second piece she wrote about the wheat, then stopped because it sounded like the beginning of a report.

The room was cold enough for her to pull the coat around her shoulders. Na’ar was already in the yard. She could hear him refusing somebody’s suggestion about loading the horses, and the familiar sound made her want to finish quickly.

She put the pen down, then took it up again.

I am going to find my mother’s people. I have their name and directions that disagree. I don’t know what I expect from them.

She read it. That would have to serve.

The queen likes the little case. I have sent the receipt with this. There are people here I wanted to see, and I have seen them. I thought that would be easier than it was.

For the last line she moved the paper closer.

I should have written before I knew what came next.

She folded the page without reading it again. By the time she reached the yard, Na’ar had secured the bow and sword behind her saddle, and the woman who had fitted the gown was waiting to collect it.

“The hem is sound,” Riarhin told her.

“I saw.”

“Thank you.”

The woman accepted that, then tucked a loose thread inside the collar of Riarhin’s coat. “There.”

They rode out while the bread carts were coming in.

Chapter Four — Beatrix Eithne Rocke

At Glenruan they mistook Na’ar for the messenger.

A girl in a wool dress came out from behind a wall, looked at the two horses, and told him her father was in the yard. She said it with the weary readiness of somebody sent to watch a road for a person who had taken too long to arrive.

“Which father?” Na’ar asked.

“Mine.”

“Yes, but which one is he?”

“The loud one.”

They followed her through a gate. Beyond it a man with his sleeves rolled up was standing inside an empty water trough, holding one end of a board while a shorter, very broad woman examined the other. Two dogs watched from a prudent distance.

“It fits,” he was saying.

“It fitted when I made it.”

“Then we agree.”

“We will agree when you stop standing on it.”

The man looked down, shifted his boot, and saw the travellers.

“Oh, thank the light. Tell your lady I’ve sent the answer. It passed you on the road.”

“I have no lady,” Na’ar said.

The man looked at Riarhin, reconsidered, and climbed out of the trough. The board tipped beneath his heel. The broad woman caught it with an exasperated cry and told him to leave the tools where she could find them.

He came across the yard smiling, tall enough that Riarhin had to lift her chin to meet his eyes. His shoulders filled the open neck of his shirt; the sleeves had left red creases above his elbows. Bea had needed doors held wide when she wore her good clothes. This man looked as though he needed it in his shirtsleeves.

“Tavish Rocke. If you’re lost, we feed you before we explain how badly.”

“I was looking for you,” Riarhin said.

“Then you’re less lost than most. Who shall I welcome?”

“Riarhin.”

He waited for the rest. She had told gatekeepers less with considerably more ease.

“My mother was Bea.”

Behind Tavish, the little girl attempted to climb into the trough and was removed by the back of her dress. The woman set her on the ground without interrupting her inspection of the board.

“Bea,” Tavish repeated.

“Beatrix. Rocke.”

His smile changed. It remained a smile, but the easy answer left it.

“Beatrix Eithne?”

“I suppose.”

“You suppose?”

“She was Bea.”

Tavish looked at her for so long that Riarhin began to regret arriving without the letter Elyas had offered. She could have given a messenger the burden of saying the thing. She could have waited at an inn while this man consulted someone who remembered a name correctly.

He turned towards the house.

“Elspeth!”

The force of it startled a dog to its feet.

“Tavish,” the woman at the trough said. “She can hear.”

“Elspeth, come out. Come and see who’s here.”

Riarhin stayed beside her horse. Na’ar took the bridle from her hand with the discretion he generally reserved for stealing food.

***

Elspeth came to the door carrying a wet cloth.

“If it’s the grain man, tell him—”

“It’s Beatrix’s daughter.”

“Whose?”

“Beatrix Eithne. My grandfather’s—” Tavish stopped. “Come in. We’ll do it sitting.”

He reached for Riarhin’s hand, checked the movement as though he had remembered she might not like it, and offered his arm instead. The adjustment was clumsy enough to make her take it.

Elspeth stood aside.

The room inside was smaller than the voice that had filled the yard. A hearth occupied one end, a table the middle, and an assortment of chairs proved that nobody had ever bought six of the same kind. A woven hanging had been lifted to admit air through a narrow window. Beneath it lay a dog with a split ear, which opened one eye and declined to move.

“Sit there,” Tavish said, pulling a chair out. “Iona, fetch cups.”

The girl brought two.

“Four.”

“There are two visitors.”

“And we might drink with them.”

She considered that a good reason and went back.

Elspeth put the cloth over a peg. “How did you find us?”

Riarhin gave the names of the man who had directed her and the woman who had corrected him. Tavish laughed at the first and approved the second.

“He would send his own mother over that road in winter. He’d apologise afterwards, mind. Beautifully.”

“Was she your mother?” Elspeth asked Riarhin.

The direct question stilled Tavish. Riarhin nodded.

“In Silkstrand.”

“She went south,” he said. “We knew that much.”

“She died there.”

“I’m sorry.”

“It was a long time ago.”

His eyes moved over her face. Riarhin watched the arithmetic fail to settle into a question. She liked him for leaving it unfinished.

“Elspeth,” he said, “where’s the little book?”

“Which little book?”

“The brown one.”

“You have several.”

“The one that proves who we’re obliged to invite.”

Elspeth fetched it from a chest beneath the hanging. The binding had split along one edge. Tavish opened it with both hands, found a page, and turned it towards Riarhin.

The name was there.

Beatrix Eithne, in a hand she had never seen, entered beneath Aedan and Eithne beside a much older brother. The ink had faded unevenly. Someone had added gone south after the entry, with a year Riarhin refused to begin counting from.

She touched the margin.

“Domnal,” Tavish said, tracing the other branch without putting his finger on the writing. “His boy was Fergus. Then Niall, and my mother Morven, and this discreditable remnant.”

He tapped his chest.

“Mother was broader. There’s a chair in the kitchen she had made because she disliked being held when she sat down. You’ll know it when you see it.”

“She could have had a chair without arms,” Elspeth said.

“She liked arms. She wanted them farther apart. A perfectly reasonable position, which I inherit.”

“Fergus was your cousin,” Tavish told Riarhin. “So I’m your cousin with three removals, if I haven’t married you to somebody by mistake.”

“First cousin,” Elspeth said. “Three times removed.”

“That’s what I said.”

“You made it sound like a punishment.”

“Some of them are.”

Riarhin looked at the page again. The names were exact enough to be read, and the page existed whether she wanted to put herself into it or not. She had imagined old people. She had prepared for an old woman who remembered Bea and would tell her she looked like her mother, or did not, or had come too late.

Tavish was perhaps thirty.

He had a daughter waiting with cups and a wife who knew where the book was. The woman Riarhin wanted to ask about was older than his dead grandfather’s memories.

“You’ve come a long way,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Well. You’ll stay.”

***

The broad woman from the yard brought in the ale herself.

“Your trough will hold,” she told Tavish. “Leave it alone.”

“This is Mairi. She mends what I improve.”

“I’d have more time if you improved less.”

Mairi set down the jug and gave Riarhin a brief, assessing look. She had a dwarf’s heavy wrists and an Ubral voice, and wore a silver ring polished thin beside the joint. Tavish pulled another chair towards the table with his foot.

“Sit. We’ve found family.”

“So I heard. They heard at the next steading.”

Riarhin took the cup with her left hand. Tavish noticed while pouring.

“Elf-handed.”

“Usually.”

“Like Ailsa’s eldest. Though he favours the right when he’s trying to impress his father.”

“His father throws with the right,” Mairi said. “The boy is copying.”

“Then somebody should tell him to copy a better throw.” Tavish filled his own cup. “You’ll know a few folk like yourself here. Half-Lethai. My mother called them elflings when she was fond of them and by their whole names when she wasn’t.”

Riarhin waited.

“Most are round of ear,” he went on. “Pretty little lasses, and lads, yes, I’m remembering the lads. May live to be older. Not much older, most of them.”

“His mother called everybody pretty when she wanted them to carry something,” Elspeth said.

“She was a woman of judgement.”

Tavish glanced at Riarhin’s ears and then, with more care, at her face.

“You’re a rarer sort.”

“So I’m told.”

“Do people tell you often?”

“Yes.”

“I’ll stop, then. There’s food coming.”

He stopped. Riarhin had been ready to fend off a further question and found herself holding the answer with nowhere to put it. She drank. The ale was stronger than she expected and had a sourness she liked.

Iona pulled up the chair beside her.

“How old are you?”

“Iona,” Elspeth said.

“It’s a question.”

“It is.”

Riarhin turned the cup between her hands. “I stopped counting.”

“When?”

“That’s the difficulty.”

The girl frowned, then decided she was being teased. “I’m eight.”

“That’s a good age to remember.”

“I know.”

***

They made room for Na’ar in the lower chamber, where a man was already putting away a travelling cloak.

He was dressed well enough to be received as a guest and practically enough to have ridden there. His left boot stood on the hearthstone drying; the other remained on his foot. He gave his name as Keir and said he had come from the Gray Tor.

“Then you are the messenger,” Na’ar said.

“I am.”

“That explains the wait.”

Tavish groaned. “Iona.”

“She said you were the loud father.”

“Accurate child,” Elspeth said, passing the door with bedding.

Keir smiled, but Riarhin saw him take in the weapons Na’ar was setting beside the wall. He looked at her own bundle and then at the coat over her arm.

“Have you come from the lowlands?”

“Valora.”

“Recently?”

“Yes.”

“Tavish tells me you’re kin.”

“I was there when he worked it out.”

The messenger’s smile thinned into something more attentive. Tavish, who had started to follow Elspeth, stayed in the doorway.

“She’s Beatrix’s daughter,” he said. “You’ll know the name.”

“I know the family.”

“Then we’ll put another plate out.”

Keir looked back at Riarhin. “Did you see the queen?”

“Yes.”

“She had a traveller from the south. Three evenings, I heard.”

News had taken a better horse. Riarhin loosened the thong around her bundle.

“That was me.”

“On her business?”

“No.”

“You understand why I ask.”

“I understand the question.”

Tavish came fully into the room and lifted a saddle from the bench, making room for Na’ar to sit.

“Lady Uria will hear she’s welcome,” he said. “And that we’re pleased about it. Tell her that from me.”

Keir inclined his head. The exchange was over because Tavish had chosen a place to end it, and because the messenger chose to accept the place. Riarhin felt the slight increase in what her arrival required of the house.

When Keir went to fetch his other boot, she said, “You could have let me answer.”

“You did.”

“There might be more questions.”

“There generally are. Will you want onions? Elspeth always asks too late.”

***

Na’ar ate with the satisfaction of a man who had been travelling through decent food and found excellent food at the end of it.

The meal was lamb, barley, and a sharp sauce that made him stop speaking long enough to identify the sourness. Tavish asked him what he thought. He answered seriously. Within a few minutes they were discussing whether the household had a pot broad enough for something Na’ar proposed to cook, and Elspeth was setting limits on the amount of fat he might use.

Riarhin sat beside Iona. The girl had an old ribbon wound around her wrist, which she unwound whenever a dish approached and retied when it had passed.

“Why did your mother leave?” she asked.

The table did not stop. Keir was telling Mairi about a horse he had borrowed; Tavish had begun explaining a dispute over a cow in which, from the sound of it, no participant could agree which cow mattered. Riarhin could answer quietly.

“I don’t know.”

“Didn’t she tell you?”

“No.”

Iona considered the omission as though Bea had failed at a duty obvious to any mother.

“Grandmother said she wouldn’t have what was offered.”

“Your grandmother knew her?”

“No. Her grandfather told her.”

“Which grandfather?”

The girl looked towards Tavish. The explanation from the afternoon had not remained as clear as she expected. Riarhin spared her.

“It was before your grandmother.”

“Was it a house?”

“What?”

“What she wouldn’t have.”

Riarhin could see Bea’s room above the street in Silkstrand, the bolt polished on the side where a hand pushed it, the window with one good shutter, the shoe beneath the bed. Her mother had never called any of it an offer.

“I don’t know,” she said again.

Iona slipped the ribbon from her wrist. “I have to go to the Gray Tor.”

“Do you?”

“They have horses.”

“So have you.”

“Different horses.”

Riarhin looked at her. The child’s attention remained on the ribbon.

“Would you like that?”

“The horses.”

“And the rest?”

Iona shrugged. Elspeth, across the table, had turned towards them without joining the conversation. Riarhin recognised the invitation to be useful and disliked how quickly she had begun to understand what use might mean here.

“You can tell me about it tomorrow,” she said.

“I can tell you now.”

“Then tell me now.”

The girl explained the rooms, the woman who would teach her, and another child she had met once and did not like. Riarhin listened until the plates were removed. She asked about the horses. Iona began again with those.

***

Elspeth showed her the upper room after supper.

There was a bed against one wall, a chest against the other, and a window small enough to leave the corners in darkness even while the sky outside remained pale. Fresh water stood beside a basin. Someone had put a folded cloth underneath to protect the wood.

“This was Morven’s room,” Elspeth said. “We use it for guests now.”

“I can sleep below.”

“You can sleep here.”

Riarhin put down her coat. From the yard came Tavish’s laugh, followed by Na’ar explaining something that apparently required both hands and a demonstration with a bucket.

“He’s pleased,” Elspeth said.

“I can hear.”

“He’ll be pleased tomorrow too. Don’t mistake the noise for all of it.”

“I haven’t.”

Elspeth nodded. She touched the edge of the blanket, straightening what did not need straightening.

“If Iona troubles you, send her to me.”

“She asked about my mother.”

“She asks about everything.”

“I didn’t have much to tell her.”

“You can tell her that.”

When Elspeth had gone, Riarhin unfastened her coat and laid the weapons where she could reach them. She checked the window. It opened inward on sound hinges; the drop beyond was onto a lean-to roof and then the yard. Somebody had stacked split wood beneath the eaves.

She washed. The water was still warm.

On the chest lay a little square of woven cloth, yellow at one edge, with a pattern worked in a darker thread. Riarhin had seen a piece like it in Silkstrand. Bea had kept hers under a bowl of pins and used it to wipe a spill of lamp oil, complaining that good cloth was always nearest when bad cloth was wanted.

She lifted the corner, then put it back.

The bed was broad enough for two. She lay on the side nearer the wall at first, then moved towards the middle because no one else was coming and the mattress was better there. Below, someone shut a door. The laughter continued in the next room.

Riarhin listened until she knew which laugh belonged to Tavish.

Chapter Five — Under the Gray Tor

The man on the bridge would not move his cart.

He had brought it there before breakfast and taken the horse out. One wheel stood on the sound approach, the other on the first plank, leaving just enough space for a person to squeeze past on foot. A sack of oats occupied the bed. He sat on it with his hands between his knees.

Tavish stopped at the top of the slope.

“Donnel.”

The man looked up. “You can see it, then.”

“I could see it from the house.”

“Good. Saves me coming up.”

Riarhin stood beside Tavish. She had followed him out expecting a short walk before breakfast and found three people already waiting above the stream. Mairi was among them, carrying a mallet and a bundle of pegs. Elspeth came down the path behind them with her cloak unfastened and an expression that suggested breakfast would now be late for everyone.

“What do you want?” Tavish asked.

Donnel held up his right hand. Two fingers were bound together.

“What we agreed to discuss.”

“We are discussing it.”

“You were sending to the Tor. Then you were receiving visitors.”

“I can do more than one thing.”

“So could I, before this.”

Tavish moved down the slope. Donnel straightened on the sack but did not stand. Riarhin followed far enough to see the water beneath the bridge, brown from a recent rain and running against the far bank where a piece of turf had fallen in.

The repair was visible. Fresh planks crossed older supports, and somebody had wedged a stone against the foot of the nearer upright. Mairi set down her pegs.

“I need him off it,” she told Tavish. “The pack train will be here by noon.”

“They can use the upper ford,” Donnel said.

“They can drown their wool at the upper ford.”

“My cow managed it here.”

Elspeth stopped beside Riarhin. “How is your brother?” she asked him.

“At home.”

“Can he stand?”

“When he has someone to help.”

“I sent—”

“I know what you sent.”

Tavish put a hand on the cart shaft, then took it away. “Get down. We’ll talk above.”

“We’ve talked above.”

Riarhin looked at the empty trace beside the wheel. A horse could be put back into the shafts and the cart pulled clear in less time than they had already spent arguing. Tavish had men. The decision to leave the cart where it was interested her more than the cart itself.

She said, “Who took the due?”

Donnel’s eyes moved to her.

“And who are you?”

“Riarhin.”

“Does that answer?”

“She’s kin,” Tavish said.

“Is she the queen’s kin too?”

“No,” Riarhin said.

“The man at the alehouse had a different account.”

“He often does,” Tavish said. “You can dispute my family after the road is open.”

Donnel sat back. “I paid your man. He waved us over. My brother went down with the third beast, and you can ask him about it when he can get out of bed.”

“Which man?” Riarhin asked.

“Muir.”

“Was Muir there when it broke?”

“Yes.”

“Then fetch him.”

The silence lasted just long enough for her to recognise what she had done. Elspeth turned her head. Tavish looked at Riarhin, then at the cart.

“I sent for him already,” he said.

***

Muir was seventeen and had washed his face without washing his neck.

He came down the path beside Keir, looking first at Tavish and then at Donnel. Riarhin moved away from the bridgehead to give him room. He took the space gratefully and stood where he could look at the water rather than at the injured hand.

“You took the crossing money,” Tavish said.

“Yes.”

“Before or after you saw the bank had gone?”

“Before.”

Donnel laughed once. Muir went red.

“The bank went after,” he said. “I heard it.”

“What did you hear?” Riarhin asked.

“A crack.”

“From the bank?”

“From under.”

She looked at Mairi. The woman had put her mallet down and was listening with a carpenter’s stillness.

“Which side were you standing on?”

Muir pointed. The place was now occupied by a pile of lifted planks.

“And the cattle?”

“The first two were over. The next one stopped. Donnel’s brother hit her and she went sideways.”

“He touched her,” Donnel said.

“I saw.”

“He touched her.”

Tavish held up a hand. “We can disagree about the stick afterwards. Did you tell them to stop?”

Muir looked at the water.

“Did you?”

“I said wait.”

“When?”

“When I heard it.”

Donnel climbed off the cart. His good hand shook as he caught the shaft.

“You said she was safe. You said you’d had a wagon over.”

“I had.”

“In the morning.”

“Yes.”

“Before the rain.”

The boy’s mouth opened. Riarhin could see him trying to preserve an answer he had given before anybody had been hurt. She knew the impulse. She had protected worse answers with considerably more skill.

Mairi picked up her mallet.

“I told you the upper bank was soft,” she said to Muir.

“You said keep wheels away.”

“I said heavy traffic.”

“You said wheels.”

She stopped. Riarhin watched the woman return to the conversation in her own head and discover what she could no longer swear she had said.

“I meant heavy traffic,” Mairi answered at last.

Donnel turned on Tavish. “Your road. Your boy. Your money.”

Tavish stood with his boots in the mud. Riarhin could have told him that nothing would be improved by letting the man say it again. She could also see that he was going to let him.

“My brother is in bed,” Donnel said. “His wife’s taking the beasts out before light. I can’t close this hand. Tell me which part you mean to mend with your pegs.”

***

They brought the cart clear without a horse.

Na’ar arrived while Tavish and Donnel were still arguing over where it should stand. He waited through another exchange about the bridge, then put his shoulder to the back and asked Muir to lift the shafts.

Donnel objected. Na’ar stopped pushing.

“It is a good cart,” he said. “I will not break it.”

“That isn’t—”

“Where would you like it?”

The question required Donnel to choose between continuing the dispute and getting his cart out of the wet. He pointed towards the grass. Na’ar nodded to Muir and they moved it there.

Mairi went to work.

Tavish asked Donnel to come to the house. This time he came, carrying his bound hand against his chest. Riarhin followed with Keir, who had made no offer to take charge.

“Is this your judgement?” she asked him quietly.

“It may be Lady Uria’s.”

“Isn’t Tavish to settle it?”

“If they agree. If they don’t, someone must.”

“Does someone need a cart on a bridge before they hear it?”

Keir looked at her. “You were heard at Valora. Donnel has a cart.”

She disliked him a little less for the answer.

Inside, Elspeth put food on the table. Donnel said he was not hungry. She set the bowl before him anyway, then took one for herself and began to eat. The others followed. After a few minutes Donnel picked up his spoon with his left hand.

Riarhin watched him struggle. She resisted moving the bowl closer. He managed it himself.

Tavish named two cattle in compensation and offered men to help his brother’s household until the man could walk. Donnel asked for four. Elspeth stopped eating.

“Four breeding beasts?” she said.

“You’ve seen the cow we lost.”

“I have. I’ve also seen what two of ours will leave us through winter.”

“My brother—”

“Needs help. We can send it. Taking the milk from another child won’t mend his leg.”

Donnel set down his spoon. “Then you should have closed the road.”

“Yes,” Tavish said.

The word came without preparation. Elspeth turned towards him.

“I knew Mairi was concerned. I left Muir to decide what her warning meant because I wanted the pack train through before the water rose. He was standing there with men asking whether they could cross. He answered as I’d left him to answer.”

Muir stared at the table.

Tavish rubbed his thumb along its edge. “Two beasts. Work at your brother’s steading. And you keep what remains of the cow.”

“Three.”

Elspeth’s face tightened. Tavish looked at her, waited, and received a small movement of her head.

“One now,” she said. “The other two after the calves are weaned. I won’t have them driven before.”

Donnel considered. He looked towards Keir, who had heard every word.

“And no due for my brother’s herd this year.”

Tavish opened his mouth.

Riarhin could almost hear the joke he had prepared, a remark about people discovering additional injuries when compensation was mentioned. He swallowed it. She liked him better for that than she had for the warmth of his greeting.

“For his herd,” he said. “You don’t begin buying your neighbours’ beasts and calling them his.”

Donnel nodded.

Keir asked each man whether he accepted what had been said. They answered. Elspeth began discussing which day the first animal could be brought down, and the room slowly became a place in which people could finish breakfast.

***

Riarhin found Tavish outside later, trying to persuade Mairi to leave the broken plank where he could see it.

“You’ll want to mend something with it,” she said.

“I might.”

“That’s why I’m taking it.”

She shouldered the wood and carried it away. Tavish watched her go.

“You let him have three,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

“He would have taken two.”

“He might.”

“And you’d promised work.”

“I heard myself.”

She folded her arms. In the yard, Na’ar had attracted a small audience by showing Muir how to keep a frightened animal from turning into the rest of the herd. He was doing it with three stones and a stick. Iona kept moving one of the stones to make the explanation more difficult.

“I’ve no right to offer advice,” Riarhin said.

“You have every right. You’re staying in my house.”

“That’s a strange reason.”

“If you tell me I’m a fool, I can tell you the bed leaks.”

“Does it?”

“No. I want you to stay.”

She looked at him. He had said it easily, then gone on watching Na’ar, leaving her to decide whether the answer required anything.

“I could have moved the cart,” she said.

“I know.”

“So could your men.”

“Yes.”

“And then?”

“He’d have moved it back. Or fetched his cousins. Or let me keep the road open and made certain nobody used it without hearing how I paid for it.”

Tavish put his hands in his pockets.

“I’ll want him at the watch fire when the next trouble comes. He’s very good at seeing things he dislikes.”

“He dislikes you.”

“At present.”

Keir came out of the house carrying his travelling cloak. Tavish’s smile returned at once, accompanied by an offer of a better horse for the first part of the journey. Keir declined the horse, accepted food for the road, and asked whether he should expect Iona before the first frost.

Elspeth stood in the doorway behind him.

“Yes,” Tavish said.

“She’ll be well received.”

“I know.”

“The lady asks that the road agreement wait until she has seen it.”

Tavish’s hands came out of his pockets.

“You have my answer.”

“I do.”

Keir bowed to Elspeth and went towards his horse. Riarhin watched Tavish look at the repaired bridge below the yard.

“What agreement?” she asked.

***

Elspeth brought it out after the midday meal.

The paper had been folded and unfolded enough for the writing at one crease to begin wearing away. A factor at Bride’s Causey, acting through O’Ruarc connections, proposed repairs to the approach, regular grain deliveries, and payment for men keeping watch on the road. Tavish showed Riarhin the sum, then the line beneath it.

“They want their own officer to hear disputes,” he said.

“Concerning their traffic.”

“Concerning the road.”

She read again. He was right.

“Lady Uria won’t have it.”

“Would you?”

“I’d have a bridge that stays up.”

Elspeth sat beside him. “And grain we know we can buy before the snow.”

“Then change the line,” Riarhin said.

“They say the line pays for the rest.”

Outside, the pack train had begun crossing. A bell sounded irregularly as the first animal shook its head. Mairi stood beside the approach, waving each beast towards the sound part of the ground.

Riarhin looked at the paper. The clerk’s hand was ordinary. She knew men in Valora who could find the clerk who had written it, and others who could have the proposal amended without ever seeing the bridge. Tavish had not asked her to name them.

“I could speak to someone,” she said.

Elspeth glanced up. Tavish left his hand on the table.

“Would you want to?”

“If it helped.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

She looked through the open door at Na’ar, who was helping a drover free a strap caught beneath a pannier. It was a task whose end could be seen from its beginning. She wished Tavish had given her one like it.

“I don’t know,” she said.

“Then don’t promise yet.”

He folded the agreement and handed it to Elspeth. She put it away with the family book.

Riarhin went down to the crossing. Mairi gave her a rope to hold. For the rest of the afternoon nobody asked her for anything beyond keeping it taut.

***

In the morning Tavish took her to the older house.

He had called it that at breakfast, as if she already knew which house he meant, and Elspeth had made him explain while she cut bread. The Rockes had lived farther up the glen before the present holding grew large enough to require them nearer the road. Some of the old walls remained. The rooms had been put to other uses.

“Bea lived there?” Riarhin asked.

“So I was told.”

“By whom?”

“My mother, who had it from hers. We can begin again with the book if you wish, but it will be a fine day wasted indoors.”

She went to fetch her coat.

The path climbed beside a stream too narrow to appear on a lowland map. It made enough noise to keep them from speaking easily until they crossed on a slab set between two rocks. Tavish tested the slab before putting his weight on it and warned her about the near edge. Riarhin had already chosen where to step. She thanked him anyway.

Above the crossing the trees gave way to ground cropped close by sheep. The wind had room to move here. It lifted his coat and brought her the smell of wet stone, peat smoke, and something flowering low in the grass whose name she did not know. She asked him. He gave a name, reconsidered it, and said Elspeth would know whether it was the same plant his mother had used for a cough.

“Did it work?”

“On the cough, eventually. On my complaints, at once.”

The house lay in the fold beyond the rise. Its upper wall had fallen and been rebuilt lower, beneath a roof which did not follow the old pitch. A woman stood outside rinsing a wooden vessel. She straightened when she saw Tavish and told him the promised lime had not come.

“It will.”

“Before the wall does?”

He went to look where she pointed. Riarhin stayed beside the doorway. The lintel was low enough that her mother would have needed to bow her head. She tried to imagine Bea doing it and discovered how much of the movement she had been borrowing from doorways in Silkstrand.

“This is my cousin,” Tavish said when he returned. “Beatrix’s daughter.”

The woman wiped her hands on her apron. She looked at Riarhin’s face, then at Tavish, giving him an opportunity to admit a joke.

“He’s telling it properly,” Riarhin said.

“Well.” The woman put the vessel upside down on its stand. “You’ll want to see the room.”

“I don’t know which room.”

“The one everybody asks about.”

Inside, milk stood in shallow pans on a shelf built across an old window. Riarhin waited for her eyes to adjust. The air was cool and smelled cleanly sour; water ticked somewhere behind the wall. The woman moved a stool so she could enter without brushing the pans, then pointed towards a recess almost filled by sacks.

“They say she used to sit there.”

“Beatrix?”

“The girl who went away.”

Tavish stopped at the threshold. Riarhin could see the effort with which he refrained from improving the answer.

“Do they say what she did while she sat?”

The woman laughed. “Wanted to go away, I expect. If you move that sack, you can see where the window was.”

Riarhin moved it. There was an older sill under the new shelf, worn smooth in the middle. The opening above had been narrowed, and beyond it she could see little but a strip of hillside. At a different height, before the lower roof was built, it might have looked towards the road.

She laid her hand on the stone.

It was cold. She had half expected something else, though she could not have named it without sounding foolish. A handprint, a recognition, the possibility of being for one instant where Bea had been without needing to invent the woman looking out. The stone offered none of it.

“She may not have sat here at all,” Tavish said.

“Then why did you bring me?”

“Because this is where we can come.”

The answer took the anger out of her before she had found anything fair to do with it. He had brought her to a place still standing. He had not promised to bring her mother into it.

Outside, the woman resumed her washing. Riarhin heard the vessel strike its stand and thought of Bea setting down a cup on the sill in their room. The sound was close enough to begin the memory. Nothing else matched: not the air, not the thickness of the walls, not the distance between this house and the next inhabited window.

“She liked streets,” Riarhin said.

“Your mother?”

“She liked to hear people going past. She complained when they woke her.”

“That sounds like liking streets.”

“She could tell which man was coming up our stair by the way he stopped at the landing. I thought everyone could do it.”

Tavish touched the edge of the shelf, careful not to disturb the milk. “Perhaps she learned it somewhere crowded.”

“Perhaps.”

Riarhin replaced the sack. On the way out she looked at the stone once more and saw a place where somebody had spilled milk and wiped it away imperfectly. She followed Tavish out.

***

They ate above the house, sitting where a fallen wall broke the wind.

Tavish had brought cheese and a little knife whose handle had been mended with wire. He cut her a piece, tasted his own, and complained that Elspeth had packed the good cheese for a walk when there was perfectly serviceable cheese for people who were already hungry. He continued eating it with evident pleasure.

“Were you expecting someone different?” Riarhin asked.

“When?”

“When I came.”

“I wasn’t expecting you.”

“After you knew who I was.”

He put the bread between them and considered.

“I thought you might want something.”

“What?”

“Land. Money. A name spoken in the right hearing. People generally begin with the relationship before telling me which bit of it they need.”

“Would that have changed the welcome?”

“It would have changed the conversation.”

She looked down towards the house. The woman had spread cloth on the grass to dry, weighing the corners with stones. Tavish owed her lime. Her mother had once lived in some part of a building now kept useful by a woman who had no reason to care whether Beatrix had liked the view.

“Is there land that ought to have been hers?”

“There was land used to make arrangements for people who married, people who stayed, people who had more children than the house could feed. What ought to have happened to it depends on whose account you’re hearing. I can’t tell you there is a little field kept empty all these years.”

“I don’t want a field.”

“I have noticed that doesn’t stop people asking whether one belongs to them.”

Riarhin smiled despite herself. He took another piece of cheese.

“If you do want something, ask,” he said. “I may tell you I haven’t got it.”

“And if I stay?”

“Then we shall eventually quarrel about something less interesting.”

“You make it sound easy.”

“No. I make it sound ordinary. Elspeth has accused me of confusing the two.”

The wind moved across the low plants. Riarhin ate slowly, letting the sharp cheese and the bread occupy her while she looked at the country around them. She could walk out of it whenever she chose. The paths were open; nothing held her except the possibility of being expected at a table and the unfamiliar fear of disappointing people who had not hired her.

Tavish wrapped what remained.

“Come. If we don’t remind the lime man, she’ll think I brought you to distract her.”

“Did you?”

“I had hoped to accomplish both.”

They went down together. At the house Riarhin asked the woman her name and made herself listen to the answer without searching it for any connection to Bea.

Chapter Six — What She Took With Her

Iona wanted the red clasp.

Elspeth had laid two on the bed and explained why either would serve. One was plain brass, polished that morning; the other had a small dark stone and a pin whose point had been turned safely inward. The red clasp lay in a separate box. Its spring had broken.

“Then mend it,” Iona said.

“Mairi has work.”

“She mends things.”

“Yes. For people who need them.”

“I need it.”

Riarhin stood in the doorway with an armful of folded linen. She had been carrying the same linen between two rooms because nobody had agreed which chest would travel. Tavish favoured the larger one. Elspeth pointed out that the larger one required two people whenever it was moved, and Iona had declared that she would carry it herself.

“What is it?” Riarhin asked.

Elspeth showed her.

The enamel had chipped beside the hinge. There was nothing unusual in the pattern, a little red leaf worked around a brass centre, but Riarhin recognised the way the leaf narrowed where it met the stem. Bea’s pin box had carried the same sort of leaf on its lid. Hers had been blue.

“It belonged to someone in the family,” Elspeth said. “Iona has decided that makes it suitable.”

“Grandmother wore it.”

“Once. When you were very small.”

“I remember.”

Riarhin set down the linen and took the clasp. The spring had cracked through rather than merely slipped. It could be made into a fixed pin, though doing so would spoil the back.

“You’d have to change it,” she said.

“Then change it,” Iona said.

Elspeth gave her a look. The girl corrected herself with visible labour.

“Please.”

Riarhin returned the clasp. “Ask Mairi what it would take.”

“She’ll say I have to wait.”

“She might.”

The girl took the box and went out. Elspeth watched her cross the yard before she lifted the linen.

“She thinks I can make the day farther away if we haven’t finished packing.”

“Can you?”

“For a day.”

Riarhin looked at the two serviceable clasps left on the bed.

“When do you send her?”

“After the next market. Tavish will ride with her.”

“And you?”

“I’ll go as far as the upper road.”

“Why not all the way?”

Elspeth folded the top sheet again, needlessly aligning the edges. “Because someone must stay. Because I’ve been before. Because she’ll look at me at the gate and I’ll want to bring her home.”

She set the sheet down.

“Choose whichever answer you find useful.”

***

They found the account beneath the clasp box.

Elspeth had asked Riarhin to empty the old chest so that its hinges could be checked. There were winter gloves in it, a broken buckle, a bundle of letters bound with thread, and three folded sheets whose edges had become soft as cloth. Riarhin lifted them together. A name on the outside stopped her.

Beatrix.

She put the other things aside and unfolded the first sheet.

It concerned a horse. Somebody had paid to recover it from a carrier south of Bride’s Causey, and somebody else had refused to pay the carrier for the days it had eaten his fodder. Bea’s name appeared beside the original taking. A second hand had added returned sound and a third had crossed out a sum.

Riarhin sat on the edge of the bed.

Elspeth came back with a brush, saw what she was holding, and closed the door behind her.

“May I?” Riarhin asked.

“Of course.”

The next sheet listed clothes. Most had been left behind. The fine blue gown had been cut down afterwards for another member of the household; a pair of shoes had gone missing, along with a silver-backed brush and a small sum whose owner expressed greater indignation about the taking than the amount seemed to justify.

She thought of the shoe beneath Bea’s bed.

It could not have been the same pair. Years had passed; leather wore out, fashion changed, and her mother had owned other blue shoes before the ones Riarhin remembered. The recognition had come before any of those reasonable objections. She let it remain a little longer.

“What did they say about her?”

Elspeth sat beside the chest.

“Morven said she took a horse and went south.”

“I can see that.”

“She said there was a place offered for her in another house. A good one, according to the people doing the offering.”

“A marriage?”

“I don’t know.”

Riarhin read the clothes again.

“She left the good gown.”

“Perhaps it was difficult to ride in.”

“Perhaps.”

The last sheet was shorter. A payment had been made to a man sent to find her, with a further amount allowed for his return. He had returned without her. No reason had been entered.

Riarhin put her finger beside the blank space.

“Did anyone write after?”

“Not in there. Tavish would know if there’s more.”

“He was born long afterwards.”

“Yes.”

Elspeth left it there. Riarhin heard her own words again, as though the lapse of years were somebody’s negligence.

“I’m sorry.”

“You needn’t be.”

The brush lay between them. Elspeth picked it up and began cleaning dust from the seam of the empty chest, leaving Riarhin the papers and something else to listen to.

***

Na’ar came in carrying a pan he had borrowed from Mairi.

Tavish followed him, protesting that the pan had belonged to his household before Mairi took it to mend. Na’ar replied that she had kept it for two years, which made the ownership uncertain. They were still arguing when they saw Riarhin at the table with the sheets laid before her.

Tavish pulled out a chair.

“What have you found?”

“A horse.”

He looked at the first page. “Ah.”

“You knew?”

“I knew she took one. My mother told the story when I borrowed a pony without asking.”

“Was she pleased with her?”

“With Beatrix? It depended on what part she was telling.”

Na’ar set down the pan and went back outside. Riarhin had not asked for privacy. She was grateful that he had supplied it without making her ask.

“Tell it,” she said.

Tavish read the page before answering.
