“Fergus said his aunt went off with a man from the south. My mother said Fergus had always preferred that version because it left someone to blame who wasn’t kin. When she was angry with me, she said Beatrix thought a wish was a reason. When she wasn’t, she said the horse knew the road better than the man sent after her.”

“Was there a man?”

“Sent after her? Yes.”

“With her.”

“I don’t know.”

“Did she choose the house they offered?”

“I don’t know that either.”

Tavish put the page down. His pleasure in answering had gone; he wanted now to give her an answer good enough for the journey she had made.

“She was a Rocke,” he said. “If she went, I imagine she had her reasons.”

Riarhin looked at him sharply.

“So would anyone.”

He accepted the correction. “Yes.”

She ran her thumb along the softened fold. The fold was nearly worn through. There was nothing about why Bea had gone.

Tavish leaned forward.

“What was she like?”

The question startled her more than the account had.

She could have described Bea exactly. The height, the hair, the fair skin that took colour quickly at the throat, the little crease beside her mouth when she was trying not to laugh. She could have repeated every word of a conversation at a window on a morning when she was seven. None of those things seemed to answer what he had asked.

“She could make people stay,” Riarhin said.

Tavish waited.

“They’d come intending to leave. She’d ask something, or begin to tell them something, and they’d sit down again.”

“That sounds useful.”

“It was her work.”

The hardness in her voice surprised him. She saw it land and wished she had chosen another answer.

“She was kind to me,” she added.

“I’m glad.”

“She could be cross. She was cross when I broke a bottle she’d told me not to touch. I thought she’d be pleased that I’d been able to reach it.”

Tavish smiled cautiously.

“Iona thinks much the same about everything above her head.”

Riarhin looked towards the door. “Bea laughed afterwards. I heard her in the next room. She didn’t want me to know.”

“Did you tell her?”

“No.”

She could hear the laugh now, muted by the wall and the hand her mother had put over her mouth. The bottle had held scent. For days the corner of the room smelled of something they could not afford to replace.

Tavish sat with her until she folded the papers.

***

Mairi agreed to mend the clasp if Iona would sort the small nails.

There were more nails than the girl expected. By the time Riarhin found her, she had divided them into three piles and was quietly moving the bent ones beneath the straight ones to make the work appear further advanced.

“Mairi will see,” Riarhin said.

“She doesn’t need all of them.”

“Then ask how many she needs.”

“She said all.”

Riarhin sat on the low wall beside her. The yard smelled of hot fat. Na’ar had persuaded Elspeth to let him cook and Tavish to cut things, and the division of work had left Tavish with the onions and a complaint about his eyes.

Iona lifted a bent nail and turned it between finger and thumb.

“Did you like being sent away?”

“I wasn’t sent.”

“Did you like going?”

“Sometimes.”

“Where did you sleep?”

“Different places.”

“Did you know where before?”

“Sometimes.”

The girl looked dissatisfied. Riarhin recognised the answer she wanted and could not give it.

“At the Tor,” she said, “you’ll know where you’re sleeping.”

“I know.”

“And who will feed you.”

“I know.”

“What don’t you know?”

Iona picked at a fleck of rust. “Who I’ll tell when something happens.”

Riarhin sat still.

Behind the shutter of Bea’s room, on a morning she had carried for years without choosing to revisit, the house had gone on making the ordinary sounds. Somebody downstairs asked for hot water. A chair scraped. Riarhin had a cut on one finger from a loose bit of metal and no one to show it to who would know how it had happened before she explained.

“You’ll tell your mother,” she said.

“She won’t be there.”

“You can write.”

“I write badly.”

“She can read badly.”

Iona smiled despite herself. “Can she?”

“For you, probably.”

The girl began sorting again. Riarhin took a handful of nails and straightened their points into the same direction on the wall.

“You’ll have someone there,” she said. “It may take a little while to know who.”

“Will you be here when I come back?”

She had not meant to arrive at that question. She put down a nail, finding its place among the others with unnecessary care.

“I expect so.”

“That isn’t yes.”

“No.”

Iona waited. Riarhin looked at the house, at the open door through which Elspeth had just emerged to rescue something from Tavish’s knife.

“I’ll tell you if I mean to go,” she said.

The girl accepted that. They finished the nails together.

***

Ailsa brought the cloth after the rain.

She arrived with the bundle under her coat and her eldest son walking behind her, carrying nothing and looking aggrieved about it. When Elspeth asked why she had not given him the bundle, Ailsa said he had complained so thoroughly about being asked that she had decided to let him enjoy the whole walk without anything useful to do.

“I said I had work,” he protested.

“And now you can begin it.”

He went towards Mairi’s shed. Tavish called after him to ask whether he had learned to hold the hammer in the hand he preferred yet. The boy made a gesture Riarhin could interpret without being told and continued walking.

Ailsa laughed. She had a broad mouth, brown hair escaping its fastening, and a red mark on one wrist where the bundle had pressed. She set the cloth down and asked whether she might sit before anyone began valuing it.

“Please,” Elspeth said. “Have you eaten?”

“Badly. It was my own cooking.”

Riarhin moved a chair nearer the fire. Ailsa thanked her, then pulled it a little farther away again.

“I’ve been walking. Give me time to want that.”

They opened the cloth together. It had been part of a larger length damaged along one edge, and Ailsa intended to sell the sound pieces separately. Elspeth wanted enough for a warm undergown for Iona. Riarhin held one end while Ailsa measured, keeping the weave straight between her hands.

“You can let it rest on the table,” Ailsa told her.

“It doesn’t trouble me.”

“It troubles the cloth. You’re stretching the edge.”

Riarhin lowered it. She heard Tavish begin to laugh and gave him a look which only improved his morning.

Ailsa’s attention had already returned to the measure. She did not make much of correcting her. Once the length was agreed she cut it cleanly and wrapped the smaller piece in the dry covering from her bundle. Then she put the knife away and accepted food, her shoulders dropping with the relief of being finished with something.

“Beatrix’s daughter,” she said to Riarhin.

“Yes.”

“I wondered when he’d stop telling people and let them meet you.”

Tavish protested that they could have come at any time. Ailsa asked whether he had considered that other people might have days which failed to arrange themselves around his delight. He offered her more cheese. She took it, and the argument ended as comfortably as it had begun.

Riarhin liked her voice. The vowels opened where her mother’s had narrowed after years in Silkstrand; she understood every word and had to listen for the shape of some sentences. Ailsa watched her listening.

“Am I speaking too fast?”

“No.”

“People from the south usually tell me that before asking Elspeth what I’ve said.”

“She’ll charge,” Elspeth said.

“For accuracy or discretion?”

“Whichever you need more.”

Ailsa turned her chair towards Riarhin, letting the others begin their own conversation about Iona’s things. She asked where she had come from most recently, and Riarhin said Taharka, then added Valora because that seemed to be the place people actually meant when they asked.

“I meant where you were,” Ailsa said. “Tavish has told us the queen part.”

“What has he told you?”

“That she asked you to stay. Three evenings. He gives the number whenever he can find room for it.”

“I did stay.”

“Did you like it?”

The question was offered without lowering her voice or leaning forward. Riarhin found she could answer it in the same manner.

“Some of it.”

“Which part?”

“The people I went to see.”

“And the queen?”

“She was one of them.”

Ailsa looked pleased by the correction. She asked about Taharka next, and this time Riarhin did not replace the place with the one she imagined must matter more. She described a morning in Adur-Gah, the heat gathering in stone before it reached the shaded rooms, and a woman who had insisted that going out without eating was an insult to the work of being kept alive.

“You liked her,” Ailsa said.

“Yes.”

“Do you write?”

“Sometimes.”

“That is a useful word. It can mean once.”

Riarhin glanced at her. There was humour there, but no invitation to confess. Ailsa took another mouthful and allowed the answer to remain unfinished.

When she rose to leave, Riarhin offered to carry the remaining cloth.

“It doesn’t weigh much.”

“I was going out.”

“Were you?”

“No.”

Ailsa smiled. “Then you may as well.”

***

Her house stood lower down the glen, beyond a bend where alder trees leaned over the water.

She walked quickly on the open path and slowed at the wet stones without making a production of the care. Riarhin adjusted to her pace. There was a way to cross the little stream farther up without wetting a boot, but Ailsa took the plainer route and accepted the water at her ankles with an annoyed sound.

“I always think it will be lower.”

“It rained all morning.”

“Yes. I was there.”

Riarhin laughed. Ailsa looked back, satisfied with something, and went on.

From the rise above the house they could see her son on the other path, heading towards the coast road with a tool wrapped under one arm.

“His father,” she said before Riarhin asked. “He has a repair to show him.”

“He lives away?”

“Now. We make better parents from separate hearths. He can admire the boy’s work without hearing me ask why the good knife has been used on leather again.”

“And the boy?”

“Has learned to ask each of us for the thing the other refused. He thinks that means we’ve stopped speaking.”

They watched him disappear behind a wall.

Riarhin wondered whether she ought to say something about the separation, whether there was a form of courtesy here which required regret. Ailsa was already describing the leaking roof of the shed beside her house and the cloth she had lost to it. Regret about that was welcome and specific. Riarhin supplied it.

Inside, the rooms were small, warm, and arranged around work which refused to remain in the room intended for it. Lengths of dyed cloth lay folded on the bed. A pair of wooden forms stood on a stool. The table held a bowl of apples and a boot with its sole turned towards the light.

“I can make you a space,” Ailsa said.

“You needn’t clear everything.”

“I couldn’t if I wanted.”

She moved the boot, left the apples, and gave Riarhin the chair with the sound back. Riarhin put the bundle where she was told and sat. She knew how to be a guest at a fire, on a road, in rooms where someone expected her to be entertaining or dangerous. Here she could see nothing she was meant to accomplish before leaving.

Ailsa took a small knife and began cutting the bruises from an apple. She ate the good slices as she worked, pushing the dish towards Riarhin whenever she had more than she wanted at once. Outside the window the water sounded loud enough to disguise approaching footsteps. Riarhin noticed that, then made herself attend to the woman’s hands instead.

There was a scar along one finger. The nail had grown unevenly beyond it.

“What happened?”

“I held something I should have put down.”

“What?”

“A pan. It’s a disappointing story.”

“I don’t mind.”

“Good. I have a number of those.”

They sat until the light began to change. Ailsa spoke about a buyer who had praised every part of a length of cloth except the amount she intended to charge for it. Riarhin told her about a merchant in Silkstrand who had called a price insulting until the moment she agreed to it, at which point it became evidence of his generosity. Ailsa wanted to know whether she had bought the thing.

“Yes.”

“Then he wasn’t entirely wrong.”

Riarhin threw an apple pip into the dish. It missed. Ailsa let her retrieve it without comment.

At the door she asked whether Riarhin would come again.

“If you want.”

“I have just asked.”

“Yes.”

“That was easier.”

Riarhin walked back up the glen without using the higher crossing. Her boots were already wet, and for once she had no wish to prove that there had been a better way.

***

Tavish took her to the Gray Tor two days later.

The visit had begun as an errand concerning Iona’s chest and acquired Riarhin when Lady Uria sent word that she would be glad to see the newly arrived cousin. Tavish repeated the invitation while pretending it was no more consequential than asking someone to eat. Elspeth told him to stop doing that in front of people who could hear him.

They rode through a country in which the paths knew more than the road. A broad track offered easy going until it reached wet ground; narrow ways climbed around it and met again beyond the rise. Tavish named the holdings they passed and the people entitled to object if their cattle wandered onto the wrong grass. Riarhin remembered the names imperfectly because he gave three generations where she needed one living person.

At the Tor a boy took their horses. Smoke lay along the inner side of the rampart, finding its way out through spaces between the roofs. Riarhin could smell food and wet wool. Somebody was splitting wood too close to the place where visitors dismounted, and a woman shouted until he moved his block.

Keir met them at the hall. He had changed his boots since Glenruan. She recognised the better leather before she remembered his face.

“The lady is finishing a hearing.”

“Has it begun to finish yet?” Tavish asked.

“Twice.”

They waited near a fire that warmed one side of Riarhin’s coat while leaving the other cold. A child was learning a passage aloud at a nearby table, losing the same word each time. The woman teaching her never supplied it quickly enough to spare her the effort. Riarhin thought of Iona sitting there and discovering that being known by everyone at home did not make a sentence easier to remember elsewhere.

Uria received them in a room which had been made private by hanging heavy cloth across one doorway. Riarhin could still hear the child beyond it. The lady wore a plain coat, and the iron fitting where her hand had been rested against the chair arm. When she moved, its little bell touched the metal without sounding.

“Beatrix’s daughter.”

“So Tavish keeps telling people.”

“He has been pleased to find something in his family which I haven’t already heard about.”

Tavish bowed with such gravity that Riarhin nearly laughed. Uria saw the effort and indicated a chair.

“I hope you will like the glen.”

“I do.”

“Enough to stay?”

“For a while.”

“Most people begin with that.”

Uria asked where she had travelled, what condition the southern roads were in, whether the peace had returned animals to the fields or merely taken the soldiers out of them. Riarhin answered what she knew. When she said she had seen only one road through a district, Uria accepted the boundary and asked about that road.

It was easier than the welcome at court had been. That ease made Riarhin attentive when the questions turned.

“If men come after you, whom will Tavish be sheltering?”

Tavish opened his mouth. Uria looked at him, and he left the answer to her.

“A guest,” Riarhin said.

“And whose quarrel?”

“Mine.”

“Until it reaches his door.”

There was no threat in the voice. Riarhin found herself wanting one, something she could push against instead of being obliged to consider the question fairly.

“I won’t make his house a meeting place for other people’s wars.”

“Your own?”

“I have finished the one I was in.”

“I’ve heard men say that with their enemy’s son outside.”

Riarhin looked at the iron hand. She knew nothing of the injury beyond the fact of it, and would not ask now merely to change which of them had to answer.

“If someone asks you for me, hear what they say. Let me answer it.”

“Here?”

“If this is where it must be answered.”

Uria studied her. Then she turned to Tavish and asked whether he had sufficient cattle left to settle another matter like the bridge. He said he hoped not to have another matter like the bridge. She waited until he gave a useful answer.

Riarhin listened. The welcome remained; it had acquired weight. Tavish was not being told to send her away, and that made his willingness to keep her more difficult to treat as a pleasant noise belonging to a generous man.

When they rose, Uria asked whether she had seen her mother’s old house.

“Yes.”

“Did it help?”

“I don’t know.”

“That’s often what the old houses do.”

Outside, Tavish went to look at the room where Iona would sleep. Riarhin waited at the threshold while he asked about blankets and the draught from the window. She could see the bed his daughter would be given, one among several, a chest already standing beside another girl’s place. That girl had tied a strip of blue cloth around the latch to recognise her own.

Iona would bring red.

Tavish came back looking more tired than he had after Uria’s questions. On the ride home he began telling her which of the hills had been used for watching the southern road. She let him talk until he ran out of names.

“You can change your mind,” she said.

“About what?”

“Having me.”

“I expect I could.”

“I wouldn’t hold it against you.”

He slowed his horse and waited for her to come alongside.

“Must I ask you twice?”

She looked at him.

“To stay. Must I ask every time someone makes it clear that I meant something by it?”

“No.”

“Good.”

They rode on. At the lower crossing he warned her about the loose slab again, and this time she let him go first.

***

The evening before Iona left, Elspeth asked Riarhin to take the end of a sheet.

They were making up a bed in the little room behind the hearth. Someone coming from the Tor would stay there on the return journey, and the mattress had spent the summer upright against a wall. Tavish had carried it in, struck his head on the lintel, and delivered an account of the offence which brought Iona running to see what had happened. Now he was outside again, inviting the girl to judge whether her chest could possibly contain all the things her mother intended to send.

Elspeth pulled the sheet tight.

“Under your end.”

Riarhin tucked it beneath the mattress. The room smelled of dried herbs and the warm dust shaken from the ticking. There was a little opening through to the kitchen, closed by a wooden shutter; somebody had scratched a game into its sill. Riarhin could have reached through and lifted the latch on the other side. She folded the corner instead.

“Will you use this room often?”

“When people come.”

“And when they don’t?”

“Mostly for things I don’t want to look at.”

Riarhin glanced at the wall where a square of cleaner plaster marked the place the mattress had stood. A pair of boots had been pushed under a stool. One had a hole large enough to admit a finger.

“His mother’s,” Elspeth said, following her look. “He says he will have them mended.”

“For whom?”

“That question has never improved the conversation.”

She spread the blanket with a brisk movement that sent the dust into the last of the light. Riarhin held her corner down. Outside, Tavish was explaining that a girl could surely survive a winter with fewer than three wooden animals, and Iona was naming each animal as though he had proposed sending away three members of the household.

Elspeth sat on the bed to test it.

“He’ll take them all.”

“Does she know?”

“She knows him.”

Riarhin leaned against the wall. Elspeth looked tired. The work of departure had left a loose strand of hair at her cheek and a small raw place beside her thumbnail. She picked at it, noticed herself doing so, and folded her hands.

“Is she frightened?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes. And proud. She wants to go very much until someone begins helping her do it.”

“Will they be kind?”

“Some of them. Some won’t. The woman who teaches the children writes to me. I know her sister.”

The answer gave Riarhin too little. She wanted the names of the unkind ones and the distances between their rooms and Iona’s. Elspeth rose to fetch a pillow.

“You could keep her here,” Riarhin said.

Elspeth stopped with the pillow against her breast.

“For another year.” Riarhin heard herself make the offer smaller. “She is young.”

“She is the same age as the others.”

“That doesn’t mean—”

“No. It doesn’t.” Elspeth put the pillow down. “If you wish to say we are sending her as a pledge, you may. We know what the arrangement is.”

Riarhin looked towards the opening into the kitchen. The shutter was closed. She had made certain of that before speaking, which now seemed worse than failing to consider it.

“I didn’t mean to accuse you.”

“You think we have not considered keeping her beside us. You can begin there.”

Outside, Iona laughed. Tavish had evidently conceded an animal. Riarhin waited until the sound was finished.

“I know what it is to have someone else decide where you go.”

Elspeth’s face altered. She sat down again, slowly, and patted the blanket beside her. Riarhin remained by the wall.

“I know you do,” Elspeth said. “I cannot make that unhappen by keeping my daughter from the people she will have to live among.”

There was no anger in the answer now. Riarhin missed it. Anger would have allowed her to apologise and leave the room while the other woman was still obliged to dislike her.

“She will have friends there,” Elspeth continued. “People who will remember her when she comes to ask for something at thirty. She’ll learn things neither of us can teach her. And there will be days when she wishes we had kept her here. I can tell you that before she goes.”

Riarhin sat on the far edge of the bed. One of the cords creaked beneath her.

“What do you do on those days?”

“Find out what happened. Decide whether she needs fetching or whether she needs to know I have heard her.”

“And if you decide wrongly?”

Elspeth examined the raw place beside her nail.

“I expect to.”

They sat while the light diminished. A man went past the window carrying a sack across his shoulders; its loose corner brushed the sill. Riarhin remembered Bea returning late with the hem of a good gown dark from the street. She had been furious at being left with a neighbour who smelled of onions. Bea had listened to the account, taken off her shoes, and asked whether the neighbour had frightened her or offended her. Riarhin had refused the distinction. Her mother had stayed up with her anyway.

She could recall every word. She had spent years remembering the wrongness of the question.

“I could write to her,” she said.

“She would like that.”

“I don’t know what to write about.”

“Tell her what you have done. She’ll be curious about what we’re doing without her.”

“Even if nothing happens?”

“Especially then. She’ll want to know we’re keeping the place for her.”

Tavish put his head round the door and asked if anyone had seen a wooden cow. He had become answerable for it and was preparing a defence based on its being an exceptionally small cow.

Elspeth shut her eyes. Riarhin bent and looked beneath the bed. There it was, lying on its side beside Morven’s boots, with a thread wound round one leg.

She passed it to him.

“My cousin,” he said. “A woman of miraculous gifts.”

“It was under the bed.”

“A place I have considered several times.”

“From a distance,” Elspeth said.

He withdrew with the cow. Riarhin rose to straighten the blanket where they had sat. Elspeth caught her hand briefly before letting her go.

“Write whatever you like. She’ll answer the parts she finds interesting.”

***

On the morning of the departure, Iona refused breakfast.

She had dressed early and now sat in the yard beside the smaller chest with the red clasp fastened at her shoulder. Mairi had repaired it with a piece of metal whose colour differed from the old brass. The girl turned it towards the wall whenever anyone came close.

Tavish was taking too long over the horses. Elspeth had checked the ties on the chest twice. Riarhin stood by the gate with a piece of bread wrapped in a cloth, waiting until neither parent was speaking.

“For later,” she told Iona.

“I won’t be hungry.”

“You can give it to your father.”

“He’s eaten.”

“He’ll do it again.”

Iona took the bread. Riarhin knelt to retie a lace which the girl had pulled so tight that she would not be able to undo it herself. She knew the knot her own hands were making. Bea had tied it in a shoe while Riarhin complained that they were late, talking all the while about the people they would see until the child forgot to pull her foot away.

Riarhin loosened the loop and drew it flat.

“There.”

“Will it stay?”

“Yes.”

She stood. Elspeth was watching from the doorway, and looked away before Riarhin could decide what her expression asked.

At the upper road the company stopped. Elspeth embraced Iona hard enough to make the girl complain about the clasp. Tavish looked at the horses. Na’ar, who had come as far as the turn, checked a strap that needed no attention.

Then they were moving. Iona turned in the saddle twice. The second time she raised a hand, impatient now to be seen going, and Riarhin raised hers in answer.

They stood until the bend took the riders.

Elspeth wiped her face with the heel of her hand and said there was bread to take out of the oven. Na’ar went with her. Riarhin remained a moment longer, looking at the empty road.

She had promised to say if she meant to go.

For the present she did not.

Chapter Seven — The Place at Table

Five years after the gift, Riarhin woke with Ailsa’s foot pressed against her calf.

The foot was cold. She moved her leg, and Ailsa followed without waking, finding the warmth again with the persistence of someone who had long ago settled that this was permitted. Outside, water struck the stones beneath the window. A shutter knocked once and stopped.

Riarhin lay still.

From here she could see the hook holding her coat, the small cleared space beside Ailsa’s things, the cup which had become hers because it stood nearest the side of the bed she used. Nobody had announced those arrangements. She had noticed each of them after it was made and found a reason not to ask how long it was expected to last.

Ailsa opened one eye.

“If you’re leaving, close the shutter first.”

“I hadn’t moved.”

“You’d begun thinking about it.”

“That makes no sound.”

“It makes the bed lonely.”

Riarhin turned towards her. The face on the pillow was creased from sleep, one eyebrow pressed into an unaccustomed angle. There was a grey strand of hair across Ailsa’s mouth. Riarhin lifted it away, and Ailsa caught her hand before she could withdraw.

“You could also stay.”

“Iona comes home today.”

“She won’t fall off the horse because you spend another quarter hour here.”

“She might.”

“Then give Tavish the pleasure of catching her.”

Riarhin kissed her. Ailsa smiled against her mouth and drew her closer. The window could wait.

***

Later they ate standing because the chairs were occupied.

One held folded cloth, the other a broken basket Ailsa had promised to mend before the younger child returned from the coast. Riarhin offered to move them. Ailsa said she had already counted the pieces of the basket and would lose one if they began improving her arrangements. They ate at the corner of the table instead, sharing a bowl of soft cheese and bread which needed to be held near the fire before it would submit to being cut.

“What did she say about the horse?” Ailsa asked.

“Iona? That there was a horse she wanted to ride.”

“Then expect that horse.”

“Her mother has told her—”

“Yes. I have heard mothers do that.”

Riarhin laughed and took another piece of bread. Ailsa wiped cheese from the knife with her thumb, tasted it, and began describing a journey she intended to make before winter. A buyer beyond Bride’s Causey wanted to see the next lot of cloth in person. She could send samples, but he had been using the difference between a sample and a delivery to reopen prices after the work was done. She meant to stand beside him when he chose.

“How long?”

“Perhaps a fortnight.”

“Alone?”

“For the first part. I’ll join people at the Causey.”

Riarhin set down her bread.

“I could go.”

“You could.”

“The lower road was troublesome last winter.”

“It was. I travelled it.”

“With other people.”

“With my own judgement, which found other people.”

Riarhin looked at her. The tone had remained pleasant. That made it harder to explain why she wanted to argue.

“I meant it would be easier with someone you knew.”

“Would you like to come?”

“Yes.”

“Then say that. Don’t make the road worse until you have to protect me from it.”

She went to the hearth to turn the bread. Riarhin watched the back of her neck, the hair which would escape again when she fastened it, and felt the familiar wish to make a kindness useful enough that it could not be refused. Ailsa had been refusing that form of kindness for years. She accepted other forms readily and had no intention of helping Riarhin confuse them.

“I’d like to come,” Riarhin said.

“Good. You can carry the light bundle while I tell everyone I’ve hired a formidable guard.”

“Only the light one?”

“I wouldn’t want the story to interfere with the cloth.”

They agreed on a day. Riarhin would go as far as the Causey and perhaps farther, depending on who met them there. She found herself considering the journey as something to look forward to. No one at the other end required her to be a person she remembered disliking.

Ailsa watched her reach for the knife with her left hand.

“He’s using that hand again,” she said.

“Your son?”

“Mairi told him she’d charge for everything he spoiled trying to please his father.”

“Will his father object?”

“He’ll say he never required it. Then the boy will be angry that he went to the trouble.”

“Is that what happened with you?”

Ailsa considered the question without taking offence. “Some of it. We mistook being able to bear something for agreeing to bear it forever.”

She put the warmed bread between them.

“I’m not doing that with you.”

Riarhin felt the quick defensive answer rise. She had never asked for forever. She had made no claim on the house, the bed, the woman’s time; she could put all of that between them and leave Ailsa to explain why an affection so carefully free of demands still required this conversation.

Instead she asked, “What do you want?”

“To be asked sometimes. To be told when you mean to go. To have a quarrel without you deciding that leaving would be the most generous answer.”

“I’ve stayed.”

“I know. I like having you.”

Ailsa came around the table and rested her forehead briefly against Riarhin’s. She was shorter; Riarhin had to bend a little to meet her. The small adjustment was so familiar that she made it without noticing until the woman moved away.

“There,” Ailsa said. “Now you can go and be useful to the entire Rocke household. Leave me some of the good weather.”

***

At the door Riarhin paused beside the basket.

One of its ribs had sprung loose of the weave. She could see how it needed to lie but knew that forcing it dry would split the wood. Ailsa caught her looking.

“You may ask.”

“Would you like me to mend it?”

“Tomorrow, when it’s soaked.”

“I’ll come tomorrow.”

“Bring the knife you like. Mine has opinions about willow.”

Riarhin left with a destination for the next day and a journey agreed beyond it. She walked up the glen slowly enough to notice the first leaves caught against the stream’s stones. At a shallow place she stopped to shift a branch that had begun diverting water towards the path. The current took it out of her hand, turned it twice, and carried it under the bridge.

She reached Tavish’s lower field in time to be asked for help with a strap.

That morning, Iona came home on a horse she was not supposed to have ridden.

Riarhin saw her from the lower field. The animal was taller than the pony on which she had gone to the Gray Tor, and the girl sat it with the stiff dignity of somebody who had been told twice to slow down and intended never to admit that she had heard. Her escort followed at a walk.

At the gate, Tavish held the bridle while she dismounted.

“Whose?” he asked.

“Lady Uria’s.”

“I had gathered she owns horses. Did she intend you to be on this one?”

“She said I could ride him in the yard.”

“And the road appeared.”

“The groom said he needed work.”

The escort reached them. He was a patient-looking man with mud on his boots and the long suffering expression of someone whose employer had entrusted him with a child too clever to describe as merely disobedient.

“She had permission to come,” he told Tavish. “The horse was discussed afterwards.”

Elspeth arrived before the answer could become a formal complaint. Iona submitted to an embrace, held herself aloof through the first moment of it, then buried her face in her mother’s shoulder. Riarhin came up from the field carrying a broken strap.

The girl looked over Elspeth’s arm.

“You said you’d be here.”

“I am.”

“I got your letter.”

“Did you?”

“You wrote about a fence.”

“You asked what I was doing.”

Iona laughed. It was lower than the laugh Riarhin had learned to expect, though nothing else about it had changed. She came across and embraced her with enough force to make the strap buckle strike both of them.

“What happened?” she asked, looking at it.

“Your father mended something.”

Tavish put a hand to his chest. “I do a great deal of good here. Nobody remembers it.”

“We’ve written it down,” Elspeth told him. “There was room.”

***

The man from Bride’s Causey waited until the family had gone inside before approaching.

He had arrived that morning with a mule and two samples of grain. Riarhin had seen him lay the samples on the table and watched Tavish compare them with a seriousness he rarely brought to food once it was cooked. The man’s name was Pell. He had given it without a title and then spent breakfast finding occasions to mention the people he knew.

He caught Riarhin beside the well.

“You might help me.”

“With what?”

“Your cousin understands the advantage.”

“Then you don’t need me.”

“He understands it. He finds the terms difficult.”

She set the strap down on the coping. Pell watched her draw water.

“Which terms?”

“The same ones. We cannot make regular deliveries on roads where every quarrel becomes a delay. The officer would hear only matters arising from the traffic.”

“Whose traffic?”

“Covered traffic.”

She drank before replying. “You’ve changed the word.”

“We’ve clarified it.”

“Does it still include everybody who uses the road?”

Pell looked towards the house.

“There are people farther south who would welcome this arrangement.”

“Then sell them grain.”

“Your cousin has bought from us for four winters.”

“At the market.”

“At a price which could be improved.”

A young man came out of the byre carrying a fork. Riarhin waited until he had passed before she said, “Tavish will answer you.”

“He listens to you.”

“Sometimes.”

“You were received at Valora.”

“Some time ago.”

“The queen remembers her friends.”

Riarhin set down the cup. The remark might have been flattery. Pell wore it as flattery, smiling slightly, offering her a place on the advantageous side of his proposal. She wondered whether he knew anything beyond the fact of her visit.

“What did she say?”

He blinked. “I beg your pardon?”

“About me.”

“I meant—”

“You said she remembers. Has she spoken of me?”

“Not to me personally.”

“Then let Tavish answer you.”

She picked up the strap. Tavish had come out carrying Pell’s grain samples and was waiting beside the door.

“Come in,” he told the factor. “Elspeth has found the last account, and I have found a reason to disagree with it. You may yet save me from having to admit she’s right.”

Pell smiled and crossed the yard. Riarhin followed as far as the step, where she had left her coat.

“There is the matter of your daughter’s horse,” Pell said.

Tavish stopped with one hand on the door.

“What matter?”

“Only that Lady Uria’s household enjoys certain arrangements with our people. Credit, deliveries. I should dislike a disagreement here to inconvenience the girl there.”

The bag of grain settled in Tavish’s hand. He laid it on the sill, taking care that the seam lay uppermost.

“Say that again.”

Pell glanced at Riarhin. “You misunderstand me.”

“Then put it plainly.”

A spoon scraped against a bowl inside. Riarhin could hear Iona describing the horse to her mother. Tavish stood clear of the doorway, leaving Pell room to pass; the factor stayed on the step.

“I meant that these things touch more households than yours.”

“Mine is the household you’re speaking to.”

“Yes, of course.”

“Look at me.”

Pell did. Riarhin had seen Tavish wait for men to finish laughing before continuing a story. He gave the factor that same undivided attention. Pell’s hand moved towards his collar and stopped.

“You’ll eat with us,” Tavish said. “You’ll have the bed you were offered. Tomorrow you can take my answer back to your employer. If you have something to say about Iona, say it now, where her mother can hear you.”

“I have nothing to say about your daughter.”

Tavish waited.

“Nothing,” Pell repeated.

From inside, Elspeth called for the grain. Tavish picked up the bag and carried it through. Riarhin moved off the step to let Pell follow. He thanked her twice.

She stayed long enough to hear her cousin tell Iona to leave room for their guest. His voice filled the kitchen again. Pell took the chair he was shown, carefully, with both hands on its arms.

***

Mairi had a boy working the bellows and Na’ar arguing about a pot.

The boy was Ailsa’s eldest, taller now than the doorpost on which his mother had marked his height before he grew too dignified to stand against it. He lifted his left hand in greeting and kept the other on the bellows handle. His ears were round. Riarhin had known him for several years before learning which side of the family had given him the small pale patch above one eyebrow.

Na’ar wanted the pot wide at the base.

“It is wide,” Mairi said.

“Wider.”

“You’ll need two fires.”

“I can make two fires.”

“You’ll need someone to carry it.”

“I can carry it.”

“Then I’ll make you a cart to go with it.”

Riarhin put the strap on the bench. Na’ar turned towards her.

“Tell her.”

“What am I telling her?”

“That the pot is too narrow.”

She looked into it. “What are you cooking?”

“Nothing yet. It is too narrow.”

Mairi laughed. She took the strap, examined Tavish’s repair, and set it aside with an expression of professional sorrow.

“Iona’s back,” Riarhin said.

“So I heard. She has borrowed another horse.”

“Does everyone know?”

“The groom came here first to ask whether I could put a shoe on before her father saw.”

“Did you?”

“No. It didn’t need one.”

Na’ar set the pot down. “She will like the little mare.”

“She’s been told not to ride anything else today.”

“She may look.”

Riarhin knew he had a particular idea of how looking might progress. She considered objecting and decided Elspeth could do it more effectively. He had taught Iona to sit a startled horse without clamping her legs to it, and then spent an afternoon apologising because he had demonstrated the startle before warning her mother.

He had a place at Glenruan that required no explanation through Riarhin. People came to the workroom to find him. He knew whose cattle had been moved and whose son had taken offence at an insult intended for his uncle. Last winter, while she was away for several weeks, he had helped Tavish recover animals driven into the wrong glen and returned with a split lip, a good bridle, and a version of events Elspeth refused to hear a second time.

She liked hearing people call his name across the yard.

She also disliked, occasionally and without cause she cared to examine, arriving at a conversation in which he had already become indispensable.

“Will you eat with us?” he asked.

“I live here.”

“That has not always answered.”

“I’ll eat.”

“Good. There will be enough if Mairi makes the pot.”

“There is already enough pot,” Mairi said.

The boy at the bellows laughed, and Na’ar turned on him with a solemn invitation to explain how much room a hungry man ought to require. The boy said it depended on the man. Mairi told him to stop enjoying himself and fetch the piece she had put beside the door.

He brought a short iron fitting wrapped in cloth. One end had been worked into a loop, with a mark showing where it ought to be bent. Riarhin reached for it when Mairi asked him to hold it steady.

“He can do it,” the woman said.

“I was only—”

“He can do it.”

Riarhin withdrew her hand. The boy set the fitting against the block and took the tool Mairi offered. His left wrist shook a little. He glanced at Riarhin, caught her watching the angle, and altered it before striking.

“Your first place,” Mairi said.

He moved it back. The blow landed well. The next did not. He swore, then looked at her as though the word required more forgiveness than the mark in the metal.

“Again,” she said.

Na’ar leaned against the doorpost with the disputed pot under one arm. The boy struck twice more, examined the result, and brought it to Mairi. She ran a thumb over the edge and told him to take it outside, where he could see it in better light.

Riarhin watched him go.

“Will it hold?”

“Yes.”

“He thought I was correcting him.”

“You were.”

“I hadn’t spoken.”

Mairi put down her tool. “You have a face.”

Na’ar studied the pot as though something fascinating had appeared inside it. Riarhin looked at him until he lifted his eyes.

“You do,” he said.

She went out after the boy. He stood beside the wall, turning the fitting in his hand. The light showed the place where the second blow had bitten too deep. He rubbed it with his thumb when he saw her approaching.

“She says it’s sound,” Riarhin told him.

“I heard.”

“Yes.”

He waited. She had come to make the interruption better and had begun by interrupting him again.

“You had it right before you looked at me.”

He shrugged.

“I shouldn’t have reached.”

“I know how to hold something.”

“I know.”

“You always look as if you’re waiting for me to drop it.”

She considered denying that. Across the yard a hen was dragging a thread from the edge of a sack, pulling back with her whole body after each peck. The boy watched it while he waited for the answer.

“I have been waiting for things to go wrong for a long time,” Riarhin said.

“Do they?”

“Quite often.”

He looked down at the fitting. “They do when you look at me.”

From the workroom Mairi called for him. He wrapped the metal again and went inside, leaving Riarhin by the wall. The hen had freed the thread. It tried eating it, found that unsatisfactory, and went away.

Na’ar came out with the pot.

“Where are you taking that?”

“To put food in it.”

“It’s too narrow.”

“Yes. I shall suffer.”

He waited until she fell into step beside him. The pot knocked against his thigh as they crossed the yard.

“Ailsa will hear about this,” Riarhin said.

“Will she be surprised?”

“Probably not.”

“Then she may tell you something useful.”

Riarhin caught the handle nearest her before it struck him again. He let her take half the weight, though it required them to walk closer and considerably slower than either would have chosen alone. At the kitchen step she waited for him to lift first.

***

Pell stayed for the meal.

Tavish had invited him before he refused the new terms, which made the refusal less convenient for both of them. Iona sat beside her father and listened with a degree of attention Riarhin had not expected. She had brought a folded letter from Lady Uria, and the letter lay beneath Tavish’s cup.

“We’ll buy as we’ve bought,” he told Pell. “At the market, with our own carriage where we can arrange it.”

“The rate will change.”

“The rate always changes.”

“This offer fixes it.”

“For three years.”

“That is three winters you needn’t worry.”

Elspeth looked at the letter under the cup. “The wagons still have to arrive.”

“They have arrived.”

“Yes,” she said. “They have.”

Riarhin watched the concession matter to him. Pell straightened, finding a less practised answer.

“My brother lost a team getting one through before the snow. He paid the replacement men himself because the factor said the delay would cost us the next contract. We’d like a road on which a man can ask a question and get an answer before his horses die.”

Tavish lowered his cup.

“So would I.”

“Then why—”

“Because the officer doesn’t leave when your wagon leaves. And when Donnel puts his cart across the bridge again, I want to answer him before somebody sends for armed men to move it.”

Pell glanced at Riarhin. She returned the glance without helping him.

Iona said, “Lady Uria has armed men.”

The room quieted a little.

“She does,” Tavish said.

“She can move a cart.”

“Yes.”

“Then what’s different?”

Pell looked delighted. Tavish looked at his daughter with a mixture of pride and regret.

“I can go to her,” he said. “She can refuse me, and she often has. But she’ll see me.”

“Will the queen refuse to see you?”

“I haven’t tried.”

Riarhin found herself looking at the table. Three nights. She could still hear the ease with which Veralyn had said she would return. It was possible the queen would see Tavish. It was possible the officer Pell wanted would be a decent man who solved a problem before it killed someone. Neither possibility made her reach for a pen.

Elspeth asked Pell whether he needed a room for the night. He did. The meal resumed, though Iona kept looking at the letter beneath her father’s cup.

***

The second traveller arrived while they were clearing the table.

Riarhin heard the horse stop in the yard. Nobody called out. A man dismounted, spoke briefly to the boy at the byre, and waited by the gate rather than bringing his animal to the door.

She saw him through the window and knew him a moment before she knew why.

The shoulders were broader. He had grown a small beard which did little to conceal the shape of his mouth, and the coat he wore had been mended at the same point on both elbows. One spur caught the light when he turned.

Berent.

She went out before the boy came to fetch her.

He looked relieved to see her, then embarrassed by the relief. He bowed too formally for the yard.

“My lady.”

“Don’t.”

“Riarhin.”

“That’s better.”

They stood looking at each other. There were several things she could have asked, and the obvious one came last because she did not want the answer delivered while he still held his reins.

“You’ve come far.”

“Yes.”

“Is the horse sound?”

“Yes. Tired.”

“So are you.”

“I can—”

“Come in.”

Tavish met them at the door. Berent introduced himself with the Sir clearly spoken, then seemed to regret the emphasis. Tavish congratulated him on arriving before the food had become entirely cold and called for another plate.

Iona watched the spurs. Na’ar recognised the young man, gave him a place beside the fire, and took the horse out of his hands. Pell stood to greet him and asked where he had travelled from.

“The south,” Berent said.

Pell waited for a more useful answer. Berent took off his gloves and turned to Elspeth to thank her for the room.

Riarhin waited for Pell to try again. Berent was still speaking to Elspeth.

***

She waited until he had eaten.

Pell went to see his mule. Iona was sent to put away the things she had brought from the Tor, and objected with such transparent concern about missing the visitor’s account that Tavish laughed and sent her twice. Na’ar remained by the fire with a strap across his knee.

“Elyas?” Riarhin asked.

“Is well.”

“Mara?”

“Well. She sent this.”

Berent drew a small folded letter from inside his coat. The paper was worn at the edges. Mara had written Riarhin’s name with a firmness that made it look like an instruction.

Riarhin put it beside her cup.

“You’ve been in Acasia.”

“On the border. Across it for a time.”

“And earned those.” She glanced towards his spurs.

He moved his foot back beneath the bench.

“Elyas knighted me.”

“For what?”

“There was a crossing.”

Na’ar looked up. Berent saw that a short answer would no longer serve.

“We’d gone too far after a raiding party. The water came up behind us. I knew a way through the orchard above the ford. I’d been there when the trees were in fruit.”

“Stealing?” Na’ar asked.

“Buying.”

“Ah.”

“I’d noticed the bank. We brought the wounded through there.”

“And you stayed until they were through,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

He met her eyes. She saw what the account omitted and did not ask him to fill it before supper had settled.

“Have you land?”

“A little.”

“People on it?”

“Yes.”

“Then what are you doing here?”

Berent looked towards the door. Na’ar stopped working the strap.

“Elyas sent me.”

Riarhin’s hand rested beside Mara’s letter. She had expected that answer from the first moment she saw him waiting at the gate. It was still possible to dislike hearing it.

“What does he want?”

“He wants you to come south.”

Chapter Eight — A Successful Queen

Mara’s letter began with the pear tree.

Riarhin read it at the little table beneath the eaves. The window had been made to fit an older opening, and whenever the wind pressed against it a narrow tongue of air reached her wrist. Someone had stuffed wool into the gap. She pushed it back with the end of her pen and laid the sheet where the light fell clear of the crosspiece.

Below, Elspeth was disputing a measure of oats. The dispute had begun pleasantly enough that Riarhin could hear both women smiling; now one had brought out the measure itself and the other wanted to know when it had last been compared with the one at the mill. She knew their voices. She could go down and have an opinion, and either woman would tell her why it was wrong. Mara’s handwriting rested in the midst of that familiar morning like an open door.

It had borne well for two years and badly for one. She had been advised to leave it alone, which she had done with considerable difficulty, and intended to claim credit if the next crop justified the effort. There was a line about the kitchen roof, another about a woman Riarhin had met at the station, and an invitation repeated without apology for having been made before.

At the bottom, in smaller writing, she had added: Listen to Berent. He has spent too much time deciding how to say it and may need help beginning.

Riarhin read that line twice.

Then she folded the letter and went to find him.

He was at the stable, holding his horse’s foot for Mairi’s boy. The young man had found a loose nail in the shoe and was trying to explain that he could set it right without requiring the visitor to stand in the draught.

“I’ve got it,” Berent said.

“So have I.”

Riarhin leaned against the doorpost. Berent looked up, put the foot down carefully, and stepped out of the way.

“Come and walk,” she said.

Berent looked towards the boy, who had returned to the shoe.

“First there’s something else.”

He took his riding coat from the peg and turned back the lining at its breast. Riarhin watched him unpick two stitches with the point of his knife. The care he took made her want to hurry him, but the boy was still close and the thing he drew out was small enough to have been mistaken for a repair to the coat itself.

“He told me to give you this where nobody could see you read it.”

“Then why bring it out here?”

“Because if I ask you to follow me somewhere private, you’ll ask the question loudly.”

She almost smiled. He folded the coat over the letter before turning towards the rear of the stable. They went into the little room where harness hung to dry, and he closed the door without latching it. She could hear the boy whistling through a gap in his teeth.

The seal was Elyas’s. There was no address on the outer fold.

Riarhin,

Berent has things to tell you which I cannot put here without making other people answer for them. Hear him before deciding that I have begun seeing enemies in every man who collects a toll. I have met some of those men. A few are better at their work than the people they displaced, and most are pleased to be paid. I would be pleased too.

She sat on an upturned feed box. Berent remained by the door.

The trouble is that each agreement appears to finish a different quarrel. A bridge repaired against its future toll. A debt extended against the right to appoint the man who collects it. A company kept in pay until a duke can afford his own again. The Duke cannot afford his own again because the revenues which would pay them have gone into the first two agreements. Harald signs the next paper and says he has saved us from the consequence of the last.

Men come to me because I cannot repair this by signing anything. That makes me seem honest. Some are my friends. Some would be delighted to make me do what they cannot persuade their neighbours to do themselves. I am trying to tell them apart. I do not always manage it.

Riarhin looked up. Berent was listening to the sounds beyond the door rather than watching her face. He had been trusted with a paper in which his lord admitted that trust could be misplaced.

I have spoken to Tylan. He thinks that if we make the arrangements work well enough, the resentment will follow the old difficulties into the past. He has made arrangements work which I thought could not. When a village complained that its horses had been taken twice, he returned the second taking and charged the officer for the feed. Then he asked why that village should refuse the queen a road after she had heard it fairly. He meant both things. Remember that about him.

Veralyn listens while I describe a wrong. She grows impatient when I cannot give her a person whose removal would end it. Last time she asked what I proposed to put in Harald’s place. I told her I had come about a ferry. She laughed, but she asked again.

The paper had been folded while the ink was not quite dry. A little of the last word had transferred to the facing surface. Riarhin rubbed it with her thumb before realising what she was doing.

I do not think they will let me go on receiving these men indefinitely. If I am arrested, I intend to answer in my own name and insist that those accused of following me be heard separately. If I am killed, you will be told that I had time to stop it. There have already been offers. I could cease receiving people, endorse a settlement I have not read, tell the queen that those who distrust her are simply men who distrust good government. I cannot make myself do all of that, and I know the distinction may seem a small thing to die for when written by someone who has not died.

Riarhin stopped. The stable boy dropped a tool outside. Someone laughed at him, and the whistle began again.

“Has he been threatened?”

“Asked what he thinks he’s doing,” Berent said. “By people who don’t want an answer.”

She read on.

This is not a request to make a war for me. It is not an instruction to Mara, and you must not use it to tell her what she is allowed to want. She knows I am sending for you. She does not know I am writing this part. That is unfair to her. I am doing it anyway because I want someone who will hear her after I cannot, without first deciding how much of her anger is suitable.

Come if you can. Bring whatever you have learned that might make me less certain I am right. I would rather be embarrassed before you than proved right before a grave.

Elyas.

Below the name, in a tighter space, he had added:

She will say the pear tree wants cutting back. It probably does. You need not settle everything on the first evening.

Riarhin folded the letter. She had known men who prepared fine accounts of the deaths they imagined for themselves. This one had made room at the end for being alive.

“How long have you carried it?”

“Since I left him.”

“Who knows?”

“He and I. Now you.”

She put it inside her shirt. For a moment she wanted to open the stable door and tell Tavish that they would need horses before noon. Then she heard Elyas’s first request again: hear the man who had come all this way before deciding what the paper meant.

“Now we walk,” she said.

***

They went above the lower fields.

Mist lay along the stream but the slope above it was clear. The roofs of Glenruan appeared and disappeared as they climbed past the hedges, each with its own thin lifting of smoke. Riarhin could have named the people beneath most of them. She had learned which path to avoid when the dogs were loose, whose gate caught at the bottom, where a person could go to be alone and still be offered something hot if the rain came in.

Berent had no such map of the place. He kept looking for somewhere their voices would not carry, and she found herself resenting him for needing it here.

The grass had been cut, leaving enough wet growth along the boundary to soak the hems of their coats. Berent chose the higher ground without waiting for her to show him. She noticed how often he looked back towards the house. Pell’s mule stood beside the wall.

“He’ll leave this morning,” she said.

“The grain man?”

“Yes.”

“I wasn’t—”

“You were.”

He accepted it. For a while they walked with only the sound of their boots disturbing the wet grass.

“What has she done?” Riarhin asked.

Berent took a breath. “Vhasia owes her more than it can pay.”

“Vhasia owes everybody.”

“Now the people who collect it answer in Valora.”

“Some of them always did.”

“More. Enough.”

“Enough for what?”

He stopped beside a low wall. There was a place where a stone had fallen out, and he moved it aside before sitting. Riarhin remained standing.

“The duke had to ask for money to meet the soldiers’ pay. Then money to settle what was owed for the last money. He gave guarantees on roads and revenues. There are officers in places where an officer sent by Harald used to be able to act. Now he brings the case to them.”

“Is the case heard?”

“Usually.”

“Better than before?”

“Sometimes.”

She let him hear his own answer. He looked up at her with sudden irritation.

“That doesn’t make it harmless.”

“No. It makes it something you must explain properly.”

He rubbed a hand over his beard. The gesture was new and did not yet suit him.

“She is Protector of Vhasia. Formally. There was a ceremony. Harald stood there and accepted it.”

“Was Elyas there?”

“Yes.”

“What did he say?”

“At the ceremony? What he was required to say.”

“And afterwards?”

“That we would discover what protection meant when someone wished to refuse it.”

Riarhin looked down towards Glenruan. Tavish had come out to see Pell off and was holding the man’s stirrup while he mounted. She could hear neither of them. The courtesy looked genuine from where she stood.

“Has Elyas refused?”

“He has asked questions.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Berent looked at the gap in the wall.

“There are men who come to him before they go to the duke. They served with him. Their fathers did. They trust him.”

“Does he send them back?”

“He hears them.”

“Does he send them back?”

“Sometimes.”

She sat on the wall beside him.

***

He had brought examples.

She could tell by the order in which he gave them, and by the way he resisted being drawn away from one before he had finished it. A baron had accepted an advance against the yield of three harvests. A toll had been assigned to a creditor who sold the right to collect it. A garrison stayed after the trouble that had justified its presence ended, and the men were paid well enough that local boys joined rather than complain about them.

“Were the roads safer?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes.”

“Was the toll higher?”

“Lower on some goods.”

“Were the boys compelled?”

“No.”

He stood, walked two paces, and turned back.

“You think I’m frightened because men are being paid.”

“I think you’ve told me men are being paid.”

“They stay.”

She waited.

“When the harvest comes, they stay. When their old lord calls them, they say they have a captain now and he must ask him. My neighbour’s younger son came home with enough money to mend his mother’s roof. He brought two men to help. They finished it in three days and rode back to their company. His father is proud of him.”

“Shouldn’t he be?”

“Yes.”

The answer came with such force that she looked at him more closely.

“I like the boy,” Berent said. “I helped him find a horse. If the queen’s men were robbing his mother, this would be easier to tell you.”

Riarhin moved along the wall to make room again. After a moment he sat.

“What frightens you?”

“That it all works.”

Below them Pell had mounted. Tavish stepped back and waved him towards the road.

“It cannot all work.”

“The things that fail become reasons for doing more of it. The things that succeed become reasons for the same. You object to a garrison, and they show you the safe road. You object to who hears a claim, and they bring the widow who won it. You ask what the duke is still permitted to do, and somebody explains what he did badly.”

“Harald does a great deal badly.”

“I know.”

“And Elyas?”

“He can get men to listen. That is what frightens him.”

Riarhin let the silence last. Berent had finally reached the thing he had come to say, and she wished he had taken longer.

***

At midday she found Na’ar and Tavish behind the hall, arguing over the movement of cattle through a narrow track.

Tavish had drawn the track in the dirt. Na’ar had placed stones along its edge and was showing him how a handful of riders could make a larger party choose the wrong direction without ever meeting it head on. Two young men watched. One had been among the raiders the previous winter and kept trying to insert the brave thing he had done into the explanation.

“You went there because the gate was open,” Na’ar told him.

“I saw an opportunity.”

“You saw a gate.”

Tavish laughed. The young man looked injured, then laughed too.

Riarhin waited until the others had gone.

“Berent wants us south,” she said.

“Does he?” Na’ar moved a stone with his boot. “What does he want us to do there?”

“He hasn’t said.”

“Then he hasn’t finished.”

“He says the queen has more power than she ought.”

Tavish leaned on the wall. “Pell would call it a service.”

“He offered you one.”

“He did. And I could use it.”

Riarhin looked towards the road. She could still hear Berent’s anger at being made to describe a decent soldier as evidence of something he feared.

“Do you think I should go?”

Tavish’s answer came more slowly than his usual speech.

“Do you want me to tell you to?”

“No.”

“Then I won’t.”

Na’ar bent to retrieve a stone he liked the shape of. He put it on the wall rather than in his pocket.

“Listen to the rest,” he said.

***

Berent stayed.

On the second day he helped Donnel move a gate which the stream had undermined. Donnel objected to receiving assistance from a knight until Berent took off his coat and asked where he should put his shoulder. Riarhin came down with tools and found them working together in the muddy silence of men who had established that both were useful.

Donnel’s fingers had healed crooked. He could close them around the top rail but had difficulty releasing it, and Berent waited without looking at the hand. Riarhin saw him adjust his grip to take more of the weight.

When they had set the post, Donnel brought ale from the house. He asked about the queen.

Berent glanced at Riarhin.

“She is well,” he said.

“And the little one?”

“Also well.”

Donnel nodded, satisfied. “Good to have a child in the house. Gives them something to be sensible for.”

Riarhin drank. Berent looked at the gate.

Later, walking back, she asked, “Matylde?”

“Yes.”

“How old now?”

“Two.”

She knew that. A letter had told her of the birth, and another of a tooth, and Jaennis had written a line about the indignity of being expected to admire the same child repeatedly for accomplishments every child eventually managed. Riarhin had smiled when she read it. The letters lay in the chest in Morven’s room.

“Tylan?”

“With the queen.”

“I meant how is he.”

“With the queen,” Berent said again, then coloured. “I’m sorry.”

She stopped.

“Explain.”

“He supports her. In council, in the army. More than before, people say.”

“Which people?”

“Some who knew him before. Some who wish he would disagree.”

“What have you seen?”

Berent thought. “A proposal to keep a company beyond its agreed service. He asked whether the men had been paid. They had. He asked whether their families had been informed. They had. Then he supported it.”

“That sounds like Tylan.”

“Yes.”

“Were the men permitted to leave?”

“They could refuse the new terms.”

“And did they?”

“Very few.”

They resumed walking.

“Marshal and Queen Consort,” Berent said. “People expected another marriage.”

“People often do.”

“The queen was advised that a foreign match would secure what she’d won.”

“And she said?”

“‘It shall matter not.’”

Riarhin could hear it. The unconcern, the lift at the end which made an objection sound as though it had arrived after the decision rather than before.

“Were you there?”

“Yes. Waiting on Elyas. She said it twice because one of them asked her to consider.”

Riarhin laughed before she could stop herself. Berent looked at her, and she let him see that the laughter was affection.

“She chose the man she wanted beside her,” she said.

“He chose her too.”

“Yes.”

At the yard, Iona was trying to teach a dog to put both paws on a low stool. The dog offered the same paw again. Riarhin watched the girl begin again.

***

The third evening was wet.

Rain kept everyone in the hall, making the space too warm and the floor dirty. Mairi brought a pair of hinges to work on by the fire. Na’ar took over part of the hearth to warm a dish which Elspeth said did not need warming, and Tavish persuaded Berent to describe the orchard crossing.

This time the account was longer. There had been a wounded horse blocking the narrow way, a man who would not leave it, and a choice Berent had made before Elyas reached him. He described the men first and himself only when Tavish asked where he had been standing.

Riarhin listened from the window.

When the others began arguing over whether the horse could have been moved, Berent came to stand beside her.

“There are other things,” he said.

“I expected.”

“The Mound. They have more men. Younger sons, some heirs. They say serving the queen is a better use of their lives than waiting for somebody to die.”

“It might be.”

“They speak of Gelfrey. Of what his heir ought to do.”

“Have you heard them?”

“Yes.”

“And the rumours?”

He looked at her.

“You’ve been keeping them until I believed the rest.”

“Yes.”

She appreciated the admission.

“Go on.”

“People say the queen and Tylan serve Myrmis. That the Mound has brought them into it. There are things said about Shadowsteel.”

“There have always been things said about swords.”

“I know.”

“What do you know of Fayd?”

“Less than Elyas wants to know. He has people abroad. He is receiving men who used to send letters. Some come from Ecktoria. Others won’t give a name until they are inside.”

“That is his work.”

“Lyra says his work is growing.”

Riarhin looked at the rain beyond the glass.

“Lyra.”

“She has her own people now. Men go to her when they want something found without asking his office. Women too. She can tell you whether a rumour began before the event or after it.”

“Is she safe?”

“She says she is useful.”

Riarhin turned back. Berent had chosen the words accurately; she could hear Lyra refusing the question through them.

“And Jaennis?”

“People who were nobody five years ago have marriages, offices, friends who answer their letters. Others can’t get into a room they once walked through. Some of that is her.”

“Some of that is any court.”

“Then come and see this one.”

The request was plain at last.

Across the room Tavish had found a reason to open another jug. Elspeth was moving Mairi’s hinges off the place where she wanted to set food. Iona had fallen asleep with the dog’s head on her foot and would deny it when she woke.

Riarhin could leave. She had left places with more reason to remain. The horses were sound, the road passable, and she knew exactly where she kept the things she would need.

“What does Elyas want me to do?”

“Look. Speak to the people who remember you before you were useful to them. Tell him whether he is wrong.”

“And if he isn’t?”

Berent’s face changed. For a moment she saw the young man he might have become if he had been allowed to arrive with a simpler message.

“He didn’t tell me to ask that.”

“But you are asking.”

“Yes.”

She looked at his hands. There was a healed cut at the base of one thumb and a small rough place on his forefinger where a rein had rubbed it during the journey. He had dependants somewhere south, and a house too small to make anyone hesitate before taking it from him. Elyas had sent this man because he trusted him.

“Stay until the rain passes,” she said.

“It may pass tonight.”

“Then stay tomorrow.”

Berent nodded. He stayed beside her, waiting.

***

That night she opened the chest.

The letters were tied together beneath a folded shirt. She found the one announcing Matylde’s birth, then the later account of the naming, with a line from Elyas about Tylan’s inability to keep from smiling when he thought nobody was looking. She read it beside the lamp while the rain touched the window in little separate bursts.

Jaennis had enclosed a piece of green ribbon with another letter, an amused suggestion that Riarhin might submit to current taste when she next honoured the court. It had slipped between two sheets. She lifted it now and laid it across her knee.

There was nothing remarkable in the colour. She had seen greener fields after rain and greener glass in a southern window. Veralyn liked it. People who served a queen found ways to like what pleased her.

Riarhin folded the ribbon into the letter.

Underneath lay Sada’s last reply. She took that out too, read the passage about the wells, and found herself returning to a sentence she had passed over twice. You needn’t know how long before you come.

She held the paper for a while.

Downstairs a chair moved. Na’ar’s step crossed the room, paused at the foot of the stair, then continued towards his own bed. He had left the decision with her.

Riarhin put the letters back in the chest and took out her travelling coat.

One fastening had worked loose. She sat by the lamp to mend it.

Part II — The Road Back

Chapter Nine — The Message After

The rain ended in the night.

Berent was still there four mornings later, when the wool buyer came up from the lower road and asked whether anyone in the house could read a proclamation. He had brought a copy because one of his carters had kin in Istovar and refused to believe what the man at the posting house said it meant.

Tavish took it at the door.

Riarhin was beside the hearth, mending the lining of her coat. She saw him read the first lines and then look towards Berent. The young man was standing at the window with a bowl in his hand.

“What?” she asked.

Tavish read the page to the end before answering.

“Elyas has been arrested.”

Berent set the bowl on the sill. It tipped against the uneven wood and he caught it, making a sound of irritation quite out of proportion to the spill.

Riarhin put down the needle.

“When?”

“The notice is dated six days ago.”

“Give it here.”

The wool buyer followed Tavish into the room, removing his cap as he came. He had the uncertain expression of a man whose ordinary request had found its way into something private.

“They’re reading it at the crossings,” he said. “The fellow who gave me this said there’d been a hearing.”

Riarhin read.

Elyas’s name occupied the space above a list of acts described in language she knew from other men’s misfortunes: solicitation, withholding, unauthorised correspondence, the assembly of persons owing service elsewhere. The charge was treason against the Protector’s lawful authority and the peace she maintained. It did not state that he had raised an army. It gave an army’s shape to the men who had come to his house.

Near the bottom, somebody had written the destinations to which the notice should be carried. One was north of Glenruan.

She looked at Berent.

“Did anyone know why you came?”

“Elyas. Mara. Lyra knew I was travelling.”

“Others?”

“My aunt knew I’d be away. I left instructions at home.”

“What instructions?”

“For the harvest. A payment. Nothing about this.”

The wool buyer held his cap between his hands.

“I didn’t know you knew him.”

“We do,” Tavish said.

“Then I’m sorry.”

Riarhin turned the page over. There was nothing else on it.

***

She questioned the buyer in the yard.

He had seen the notice copied at the posting house. A clerk had made four while he waited, complaining about his supply of paper. The buyer could describe the clerk, the room, and the argument over whether reading it aloud entitled the keeper to charge him for a copy. He could not tell her where Elyas was held.

“The capital, I suppose.”

“Did somebody say the capital?”

“No. I supposed.”

She nodded. He watched her with growing unease.

“Is there something wrong with it?”

“I don’t know.”

“The man with me wants to go home. He says if there’s fighting, his people will need him.”

“Where are they?”

He named a place Riarhin knew. She pictured the road to it, then stopped herself from turning a man’s family into a route before she had answered.

“There’s no fighting in this notice.”

“There wasn’t in the last one either, until there was.”

She could not correct him. Tavish came out carrying bread wrapped for the road and asked where the buyer’s cart stood.

When the man had gone, Riarhin said, “I’ll need passage south.”

Tavish looked towards the lower road.

“I know someone taking cloth as far as Valora.”

“When?”

“Tomorrow, if the water’s fallen. The day after if it hasn’t.”

“Too slow.”

“His wagons are slow. His horses are sound. He knows which road was washed away last week and which keeper has a bed you can buy without your name going ahead.”

She looked at him.

“I know what people ask when a knight and a woman from the queen’s table go riding south after that notice.”

“We could ride separately.”

“And meet at every inn where both of you have asked the same question?”

The answer annoyed her. It was a good answer, which did nothing to improve it.

“What does he carry besides cloth?”

“Tools. Wax. Two families travelling to the lower towns. He lost a guard to a better offer at the market.”

“One guard.”

“He may be persuaded he needs two.”

***

Berent wanted to ride at once.

He had brought his horse into the yard and was tightening the girth when Riarhin came back. Na’ar stood nearby, offering no help and making no attempt to stop him.

“I can reach the next posting house tonight,” Berent said.

“Yes.”

“There may be more news.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her sharply. She held out the notice.

“Read the last part.”

“I have read it.”

“The places it’s being sent.”

He looked down. The horse moved a hind foot, impatient at being held ready without going anywhere.

“They may have your description,” she said. “They may only know a knight went north. They may know nothing. What will you do when the keeper asks why you’re returning in a hurry?”

“Tell him I’ve business.”

“And when he asks whose?”

Berent’s jaw tightened. “I haven’t committed a crime.”

“I haven’t said you have.”

“They can’t arrest everyone who served him.”

“Probably not.”

He looked at Na’ar, who remained beside the gate with his arms folded.

“What would you do?”

“Listen to her. Then decide whether she has finished being right.”

Riarhin handed Berent the paper.

“Tavish can put us with a caravan. We work. We’re travellers who are paid to be there.”

“And if Elyas needs me before we arrive?”

She could offer him the distance, the condition of the roads, the fact that the notice was already six days old. None would answer the thing he asked.

“I don’t know.”

His face changed. He looked at her for a moment, then at the horse.

“I should have come back sooner.”

“You came for me.”

“Yes.”

The word hurt. She accepted it without trying to make him retract it.

“Loosen the girth,” she said. “We’re going to speak to a carrier.”

He stood a moment longer before he did.

***

The carrier had one blind eye and a very good memory for people who owed him money.

He greeted Tavish by naming a sum, which Tavish contested on the ground that part of it had been paid in kind. The carrier, whose name was Ewen, agreed that the payment had been kind to Tavish. They settled the old business before discussing the new.

Riarhin liked him for that.

“You’ll take orders,” Ewen told her.

“When they concern the road.”

“When they concern my wagons.”

She nodded.

“And if you’ve urgent business, it stays your urgent business. I don’t take a wheel off getting you to it.”

“I understand.”

He looked at Berent. “Does he?”

“I’m here,” Berent said.

“Then answer.”

“Yes.”

Ewen looked at his horse, his sword, and the worn spurs. “You can ride the rear. If a load slips, you tell me before it leaves the wagon. If somebody falls behind, you find out why. I’ve women and children on this journey. They’ll hear enough frightening nonsense at the inns without their own guard improving it.”

Berent agreed.

Riarhin asked about the route. Ewen described the first three days and the crossing at which they might have to wait. She asked about an alternative track she remembered. He said it had been cut by water and that a man who tried it last month had come back with two horses and no cart.

“You ride it yourself?”

“As far as the break. I like my cart.”

They agreed a wage. It was less than Berent expected. Riarhin accepted it after Ewen agreed to cover their feed and lodging, and made sure Berent heard that part of the undertaking. Ewen counted them into his party with marks on a board and asked Tavish to witness their undertaking.

Tavish did.

Outside, Riarhin caught his sleeve.

“If somebody comes asking—”

“I found guards for a man who needed guards.”

“They can make that difficult.”

“They can make most things difficult. Go and pack.”

***

Na’ar had packed already.

His bundle stood beside the door, and he was explaining to Mairi where he wanted the pot left when she finished it. She said that if he wanted a pot as large as a bath he could return and help carry it. He said he intended to.

Riarhin stopped in the doorway.

“You’re coming.”

“Yes.”

“I haven’t asked Ewen.”

“I have.”

“What did he say?”

“He asked whether I eat a great deal. I told him I work a great deal. We will discover whether he understood.”

Mairi picked up the pot and took it away. Na’ar watched her go with an expression of attachment entirely disproportionate to the amount of cooking he had managed in it.

“You needn’t,” Riarhin said.

He turned back.

“I know.”

“I mean it.”

“So do I.”

She could have thanked him. He was already bending to fasten the bundle, and the words seemed to require him to stand still in a role he had not offered to play.

“Your horse?”

“Ready.”

“Your leg?”

He looked up. “Still attached.”

She left him to it.

***

Iona found her in the upper room.

“You said you’d tell me.”

“I’m telling you now.”

“Everyone knows already.”

Riarhin put down the coat. “Yes. I should have come to you.”

The girl stood against the door, uncertain what to do with an apology delivered before she had finished constructing her grievance.
