“Will you come back?”

“I mean to.”

“That isn’t—”

“I know.”

Iona looked at the weapons laid on the bed. Riarhin had unwrapped Remorse to examine the edge. Its narrow, double-edged blade lay across a folded cloth, the guard dull in the window light.

“Are you going to fight?”

“I’m going to find out what has happened.”

“That’s why adults take swords?”

“Sometimes because they know less than they like.”

The girl came closer. She did not touch it.

“Father says Elyas is a good man.”

“He is.”

“Then why has the queen—”

“I don’t know enough yet.”

Riarhin picked up the sword and slid it into its scabbard. Iona watched her fasten it where her left hand could reach.

“You look different.”

“I have the same face.”

“I know.”

The girl embraced her suddenly. Riarhin held her, feeling the height she had gained, the breadth coming into her shoulders. Another year or two and she would be looking across at her father over the heads of half his visitors. She could remember every day she had attended to and still be surprised by how much time had passed.

“I’ll write,” she said.

“About something besides a fence.”

“I’ll try.”

They went downstairs together.

***

Ailsa had finally put the basket to soak when Riarhin came.

She had put it in a shallow tub outside the door and weighted it with a stone. The loose rib stood above the water, refusing the shape it was meant to take. When she saw Riarhin’s face she left the tub where it was.

“What has happened?”

Riarhin told her about the arrest. She kept to the news they had received and the road Tavish had found for them. The particulars of Elyas’s hidden letter remained between the people it named; she could tell Ailsa what she feared without making her responsible for keeping every secret she carried.

Ailsa listened from the doorway. She did not begin packing food or reaching for a coat. Riarhin found herself waiting for one of those movements, something that would turn her departure into work they could do together.

“When?”

“Tomorrow.”

“So the cloth can go another way.”

“I can ask Ewen—”

“I’ll arrange it.”

Riarhin looked at the basket. She had brought her knife.

“I’m sorry.”

“Yes.”

“I don’t know how long.”

“I hadn’t asked.”

The stream sounded louder here than it did at Tavish’s house. Riarhin could hear it filling the spaces she kept leaving. She had made it easier to say difficult things in other rooms by bringing them a little at a time, waiting until the other person helped her finish. Ailsa stood in her doorway and let her carry the whole of this one.

“I want to come back.”

“I believe you.”

“But you don’t believe I will.”

“I don’t know whether you will. Neither do you.”

Ailsa stepped aside to let her in. The cup stood near her usual place. Riarhin looked at it and wished the woman had moved it already, then was horrified by the wish. Being spared the sight would not spare Ailsa the absence.

“Will you write?”

“If I can do it without bringing trouble here.”

“Say that in the first letter. Then I won’t have to decide it every morning the next one fails to come.”

Riarhin nodded.

Ailsa drew the knife from her hand and put it on the table. She had been gripping it by the closed sheath.

“You aren’t fighting anyone in this room.”

“I know.”

“Come here, then.”

Riarhin went into her arms. For a little while neither spoke. Ailsa’s hand moved once between her shoulders and then rested there; she did not keep making the movement until it became something Riarhin could pretend to endure instead of wanting. Riarhin held her tightly, aware of the low ceiling, the familiar smell of dried cloth, the warmth at the woman’s throat.

It would be possible to remain. She had to allow herself that much honesty. Berent could go back with an answer she had chosen, and Elyas might be released before they reached Valora. She might arrive too late to help, or be of no use when she did. None of those possibilities made this house less real.

“I have to go,” she said.

“You’ve decided to.”

“Yes.”

Ailsa kissed her, gently enough that Riarhin had to stop holding herself so rigidly to receive it.

“Then go as someone who decided. Don’t make all the roads do it for you.”

They sat together until the light left the doorway. Riarhin helped lift the basket from its tub and wrapped the damp rib in cloth so Ailsa could finish it later. She laid her knife beside the work.

“You’ll need that.”

“I have others.”

Ailsa raised an eyebrow.

“For cutting willow,” Riarhin said. “Not a promise you must keep on a shelf.”

“Good. I may ruin it.”

“You won’t.”

“You should allow me some mysteries.”

At the door she made Riarhin take the remains of the bread. Riarhin walked home carrying it under her coat, warm against her ribs, and did not turn until the bend hid the house.

Chapter Ten — Southbound

Ewen put her at the front.

For the first morning this meant riding beside a carter who asked questions without needing answers. He wanted to know whether the road improved after the next turn, whether the clouds to the west looked like rain, and why his brother had married a woman who thought lending a horse meant surrendering it forever. Riarhin answered the first two. The third appeared to have enough witnesses already.

Behind them the wagons moved at the pace of the oldest team.

The road smelled of wet leather warming under the first weak sun. A blue cover had been tied over the last wagon with string of three different thicknesses, and each gust raised a corner until the woman riding beneath it reached up and pulled it down. Riarhin had watched her do that six times before the bend. The seventh time she rode back and offered a spare strap, expecting gratitude and receiving instructions about where to thread it so the woman could still reach the kettle.

She did as she was told. It took longer than handing over a strap. When she regained the front, the impatient space inside her had not grown smaller, but the blue corner stayed where it belonged.

She had known the pace when she agreed. Knowing did little to make it bearable. At a rise she turned in the saddle and saw Berent riding the rear, his horse taking three unnecessary steps for every two required. Na’ar was walking beside a loaded mule, talking to a woman whose small son had refused to sit on the wagon because the wheels might come off.

“They might,” Na’ar said when Riarhin rode back to see why they were slowing.

The woman closed her eyes.

“You shouldn’t tell him that.”

“He knows. He can see the wheels.”

The boy clung to her hand.

Na’ar crouched beside the nearer hub and showed him the pin. “This keeps it there.”

“What if it breaks?”

“Then we stop.”

“What if we don’t?”

“I will.”

The boy looked at him, then at the wheel. After a long interval he allowed himself to be lifted into the wagon. Na’ar stood and rubbed one knee.

Riarhin waited until they were out of the woman’s hearing.

“You’ve promised to stop a wagon.”

“It was already stopped.”

She went forward again.

***

The first wheel failed before evening.

It was on a different wagon. A spoke cracked on a stony descent, and the driver stopped before the rim could collapse. By the time Riarhin reached him, Ewen had climbed underneath and was naming the man who had sworn the repair would last to Valora.

“He said a season,” the driver offered.

“He was enjoying a different season.”

They unloaded the near side. The cargo belonged to a woman travelling to join her sister, and she objected to the way Berent lifted a chest before explaining that one of its handles had been replaced and would not bear the weight. He lowered it carefully. Inside, something broke anyway.

She opened the lid.

Riarhin saw cups wrapped in cloth, a wooden figure whose purpose she did not know, and a bowl broken cleanly across its middle. The woman took out the two pieces and held them together.

“My mother’s,” she said.

Berent began to apologise. She shook her head once, sharply, without looking at him.

“Put the rest there. On the grass. Not on the stones.”

He obeyed. Riarhin helped her close the chest.

Ewen came out from beneath the wagon. They could lash the damaged wheel well enough to reach a farm visible beyond the next field, but could not take the loaded wagon farther without replacing it. The spare they carried had a different hub. He looked at Riarhin.

“Go and ask.”

She went.

***

The farmer had a wheel.

He also had a daughter returning from a nearby holding with children and belongings, and the cart on which he meant to fetch her needed the wheel. Riarhin stood in the byre and listened while he explained this twice, the second time more slowly because she had offered money after the first.

“When must you go?”

“Tonight.”

“How far?”

He told her. It was less than an hour’s ride, longer with children and a loaded cart.

“Could we bring them?”

“In what?”

“We have another wagon.”

“Then put your load on it.”

“It’s full.”

“And mine is needed.”

She looked at the wheel. It stood against a wall beside a sack of beans. A man carrying weapons could make this a simple transaction, and she knew exactly how to begin it without appearing to threaten him outright. The first words were waiting in her mouth when she remembered Tavish with his boots in the mud.

“How many children?” she asked.

“Two.”

“How much baggage?”

The farmer folded his arms. “My daughter will decide what she brings.”

“Of course.”

She went back to Ewen.

He listened, then sent her to the wagon carrying the frightened boy. Its occupants could sit at the farm while Berent took their wagon to fetch the daughter. They would unload enough space for her belongings. In return, the farmer would sell the wheel and lend them the yard for the night.

The woman with the boy objected to having her possessions moved twice. The farmer objected to entrusting his daughter to a stranger. Berent stood through both objections until Riarhin told him to fetch the horse and let the farmer travel with him.

“Why didn’t you say that?” Ewen asked.

“I’ve said it now.”

The arrangement took most of the remaining daylight. By the time the farmer returned with his daughter and two sleeping children, the damaged wagon was standing on its new wheel and the party had made a fire in the yard.

The woman whose bowl had broken sat beside it with the pieces in her lap. Na’ar asked whether she wished them wrapped. She looked at him a long time before nodding.

Riarhin ate standing up. Berent joined her after seeing the horse watered.

“We’ve lost half a day.”

“Yes.”

“We could have ridden on.”

“We agreed to work.”

He looked across the yard at the daughter, who was embracing her father beside the newly emptied wagon. His frustration did not leave his face, but he put down the argument.

Riarhin wished she could put down hers as easily.

***

In the morning the farmer brought eggs.

He had counted them for the children first. The adults received what remained, which led to a dispute between two carters who each insisted the other should have the last one. Ewen cut it in half with his knife and told them to save their generosity for the next wheel.

Riarhin helped load the woman’s chest. This time she held it beneath the base while Berent guided it into place. The woman inspected the lashings, thanked them both, and climbed up beside it.

As they left, the farmer raised a hand. His daughter stood in the doorway with one child against her hip and the other holding her skirt. Riarhin looked back once, then faced the road.

Ahead, Berent had ridden as far as the bend and stopped to let the wagons catch him. Riarhin took the mare out of the line, then checked her when the new wheel struck a rut. It held.

She pressed the mare forward until she reached Ewen.

“Where can we ask for news?”

“Midday, if the second wheel shares our intentions.”

***

The posting house had soldiers in the yard.

They wore coats cut to the same pattern, though several had been altered at the cuffs. One was teaching another how to bind a horse’s pastern. A third stood at the well, waiting for a woman to fill her buckets before he filled his own. Riarhin watched him take one of hers to the doorway, return, and begin pumping without any sign that he expected to be thanked.

Ewen went inside. Berent stayed with the rear wagon. Riarhin dismounted near the well.

“How is the road south?” she asked.

“Dry where it isn’t broken.”

The soldier glanced at her sword, then at her face. He was young, with a red mark along one cheek where he had slept against a seam.

“Where are you going?”

“Valora.”

“Keep the lower road after the mill. The other’s being worked.”

“By you?”

He smiled. “We protect the men who do the useful part.”

“From whom?”

“People who believe the road should be somewhere else.”

He filled the second bucket. Riarhin waited while he moved it clear of the trough.

“Your family near here?”

“Two days west.”

“You’ll see them?”

“After the next relief. If the captain keeps to it.”

“Does he?”

“He has so far.”

There was satisfaction in the answer. His boots were good and newly repaired. She looked at them, then at the men binding the horse’s leg.

“Better than your old service?”

The soldier considered her. “In my old service I waited three months for pay and was told to be grateful for the honour. My mother can’t mend a roof with honour.”

Riarhin remembered Berent’s account of his neighbour’s son and wondered how many roofs were being mended by the same argument.

“Then I hope your relief comes.”

“So do I.”

He carried the buckets away.

***

Ewen had learned nothing he trusted.

One man inside said Elyas would be sent north into confinement. Another had heard that the queen would pardon him if he named the people who had urged him to oppose her. The keeper said a hearing had concluded and that the result would be posted when it arrived, which was the only answer Ewen thought worth repeating.

Riarhin went in herself.

The keeper had a scar above one eyebrow and an irritation about being asked the same question by every traveller. He showed her the board. The arrest notice hung beside instructions concerning a damaged crossing and a list of charges for beds.

“No later paper?” she asked.

“If there were, I’d put it up.”

“Who brings them?”

“The people who bring them.”

She placed a coin on the counter for ale. He took it, filled a cup, and looked slightly less persecuted.

“A rider from the next station,” he said. “Usually by evening.”

“Today?”

“Usually.”

Outside, Ewen called for the wagons to move. Riarhin drank the ale and returned the cup.

She could remain, wait for the rider, and catch the caravan at its next stop. She could also lose another half day for a paper which had not arrived because there was no paper to bring. Berent was watching from beside his horse. He would stay if she stayed. He would call it obedience rather than hope.

She went out.

“Nothing?” he asked.

“Nothing certain.”

They rode south. At the next turn she looked behind, where the road from the station remained empty in the afternoon light.

Chapter Eleven — The Words They Remembered

The man who told them was buying a belt.

He stood beneath the inn’s porch with his old belt unfastened and the new one held around his waist, trying to decide whether he could afford the buckle he wanted. His companion said he had seen Elyas die. The man looked over, irritated at being made interesting while his clothes were in disorder.

“I was there,” he said. “That isn’t the same.”

Riarhin stopped beside the porch.

“When?”

He gave the day.

She knew the day. They had been above the lower fields at Glenruan. Berent had described the boy who repaired his mother’s roof, and she had asked whether the roads were safer.

The belt seller pulled the leather tighter.

“Like that?”

“Yes,” the man said. Then, to Riarhin, “You knew him?”

“Yes.”

His hand left the buckle.

“I’m sorry.”

“Where were you standing?”

“By the fountain. At the side. I went for the proclamation.”

“Did you hear him?”

“Some.”

The companion began to say that Elyas had called on every loyal man in Istovar to rise. The man with the belt turned on him.

“I didn’t tell you that.”

“That’s what people said.”

“People where?”

“At the table last night.”

“I wasn’t at your table last night.”

Riarhin put her hand on the porch post. The wood had a splintered place where a nail had been pulled out. She could feel it beneath her palm.

“What did you hear?” she asked.

The man looked at the seller, who lowered the end of the belt and stood aside.

“He said he had served Vhasia before she was its Protector. That he’d sworn to defend people who were now told their own debts were a foreign matter.”

“Those words?”

“I can’t give you all the words.”

“What can you give me?”

The man looked at her hand on the post. He took a breath.

“‘They came to my door. I opened it.’ That I remember. He said it twice. Somebody near me called out that there were other doors. I thought it a cruel thing to say.”

Berent had come up behind Riarhin. She knew from the sudden stillness in the man’s face before she heard him speak.

“Was there a priest?”

“Yes.”

“Did he speak to him?”

“Yes.”

“Did he—”

The question failed. Riarhin turned enough to see Berent’s mouth close.

The man fastened his own belt again, leaving the new one in the seller’s hands.

“I stayed,” he said. “I didn’t mean to stay for it. There were people behind me, and then I thought leaving would look as though I’d never intended to listen.”

Riarhin asked, “Did you see his face?”

“Yes.”

“After?”

He shut his eyes briefly. “Yes.”

There was no useful question left.

***

Ewen found them behind the inn.

Berent was standing beside his horse, working at a knot in the halter which did not need untying. Riarhin sat on a low stone trough with her hands between her knees. Na’ar had taken the water bucket away from her when she picked it up without knowing what she meant to do with it.

“We’ll stay here tonight,” Ewen said.

“The light’s good,” Riarhin answered.

“We’ll stay.”

He went to speak to the keeper. She heard a price named and disputed. A room would have to be cleared; somebody had left sacks in it. Ewen agreed to move the sacks himself.

Berent pulled the knot tight again.

“He was still alive when I reached you.”

Riarhin looked at the ground.

“Yes.”

“If I’d turned back—”

“You couldn’t have reached him.”

“You don’t know.”

She knew the distances. She could have set out the roads, the days, the condition of his horse, every small fact that would leave him with the same death and a more carefully measured grief. He had not asked for measurement.

“No,” she said. “I don’t know everything.”

He pressed his forehead against the saddle. The horse turned its head, seeking something from him, and he lifted a hand to its cheek.

Na’ar set the filled bucket beside the trough.

“Drink,” he told Riarhin.

She took the cup. The water tasted of old wood.

***

The man from the porch came to them after dark.

He had bought the plain buckle. Riarhin noticed it before he spoke and hated that she had noticed. His companion was elsewhere. He stood beside the door, holding his cap, and said there was something he had not answered properly.

Berent sat up on the bed.

“He wasn’t calling for a rising,” the man said. “I didn’t hear that. He said he’d answer for what he’d done. He asked that the men who’d come to him be heard before they were named traitors for asking help.”

Riarhin watched the man turn the cap in his hands.

“Who read the sentence?”

“A clerk. I don’t know his name.”

“Was the queen there?”

“I couldn’t see her. That doesn’t mean she wasn’t.”

“Did he ask for anyone?” Berent said.

The man looked at him.

“I don’t know.”

They let him leave. Before the door closed he said his name was Perrin, if they needed to find him, and that he traded in wool combs along the lower road. Riarhin repeated the name once so that he knew she had heard it.

Afterwards Berent asked whether she believed him.

“Yes.”

“Because he gave his name?”

“Because he knew what he hadn’t heard.”

Berent lay back without taking off his boots. Na’ar sat on the floor near the door, with his coat folded beneath him. He straightened the leg the stone floor had caught in Ashaan and rubbed beside the shin with the heel of his hand. Riarhin wanted to ask whether his leg hurt and could not bear the ordinary shape of the question.

***

She did not sleep.

A man laughed in the room below. Someone struck a cup against a table in approval, and the laugh rose again, louder, then gave way to a song whose second line nobody agreed on. Riarhin could hear Elyas laughing beneath the damp blankets in Valora. His chair had rocked when he moved away from the drip. Mara had steadied it with a foot.

She had watched him turn towards his wife.

The memory was complete. She could attend to the cup, the wet patch on the collar, the scar on his hand, and each was available with the same indifference to what she needed from it. There was no hidden warning in the movement. Nothing would improve if she looked at it longer.

She looked at it longer.

The letter was beneath her shirt.

She took it out without lighting the lamp. She had read it in Glenruan, then once more at the first caravan halt, looking for an instruction she might have missed. Now she could follow the writing by the places where the sheet had been folded, reading each line from memory while her thumb moved over the actual paper.

If I am killed.

When she first read those words she had thought him too willing to make himself important to the danger. She had known princes who mistook being contradicted for being threatened. Elyas had never been one of them, yet she had found the comparison useful for the small comfort it offered: he might be frightened without being right.

Across the room Berent shifted on the bed.

“Is that his?”

“Yes.”

“Did he tell you what to do?”

“He told me what not to do.”

Berent sat up. The bed rope creaked beneath him. She could see the shape of his head against the lighter square of the window and remembered him standing with the letter inside his coat, careful to keep it away from the boy in the stable.

“I wondered,” he said.

“Whether he had given me a better message than yours?”

“Whether I was supposed to get you back whatever you said.”

“He didn’t say that.”

“No. He said to bring you if you would come.”

Riarhin held the paper against her knee. If she told him Elyas had asked her not to make a war, he might hear an instruction never intended for him. If she kept the sentence, she would become the person who decided which part of a dead man’s voice belonged to his friends.

“He wanted someone to hear Mara,” she said. “Without deciding what she ought to feel.”

Berent lowered his head. “She’ll be angry.”

“Yes.”

“With me?”

“I don’t know.”

He rubbed his face with both hands. She wanted to tell him that carrying a message did not make him responsible for what happened while he was away. She knew how little that would leave him to do with the fact that he had been away.

“Do you want to read it?”

“Did he give me leave?”

“He gave it to me.”

Berent considered that. At last he shook his head.

“Let her see it first.”

Riarhin folded the paper carefully in the dark. She had been treating it as something Elyas had left her. Berent, who had carried it farther, had found another way to regard it.

Before dawn Berent got up and went outside. Na’ar stirred but did not follow. Riarhin waited, then rose when the room became too close.

Berent stood at the horse trough washing his face. The water ran from his chin into the open neck of his shirt. He did it again when he saw her.

“Where is he?” he asked.

She knew he meant the body.

“We’ll find out.”

“Will they let Mara have him?”

“We’ll find out.”

He nodded, accepting the only answer she could give. From the stable a horse stamped. The keeper came out carrying a lantern and asked whether they intended to eat before leaving.

Riarhin said yes.

She had to ask him to repeat what he had offered. She had heard the words the first time and retained them perfectly. They had failed to become a meaning.

***

At breakfast the woman whose bowl had broken set a piece of bread beside Berent’s plate.

He thanked her. She moved away before he could say more. Riarhin watched her tuck the wrapped pieces of the bowl into the chest, setting them where they would not move beneath the other things. Her hands were careful.

When Ewen called the party out, Berent went to his place at the rear.

Riarhin caught him by the sleeve.

“We’re going to Mara.”

“Yes.”

“If you hear another account on the road, bring the person to me. Don’t go chasing a man somebody else heard.”

He looked at her hand, then at her face.

“I know.”

She released him.

They rode out after the last wagon. Na’ar stopped at the porch to ask the keeper to hold a message if a wool-comb seller returned. Riarhin waited while he arranged it, then took the road south beside him.

Chapter Twelve — The Empty Place

The town had made a market out of the space left by a burned storehouse.

Its old threshold remained across one entrance, black stone polished by feet which no longer entered a room. Stalls stood behind it beneath patched covers, their ropes fastened into holes where floor beams had been. Riarhin stepped over the threshold to let a woman carrying eggs pass, and for a moment smelled the ruin beneath the newer smells of onions and hot fat. Nobody selling there looked at the missing roof. They watched the sky beyond it, deciding how much longer the weather would allow them.

At the next large town, a woman asked whether there would be war.

She was selling hot food from a pot beside the market wall. Ewen had paid for the party to eat there because the inn’s kitchen was full, and Riarhin stood with a bowl in her hand while the woman looked at Berent’s sword.

“With Istovar,” she said. “My sister has a boy there.”

“Where?” Berent asked.

The woman named a place. He knew it and told her the road was open when he last travelled it.

“That was before.”

“Yes.”

“They say the duke won’t bear it.”

Riarhin watched the woman stir the pot after it no longer needed stirring. The fear was practical. She wanted to know whether to send a message, whether a son-in-law would arrive asking for money, whether the next cart of meal would cost more because someone had decided armies would be moving.

“I don’t know what he’ll do,” Berent said.

“You served them?”

“I served Elyas.”

The woman’s spoon stopped. She put another portion into his bowl without asking and moved to the next customer.

Two days later they heard that Harald was dead.

***

Ewen brought the news from a roadside house where he had gone to collect payment for a delivery.

“They say he fell.”

“From a horse?” Berent asked.

“Stairs.”

Riarhin looked at the carrier. He rubbed a thumb along the edge of a coin before putting it away.

“That’s what they say.”

“Who?”

“The steward. His mistress has kin in the duke’s household.”

“Did she write it?”

“Her man brought the news this morning.”

Berent asked about the succession. Ewen had heard three answers, all delivered with confidence. He gave none of them the dignity of repeating it as fact.

A carter standing nearby said, “Bloody Table.”

Ewen turned. “Do you know something?”

The man shrugged.

“Then keep your hands on the trace.”

Riarhin waited until the wagon had moved before asking where he had heard the name. The carter looked pleased to be consulted after being corrected.

“People say they do that sort of work.”

“Which people?”

“At the houses.”

“Have you met one?”

“No.”

“Has someone told you he has?”

The pleasure left him. “You think a duke falls down stairs just then?”

“I think you haven’t told me who pushed him.”

She rode ahead. The phrase remained, ugly and useful, capable of fitting whatever people were frightened enough to believe. She had heard names used that way before. Some belonged to real people.

At the next halt Berent sat apart from the others.

“Elyas would have—” he began when she approached.

He stopped. Riarhin sat beside him without requiring him to finish.

***

They reached the last turning before Valora in clear weather.

The fields had been harvested. New green showed along the drainage cuts, and a team worked slowly across a distant strip where the soil had dried enough to turn. The city stood beyond its farms with more scaffolding than Riarhin remembered and the same pale line of wall.

Ewen counted their wages beside the lead wagon.

“You’ve earned the whole,” he said. “Don’t argue me into keeping some.”

Riarhin took the coins. Berent asked whether he wanted them as far as the gate.

“I have men for the last mile. Go.”

The woman with the broken bowl raised a hand from her wagon. Her son sat beside her holding a strip of leather Na’ar had cut for him. He waved it like a small pennon.

Na’ar waved back.

They left the caravan where Mara’s directions required them to leave it. Riarhin remembered the turn exactly, including the warning about the larger gate. She had never been there, and the accuracy of the description made her feel expected in a way she did not want.

The smaller gate stood open.

***

Mara was carrying a basket of pears.

She came round the side of the house with it held against her hip, saw them at the gate, and stopped. The fruit was small, mottled yellow and green, with leaves still caught among it. Riarhin dismounted.

For a moment neither moved.

Then Mara set the basket on the ground so abruptly that two pears rolled into the path. She walked past Riarhin to Berent and caught his face between her hands.

“You’re here.”

“Yes.”

“He wanted—”

She stopped, drew him against her, and held him. Berent bent his head. Riarhin stood beside the mare with the reins in her hand.

Mara let him go and turned.

Riarhin had prepared nothing. She had meant to say Mara’s name, or that she was sorry, or one of the several useless true things people brought to a house because arriving empty-handed seemed worse. Mara crossed the space between them and struck her shoulder with the heel of one hand.

“You took so long.”

Riarhin accepted the blow. Mara caught her coat where she had struck it and pulled her close.

“I know,” Riarhin said.

Mara held on to her coat.

***

The house was occupied.

A woman named Nelle came from the kitchen and took charge of the horses before anyone thought to ask her. A boy brought water. Somewhere upstairs a shutter banged, and Nelle sent him to fasten it before he returned with the cups.

Mara sat at the table. One chair had been moved against the wall to make room for a basket of mending. Riarhin looked at it, then chose another.

Berent remained standing.

“Sit,” Mara told him.

He sat.

“Have you eaten?”

“Yes.”

“When?”

“This morning.”

“It’s afternoon.”

“Nelle is bringing something,” Riarhin said.

Mara looked towards the kitchen. Riarhin remembered the woman telling them. She waited while Mara looked towards the door.

“They brought him back,” Mara said.

Berent lowered his head.

“When?”

“Four days after. They’d written that I might receive him. I was to make arrangements.”

She looked at her hands on the table.

“Arrangements.”

“Where?” Riarhin asked.

Mara gave the place of burial. She had chosen it with advice from the minister, and paid the men who carried him, and refused a suggestion that the service be kept private for her comfort. More people had come than she expected. Some stood beyond the gate and left before speaking to her.

“I don’t know who they were.”

“Did they cause trouble?”

“No. They looked. Then they went away.”

Riarhin wanted to ask whether anyone from the queen’s household attended. She waited.

“Tylan sent a message,” Mara said. “It said he was sorry for my loss.”

She got up and went to a small chest beside the hearth. Her movement was swift and purposeful; for a moment Riarhin thought she would fetch the message. Instead she shut the lid with both hands and came back.

“I haven’t answered.”

“You needn’t,” Berent said.

Mara looked at him. “I may.”

Nelle brought bread and a dish of cold meat. They made room for it. The boy followed with cups, then lingered until Nelle touched his shoulder and took him away.

Mara ate a piece because Berent was watching. Riarhin could see the effort in her throat.

***

They went to the burial place the next morning.

Na’ar remained at the house to help Nelle with a door which had swollen in the damp. He offered the task himself. Mara thanked him, and he answered by asking where the tools were, sparing her an exchange of gratitude she could not finish.

Berent rode with them.

Riarhin had known where Braedyn lay and deferred going. Here the choice had been made by someone who would walk beside her. She dismounted at the boundary and led the mare rather than ask how much farther.

Mara stopped before a recent grave.

The earth had settled in one place. A narrow stone bore Elyas’s name, cut plainly, and a temporary wooden support kept it upright until the ground hardened. Somebody had left flowers. They were drying from the outside inward.

Berent knelt. He began a prayer under his breath and stopped at a response he could not finish. Mara gave it for him, her eyes on the stone. Riarhin knew the words well enough to join them and found that she could not. She stayed beside Mara until the prayer was done.

Riarhin remained standing, looking at the last letter of the name. The cutter had made its lower stroke slightly deeper than the others. She wondered whether the stone had resisted him there. The thought arrived with such exactness that she wanted to strike something.

Mara put a hand against her own mouth.

“He would have hated that,” she said.

“What?”

“The supports. He’d say they’d done the work in the wrong order.”

Berent made a sound that was almost a laugh. Mara looked down at him, and for an instant her face changed towards the woman at the table beneath the blankets.

Then she began to cry.

Riarhin went to her. There was nothing skilled in what followed. She held a person who leaned too hard and then pulled away, and waited while Mara tried to apologise for wetting her coat. Berent remained kneeling until he could stand without anyone watching him do it.

On the way home Mara said, “I want her to answer.”

Riarhin kept her horse beside hers.

“I know.”

“No. I want you to hear what I’m saying. I don’t want a letter saying the law was followed. I don’t want somebody telling me he should have been more careful.”

“I hear you.”

“Can you reach her?”

The road ran between two fields. A man mending a gate watched them pass and touched his cap to Mara. She returned the gesture without looking away from Riarhin.

“I can ask to be received.”

“Would she see you?”

“Perhaps.”

“And then?”

There it was: the room, the distance across it, the people standing near enough to interfere. Riarhin could imagine the approach before she imagined the words. Her left hand shifted against the rein.

Mara saw it.

“You’re thinking about it.”

“Yes.”

“Then don’t tell me I’m wrong to want it.”

“I haven’t.”

Riarhin looked towards the house ahead.

“I need to know what happened. Who spoke. What she was told. What she chose after she knew.”

“She chose this.”

“Yes.”

The agreement cost less than the qualification she had been preparing. She let Mara hear it.

“But I don’t know what else I’d be doing if I went into that room today.”

Mara rode on without answering. At the gate she dismounted and waited while Riarhin secured the horses.

“How long?”

“To find out?”

“Yes.”

“I won’t promise a number I don’t have.”

“Then promise you won’t find questions forever.”

Riarhin met her eyes.

“I won’t.”

***

Before they went into the house, Riarhin brought out Elyas’s letter.

Mara recognised the seal, though it had been broken. She took the paper without asking where it came from and stood with one hand resting on the gate while she read. The horses shifted behind them. Riarhin led them farther from the woman’s skirts, giving herself something to do which did not require watching every movement in her face.

Mara went back to the beginning once. When she reached the postscript, she put her thumb over it.

“He always did that.”

“What?”

“Something ordinary at the end. As though he’d made it safe to put the letter down.”

Riarhin looped the reins over the rail. Mara read the last line again without uncovering the whole of it.

“Did he ask you to keep this from me?”

“No.”

“Did he tell Berent?”

“Only to give it where nobody could see me read it.”

“Where have you kept it?”

“On me.”

Mara turned towards the house. “Come inside.”

She took Riarhin into the little room off the hall where Elyas had received people when the weather made the garden useless. A chair had been pushed beneath the table at an angle; when Mara drew it out, a small shaving of wood fell from a repaired leg. There was a shelf for papers, empty except for a weight shaped like a sleeping dog. She set the letter beneath it, leaving the signature exposed.

“He told me he was worried,” she said. “He didn’t tell me he was writing about being killed.”

“He may not have meant to frighten you.”

“I know what he meant.”

Riarhin accepted the correction. Mara sat, rose almost at once, and went to close the outer door. From beyond it came Nelle’s voice asking Na’ar to lift something while she swept underneath.

“He could be very tender when he was deciding something for both of us,” Mara said. “Sometimes I let him because I wanted what he wanted. Sometimes I didn’t notice until he had gone out and I was left explaining it.”

She looked at the dog on the paper.

“I love him. I am furious with him. You needn’t choose which one I mean.”

“I won’t.”

“Did you think I was going to ask you to raise his friends against her?”

“I didn’t know what you would ask.”

“He’s told you not to.”

“He’s told me he isn’t asking it. That isn’t the same thing.”

Mara drew the letter free and read the passage about herself. She pressed the edge of the sheet between her fingers, flattening a crease which would return as soon as she folded it.

“At least he knew he was being unfair.”

Riarhin almost smiled, and stopped herself until Mara looked up with something like the same impulse. It passed between them, small and painful, neither of them required to apologise for it.

“Keep it,” Riarhin said.

Mara shook her head.

“They’ve already asked to see what he left here. You brought this from elsewhere. Keep it elsewhere.”

“Would you like a copy?”

“Yes. Not today.”

She folded it along the lines Riarhin had made and held it out. For a moment Riarhin saw both their hands around the paper and thought of the man who had written about wanting to be embarrassed in front of her. He had denied them the easy comfort of believing him unaware. He had also left a quarrel unfinished which Mara would never be able to have with him.

Riarhin put the letter away.

When Mara opened the door, the noise of the house returned: a chair being set down, the boy asking where to put a pair of muddy boots, Nelle saying he could begin by moving them out of the middle of the floor. Mara stood listening as if she had come back after an absence.

***

Lyra’s first message was waiting in the kitchen.

Nelle had accepted it from a woman who sold thread and had been asked to bring it only if the travellers had arrived. The paper contained a place, an hour, and a line in Lyra’s hand saying she could come alone.

Mara read it, then passed it to Riarhin.

“She wrote after the arrest,” she said. “I didn’t answer well.”

“What did she say?”

“That she was trying to find out what had been used.”

“And you?”

“I asked her what use she was if she couldn’t find out before.”

Riarhin folded the message.

“Has she written again?”

“That is again.”

Nelle returned to the stove. The swollen door stood open behind her, newly planed, and Na’ar was outside shaking shavings from his coat.

Mara put a hand on the table.

“Tell her I’ll hear what she has.”

Riarhin nodded. She put the message inside her coat and went to fetch her horse.

Chapter Thirteen — The Visit Lyra Remembered

Lyra chose a room above a seamstress’s shop.

The street was too narrow for the delivery wagon standing in it. Its driver had unharnessed one horse so that people could squeeze past the shafts, and the freed animal was trying to eat a bundle of rushes meant for the floor of the house opposite. Riarhin waited for a woman with a basket to pass before stepping through. Above her, cloth hung from upper windows to air, pale shapes turning slowly whenever the wind found its way between the roofs.

She had dressed in the clothes she had travelled in, brushed clean enough to enter a shop. Once she had let women in Valora fit her for evenings with the queen. She wondered whether any of them would recognise her here, waiting to be admitted above a room full of cut cloth.

Riarhin arrived early and was made to wait below while a woman measured a sleeve on an unwilling boy. The boy wanted to know whether he could remove the coat. The woman said he could when it fitted. He pointed out that she could make it fit more easily if he were not wearing it, and she told him that a coat which fitted a chair would be of very little use at his sister’s wedding.

Riarhin stood by the window.

The shop smelled of heated cloth. A young woman sat near the back, drawing stitches from a hem with a small hooked tool. She looked up at Riarhin’s sword and then returned to her work without changing the pace of it.

“Who shall I say?” the seamstress asked.

“Riarhin.”

“She’s expected you.”

“Is she here?”

“She’ll come down.”

The boy dropped one shoulder. The seamstress lifted it again.

Riarhin waited. There were two doors from the shop, one to the street and one to the stair, and a yard beyond the rear window which could be reached only by going through the workroom or by climbing over a wall. She stopped herself at the wall. Nobody had asked her to escape.

Lyra came down carrying a folded coat.

“This one needs the inner pocket moved,” she told the seamstress. “He wants to reach it without opening the front.”

“Then he should have asked before I stitched it.”

“He has learned that now.”

“Has he?”

“He has paid for learning.”

Lyra set the coat on a clear place and turned. She wore a blue gown with a narrow dark binding at the neck. Her hair was fastened well. The pin that had slipped outside the baker’s five years before had no reason to be in Riarhin’s mind, but there it was.

“Come up,” Lyra said.

***

The upper room had a table, three chairs, and a bed screened behind hanging cloth.

Lyra closed the door. For a moment she stood with her hand still on the latch, looking at Riarhin as though she had brought the wrong person upstairs.

Then she crossed the room and held her.

Riarhin had come prepared for questions. The embrace displaced them. Lyra’s shoulders shook once, stopped, then shook again with an effort she was making to prevent it. Riarhin put her hand at the back of her head and waited.

“I meant to come,” Lyra said against her coat.

“To Mara?”

“Yes.”

“She’ll hear you.”

“That isn’t what I asked her.”

Riarhin closed her eyes. She had offered permission when Lyra was trying to tell her what she had lost.

“I’m sorry,” she said.

Lyra stepped back and wiped her face with the side of a finger. “For what?”

“That answer.”

“Oh.”

She looked almost amused, then the expression went. She fetched water from a jug and poured it into two cups, spilling some on the table. Riarhin moved a sheet out of the wet.

“Leave that,” Lyra said quickly.

Riarhin put it down without turning it over.

“It concerns someone else,” Lyra said.

“I gathered.”

“They haven’t agreed to be involved in this.”

“Neither had Elyas.”

Lyra’s face tightened. Riarhin wished the words back too late.

“Sit,” Lyra said. “Please.”

***

She began with what she had given Berent.

An introduction to a carrier at a northern stop, useful if his horse went lame or he needed a packet forwarded. Berent had told her Elyas wished Riarhin found quietly. She had asked whether Mara knew, and he had said yes. That was all she had needed to know.

“Why you?” Riarhin asked.

“Because I could give him a name.”

“Why did he trust the name?”

“Because I’ve used the man. Because I’d promised to answer for the introduction. Because everything wasn’t already ruined.”

Riarhin let her finish.

Lyra took a folded invitation from a small case. Jaennis’s name appeared in the opening, in another hand. The invitation concerned supper and a woman visiting court who wished to hear of arrangements for travellers between Viterra and the southern cities.

“Ordinary business,” Lyra said. “There were other people. I stayed after they left.”

“Who asked you to stay?”

“Jaennis.”

“What did she want?”

“She asked about Elyas. About whether he was happy at home. Whether Mara found the house convenient.”

Riarhin looked at the invitation. Its pleasant language made no promises it had failed to keep.

“What did you say?”

“What I’d say to anybody. That they were well. That he’d been away. That he worried.”

“About the queen?”

“Among other things.”

Lyra took her cup and put it down without drinking.

“I remember deciding not to say more.”

Riarhin waited.

“That is the part I remember most clearly. I knew where the conversation was going. I’d answered enough. She was kind, and I thought I could leave it there.”

“What happened?”

“She asked whether he’d sent for anyone who might reassure him.”

“And?”

“I told her he had sent Berent north.”

“Why?”

Lyra looked at her.

“Because I wanted to tell her.”

The answer came almost angrily. Riarhin stayed still.

“I wanted her to understand. I wanted her to be pleased that I trusted her. I thought how absurd it was to treat a friend as an enemy because other people had made us cautious.”

“Had you been drinking?”

“A little.”

“Something she gave you?”

“The same wine other people had. I asked afterwards.”

“Did you feel ill?”

“No.”

“Confused?”

“No. Relieved.”

Riarhin looked at the cup between Lyra’s hands. The water inside it was still.

“When did that change?”

“Outside. Not all at once. I thought about the conversation and realised I’d told her something I had meant to keep. I wrote to Elyas the next morning.”

“Did he answer?”

“He said Berent was carrying a private request to an old friend. He would answer for it if asked.”

Riarhin could hear him saying that. It was true. He had died after insisting on several true things.

***

“Do you think there was a working?” she asked.

Lyra looked towards the window.

“I think I know when I am being persuaded. I think I have wanted people’s approval before and kept my mouth shut while wanting it.”

“That isn’t certainty.”

“No.”

“Could Jaennis do it?”

“She’s been learning. Small things. Things that make an evening go more easily.”

“From whom?”

“I don’t know all of them.”

“Who do you know?”

“Maelis serves Mab. She has been there for years. She alters cloth and light for entertainments. Sometimes more than that.”

“Was she there that night?”

“At supper. She left before I did.”

“Did she touch you? Give you anything?”

“No.”

“Then why her?”

“Because she would understand the difference between being made comfortable and being made to confide. Because if I asked her, the question would reach Jaennis before I got home.”

Riarhin leaned back.

“Has Fayd heard this?”

“Part of it.”

“Which part?”

“Enough to know I made an error.”

“Does he call it that?”

“He asked me what I meant to do about it.”

“What did you tell him?”

“That I meant to find out whether it was mine.”

Below them the boy shouted that the sleeve was too tight. The seamstress told him to stop flexing his arm while she held a pin. Lyra listened for a moment, grateful for an interruption neither of them had to provide.

“Are you working for him?” Riarhin asked.

“When we have business together.”

“Is that safe?”

“No occupation answers that by its name.”

Riarhin almost smiled. Lyra did not.

“I have clients. People whose affairs need to remain theirs. Sometimes he can help. Sometimes I can tell him something he wants without giving him someone I’ve promised to protect. I don’t sit in his office waiting to be called.”

“And when he wants the people?”

“I refuse. Sometimes they go to him anyway.”

Lyra picked up the damp sheet and moved it beyond Riarhin’s reach.

“I won’t give you their names because you’re angry. I won’t give them to Mara because she’s right to be angry. I have done harm already.”

Riarhin looked at the empty place on the table.

“You think I’d use them.”

“I think you’d decide they were necessary.”

The answer settled between them. Riarhin had no reason to resent it except that she wanted a different one.

“All right,” she said.

***

Lyra described Maelis’s ordinary movements.

There was a linen seller she visited, a house where she rehearsed with musicians, and a room she hired when work required more privacy than Jaennis’s household could supply. She gave Riarhin only the last two places and warned her that the servants there were not part of whatever had happened at supper.

“What are you going to do?” she asked.

“Speak to her.”

“How?”

“I haven’t met her yet.”

“That doesn’t answer.”

“I know.”

Lyra’s eyes stayed on hers. She had expected the evasion and disliked receiving it anyway.

“Don’t make me wish I hadn’t told you.”

Riarhin put the invitation back on the table.

“May I take this?”

“You may copy the date and the names. I need the paper.”

“I can remember them.”

“Yes,” Lyra said. “You can.”

There was a tiredness in the words that made Riarhin stop reaching for her coat.

“What?”

“Nothing you can mend by remembering better.”

Riarhin sat down again.

Lyra turned her cup between her palms. They stayed that way while the shop below emptied of the boy and his mother. A door opened, a woman called thanks after them, and the building became quieter.

“He liked the house,” Lyra said at last.

“I know.”

“He kept asking me whether Mara liked it. I told him to ask her.”

“She’d have told him.”

“She did. He thought she might be sparing him.”

Riarhin smiled despite herself. Lyra looked at the smile and began to cry again, more quietly this time, without trying to talk through it.

Riarhin stayed until she could.

Chapter Fourteen — The Woman Outside the Gate

Mara wanted to come.

Riarhin had known she would. She described the music house, the side entrance, and the room above it in which Maelis met a woman who altered costumes. Mara listened until the description became a plan.

“You’re going to frighten her.”

“I’m going to ask questions she may not want to answer.”

“Then say frighten.”

Riarhin stood beside the table. Na’ar was outside with Berent. The house had acquired the quiet that comes when people begin to know which conversations require them elsewhere.

“Yes.”

“Does Lyra know?”

“She knows I’m going to speak to her.”

Mara folded the cloth she had been holding. “I’ll come.”

“If she knows your face—”

“She may. I’ve been at court. You’ve been at court. Neither of us becomes innocent by sending the other.”

“That isn’t why.”

“Then tell me a reason that is.”

Riarhin looked at the doorway. She wanted Mara safe in the house. She also wanted the freedom to decide what might be necessary without having Elyas’s widow standing beside her when she decided it.

“She could name you,” she said.

“Yes.”

“They might make use of that.”

“They have already made use of him. I am still here.”

Riarhin sat down.

They planned the journey again, this time with two women entering and one man waiting beyond the house. Berent would remain at the manor. His presence on a city street might invite questions they had no need to answer.

He disliked the decision and accepted it.

***

Maelis came out carrying a wicker box.

Riarhin had watched her arrive with a musician and leave him arguing about a payment. The votary wore a dark gown with one sleeve caught back at the wrist. Her full mouth was set in the controlled expression of someone determined not to return and say the thing she had thought of too late.

She stopped beneath the side arch to rearrange the cloth over her box.

Mara stepped into view.

“Maelis.”

The woman looked up. Recognition came slowly, then reached her face all at once.

“Mara.”

“I need to ask you something.”

“I’m very sorry. About Elyas.”

“Then hear me.”

Maelis glanced towards the street. Riarhin moved from the deeper shadow of the arch. The woman’s eyes went to her hand and the knife she was holding low against her skirt.

“Oh,” Maelis said.

It was a small, disappointed sound, quite unlike the beginning of a cry.

Riarhin put her free hand on the door behind them. Na’ar had lifted the latch earlier from the yard side. It opened into an unused preparation room smelling of dust and old flowers.

“In,” she said.

Maelis stayed still. The box moved against her fingers.

“There are people—”

“Inside.”

Mara took the box before it could fall. Maelis let her, and stepped through the door.

***

Riarhin closed it.

The room had a table, two chairs, and a shelf carrying an assortment of cracked vessels which somebody intended to repair. A narrow window admitted light from the yard. Na’ar stood beyond it where he could watch the passage without looking in.

Maelis backed against the table.

“Put that away.”

“Sit.”

“Put it away first.”

Riarhin moved closer. The point of Toothpick rose a little. Maelis sat so quickly that the chair struck the wall.

Mara put the wicker box on the table. Something within it made a brief scraping sound.

“What is it?” Riarhin asked.

“A cricket.”

“Does it do anything?”

“It is a cricket.”

Riarhin looked at her. Maelis’s face had gone pale around the mouth.

“It hears things,” she added. “So do you. Please move the knife.”

Riarhin rested the flat of the blade against her own thigh. Maelis watched the blade. Her hands stayed in her lap.

Mara gave the date of Lyra’s supper.

Maelis frowned. “I was there.”

“Who stayed after you left?”

“I don’t know. I’d left.”

“Lyra stayed.”

“Then you know more than I do.”

Riarhin shifted her weight. Maelis’s eyes returned to the knife.

“I am answering,” she said. “I can’t answer better by being frightened.”

Mara touched Riarhin’s arm. “Let her.”

They described the conversation as Lyra had given it. Maelis listened without interrupting. By the end, one hand lay flat against her own knee, pressing the fabric as though she needed the pressure to keep it still.

“Could it have been a charm?” Mara asked.

“Yes.”

“Could Jaennis have done it?”

“Yes.”

“Did you teach her?”

“No.”

“Who did?”

“I don’t know.”

Riarhin stepped closer.

Maelis flinched. “I don’t. She has asked me about things. I have shown her things. That does not mean I have shown her everything she can do.”

“What things?”

“Light. Colour. A room’s attention. How to make a listener more willing to remain. There are differences.”

“Explain them.”

The woman looked at Mara, then back at Riarhin.

“If someone is at ease, they may speak. If you incline them to trust, they may speak where they would have kept silence. The words remain theirs. They can remember choosing them.”

“Can they resist?”

“Sometimes.”

“Would they know?”

“Sometimes.”

“Could you know afterwards?”

Maelis shook her head.

Riarhin put the knife against the table. Its point touched wood beside the woman’s hand. Maelis snatched the hand back.

“You’re very certain about what you can’t tell us.”

“Because I would rather tell you something you can believe than guess and have you return.”

Her voice broke on the last word. Riarhin saw the wetness begin in her eyes and felt, with an unpleasant clarity, how effective the fear had become. She could use it again. She was already deciding how.

Mara stepped between them.

“She doesn’t know.”

“She may—”

“She has told us what she knows.”

Riarhin looked at Mara’s shoulder. The space beyond it was still available. She would have to move Mara or ask her to move, and either act would be something she could no longer pretend belonged only to Maelis.

She lowered the knife.

***

Maelis asked for water.

Mara found a clean cup among the vessels and rinsed it from a jug left beneath the shelf. Riarhin examined the water before letting her offer it, which made Maelis laugh once with no pleasure at all.

“You are careful.”

“Yes.”

“With some things.”

Riarhin accepted the remark.

Maelis drank with both hands. The cricket sounded from the box, one clear note. She touched the lid and it stopped.

“What do you want now?” she asked.

“To get close enough to ask the queen.”

“Then ask for an audience.”

“And be brought to a room chosen for the question she expects.”

“Yes. That is what an audience is.”

Mara sat in the other chair. “Could you bring someone into the household?”

“People come into the household every day.”

“Someone the queen doesn’t expect.”

Maelis looked at Riarhin more closely. Fear had narrowed her attention before. Now she took in the face, the ears, the scar, and the way she held herself despite the plain travelling clothes.

“You’re Riarhin.”

“Yes.”

“Oh, that makes this worse.”

“Why?”

“Because people might enjoy discovering it.”

Riarhin waited.

“They know you,” Maelis said. “Some of them. You could cover your hair and call yourself anything you liked. You would still move through a door as if you had already decided which part of it would break.”

“Could you change what they see?”

The woman’s hand tightened around the cup.

“Yes.”

“How much?”

“That depends.”

“On what?”

“On whether you mean to let me leave this room alive.”

Mara looked at Riarhin. The question could not be answered by somebody else.

Riarhin sheathed Toothpick.

“If you help me, you leave.”

“And afterwards?”

“You keep what happened here to yourself.”

“That is a separate demand.”

“Yes.”

Maelis put down the cup carefully.

“I can support an introduction. I can make you look like someone else, under conditions we would have to discuss. I can arrange work for a woman who knows how to be received in those rooms. I cannot make every guard forget his duty or every friend forget your voice because you have frightened me.”

“Then we discuss the conditions.”

“And when I have done what we agree?”

“I leave you alone.”

“Those words need to include coming back with a new requirement and the same knife.”

Riarhin looked at her for a long moment.

“They include it.”

Mara rested both hands in her lap. She had heard the promise. Riarhin would not be able to remember it differently later.

***

The first condition was a private room.

Maelis refused the room in which they stood, refused Mara’s house because it might be watched, and named the hired room Lyra had already described. She would bring what she needed there the following afternoon. Riarhin would arrive by the rear stair, alone.

“No,” Mara said.

Maelis looked at her.

“I am coming.”

“Then two. Nobody else. If I have to work while looking at a man filling the doorway, you may find yourselves wearing an expression neither of you wanted.”

Na’ar, visible through the narrow window, turned his head as though he had heard his description.

Maelis followed the glance.

“Who is that?”

“A friend,” Riarhin said.

“Does he frighten people professionally?”

“He has.”

“Good. I shall need someone.”

Riarhin stared at her.

“I’ve been set upon,” Maelis said. “Outside a house where I have business. I have reason to fear for my life.”

“That’s true,” Mara said.

“It is entirely true. I’ll require a guard, and if you wish to know what I’m doing, you can pay half his wages.”

“He isn’t yours to hire.”

“Then ask him whether he wants work. I should prefer him to somebody who might prove brave at the wrong moment.”

There was colour in her face again. Her fingers still shook when she lifted the box, but she had found something she could arrange and intended to arrange it well.

Riarhin opened the door.

Maelis paused beside her.

“If we are to do this, you must begin by understanding that being noticed is sometimes the useful part.”

“I know.”

“I hope so. It is very difficult to introduce a woman who thinks every eye upon her is an injury.”

She stepped into the passage and waited for Mara to give back the cup before she remembered she did not own it. The confusion cost her the composed departure she had been preparing. She went quickly towards the street.

Riarhin watched her turn the corner.

Mara stood beside her.

“You meant it,” she said.

“Which?”

“The knife.”

Riarhin could have distinguished threat from intention, intention from act. Mara had been in the room.

“Yes.”

They walked back to the horses.

Chapter Fifteen — Her Mother’s Mouth

The room was above a house where nobody asked their names.

A woman at the door told them the upstairs tenant had left instructions, then returned to a pot on the hearth. Riarhin looked towards the rear passage. The woman pointed without turning round.

“Stair’s there.”

They climbed. Mara went first, carrying a small bundle of clothes. Riarhin had questioned the need for them until Mara asked whether she meant to appear in another woman’s face and the same coat she had worn to the queen’s table.

Maelis was waiting beside a covered mirror.

She had brought the wicker box and a folded length of silk with knots worked into it. A pin held one corner to the table. Two candles lit the room. Riarhin looked from the pinned silk to Maelis’s hands.

Riarhin found that almost disappointing.

Maelis noticed her inspection.

“If you need a procession, I can charge for one.”

“I need to know what this does.”

“Yes. Sit.”

Riarhin remained standing. Maelis looked at the chair, then at Mara.

“Does she ever sit when first asked?”

“Occasionally,” Mara said. “When she was going to.”

Riarhin sat.

***

“You must know the person,” Maelis said.

“How well?”

“Enough to hold them in your attention without borrowing a stranger’s guess. Their face, their voice, how they inhabit a pause. If you have only seen them across a room, I can give you something that will please a room. I cannot give you a person at speaking distance.”

Riarhin looked at the silk. Its knots seemed ordinary until she tried to follow which length passed through which, and then the arrangement became inconvenient to remember as a flat thing.

“I know people.”

“They must be dead.”

Mara’s hand tightened on the bundle.

“Why?” Riarhin asked.

“Because this is the working I can offer you. I can work with a likeness that no longer has a living face disputing it. I won’t promise you what I cannot hold.”

“Other workings—”

“May do other things. Other practitioners may offer them. You brought me here.”

Riarhin leaned back. The answer had the useful hardness of a limit Maelis was prepared to defend.

“How much does it change?”

“What people perceive. What you present to them. It will not lengthen your bones or make a chair move because your borrowed hip appears to touch it. You will have to learn where the likeness exceeds you and where you exceed it.”

“My voice?”

“If you know it.”

“My hands?”

“As seen. As used, they remain your hands.”

Maelis’s gaze fell to the left hand resting on the chair arm.

“You favour that one.”

“Yes.”

“The likeness will not make you another woman’s habits. Nor give you her memories. It will ask for your attention every moment you keep it before someone.”

“And asleep?”

“Alone, you may let it go. If you sleep beside someone who believes you have another face, arrange not to wake.”

Mara looked sharply at her. Maelis lifted a hand.

“I mean arrange not to fall asleep there. I would like this undertaking to contain fewer opportunities for misunderstandings about death.”

Riarhin almost smiled. The impulse surprised her. The woman she had frightened yesterday was asking for care with a sentence while preparing to lend her another body’s appearance.

“How often do you renew it?”

“We will see how you hold it. At first, you come to me before each appearance. Later, perhaps less often. I will not tell you it lasts a month and discover at supper that I was optimistic.”

The cricket moved inside its box. Maelis touched the wicker with one finger, then drew back the cloth covering the mirror.

“Who?”

***

Riarhin had known from the first condition.

She had let Maelis explain the rest because understanding the limits gave her something to do before speaking the name. There were dead people she remembered with exquisite clarity. She could hold a man’s expression through an entire remembered conversation, reproduce the angle of a head, the impatience in a voice. She had never inhabited the space beside most of them long enough to know what a face did when it stopped being offered to other people.

There was one woman she had watched without knowing she was learning.

“My mother.”

Mara turned towards her. Maelis accepted the answer with a small nod.

“Her name?”

“Bea.”

“You’re certain?”

“Yes.”

“Then tell me something about her.”

Riarhin looked into the mirror. Her own face looked back, narrowed by the poor silvering at one edge. She moved slightly and the distortion changed.

“She was taller.”

“Than you?”

“Yes.”

“How much?”

Riarhin held her hand above her head, then stopped. She had been a child. The measure she first reached for belonged to a child’s height beside a grown woman.

She could find another. A doorframe, a visitor whose height she knew later, the length of a gown laid out for mending. She worked through them while Maelis waited.

“Some,” she said at last, giving the difference more carefully.

“Good. That matters near a lintel. What else?”

“Fair. Red hair.”

“Like yours?”

“Redder.”

“Show me the colour as you remember it.”

Riarhin closed her eyes.

Bea stood by the window after washing it, the hair darkened by water and spread over her shoulders. She had a comb in one hand and a broken tooth from the comb in the other. She was complaining about somebody who had borrowed it, though she knew perfectly well it had been broken for months.

The room in Silkstrand came with her: the sill, the light, a stain on the wall, a cart outside whose wheel made the same complaint every morning. Riarhin had been playing on the floor with pieces of ribbon. She could see them without moving her eyes.

“Stay there,” Maelis said.

***

The silk touched her wrists.

Maelis had asked before doing it, and Riarhin had agreed. The contact remained difficult. She kept her eyes closed while the woman laid a knotted corner across the back of each hand and began speaking too softly for the words to separate cleanly from the scrape of the cloth.

Bea drew the comb through her hair.

Riarhin’s first thought was that she had chosen the wrong moment. Her mother was annoyed. The face she wanted ought to be smiling, or at least still, something simple to maintain while a stranger worked. But Bea lifted the comb, caught a snag, and swore beneath her breath in the accent Riarhin had spent the last five years hearing from Tavish’s neighbours.

She had remembered that accent. She had never heard how much of home remained in it until she had a home to compare it with.

“Breathe,” Maelis said.

Riarhin did.

The remembered woman turned, saw the child on the floor, and smiled at something she had been making. The smile changed one side of her mouth before the other. Riarhin had seen courtiers practise an approximation of it in mirrors. Bea had used it while looking at a tangle of ribbon.

“Open your eyes.”

She did.

Her mother sat in the chair.

***

Riarhin reached for the mirror.

The silk slipped across her wrist. Maelis caught it and said something sharply, but Riarhin had already stopped because the hand reaching towards the glass was Bea’s hand and the sensation inside it remained her own.

The mismatch turned her stomach.

She watched the hand withdraw. The face in the mirror looked frightened, and that was worse. She remembered Bea frightened. She had not chosen that memory.

“Let it go,” Maelis said.

Riarhin tried. The effort made the face more exact.

“Look at me. At me, not her.”

She turned towards the voice. A woman with a pale mark at the chin stood beside the table, holding silk in both hands. Mara was behind her chair. The room smelled of warm cloth and the faint dry smell from the cricket’s box.

Riarhin found her own hands again.

Maelis lowered the silk.

“Good.”

Mara knelt beside the chair.

“Riarhin.”

“I’m here.”

“I know.”

She wanted Mara to stop looking at her. She wanted the mirror covered. She wanted, with an appetite so sudden that it embarrassed her, to see the face again.

“Did it work?” she asked.

“Yes,” Maelis said.

“Was it right?”

“I didn’t know your mother.”

Riarhin shut her eyes. Of course she had not. There was nobody in the room who could answer the question except the person asking it.

“Give me a moment.”

Maelis put down the silk and went to the window. Mara remained beside her until Riarhin touched her hand, then stood and moved away too.

***

The second attempt lasted longer.

Riarhin chose Bea coming home in the evening, before she had removed her shoes. She knew the movement from the door to the bed, the care with which her mother set down the things in her hands before releasing the day’s expression. She took the moment before the release and held it.

In the mirror, Bea waited.

Maelis adjusted one knot. The light along the hair changed, settling into a shade less brilliant and more convincing than the first. Riarhin turned her head. The woman in the glass turned with her.

“Speak.”

“What shall I say?”

The voice was wrong for the first word, her own, then shifted through a roughness into the remembered sound. Mara looked away.

“Again,” Maelis said.

“What shall I say?”

This time it was Bea.

Riarhin gripped the chair arm. The long fingers in the mirror gripped it too. She loosened them deliberately.

“Something you’ve heard her say.”

That would have been easier. She could choose a sentence and reproduce it, give herself the comfort of a thing already done. Instead she looked at Mara and said, “You can sit down. You don’t have to stand for this.”

Bea’s voice delivered words Bea had never spoken to a woman she had never met.

Mara sat.

Riarhin felt the work then. Not a weight pressing on her, nor a pain. She had to keep attending to the person she was showing while listening to another person answer, and the moment she tried to give Mara the whole of her concern, the face in the mirror loosened at its edges.

Maelis lifted a hand.

“There.”

Riarhin restored it.

“What did you see?”

“A little of your ear. A change at the mouth. Someone looking at you might think the light had moved. Someone already suspicious would look harder.”

“And if I kept losing it?”

“They would see you.”

Riarhin nodded. She wanted the failure described exactly. It was something she could work with.

***

They tried standing.

The first time, she struck the apparent hem of a dress she was not wearing and checked a movement that needed no checking. Maelis made her look down at her actual feet and then back at the mirror. The image followed the room’s light well enough to be convincing. It could not make her body occupy the space it suggested.

“You’ll dress to support it,” Maelis said. “Nothing so close that a hand upon your waist discovers what an eye has missed. A woman of her height and bearing can wear cloth that moves.”

“She did.”

“Good. Show me.”

Riarhin crossed the room.

She had walked beside Bea, behind her, towards her. Watching from within the movement was different. Her first attempt gave the shoulders too much certainty. Her mother’s apparent ease had included a willingness to let other people make room, and Riarhin kept choosing the gap before anybody offered it.

Maelis moved a chair into her path.

Riarhin went round it.

“No,” the votary said. “Look at me.”

She looked.

Maelis moved the chair away.

“There.”

Mara laughed suddenly. Both women turned.

“I’m sorry,” she said. “It’s only that you look so offended by needing help.”

Riarhin could feel the expression and see it in the mirror at the same time. Bea was looking furious about a chair. The absurdity broke through the effort, and she laughed too.

The glamour flickered.

She caught it before Maelis spoke.

“Once more,” Maelis said. “Then give Mara your hand.”

Riarhin steadied the likeness. Mara held out her palm, and Riarhin laid her left hand in it. The hand she saw was longer than the one she felt. Mara’s thumb rested across two fingers and appeared to sink a little into the space between them.

Mara looked up.

“Yes,” Maelis said. “That.”

“Would anyone notice?” Riarhin asked.

“You just did.”

“I was looking for it.”

“So might someone who wishes to kiss your fingers. Hold still.”

Maelis asked Mara to close her eyes and find Riarhin’s wrist with her other hand. She did it easily. With her eyes open she tried again, hesitated, and put her fingers too high before correcting the movement.

Riarhin watched Mara’s face. Mara frowned at her own hand. Bea’s face remained in the mirror.

“It invites people to believe their eyes,” Maelis said. “Don’t make them choose.”

Mara let her hand go. Riarhin rubbed the place where the thumb had rested. A companion’s work included being touched. She had thought about the hands she would permit and those she would remove, with a knife if necessary. She had spent less thought on a woman taking her wrist to draw her towards a chair.

“Show me an ordinary greeting,” Maelis said.

Mara stood, looking apologetic before she began. She put one hand at Riarhin’s shoulder and leaned towards her cheek. Riarhin brought the likeness to meet her, moving her own body too far, and their foreheads struck.

“Oh, very good,” Maelis said.

Mara began laughing. Riarhin pressed a hand to her brow and found herself laughing too, badly enough that she had to let Bea go for a moment.

“Again,” she said.

This time she let Mara come to her. The touch landed near the corner of her mouth. She could feel the true position and hold the appearance above it, provided she stopped trying to make her bones follow what she saw.

They tried sitting close on the bench, an arm offered while walking, a hand guiding her through the door. Maelis made her take the outside of the turn so the sleeve fell between Mara’s hand and the apparent outline of her arm. Loose cloth could cover a small discrepancy. It could not persuade a determined hand that a waist was where no waist existed.

After a while Mara withdrew and rubbed her own forehead.

“You could simply tell people you dislike being embraced.”

“They will want to know why,” Maelis said.

“Let them wonder.”

Riarhin looked at her. Mara was waiting for an answer.

“I can do that,” Riarhin said.

She held out her arm for one last attempt. Mara took it, and they walked the length of the room without either woman looking down.

***

The borrowed clothes did not fit the likeness.

They had brought a gown of Mara’s, but it suited a woman who meant to work while wearing it. Maelis examined the seams, then asked Riarhin to describe what Bea wore when she wanted to be noticed.

“A dark gown.”

“What sort?”

“Good cloth.”

“Riarhin.”

She knew the answer. Giving it to this woman felt like handing over something that had survived because it had remained hers alone.

“Blue, sometimes. Open here.” She touched the base of her throat. “The sleeve narrow at the wrist. She had a chain she would move while somebody spoke to her.”

“How?”

Riarhin showed her. The movement was small: the fingers finding a link, the thumb turning it, the hand remaining where the person watching might continue to look.

Maelis nodded.

“She understood.”

“It was her work.”

The same hardness she had heard at Glenruan entered the words. Mara looked at her. Maelis only asked, “Was she good at it?”

“Yes.”

“Then learn from her.”

Riarhin turned away from the mirror.

“I was a child.”

“I know.”

“You don’t.”

Maelis stood with her hands resting on the back of the chair. Her fear from yesterday had not vanished; Riarhin could see it return when she raised her voice. The woman kept her place anyway.

“I know that you were a child,” she said. “I don’t know what she kept from you. I can’t give you that. I can help you use what you saw.”

Riarhin looked at the cloth on the table.

Bea had sent her downstairs before some visitors came. Others had brought sweets and asked questions whose answers interested them less than the act of asking in her mother’s hearing. Riarhin had known which men to dislike and had been wrong about some of them. She had not forgiven the wrongness because it required admitting that Bea possessed a life she could neither protect nor completely understand.

“What would I be expected to do?” she asked.

“At court?”

“Yes.”

“Converse. Listen. Remember who wishes to be remembered and who would prefer not to be mentioned together. Make an evening pleasant without making your labour its chief subject.”

“And after the evening?”

Maelis looked at her steadily.

“Decide what you want. Your invitation is not an agreement to every request made under it. I’ll introduce a companion, not sell a captive.”

Mara’s eyes moved to Riarhin. The words belonged painfully to the room in which they had made this arrangement.

Riarhin nodded.

***

At the end Maelis covered the mirror.

The ordinary movement released something in Riarhin’s shoulders. She let Bea go and looked at her own left hand against the dark fabric of her coat. The scar beside a knuckle remained where it had been.

“Tomorrow,” Maelis said. “We’ll try the clothes.”

“And the introduction?”

“When you can cross a room without looking as though the chair has challenged you to a duel.”

Riarhin accepted that.

Mara gathered the unused gown. Maelis took up the wicker box and checked the small creature within it. For a moment her expression softened in a way neither of the other women had earned.

“Your friend has agreed to guard me,” she said.

“Na’ar told me.”

“He has opinions about where I ought to stand while waiting for a carriage.”

“He has opinions about carriages.”

“So I’m discovering. I shall be wonderfully safe and unable to get anywhere.”

Riarhin moved towards the door. Maelis called her back.

“Another thing. If your friends recognise you, don’t immediately pretend they are fools. People resent that longer than they resent a disguise.”

“I expect some of them to recognise me.”

The votary’s face changed. A little of her colour returned, and she looked at Riarhin with a new, almost eager attention before arranging it into concern.

“Oh,” she said. “That is much more dangerous.”

“Yes.”

“We shall have to be very careful.”

Riarhin left her touching one of the knots in the silk, already thinking.

***

On the way home Mara asked whether she would do it.

They walked their horses along a stretch where carts had broken the road. Riarhin watched a wheel pass through a rut and emerge shedding mud from its rim.

“Yes.”

“Because it works?”

“Because it gets me into the rooms.”

Mara nodded. They went a little farther before she said, “She was beautiful.”

Riarhin looked at the road.

“Yes.”

“I meant your mother.”

“I know.”

Mara left it there. Riarhin was grateful, then wished she had said more, then was angry with herself for making even kindness difficult to receive.

At the house, Na’ar had moved his bundle from the lower room into the passage. He would begin work for Maelis in the morning. Berent was writing at the table. The paper before him concerned the people on his small holding and a payment he could not now be there to collect.

He looked up when they entered.

“Will it work?”

Riarhin put down the reins she had carried inside by mistake.

“It works.”
