That night she stood before the small mirror in her room and began again without Maelis beside her.

For a few breaths the likeness would not settle. Then her mother looked back from the glass, and Riarhin held her there long enough to say good night.

Part III — An Invited Woman

Chapter Sixteen — The Introduction

The dressmaker measured the wrong shoulder.

Riarhin felt the tape pass above her skin and stop where Bea appeared to end. The woman frowned at the space beneath her fingers, tried again, and looked at Maelis.

“You might have told me.”

“I was going to.”

“Before I cut, preferably.”

Maelis took the tape from her and laid it on the table. “We need the garment to fit the body wearing it and favour what people see.”

“I understand a glamour. I don’t understand why you let me measure one.”

The dressmaker was the woman from the music house. Her name was Vessa, and she had brought two gowns, neither new, and enough pins to make Riarhin careful about breathing. She looked directly at Riarhin.

“Can you take it off?”

“No,” Riarhin said.

Maelis put a hand on Vessa’s arm. “Let me give you the measurements.”

“Then you’ll pay for every alteration.”

“Yes.”

“And she can stand still.”

Riarhin stood still. She disliked having her body discussed as an obstacle to dressing a more desirable woman, though neither of them had said anything of the kind. Vessa held the blue gown against her, reconsidered it, and chose the other.

The cloth was a deep brown with a reddish warmth where the light struck. Its sleeves opened below the elbow over a lighter lining. Bea had worn something similar once, with a clasp whose point caught in the lining whenever she moved too quickly. Riarhin remembered helping free it while her mother held an arm above her head and complained about the price of careless work.

“Not there,” she said as Vessa reached for a pin.

The woman stopped. “Where, then?”

“A little higher. It will pull when I turn.”

Vessa tried the movement against her own wrist, then placed the pin where Riarhin had indicated.

“You’ve worn this cut.”

“Yes.”

The answer escaped before she could weigh it. Vessa had already gone to the next seam. In the mirror Bea’s mouth remained composed.

***

They chose the name over bread and cheese.

“Aveline,” Maelis said. “It is ordinary enough to answer to without making a room ask about your parents.”

“Marr,” Riarhin added.

“Why Marr?”

“It’s short.”

“So is No. You answer to that readily.”

Mara laughed. She had come with the shoes, which Vessa declared serviceable after inspecting the heels. It was the first time that morning Riarhin had seen her laugh without then remembering herself.

“Aveline Marr,” Maelis repeated. “From the north. Recently arrived. I have heard you converse and seen you entertain a room.”

“You’ve seen me threaten you.”

“You were perfectly convincing. We shall find you another subject.”

Vessa had left. Maelis could make the joke now, and Riarhin could see the effort with which she chose to make it. Her fingers flattened a crumb against the table.

“Who asks for me?” Riarhin said.

“Margery wants another woman at supper. Her guest is arriving without the person who was meant to accompany her. An evening, with a fee agreed beforehand. If she enjoys your company, she can ask again.”

“And if she asks where I’ve worked?”

“You’ve travelled. You don’t discuss every household you’ve visited. You are willing to give this one evening on my recommendation.”

“That’s very little.”

“It is all I can truthfully recommend. You wanted an introduction, not a dead husband, a ruined estate, and three witnesses who must remember the same funeral.”

Mara broke another piece of bread. “Where will she sleep?”

“Here for now. Alone.”

Riarhin looked up.

Maelis gestured towards the floor. “The room below is available. The owner will accept my assurance. You can’t ride to Mara’s house after every supper and expect the only person watching to be me.”

It was an obvious precaution. Riarhin had been avoiding it because Mara’s house contained people to whom she could speak without doing anything to her face first.

“I’ll pay,” she said.

“Yes, you will. And if you go out as yourself, use a different cloak and keep away from the people I am introducing you to. I can improve an appearance. I cannot make a whole street forget its breakfast.”

Mara passed her the bread. Riarhin took it, then set it down untouched.

***

Na’ar asked to see the room in which Maelis kept her money.

She took offence until he explained that a bodyguard who did not know where thieves might try to enter would have difficulty standing in the right place. Then she took a different sort of offence, concerning the assumption that there was enough money to justify a thief, and showed him the little inner room anyway.

Riarhin heard them from the stair. By the time she reached the landing, Na’ar was moving a stool away from the window.

“I sit there,” Maelis said.

“You may sit there. Move it after.”

“Why?”

“A man on it can reach the fastening.”

“A man already in the room?”

“A man leaving the room. You will look at the door first.”

She considered the window with fresh resentment.

“Must everything become disagreeable?”

“No. I also cook.”

He looked at Riarhin and stopped. His hand remained on the stool. For a moment the room became very still.

“It’s me,” she said.

“I know.”

He had not known immediately. She was glad and unexpectedly hurt.

Maelis watched the exchange with the attention she gave the knots in her silk. “Speak to him for a while. I need to see whether you hold it while listening.”

Na’ar put the stool down. “There is more cloth.”

“Apparently necessary.”

“Does it catch on things?”

“Yes.”

“Then learn where.”

He returned to the window. Riarhin found herself smiling, and caught the glamour when the smile nearly became her own. The adjustment required less effort than yesterday. It also made the smile feel chosen after it had already happened.

Maelis went into the inner room to fetch a purse. Na’ar waited until the door closed.

“She asked what I’d do if someone paid me to leave her unguarded,” he said quietly.

“What did you say?”

“That I would tell her who.”

“Would you?”

“Yes.”

Riarhin met his eyes.

“I agreed to guard her,” he said. “If you mean something else, tell me before she pays.”

“She is to be safe.”

“Good.”

Maelis returned. They settled wages, food, and the hours when another person would have to accompany her if Na’ar was absent. He required a place to sleep from which he could hear the street door. She objected that it would put him beside the room where she practised.

“I have slept beside armies,” he told her.

“Armies stop occasionally.”

“Then I shall complain.”

Riarhin left them agreeing on the first week.

***

The invitation came by a maid who wanted an answer before going home.

Maelis read it, then handed it to Riarhin. Supper in two days. The guest’s name was Dame Hester, and a further line asked whether Aveline knew any songs suitable for a mixed company.

“What is a mixed company?” Riarhin asked.

“One containing somebody whose objections we haven’t learned yet.”

“I don’t sing.”

“You needn’t. Can you listen to someone who does?”

“Yes.”

“Without looking as though you are identifying the weakest place in his throat?”

The maid laughed and then looked appalled at herself. Maelis gave her the answer to take back and a coin for the extra journey. Riarhin watched her pocket it, pleased enough to forget the laugh.

When she had gone, Maelis put the invitation beside the mirror.

“We’ll begin with entering.”

“I’ve entered rooms.”

“Then this should be quick.”

It took an hour.

Riarhin kept looking past the person receiving her. She could feel the room assembling itself around her attention, door, window, unfamiliar movement, while Bea’s face offered warmth to a woman whose question she had barely heard. Maelis made her leave and return until she could attend to the greeting without losing the likeness.

By the fifth attempt she was angry. By the seventh she knew how anger altered Bea’s smile. On the ninth she saw that Maelis was tired too and asked whether she wanted to sit.

“Yes,” the votary said. “And that will do.”

***

Mara stayed while Riarhin moved her things into the lower room.

There was little to move. The weapons remained wrapped, with Remorse where she could reach it and Toothpick beneath the folded shirt she would wear away from court. She could hide a knife beneath the gown. She could not make it cease to be a knife when someone helped her dress.

“You’re leaving them?” Mara asked.

“For the supper.”

“Does that trouble you?”

“Yes.”

Mara sat on the edge of the bed. “It troubles me too.”

Riarhin put the bundle down.

“I could take the small one.”

“And have them find it.”

“Yes.”

They looked at each other. The plan had been more comfortable while it consisted of questions to ask and a face to wear.

“Berent will come for me the morning after,” Riarhin said. “If I haven’t sent word.”

“What word?”

“An ordinary message. You’ll know my hand.”

Mara touched the bedcover, smoothing a crease.

“Don’t become so good at this that you forget why you’re there.”

Riarhin wanted to promise. Instead she asked Mara to help her with the upper fastening of the gown. Her mother’s hair fell across Mara’s fingers, visible and impossible to take hold of where it extended beyond Riarhin’s own.

“Lift it,” Mara said.

Riarhin lifted her real hair and held the other in her attention. Mara found the fastening beneath.

Chapter Seventeen — The Price of an Evening

Margery was quarrelling with a lamp when Riarhin arrived.

It had been placed where its light fell into the eyes of anyone seated on the nearer bench. The servant who had carried it insisted he had been told to put it beside the window. Margery pointed out that the window occupied most of the wall and allowed several interpretations.

“Here?” he asked, moving it the breadth of a hand.

“Farther.”

“Here?”

“Give it to me.”

He did, with relief. Margery turned and found Riarhin waiting beside Maelis.

“Aveline. Forgive me.”

She transferred the lamp to a side table, tested the view from the bench, and smiled. Riarhin had seen her five years earlier beside Veralyn, leaving a room reluctantly while the queen watched her go. The intervening years had put a little more weight beneath her chin and none in her movements.

“Maelis tells me you have come a long way.”

“Far enough to be pleased with a chair.”

“Then take this one before someone improves its lighting again.”

Riarhin sat. Maelis made the remaining introduction, accepted a cup, and was called away to rescue a musician who had brought the wrong instrument or been given the wrong instruction. The distinction was already under dispute.

Margery bent nearer.

“Hester will ask what you think of everything. Please don’t feel obliged to agree with her.”

“Would you prefer I disagree?”

“I’d prefer she enjoyed herself.”

Riarhin waited. Margery glanced towards the door.

“Her daughter is marrying. Hester has arranged the feast, the rooms, the journey, the musicians, and the people who will be offended by each of those arrangements. Her daughter has chosen a song Hester dislikes. She wants someone here to tell her it is unsuitable so that she may go home and say we said so.”

“Is it unsuitable?”

“No. She dislikes the man who first sang it to the girl.”

“Is he the bridegroom?”

“Fortunately.”

Riarhin laughed. Margery looked pleased and straightened as a woman entered behind a maid carrying her cloak.

***

Dame Hester had dressed with great care.

The care showed in a collar which would not lie flat. She adjusted it as she crossed the room, took her hand away when she noticed Riarhin watching, and then reached for it again before Margery had finished the introduction.

“Aveline has come from Ubral,” Margery said.

“How very cold you must find it here,” Hester replied, then closed her eyes. “That’s backwards.”

“The rooms are warmer,” Riarhin said. “I keep bringing the wrong clothes.”

Hester looked grateful. She sat on the bench beneath the lamp Margery had moved and asked whether Riarhin had family in the north. Riarhin said yes, held Bea’s face while she said it, and found the truth steadier than the little history she had prepared.

“They let you travel alone?”

“They failed to stop me.”

Hester smiled cautiously. “I used to travel more.”

“What made you stop?”

“Children. Then other people’s children. Now a wedding that apparently requires everybody I have ever met to sleep under the same roof.”

“Did you invite them all?” Maelis asked, returning with her cup.

“I invited my sister to bring whoever she needed.”

Margery lowered her eyes.

“She has a maid,” Hester said. “I meant the maid.”

“How many is she bringing?”

“Six. And her husband, though I cannot think what she needs him for.”

Maelis sat down. “Tell her there are only two beds.”

“I did. She thanked me and asked that they be aired.”

Riarhin heard the laugh escape through her nose. Hester looked at her, then began to laugh herself, folding the letter she had drawn out to demonstrate the enormity of it.

“I said she might have the blue room. I forgot I had offered it to the groom’s mother.”

“Write again,” Margery said.

“I have. They both think I’ve apologised.”

“Will they fit?”

“No.”

They talked about beds. Hester knew the dimensions of every room in the house where the wedding would be held, which guests could share and which could not, and how long her brother would endure an inferior mattress before telling everyone he had offered to stay elsewhere. Riarhin listened without asking for names she might later use. There was pleasure in the exactness of the woman’s knowledge and in the gradual permission she gave herself to make fun of the people she loved.

When supper came, Hester moved her cup to allow Riarhin room at the table.

“Tell me if the collar has turned,” she whispered.

“It has.”

“Oh, damn it.”

“May I?”

Riarhin lifted the folded edge with two fingers and settled it. Hester went still, accepting the touch with an awkward gratitude that made Riarhin suddenly remember doing the same thing for Bea before a visitor arrived.

“There.”

“Thank you.”

Across the table Margery was watching. She looked away before Hester turned.

***

The song began after the fruit.

Hester recognised the first phrase and sat back with an expression of injured patience. Riarhin had heard it before too, though she could not at first place the words. She kept her attention on the singer, a young woman whose right foot moved slightly beneath her gown as she counted the musician’s time.

At the second verse Hester leaned towards her.

“You hear?”

Riarhin did. The lover in the song had climbed to a window and found a husband where he expected a woman alone. The exchange that followed was considerably less scandalous than the song promised, since the husband wanted help moving a chest before his wife returned and had recognised the visitor’s usefulness before his intentions.

“The ending is worse,” Hester said.

The ending gave the husband the woman’s thanks and the lover a strained back. Half the room laughed before the last line. Hester held her cup with both hands.

“A wedding,” she said.

Riarhin looked at her. “Does your daughter laugh at it?”

“Constantly. He sang it badly the first time, and now she believes it should always be sung badly.”

“Will they sing it together?”

“He will. She’ll correct him.”

“Then I should let them.”

Hester’s face closed.

Riarhin had felt the room becoming easy and mistaken that ease for permission to answer too directly. She could mend it with agreement. Bea had done that sometimes, turning a sentence before the other person felt obliged to defend against it. Riarhin could remember the exact movement of her mother’s mouth and could not make herself use it.

“Does she know what you have arranged for her?” she asked instead.

“She knows there will be supper.”

“And the people in all the beds?”

“She has complained that I invited too many.”

“Did you want her to thank you tonight?”

Hester looked down at the cup.

“I wanted her to ask what I would like.”

The musician had started another tune. Riarhin waited while two people behind them moved chairs, scraping the floor badly enough to draw Margery’s attention.

“Have you told her?”

“I should not have to.”

“No.”

Hester looked up, surprised by the agreement.

“But you could.”

For a moment Riarhin thought she had lost her. Then Hester drew a slow breath and began to explain a song her own mother had sung. She remembered only the first verse. Riarhin knew the second, heard long ago in a household that had no connection with either of them, and supplied the opening words before she considered whether Aveline ought to know it.

Hester caught her hand.

“That one.”

They called the singer over when the tune ended. She knew it too, though her version differed at a line Hester insisted belonged to a different county. The three of them argued until Margery asked what had become so important.

“My song,” Hester said. “I’m having one as well.”

***

Later, Riarhin went in search of the retiring room and found a woman waiting with a basket of linen.

The woman stood before a closed door with one hip against the wall. A guard at the farther end of the passage watched both of them. She had already shifted the basket twice when Riarhin approached.

“Am I going the wrong way?” Riarhin asked.

“Not if you want the little room. That door. I’m waiting for the bedchamber.”

“Is it locked?”

“It’s waiting for permission.”

The guard came nearer. “Her ladyship has someone inside.”

“I know,” the woman said. “I’ve known for half an hour.”

“You can leave the basket.”

“And come back tomorrow to find somebody has used the lower sheets for the upper bed. I’m to set it myself. Those were the instructions.”

Riarhin looked from the basket to the door. The woman wore the same plain apron as several others she had passed. It gave her work, not admission. In Silkstrand a bundle of towels had served for both until the wrong question made the difference visible.

“What’s your name?”

“Enna.”

“Who sent you?”

“Joan. Who said it would take a moment.”

“I know where she is.”

The guard opened his mouth. Riarhin smiled at him before he could speak.

“I’m going back to the room I came from.”

He stepped aside.

Joan was at the table, trying to make a guest admit that a cup was broken rather than merely defective in its shape. Riarhin waited until she could speak without carrying the dispute into another one, then told her about Enna.

“She’s still there?”

“Yes.”

Joan stood at once. “I told them to let her in.”

“Not him, apparently.”

They went together. Joan gave the guard the permission in terms he could act on, then took the basket’s far handle herself and helped Enna through the door. Enna looked back once at Riarhin.

“My sister’s waiting at the gate,” she said. “Thank you.”

Riarhin returned to supper with nothing in her hands and a name she had chosen to remember.

***

Margery paid her before she left.

The purse was small. The amount inside was what Maelis had agreed. Riarhin took it with Bea’s composure and felt the old, unreasonable resentment at receiving money for making someone glad she had come.

“Hester wants you to visit,” Margery said.

“For the wedding?”

“For the argument about the song. She intends to finish it before the wedding.”

“I didn’t know it was unfinished.”

“You’ll learn.”

Margery touched her arm lightly. “Come on the sixth day, if you can. Jaennis has heard about you.”

Riarhin held the likeness while her attention sharpened.

“What has she heard?”

“That Hester stayed past the last course and failed to complain about anyone’s daughter.”

Outside, Maelis waited beneath the arch with Na’ar. He took one look at Riarhin’s face and held out the cloak without commenting on it.

“Well?” Maelis asked.

“I’ve been invited again.”

“Good.”

“To hear another song.”

“That is also good.”

Riarhin put the purse inside her cloak. Through the open window behind her came Hester’s voice, trying the remembered verse once more. She waited until the singer joined her before she went down the steps.

Chapter Eighteen — What Jaennis Sees

Jaennis sent a chair away.

The servant had brought six into a room where five already made the company sit closer than their gowns preferred. Jaennis looked at the new one, looked at the woman expected to occupy it, and moved the cushion beside her instead.

“Here, Vaelrie.”

Vaelrie came with a letter still in her hand. She sat, folded it carefully, and put it inside the small case at her waist before looking towards Riarhin.

“Aveline. I’m sorry. My brother has found a new cause for indignation.”

“Only one?” Jaennis asked.

“He is saving the rest.”

Riarhin remembered a younger woman moving through other rooms under another name. Vaelrie had grown easier with being watched. She wore a pale gown whose sleeve Jaennis adjusted without looking, smoothing a fold where it had caught beneath her arm. The movement required no acknowledgement from either of them.

“Does your brother know you read his letters aloud?” Riarhin asked.

“I haven’t read it aloud.”

“You’ve given us a promising beginning.”

Vaelrie smiled. “He dislikes a man who is right about his roof.”

“Then we should leave him to it,” Jaennis said. “It may take several letters.”

She offered Riarhin fruit from a shallow dish. Riarhin reached with her left hand.

Jaennis watched the hand, then met her eyes.

“Maelis has been keeping you to herself.”

“She has been making me presentable.”

“That must have been difficult.”

The answer was ready: You should have seen the material. Riarhin heard its shape before she spoke, the dry ease with which she had answered this woman five years earlier, and chose to let it come.

Jaennis’s smile remained. Her attention changed.

Riarhin bit into the fruit and gave herself its sharpness to hold while Bea’s face continued smiling.

***

The gathering had no musician.

Catrin was mending a sleeve badly enough to invite assistance. Joan had brought a book from which she intended to read an account of a southern wedding, though two women had already explained why they did not believe it. Margery wanted the window opened. Vaelrie wanted it shut until the rain stopped hitting the sill. Jaennis let them settle the matter by moving Margery’s chair.

Riarhin sat with a piece of fruit in her hand and realised nobody was going to ask her to begin performing.

Joan read. At the part concerning the bride’s procession, Catrin interrupted to ask where the animals had been kept, which led to an argument about whether the writer had ever attended the wedding at all. Riarhin knew the region slightly. She could have settled one point and concealed the pleasure she took in knowing it.

Instead she asked to hear the sentence again.

Joan passed her the book. The page described a courtyard too small for the procession it contained. Riarhin laughed, quietly.

“What?” Vaelrie asked.

“He’s put the road inside the house.”

“How can you tell?”

She showed her. Vaelrie moved nearer to follow the line, bringing the clean smell of her hair and the warmth of her shoulder. Riarhin could feel the distance between her own body and the slightly taller woman Vaelrie believed sat beside her. She adjusted the angle of the book so the touch fell where both appearances agreed.

“He may mean the family grounds,” Vaelrie said.

“He may. But then the bride rides through her uncle’s bedroom.”

Catrin dropped her sewing.

“That improves the account.”

They began reading it that way, supplying doors the author had omitted and relatives obliged to get out of bed. Riarhin found herself laughing hard enough to make the glamour require attention. She let the others finish the joke while she recovered the set of Bea’s mouth.

Jaennis saw the pause.

“Are you tired?”

“A little.”

“We’ll have something brought.”

“I needn’t—”

“You needn’t anything. Sit.”

It was said without a change in warmth. Riarhin sat back and allowed a woman she had come to suspect of coercion to make her comfortable.

***

Vaelrie asked about the north while the food was coming.

She wanted to know whether the roads became impossible in winter, and whether people truly carried children between houses through snow deep enough to hide a horse. Riarhin gave her one ordinary winter and one difficult journey, then found herself describing Iona riding home on the wrong horse.

“A cousin’s daughter,” she added before the name could follow.

Vaelrie delighted in the theft.

“Borrowing,” Riarhin said.

“Did the owner agree?”

“Eventually.”

“That sounds like my brother’s account of borrowing.”

Jaennis was listening from the other side of the room while Joan showed her a torn page. She said, “Perhaps the horse enjoyed it.”

“The groom did not,” Riarhin answered.

“Grooms are paid to have reservations.”

“So are bodyguards. Maelis is discovering it.”

A brief stillness passed through Jaennis’s face. She turned the page in Joan’s hand, asked a practical question about its repair, and then came to sit beside Riarhin.

“Does her new man suit her?”

“He seems useful.”

“You know him?”

“Enough to trust him near a door.”

Jaennis looked at her for a moment longer than courtesy required.

“And elsewhere?”

“More than I trust most people.”

“That is either high praise or very little.”

“It can be both.”

Vaelrie called Jaennis to see something in the wedding account. The exchange ended, but Riarhin felt its unfinished shape throughout the meal.

***

When the others moved to the farther room, Jaennis asked her to remain.

The request came while she was returning the book to Joan. Riarhin had to finish the ordinary act before turning, and those few seconds gave her enough time to maintain the face without appearing to prepare herself.

“Sit a little longer,” Jaennis said. “If you can bear the company.”

“I was enjoying it.”

“I saw.”

Jaennis stood by the window until a servant had removed the dishes. She thanked him by name, asked after a child who had been ill, and waited for the answer. Riarhin watched the care with which she listened. It would have been convenient if every kindness announced its price before being accepted.

At last they were alone.

“You have come at a troubled time,” Jaennis said.

“People tell me that about most places.”

“Do you choose them?”

“Sometimes they become troubled after I arrive.”

Jaennis smiled. Riarhin felt the answer approach too closely to admission and decided against taking it back.

“Have you found rooms?”

“Through Maelis.”

“Good. I would dislike to think you were making a long journey after these evenings.”

“I’m close enough.”

“Yes.”

The word was mild. Jaennis crossed to the dish left on the side table and selected the last unbruised fruit.

“Margery says you are kind to people who arrive expecting to be ridiculous.”

“Hester wasn’t ridiculous.”

“She thought we might find her so.”

“I know.”

“And you corrected her.”

“She did most of it.”

Jaennis offered the fruit. Riarhin shook her head.

“I have another guest who requires much the same consideration. Master Orrel. He arranges papers for hearings and believes that accepting a cup commits him to considering a petition.”

“Does it?”

“Not in my rooms, unless I say so.”

Riarhin looked towards the doorway. A hearing clerk. The invitation had moved close enough to what she wanted that she distrusted how quickly she wanted it.

“What would you have me do?”

“Keep him from spending the evening defending an office nobody has attacked. He likes gardens. He knows more about growing things than most people who speak of them.”

“And if I know very little?”

“Ask. People seldom object to being asked about something they love.”

Jaennis bit into the fruit, then set it aside with a look of disappointment. It had been bruised beneath the skin.

“No petitions,” she said. “That is the condition of his coming.”

Riarhin let Bea’s face receive the condition.

“I understand.”

***

Outside, the rain had stopped.

Na’ar was waiting with Maelis’s cloak over one arm and Riarhin’s over the other. Maelis remained inside, engaged in a disagreement whose sounds reached the arch in occasional clear phrases about a missing stool.

“Which?” he asked, lifting the cloaks.

“The brown.”

He put it round her shoulders and adjusted it without looking at the face. Riarhin found the familiarity unexpectedly difficult.

“Did you enjoy yourself?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

She waited for him to ask what she had learned. He was watching the street, where a cart had stopped too close to their hired carriage.

“Na’ar.”

He looked back.

“If somebody knows it’s me and says nothing—”

“They may be waiting.”

“For what?”

“That is what you wanted to find out.”

Maelis appeared with the missing stool in a servant’s hands behind her. She gave Riarhin one look, then asked whether she had eaten enough. Riarhin answered yes, and caught herself wanting to tell her about the book and the bride riding through her uncle’s bedchamber.

She told her in the carriage. Maelis laughed, and asked her to begin again because she had missed the part about the horse.

Chapter Nineteen — A Door That Answers

Orrel had brought a melon.

He carried it in a basket lined with wool and looked offended when a servant offered to take it to the kitchen. The offence receded as soon as the man explained that he meant to keep it cool, but Orrel continued holding the handle.

“If it’s cut too soon, they’ll put it beside the fire to improve it.”

“Will that improve it?” Riarhin asked.

“It will improve the cook’s opinion of himself.”

She laughed. Orrel looked surprised, then pleased enough to let the servant take the basket after extracting a promise that nobody would do anything to it until asked.

He was a lean man whose clothes had been made for a slightly larger body. The loose cuffs annoyed him. He pushed them back whenever he reached for something and forgot them until they slipped again.

“Yours?” Riarhin asked.

“The melon? Yes.”

“Here in Valora?”

“Against a wall. Sheltered. Most people give them too much water at the wrong time.”

“What’s the right time?”

Orrel considered whether she wanted an answer. Riarhin let the question remain uncomplicated, and he began to tell her.

***

By the time Jaennis joined them, Riarhin knew how he raised the young plants and which neighbour had complained about the frames beside his wall.

The neighbour’s complaint was sound. Orrel admitted this with reluctance. He had borrowed space for one season, kept using it for three, and was now obliged to move the frames before the woman built a covered place for her washing. He believed she had chosen an inconvenient moment.

“Would there be a convenient one?” Riarhin asked.

“Winter.”

“When her washing would dry badly.”

“Yes.”

“Have you offered her a melon?”

“Several. She says they won’t mend a wet shirt.”

Riarhin could see the little garden through his description: the frames, the wall that gave back the day’s heat, the narrow strip along which he carried water carefully enough to keep his shoes dry for the office. She asked whether he had somewhere else for the plants. He had, if he could bear to admit it received enough sun.

Jaennis listened, smiling.

“You see?” she told Orrel. “It is possible to come here without someone handing you a case.”

He looked embarrassed. “I didn’t mean—”

“I know what you meant. Where is this famous fruit?”

He went to fetch it himself. Jaennis sat beside Riarhin.

“You’ve done well.”

“He has a great deal to say.”

“And someone is listening.”

She left before Riarhin could decide whether the words carried an accusation. Across the room, Orrel was explaining to the servant where to place the knife.

***

The melon was excellent.

Riarhin said so before she remembered the purpose for which she had come. The flesh was cool and sweet, with a firmness she had not expected from its scent. Orrel watched her take another bite and relaxed visibly.

A younger man at the far end of the table asked whether it was worth all the labour. Orrel answered that he had grown it, which Riarhin thought sufficient until the man began comparing its cost with fruit brought from the south.

“You’re buying an account of the journey,” she said. “He’s eating his garden.”

The man laughed and accepted the correction. Orrel looked at her gratefully. She wished he had not.

After supper they went into a small courtyard to see plants Jaennis had been given. The evening was cool. Riarhin kept her cloak open because Bea’s larger shoulders required room for the fall of it. Orrel bent over a pot, touched a leaf, and asked who had been watering it.

“Everyone, apparently,” Jaennis said.

“That would explain it.”

She was called away. Orrel remained with the plant, considering a remedy which did not require him to criticise the gift. Riarhin heard a door open behind her and the sound of trays being carried down the passage.

“How much of your day do they leave you for this?” she asked.

“Less this year.”

“More hearings?”

“More papers about the hearings.”

She turned a leaf to examine its underside. “I suppose people send things too late.”

“They send them at every stage.”

“Even after a judgement?”

Orrel straightened.

Riarhin kept her eyes on the plant. She had begun. The question sounded almost harmless if he chose to leave it so, and she could feel herself inviting him to make that choice.

“Sometimes,” he said.

“Does anyone read them?”

“Who has asked you to ask me?”

She looked up.

“No one.”

“Aveline.”

It was the first time he had used her name without a courtesy attached. His disappointment was plain enough to make an evasion feel ugly before she chose it.

“I knew someone who served Elyas,” she said.

Orrel took a step back from the plant.

“I was invited here on an understanding.”

“I know.”

“Then why?”

She could tell him grief. It was true and insufficiently hers alone to spend this way.

“There is a report from the north. I want to know when it entered the papers.”

“I won’t discuss evidence.”

“The date.”

“A date can be evidence.”

“Yes.”

They stood beside the sickly plant. Riarhin felt the concentration required to keep Bea’s face patient while another part of her began counting the possible entries to Orrel’s office. He had trusted her with the awkwardness of a garden dispute. She was already looking beyond his refusal.

He saw something of it.

“I did not sit in judgement,” he said.

“I haven’t said you did.”

“You have brought the question to the person you could get beside a wall.”

Riarhin lowered her hand from the leaf.

“I have.”

***

Orrel walked back to the door, then stopped.

“The northern communication was entered the morning after the supper at which Mistress Lyra remained with the dowager. I know the entry because its enclosure was described incorrectly and I had it amended. I will not tell you what was enclosed.”

Riarhin held still. “Was it read then?”

“I cannot tell you.”

“Cannot, or—”

“Both.”

He opened the door. The lamp inside struck the glass of the courtyard window and threw Bea’s reflection beside him, taller than Riarhin felt, attentive in a way that seemed suddenly cruel.

“Who told you about that supper?” he asked.

She saw her own expression shift in the glass and restored it. Orrel continued speaking while she attended to the mouth. When she looked back, he was waiting for an answer.

“I’m sorry?”

“I asked whether a servant brought you the name.”

“No.”

“Be certain of that. A woman put me on this guest list whose work should not be endangered because someone thinks she has access to my papers.”

“Who?”

He shook his head and went inside.

Riarhin remained in the courtyard until she could trust the face she was wearing. She had missed part of his sentence. She could replay the sound of it, but the words occupied the same instant as the effort to keep the glamour, and they had never become a thing she knew.

She went in by another door.

***

The passage beyond was empty.

Riarhin had expected it to return her to the room where they had eaten. Instead it ran behind a set of smaller chambers, with a stair at the far end screened by cloth and two boards set across its lowest step. She turned towards the light spilling from a nearer doorway.

A guard came from the other direction before she reached it.

“Were you in the courtyard?”

“Yes. I’ve taken the wrong door.”

“This way.”

He had not challenged her at the courtyard entrance. Nobody had been visible in the passage when she opened it, and the door had closed behind her with an ordinary weight. Yet he had arrived directly, without looking into the other rooms.

She let him escort her. At the turning she saw Enna carrying a covered basket out of a chamber.

The guard asked her, “Did you admit this lady?”

“No,” Enna said.

“I came from the courtyard,” Riarhin repeated.

He nodded and took her farther. Enna waited until they had passed. Her mouth had tightened, and Riarhin knew the look of someone adding a small incident to a day already burdened by other people’s mistakes.

Back in the room, Orrel was preparing to leave. He bowed to Jaennis, thanked her for the evening, and acknowledged Riarhin with a brief inclination of his head.

Jaennis watched him go.

“Was the garden disappointing?” she asked.

“The company was.”

“Whose?”

“Mine.”

Jaennis put down her cup. “I gave you a condition.”

“Yes.”

“Then you owe him more than an apology made to me.”

***

Riarhin returned the next afternoon without a supper invitation.

She brought a short letter for Orrel and asked the porter to deliver it when convenient. It said she had abused his hospitality and that he owed her no further answer. She had written it twice because the first version tried to explain why she had done it.

The porter took the letter and told her she could leave. She asked whether Joan might spare a moment. He looked at the time, then at the name Maelis had put on her introduction.

“Wait here.”

Joan came carrying a bundle of papers tied with red thread. She did not invite Riarhin farther in.

“I may have caused a difficulty yesterday,” Riarhin said.

“So I heard.”

“Enna had nothing to do with my being in the passage.”

“Enna has told us that.”

“I want you to hear it from me.”

Joan studied her face.

“And Master Orrel?”

“I asked him something I had agreed not to ask.”

“Did Enna send you to him?”

“No. Did someone say she did?”

“She delivered the invitation. The questions began after he asked who had brought you there.”

Riarhin felt the small fact settle. Enna had needed to get home to her sister. Yesterday she had carried linen. Between those duties she had brought a paper to a man whose confidence Riarhin had damaged.

“I was introduced by Maelis, at Jaennis’s invitation. I chose what to ask. No servant gave me anything.”

“I’ll have you put that in writing.”

“Yes.”

Joan led her to a table near the porter. Riarhin wrote it in the hand she had chosen for Aveline, careful enough to avoid a signature anybody elsewhere might recognise. The caution disgusted her while she practised it.

When she finished, Joan read the statement and sanded it.

“Thank you,” she said. “Wait here. I’ll tell Enna.”

Riarhin waited until Enna came through the yard. The woman glanced at her, then away, then came nearer of her own accord.

“They’ve said it’s settled.”

“I’m sorry.”

Enna shifted the basket on her arm. “Please use the door they show you.”

“I will.”

She left without another word. Riarhin stood by the porter until she remembered that she no longer had a reason to remain.

Chapter Twenty — The Guard’s Supper

Maelis objected to the smell of onions in the upper room.

Na’ar opened the lower window. She objected to the draught. He closed it halfway and asked whether she preferred the onions brought up or the air sent down.

“I prefer you to have asked before cooking.”

“You said there was supper.”

“I said something would be brought.”

“It has been. I brought it.”

Riarhin stopped on the stair, listening. Below, someone laughed who was neither of them. She had come to have the glamour examined and expected a private hour. The table was laid for five.

Maelis saw her in the doorway.

“Aveline. Good. You can tell him what he has done to the curtains.”

“Improved them,” said the stranger at the table. “They smelled of damp.”

The woman had a narrow face, wind-reddened at the cheeks, and a short nail on one thumb where the other nails had been allowed to grow. She wore a dark wool coat over a plain gown and had put her wet shoes near the hearth without asking where they belonged.

“Deirdre,” Maelis said. “Aveline. Deirdre carries things across the Dolmis and then complains about the people at either end.”

“Only when they deserve it.”

“Which, fortunately for her conversation, is often.”

Deirdre offered Riarhin a place. Na’ar set a dish in the middle of the table and told them to eat while he brought the bread.

***

The fifth place belonged to Deirdre’s niece, who arrived with the bread because she had met Na’ar at the foot of the stair and taken the basket from him.

They made room for her by moving Maelis’s work from the end of the table. Maelis objected to the handling of a shallow box until Eda asked where it could go; then she had to stand and find somewhere herself. The effort improved the room. With the box removed, everyone could reach the food without arranging their sleeves around somebody else’s secrets.

Riarhin took the chair nearest the wall. She could see the hearth from it, the shoes drying, the damp dark edge of Deirdre’s coat. The window had misted at the corners. For a few minutes she wanted nothing changed.

Her name was Eda. She was old enough to resent being introduced as someone’s niece and young enough to show the resentment. Maelis asked after her work before Deirdre could answer for her.

“The company is still travelling,” Eda said. “I’m not.”

“Why?”

“Because they wanted me to mend clothes all day and sing all evening, and paid me as though either occupation were a favour.”

“Did you tell them?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

“They asked whether I knew who’d got me the place.”

Maelis looked up.

“I hope you told them I had not promised to keep you in it forever.”

“I did.”

“Better.”

Deirdre passed the dish. The meal began with Eda’s account of the company and gradually became a dispute about whether a man who could sing beautifully was entitled to be impossible about his washing. Riarhin listened, amused by the certainty with which Maelis defended the singer’s art and condemned his arrangements.

Na’ar sat down at last. He ate for some time before asking Eda whether she wanted other work. She said she already had it, helping a woman who supplied costumes to three households and paid for the hours she requested.

“Then why are you angry?” he asked.

“Because he still thinks I should be grateful.”

Na’ar considered that. “He may continue thinking it while you eat.”

Eda laughed reluctantly and reached for the bread.

Riarhin wanted to hear her sing. She asked after the dish had been taken away.

“Now?”

“If you wish.”

Eda looked at Maelis, who lifted both hands in a deliberate refusal to decide for her. She thought a moment, then asked Riarhin what sort of song she liked.

“One you like singing.”

“That could be a mistake.”

“I’ve made several this week.”

Eda chose a lively song about two sisters attempting to exchange an unwanted suitor without either being seen to reject him. Riarhin had heard another version in Ubral, in which the suitor escaped both and took their best horse. She told Eda afterwards. The young woman demanded the words, and they spent half an hour discovering how differently the verses travelled.

Deirdre said the elder sister should have kept the horse and married the miller.

“The miller is married,” Eda told her.

“He is in your version. You’ve added a wife and lost a horse. I dislike it.”

“You dislike every song in which a girl chooses for herself.”

“I dislike the ones in which she chooses an idiot.”

Na’ar reached across for the empty dish. “Does the miller have a horse?”

Eda put her face in her hands. Maelis began laughing, then caught Deirdre’s eye and attempted to turn it into a cough. That made it worse. Riarhin passed her the water.

“Sing something without a wedding,” Deirdre said.

Eda lowered her hands. “Gladly.”

She chose a lament. By the time the third son had drowned, her aunt had surrendered and asked for the sisters again.

***

Deirdre’s business emerged when Maelis asked whether the harbour claim had been heard.

“It has been received. That’s what they call it when they put it somewhere I can’t reach.”

“What do you want from me?”

“A name. Someone who can tell me which office actually has it.”

Riarhin leaned back while the women talked. Deirdre carried lawful cargo as well as things whose owners preferred not to describe them at a quay. The present dispute concerned a bond held against a consignment delivered in good order. The harbour clerk would not release it because a receiving officer’s signature had been challenged. Deirdre had paid her crew and the owner of the goods; until the bond came back, her next voyage would be made with money borrowed at a rate she disliked.

“Who challenged it?” Maelis asked.

“A man who has never been aboard my ship and knows more about its movements than I do.”

“That narrows it less than I’d like.”

Deirdre produced a copy of the claim. Riarhin could read the heading from where she sat. An Ecktorian house stood among the interested parties.

Maelis read slowly.

“I can ask where this is. I can’t make them decide it.”

“I know. Last time you found Eda honest work. I’m trying a larger difficulty.”

Eda rolled her eyes. “I found the second place myself.”

“And I’ve praised you for it.”

“Twice, while complaining that I left the first.”

Riarhin smiled into her cup. Deirdre caught the smile and gave her an exaggeratedly injured look.

“You have children?”

“No.”

“Then keep your opinions ready. You’ll be asked for them by people who don’t intend to use them.”

***

After they left, Maelis took the claim upstairs.

Na’ar stayed at the table with a scrap of paper and the small heap of coins left from paying for the food. He counted them twice, put three aside, and asked Riarhin how to spell the name of the carrier who had brought them from Ubral.

“Ewen. Why?”

“I need to find him before he goes north.”

“He may have gone.”

“Then I need to find who went with him.”

She drew the paper towards her. He had begun a letter to Mairi, in a hand which made every word look like a separate practical problem. There was a sketch at the bottom, something wide with two handles.

“A pot?”

“A good one. The one at Glenruan burns everything on one side.”

“You burnt something.”

“On one side.”

He retrieved the letter before she could read more. She had no right to be surprised that he had things to say to Mairi without asking her to carry them. The coins were for a deposit; Mairi knew someone who could get the casting done and had argued about the handles until Na’ar drew what he meant.

“Will you have it sent here?”

“No.”

“Then it’s for Tavish’s house.”

“For the people who use it. Tavish will claim it’s his because everything happens on his land.”

Riarhin looked at the broad shape on the paper. It would feed more people than the little pot he had complained of. She could see the hearth it would stand beside, the benches drawn close, Iona appearing with someone else’s dog and being made to take it out before it ate what had been set down.

“You intend to go back.”

“Yes.”

“When?”

“When this is done.”

“What is this?”

He gave her a long look. She had asked him the question people asked her when she had stayed just long enough to make leaving a decision that involved them.

“I agreed to help you get here. I agreed to watch Maelis. I haven’t agreed to live wherever the next thing takes you.”

“I didn’t ask that.”

“No. You leave a space where asking ought to be.”

Riarhin sat back. From upstairs came the sound of Maelis opening a drawer, then closing it harder than necessary. The room remained warm. She could still choose to make a quarrel out of being told something accurately.

“How long did you tell Mairi?”

“That I didn’t know. She said the casting would take longer than I thought, so I should pay now.”

“That sounds like her.”

“Yes.”

She wrote Ewen’s name on a clean corner and asked whether he wanted the other names he might need. Na’ar said he did. She gave him the posting places she remembered, then moved her hand away so he could add whatever belonged in his own letter.

He read what she had written before putting it with the coins.

“You can come back too,” he said.

“I know.”

“Then remember it occasionally.”

Riarhin helped Na’ar clear the table. The ordinary work let her move without wondering what Bea would do with a knife or a plate. Her mother had washed cups. She had seen it. She had no need to remember which hand held the cloth each time.

“Does she treat you well?” she asked.

“Who?”

“Maelis.”

“She complains.”

“I’ve heard.”

“She pays.”

“And otherwise?”

Na’ar set the empty dish down.

“She asks whether I’ve eaten. Then she tells me I have eaten too much. Yesterday she made a man leave by the front door because he wanted to use the back without telling me. She said he was making my work difficult.”

Riarhin looked towards the stair.

“She likes you.”

“She likes many people. It inconveniences her.”

He gave her a dry cloth. They worked for a while before he added, “A clerk waited here yesterday. She told him she had another appointment. He came back after dark.”

“Did you hear them?”

“No. I was downstairs.”

“What did he bring?”

“A packet. He took it away again. If you want to know what was in it, ask her.”

She nodded. His reports remained exact at the point where he could no longer be useful. She trusted him more for it and found it frustrating anyway.

“Does she know you tell me?”

“She hired me knowing whose friend I am.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It is the answer I have.”

Maelis called from above. Na’ar took the cloth from Riarhin and sent her up before the votary could begin describing how long she had been waiting.

***

The examination was brief.

Maelis asked Riarhin to maintain the face while reading a paragraph she had not seen before, then interrupted with a question about its meaning. Riarhin answered correctly. The votary changed one knot and asked her to do it again.

“What changed?” Riarhin asked.

“You were holding the mouth too carefully. People do move while they listen.”

“It felt right.”

“It looked like patience being performed.”

Riarhin thought of Orrel beside the plant. She let the likeness fall. Maelis folded the silk and put it away without asking why she had become quiet.

“The claim,” Riarhin said. “Can you help her?”

“I can find someone to speak to. She has helped me when a journey became disagreeable. I would like her next voyage to leave with enough food for her crew.”

“Where does she live?”

“Wherever the ship is. She has kin in Theona. Sometimes they are pleased to remember it.”

“And sometimes?”

“One branch wants the crown to uphold a landing right. Another objects to the officer who would uphold it. They share a mother and refuse to share a quay. Deirdre loves them both and moors elsewhere when she can.”

Maelis covered the mirror.

“The islands contain people,” she said. “A surprising number, when one begins asking favours.”

Riarhin stayed while Maelis unpinned the silk from its frame.

The frame occupied the best light in the room. On the sill behind it lay a little stone house, scarcely longer than a finger, with a crooked chimney and four windows picked out in white. One side had been broken and joined with a dark adhesive. Riarhin had assumed it belonged to the working until Maelis lifted it to hold down the loose end of a bill.

“Where is that?” she asked.

“Nowhere. A child made it for me.”

“Your child?”

“No. A child who owed me nothing and gave me something. I keep it to remind myself that it happens.”

Maelis examined the corner of the silk where a pin had caught a thread. She picked at it with a blunt needle, holding the cloth near her face. Riarhin moved the lamp closer.

“Thank you.”

The word came automatically. Maelis looked annoyed with herself a moment later, then went back to the thread.

“Do you want me to go?” Riarhin asked.

“I want this to come free before I make the hole larger.”

She worked in silence until it did. The thread slid into place, leaving a pale mark which she stroked with her thumb.

Riarhin asked whether she had made all the things kept in the room.

“Most. Some are attempts.”

“At what?”

“At things I was told couldn’t be done. Sometimes the person telling me was correct.”

She gave Riarhin a small piece of cloth from the basket. It looked grey. Turned towards the lamp, it showed green at the fold, though the flat surface remained grey. Riarhin moved it again. The colour appeared only at one angle, delicate as the skin inside a leaf.

“What is it for?”

“Nothing, yet.”

“Does it need renewing?”

“Frequently. And it won’t survive washing. You see why nobody has offered me a fortune.”

Riarhin let the fold fall. The green vanished. She wanted to ask whether the same trick could hide a blade, and kept the question to herself.

“I wanted a room with two windows,” Maelis said. “One for daylight and one where I could leave a thing to dry without a servant deciding it was untidy. That was what I asked for when they wanted me to stay another winter.”

“Did you get it?”

“This is it.”

Riarhin looked around. The second window was behind the hanging she had taken for a decoration. Its lower pane had been replaced by oiled cloth, and the sill was crowded with little frames.

“The roof drips when the wind comes from the east,” Maelis added. “There is probably a lesson in that. I use a bowl.”

“Why stay?”

“Because people here pay for things they don’t need.”

She took back the grey silk, handling it by the edges.

“They want to enter a room and have everyone turn. They want a sleeve remembered. A jealous woman wants her rival to look at her twice before deciding what to despise. I can work with those wishes. They know what they want, even when they invent a better reason for wanting it.”

“And you?”

“I want to make something I haven’t made before. I want to be paid enough to try again when it fails. I want to be invited back because the evening was pleasant, and occasionally to refuse because I am tired.”

She smiled at the last part, then folded the silk over her wrist.

“Your arrival has been difficult for the final wish.”

Riarhin looked down at her own hands. She had made Maelis’s fear into a door and walked through it. Enjoying the woman’s company did not put her on the other side of that act.

“I won’t ask you to renew the face tonight.”

“You don’t need it tonight.”

“I know.”

“Then that’s hardly generosity.”

“No.”

The cricket scraped in its box. Maelis lifted the cover and studied it for a moment before lowering the cloth again.

“What would you ask if I hadn’t frightened you?” Riarhin said.

“For this working? A great deal of money. Permission to stop before it broke. An answer when I asked where you meant to wear it.”

“I couldn’t give you the last.”

“You could have told me so. I would have had the pleasure of refusing.”

Riarhin moved the lamp back to its former place. There was nothing useful she could do with an apology large enough to cover the room, so she kept it small.

“I’m sorry for how we came to you.”

Maelis watched her for several breaths.

“Yes,” she said. “You should be.”

She put the stone house back on the bill. Riarhin rose, but the votary pointed to the basket beside her chair.

“If you mean to sit there, wind that for me. Loosely. If you pull it tight, it will remember.”

Riarhin took the thread. Maelis returned to the frame and began explaining where she wanted the next fold to catch the light. She spoke more quickly as the explanation advanced, abandoning the caution with which she had first handed over the work. Once she reached across to loosen Riarhin’s grip, touched her fingers, and continued without drawing back.

***

Riarhin returned to the lower room after Na’ar had gone to check the street door. She could still hear Eda’s song. She wrote the northern verse on a scrap of paper before sleeping, so the young woman could take it when she next came.

Then she took another sheet for Ailsa.

She had sent a short note from the road, saying that the news had worsened and she might not be able to write regularly. She had put no name to the worsening. Ailsa had asked for enough warning to bear a silence, and Riarhin had given her a sentence which would require being read over every time a carrier came without anything for her.

Now she wrote about the song. Eda had refused to surrender the miller’s wife; Deirdre had demanded the return of the horse. She included the disputed verse so Ailsa could show it to whoever would be most offended by its being sung differently in the south.

She paused above the next line. On the table the covered mirror showed her the edge of a shoulder, her own again. Ailsa would have asked about the room before the song: was she warm, did she like the people, had she remembered to eat before becoming helpful? Bea had asked questions in that order. Riarhin could hear both voices and, for the first time, hear how readily she had been answering the second without defending herself against the first.

It went beyond being fed. Ailsa could bring a shy visitor into a conversation, then quarrel over a price without making the welcome a lie. She laughed at her own mistakes while retaining an exact account of other people’s. Bea had done those things. She had enjoyed making an evening go well. Riarhin had spent years treating that enjoyment as part of the work she resented.

She put down the pen.

At Glenruan, she had once found Ailsa making room for a neighbour who had come to apologise. The apology was poor. Ailsa had let the woman finish, told her which part she did not believe, and then asked her to stay for food. Riarhin had desired her so suddenly that she had left the room to fetch something nobody needed. Later she had told Ailsa why. Ailsa had laughed and asked whether next time she might be allowed to finish serving supper.

That memory belonged wholly to them. She could keep it even while recognising something older in what had drawn her to the table.

She picked up the pen and wrote that she missed her. The words looked insufficient, so she described the corner of the bed, the cold foot, the cup she hoped was still being used even if someone else had it. She crossed out that last concession. Ailsa could give a guest a cup without asking her permission, and would hear the injury hidden inside so much obliging freedom.

I miss my cup too, she wrote instead. You may laugh at that.

She would have to send the letter by a carrier with no reason to connect Aveline Marr to the name outside it. It could wait with the northern verse until Na’ar found news of Ewen. She folded it and left space for the date.

Chapter Twenty-One — The Heir at Play

Matylde wanted the ribbon.

The room had been warmed too thoroughly for the number of people waiting in it. Riarhin could feel the fire through the back of her gown while cold air reached her ankles from an imperfectly closed garden door. Someone had spread a cloth over the painted floor where the child usually sat. A corner had escaped its weight, and the nurse kept smoothing it down with her foot while answering questions addressed to her by adults who seemed to imagine she had no other occupation.

There were almonds in a little silver dish. Matylde was not permitted them. This injustice had apparently preceded Riarhin’s arrival and remained available to resume if the ribbon failed her.

She had been given a wooden horse, a small cup, and permission to empty a box whose contents would take someone several minutes to put away. She ignored them all in favour of the fastening on Riarhin’s sleeve.

“May I?” the nurse asked, reaching to lift her.

“It’s all right.”

Riarhin lowered her arm. The child caught the ribbon and pulled with the entire weight of her attention. Riarhin moved closer before the sleeve could strain against her elbow. Bea’s apparent arm followed the movement; the hand the child held remained flesh.

The nurse watched carefully.

“She has broken two this morning.”

“I’m honoured to be third.”

Matylde looked up at the voice, uncertain whether it concerned the ribbon. Her eyes were very like her mother’s. The rest of the face refused an easy division between parents.

Veralyn entered while Riarhin was negotiating the release of her sleeve.

“You need something better,” the queen said.

“Have you brought it?”

Veralyn picked up the wooden horse and made it walk along the edge of the table. Matylde considered the improvement, abandoned the ribbon, and demanded the horse.

“There,” the queen said. “A settlement.”

Riarhin straightened. She had not bowed. Veralyn’s gaze moved over her face as she began to do so.

“Aveline. You’ve been much discussed.”

“I hope the accounts agree.”

“Why would you hope that?”

The question struck too near Riarhin’s own sense of the disguise. She smiled with Bea’s mouth.

“For convenience.”

Veralyn laughed and took the child from the nurse.

***

They had been brought together for a small presentation.

Margery had asked Riarhin to accompany her because the woman receiving thanks had spent the morning worrying about what to say. The woman supervised a weaving household which had completed winter cloth ahead of the promised date, replacing a consignment damaged in transit. Her work had kept several other households occupied, and she had come expecting to be questioned about how much she had charged.

Instead Veralyn asked after the people who had helped her.

The woman named three, then apologised for omitting the dyer. The queen asked the dyer’s name too and had it entered beside the others. Riarhin watched the woman’s shoulders ease.

Tylan arrived while she was explaining the difficulty of matching the older cloth. He listened from the doorway until Veralyn asked whether the army cared if two sleeves differed slightly.

“The men care whether they’re warm,” he said. “Their officers may discover a grievance.”

“Then the officers can write it to you.”

“They do.”

Matylde reached for him. Veralyn handed her over with the practised relief of someone whose arm had begun to ache. Tylan settled the child against his shoulder and continued asking about the cloth without changing his tone.

Riarhin remembered him beside a much smaller company, making decisions whose consequences could be counted at a fire. Here the questions concerned stores for men he might never know by face. He still asked whether the cloth would dry after rain and whether replacing it required taking blankets from households that needed them.

The woman said the household allotments had been protected in the agreement. An officer standing behind her corrected the word: they had been allowed for. Tylan shifted Matylde to the other arm and asked him to explain the difference.

“The stores can be called on if the levy falls short.”

“Whose stores?”

“The surplus, my lord.”

The woman looked at her hands. Tylan saw it.

“How much surplus have you?”

“Until I know what you are taking, I cannot say.”

Matylde caught at the fastening on his collar. He freed her hand, gave her a finger to hold, and told the officer to bring the agreement. While they waited he asked the woman about the rooms in which her people worked. She described a leaking roof, then began withdrawing the complaint because the landlord had promised to attend to it. Tylan let her finish both accounts.

The officer returned with the paper. Tylan read one passage aloud and asked whether it meant that the weaving household’s completed civilian orders could be requisitioned before delivery. It did. The woman had been paid deposits on those orders; taking the cloth would leave her owing money to people who had received nothing.

“Strike that,” he said.

“The price assumes the reserve.”

“Then show me the price without it.”

Veralyn had been listening beside the window. “We will need the reserve.”

“We can buy it.”

“Can she make it?”

Tylan looked to the woman instead of answering for her. She worked the sums on her fingers, forgetting for a moment to keep them folded. With another loom and leave to hire two journeymen from a less busy household, she could undertake the additional work after the civilian orders were met.

“Give her the loom,” Veralyn said.

“Lend it from the stores,” Tylan answered. “Let her buy it from the next contract if she wants to keep it.”

The queen smiled. “You intend there to be a next contract.”

“Yes.”

He said it looking at the weaver. Riarhin saw her understand that the help would bind her to a customer who could keep her rooms busy through every season. She would be foolish to refuse it. Tylan had removed the clause which might ruin her and offered something she would come to depend on, all while his daughter worried the edge of his collar.

The woman asked what would happen if the queen no longer needed cloth.

“Men wear it out in peace,” he said. “I would like them to have time to try.”

Veralyn laughed at that. The officer made the alteration. Tylan returned Matylde to the nurse before signing, though the child objected so loudly that he had to wait for the nurse to carry her farther from the table. She returned to his arms as soon as the ink was dry.

Once the altered agreement had been read back, Tylan asked how she had finished the first delivery so early. The woman found she could give a better account than she had expected. A delayed supply had arrived early. A replacement team had been available when one of hers went lame. Work once promised for the end of the month had been finished with days to spare.

“You’ve been fortunate,” Tylan said.

“We’ve worked,” the woman answered, then blushed at the sharpness.

“Yes,” Veralyn said. “You have.”

Riarhin felt the force of the answer. The woman had deserved it. It was pleasant to hear a sovereign give credit without first making the recipient grateful for the chance to earn it.

Then the queen moved, and the green glass at her throat caught the window’s light.

***

Riarhin had seen it since her return, at a distance in a passage, in a glimpse beyond an open door. Close to it, she recognised the narrow flaw and the tiny unevenness in one chased leaf. She had carried both across too much country to mistake them.

Veralyn caught the amulet before Matylde could reach it.

“Mine,” she told the child.

Matylde disputed that with a sound whose meaning required no translation. Tylan turned her towards the horse.

“You may have this.”

The queen adjusted the ribbon. It was green now, darker than the glass, against a black gown. The colour suited her. Riarhin disliked how readily the thought came.

“Do you like it?” Veralyn asked.

Riarhin looked up. The queen had caught her looking.

“It’s beautiful.”

“I think so.”

“An old piece?”

“Very old, I’m told. It came a long way.”

“Some things do.”

Tylan glanced at her. Riarhin turned her attention towards the weaving woman, who was trying to decide whether the audience had ended. She moved her chair a little to give the woman a clear path and received a grateful look.

Veralyn called her back before she reached the door.

“One more thing. Your petition about the apprentices.”

The woman stopped.

“The recommendation has been accepted. They’ll be allowed to complete their terms with you.”

All the careful composure went out of her face.

“Both?”

“Both.”

She began to thank the queen and could not get the words into order. Margery took her arm and brought her towards the door, speaking softly. Riarhin watched them go and knew that the woman would tell this account honestly for the rest of her life.

She had to hold Bea through the knowledge.

***

Matylde dropped the horse behind a chest.

The nurse could not reach it without moving the chest. Tylan handed the child back to Veralyn and went down on one knee to see whether he could reach from the side. He could not. Riarhin moved before thinking, kneeling at the other corner where the gap was wider.

Her apparent height made the movement awkward. She steadied herself with her right hand and used the left to feel along the floor. Dust caught beneath her nail. There was a small metal clasp, a piece of dried fruit, and the horse lying just beyond reach.

“Do you have a ruler?” she asked.

Tylan looked at her over the chest.

“A poker.”

“That will do.”

He fetched it and passed it handle first. She drew the toy towards her, caught it, and stood with dust on her sleeve. Matylde held out both hands.

The nurse took the horse to wipe it. The child objected. Veralyn endured the objection with her forehead briefly against the child’s hair.

“Thank you,” Tylan said.

Riarhin handed back the poker.

“You’re welcome.”

His gaze rested on her hand, then her face. He had seen her do practical things in inconvenient clothes before. She could not tell which resemblance had reached him, or whether any had.

Veralyn asked for water. He brought it, holding the cup while she helped Matylde drink. Their hands met around it and separated easily.

Riarhin looked towards the window. A cart crossed the far yard, piled with folded cloth, and the weaving woman stood beside it speaking excitedly to a man who had waited for her. The life inside the room pressed warmly against the life beyond it.

Mara’s table came into Riarhin’s mind without warning, the chair moved against the wall to make room for mending. She had wanted Elyas to sit in it while she was there. The wanting had become so familiar that it could return even in a room where his death was an accomplished decision.

“Aveline?” Veralyn said.

Riarhin turned.

“Will you stay? Margery will come back.”

“Yes,” she said.

***

Veralyn was called away before Margery returned.

The nurse had taken Matylde to change her clothes. Tylan remained by the window, turning the little horse in his hands. One of its legs had loosened where the peg entered the body. He tried it gently and set the toy on the sill, out of the child’s reach when she came back.

“Someone ought to mend that,” Riarhin said.

“I intend to.”

“Do you mend things?”

“Badly enough to be allowed only the unimportant ones.”

He smiled without looking round. She thought of Tavish and the strap on Mairi’s bench. In this room the resemblance was unwelcome. She moved to the chair Margery had left empty and settled her skirts before the habit of finding the nearest exit took possession of her again.

A young officer came to the door with a folded paper. He began to speak, saw Riarhin, and stopped.

“Discharges,” Tylan told her. “There is no secret in them.”

The officer brought it across. A man whose term ended at the close of the month had asked to leave six days early because his father was dying. His captain recommended refusal until the company had completed its movement south. Tylan read the recommendation, then asked how many men the captain had fit for duty.

The officer gave the number.

“One less will not stop him moving.”

“He says the example will be noticed.”

“Yes.” Tylan held out his hand for the pen. “Tell him I expect it to be.”

He wrote beneath the request. The officer read the addition and asked whether the man’s outstanding pay should go through the company chest.

“Give it to him before he leaves. If the accounts aren’t ready, make an advance and settle it afterwards.”

The officer bowed and went. Tylan cleaned the pen’s point with a scrap of cloth, taking more care than Riarhin expected from someone who could have left it for a servant.

“Will the captain resent that?” she asked.

“Probably.”

“Will he make the next man pay for it?”

Tylan looked at her then.

“He may. The next man can appeal.”

“Before his father dies?”

The pen remained between his fingers. Outside, a wagoner shouted to someone beyond the arch. Tylan put it down.

“I should have asked when the request was made.”

She had prepared for an argument. His agreement left her sitting too straight in the chair, with Bea’s expression waiting to be used.

“Will you ask?”

“Yes.”

He rang for a servant and sent after the officer. The man was to return after arranging the payment, with the date of the soldier’s first request. Then Tylan picked up the horse again.

“You don’t think much of the appeal.”

“I’ve seen people wait for one.”

“So have I.”

The answer had an edge. She let it remain between them. He pressed the loose leg into its socket, watched it fall back, and stopped trying to improve it with his thumb.

“When I began,” he said, “a man who wanted to leave had to ask the person who had brought him. He might have fulfilled every day he owed. There was always a reason to stay another week. His lord’s horse was lame. His lord’s cousin was expected. The other men had agreed and he was shaming them.”

“And now he asks you.”

“He has a date. He has a sum written beside his name. If I keep him past one or cheat him of the other, he can point to what I promised.”

She looked at the door through which the officer had gone.

“Who makes you keep it?”

“The men who will refuse to come next time.”

“Unless you make them.”

His eyes narrowed. For a moment she expected him to ask who had sent her to sit here and test the joints in every kindness. Instead he drew the other chair away from the table and sat.

“I want men who choose it. Men who learn something worth paying for and are paid well enough to stay. When they leave, I want the next recruit to hear that they came home with what they earned.”

Riarhin heard Berent saying that the army frightened the lords. She could also hear Na’ar asking what the pay included before agreeing to any road. She wished the room contained a worse argument.

“What do they learn?”

“How to remain together when they’re frightened. How to keep a horse fit. How to put a bridge back and find water without sending half a company into a ditch. Enough reading to know whether the order in their hand is the one they were told to carry.”

“And killing.”

“Yes. I would prefer them to survive that part.”

He said it evenly. She had heard men enjoy making such answers. Tylan seemed tired of needing to give this one.

The nurse returned with Matylde on her hip. The child saw the horse and reached. Tylan put it behind his chair, showed her his empty hands, and received an immediate accusation. He offered to take her; she clung to the nurse instead.

“Your judgement improves,” he told her.

Riarhin laughed before remembering whose father he was. The sound caught awkwardly in Bea’s throat. Tylan noticed, but the nurse was explaining that Matylde needed sleep and had refused to believe her. He rose to fetch a folded cloth from the chest and laid it over the woman’s arm.

“Try the little room. I’ll keep this one quiet.”

When they had gone he picked up the horse and found a narrow piece of wood in the basket beside the hearth. He tested its thickness against the loose peg.

“Will she remember any of this?” Riarhin asked.

“This morning?”

“The people coming. The papers.”

“Some. Less than we imagine, more than we intend.”

He set the wood beside the horse.

“I would like her to grow up without having to learn every man’s grievance against her grandfather before she can speak to him. Or his father’s against hers. There are better things to spend a childhood on.”

Riarhin thought of Iona’s letters, written in a hand growing surer while the grievances around her grew older. She could have asked whose father had been removed to make that future possible for Matylde. Elyas’s name was close enough to her mouth that she tasted the effort of keeping it there.

Margery came in before she chose whether to say it. Tylan stood, collected the broken toy and the sliver of wood, and asked her to keep their guest company. At the door he stopped to hear the officer’s returning steps.

***

Later, alone with Margery, she asked about the apprentices.

“Their former master wanted them back before their term was finished. He has a claim which would take months to hear, and meanwhile the woman who taught them would lose the work she has trained them to do.”

“The queen decided it?”

“She allowed the present arrangement to continue until the claim can be heard.”

“Would that happen for someone who hadn’t brought the cloth?”

Margery looked at her.

“It should.”

“Does it?”

“Sometimes. More often if someone asks.”

Riarhin waited for resentment. Margery gave her none.

“That’s why people come here,” she said. “To ask. You can dislike the necessity and still be pleased when someone gets what she needs.”

“I am pleased.”

“Then let her have that much today.”

Riarhin looked down at the dust still marking her sleeve. She brushed it away carefully, catching the loose ribbon before it could come undone.

Chapter Twenty-Two — Young Men With Reasons

The last stretch of the visit smelled of harvested ground.

Their carriage had left the city behind and passed between fields where the stubble showed pale against freshly opened strips. At one turning a wagonload of roots had shed enough earth to make the road soft; the driver brought them through it slowly while Joan held a hand above her lap, guarding against a jolt she could do little to prevent. Riarhin watched a pair of women working along the ditch with baskets. They straightened to see the carriage, then bent again before it had passed.

The estate roof appeared above the orchard first. From this side the house looked like a place which had grown whenever another generation needed rooms, a long old range joined to newer walls whose mortar had not yet darkened. Men crossed the yard carrying benches. Someone had put red-berried branches in a jar at the entrance, and their colour looked very bright against the stone.

Beyond the low wall, a cart waited outside the service entrance. Two men were taking barrels from it while a third insisted that they had brought the wrong number.

Joan leaned past her to look.

“We’re early.”

“Is that troublesome?”

“Only if they expected us late.”

Their host came through the main gate as the carriage stopped. He was young, with a broad forehead and a small scar cutting through one eyebrow. His coat had been brushed carefully enough for Riarhin to see where an older stain resisted the effort.

“Lady Joan. Welcome.”

He gave his name as Garin and offered Riarhin his hand when she stepped down. She accepted it, accounting for Bea’s height before her foot found the ground. His grip was firm without being possessive.

“Aveline Marr,” Joan said. “You may have met her at the dowager’s.”

“I would remember.”

It was said with enough pleasure to remain a compliment rather than an inspection. Riarhin thanked him. Behind him a dog came out through the gate, saw the carriage, and returned inside to consider whether its arrival required barking.

The Mound had put a fresh latch on the gate. The old holes remained in the wood.

***

They had been invited to hear a young man read a letter from home.

That was how Garin explained it. In the room beyond the hall, Riarhin discovered a more complicated purpose: several new members had arrived, their families had been asked to visit, and someone had decided that an afternoon shared with people from court would reassure those families about the service their sons had entered.

Some required reassuring. Others wanted to be seen requiring it.
