Joan greeted a woman whose son stood too close to her chair, then drew Riarhin towards a bench near the hearth. The woman asked whether the queen came often. Joan answered that the household had duties which took its members to the queen more often than the reverse.

“Guarding her?”

“Among other things,” Garin said.

“What other things?”

He looked at the son, who had been waiting to answer.

“Escorts. Carrying instructions. Seeing that they’re received.”

The mother adjusted her sleeve. “You had instructions enough at home.”

The young man flushed. Riarhin watched Garin turn the remark with a question about the journey. He did it competently, without making the woman feel managed.

Later the letter was read. It concerned a younger sister who had found the brother’s abandoned clothes useful and a horse which apparently missed him less. The reader took the teasing well until he reached a brief line from his father. Then he slowed, read it carefully, and folded the paper too quickly.

Garin thanked him. Someone asked about the horse. The room resumed its noise.

Riarhin had expected declarations. The affection was harder to stand apart from.

***

They ate outside while the weather held.

Garin carried benches into the sun, then stayed beside Riarhin when she asked how long he had served. Nearly three years. Before that he had lived in his elder brother’s household, where there was work for a younger son but little occasion to finish anything of his own.

“Did you quarrel?”

“Of course. We still do. He sends me advice concerning matters he hasn’t asked about.”

“Do you answer?”

“After I’ve stopped wanting to write the first answer.”

Riarhin smiled. “Wise.”

“He doesn’t think so.”

A man called Alard joined them, carrying bread and the certainty of someone slightly longer established. He asked whether Garin was describing his brother unfairly and received permission to improve the account. He chose instead to explain a recent errand in which both men had accompanied an officer sent to enforce the return of seized cattle.

The cattle belonged to households whose claims had been put aside for two years. Garin named one owner, an older woman who had followed the herd along the road to make certain the officer returned the right animals. She had corrected him three times and given him supper afterwards.

“Had you been there before?” Riarhin asked.

“No.”

“Did she trust you?”

“Not until the animals were in her field.”

“And the man who took them?”

Alard broke the bread. “He found the queen’s authority difficult to dispute with an escort in his yard.”

“Could she have got them back without you?”

“No,” Garin said. “That was why we went.”

The answer was sufficient. Riarhin looked at the hands passing the bread, young and strong, with a scrape along Garin’s wrist where something had rubbed during work. She could imagine Iona approving of him. She could imagine approving of him herself.

“What if the queen had decided against the woman?” she asked.

Alard watched her.

“The claim was sound.”

“But if it hadn’t been?”

“Then she would have had her answer.”

“And you would have stood in her yard.”

“Yes,” Garin said.

He looked uncomfortable, but he answered. Alard appeared more troubled by the question.

“People have had years to settle these things. They object to an answer because they preferred to keep the advantage while the question remained.”

“Sometimes,” Riarhin said.

“Very often.”

She took the bread he offered. It was warm from the kitchen, and she had been hungry before they sat down.

***

Garin showed her the little room where they kept the names of men lost in service.

He had offered because she asked about a small strip of dark cloth worn by one of the others. The man had been absent from an errand on which his friend died. He kept the cloth until a day agreed with someone whose authority Garin did not explain.

The room was plain. Riarhin saw a narrow shelf, a lamp, and sheets set within a covered board. There was a bowl of water beside the lamp, clean enough to have been changed recently.

“Who reads them?” she asked.

“We do.”

“All of you?”

“Those who were with them. Those who take their place.”

“Do their families come?”

“If they wish.”

He gave her room to look without standing at her shoulder. Riarhin read the names she could see. None belonged to a man she knew. That should not have made the room easier.

“Does Myrmis hear them here?” she asked.

Garin turned towards her, then nodded.

“Some of us pray.”

“And the others?”

“They remember.”

She looked at him. He had offered neither a conversion nor a warning. The belief was in his willingness to remain while she read and in the care with which he replaced the cover when she finished.

Outside, Alard was laughing with the young man whose father had written. Garin went to join them. Riarhin stayed near the doorway until Joan called her to help an older guest back to her chair.

***

The return to the carriage took them round the service yard.

The barrels had been put away, and the carter was arguing over a torn sack for which he expected to be charged. At the main gate a horse had lost a shoe. Its rider had left it standing across the narrow passage while he looked for the missing piece, so Garin led the women out by a smaller gate beside the old granary.

“Mind the step,” he said.

It was higher on the yard side. Beyond it a track ran between the estate’s boundary and a worked field, with a shallow drainage cut along the farther edge. A woman was gathering loose stalks near the neighbour’s fence. She looked up as they passed and called to Garin about a hen which had found its way into the yard again.

“I’ll bring her back.”

“You said that yesterday.”

“I brought her back yesterday.”

“Then shut your gate.”

He grinned. Riarhin looked along the drainage cut. A person could get below the line of the track there, but the woman in the field would see him from her fence. The observation came as readily as noticing the damp on her hem.

The carriage waited where the track joined the main approach. From here the house’s newer wing hid the public rooms. A small upper window stood open, with a cloth hung over the sill to dry.

Garin helped Joan in. When he turned to Riarhin, she asked whether he meant to remain in the queen’s service.

“Yes.”

“Your brother will miss your advice.”

“He gets it anyway.”

She laughed and took his hand. As they drove away, he turned back to answer the carter, already called from one part of his afternoon to another.

Joan settled her cloak.

“They are good young men,” she said.

“Some of them.”

“Yes. Some of them.”

Riarhin looked back once at the fields. The woman had recovered her hen. She carried it against her hip towards a house low enough to disappear behind the hedge.

Chapter Twenty-Three — Fayd’s Courtesy

Fayd received her with Deirdre’s claim on the table.

Riarhin had expected a clerk. Maelis’s message had named an office and a time, and the man at the door had taken her cloak without telling her that the appointment had changed. She entered with a question prepared about the harbour bond and found Fayd standing beside the window.

“Aveline Marr.”

She bowed. “You have the advantage.”

“So I’m told.”

He offered a chair. The room contained a second table occupied by an Ecktorian visitor, who rose politely before Fayd asked him to give them a moment. The man gathered his papers, leaving one sealed packet behind, and went into the adjoining room.

Riarhin sat where she had been shown. The chair faced the window obliquely. Fayd could watch her without making her stare into the light.

“Maelis has asked about this,” he said, touching the claim. “You know the carrier?”

“A little.”

“Enough to come.”

“Enough to ask which office holds her bond.”

“That answer has become more complicated than it deserved.”

“Can it become simple again?”

“Yes.”

He put another paper beside the first. A receiving officer had changed posts. His successor disputed instruments issued shortly before his departure; a creditor had made use of the delay, and an Ecktorian house had asked its own agents to protect an interest which did not require Deirdre’s money to remain in custody.

“The consignment arrived,” Riarhin said.

“It did.”

“Then release the bond.”

“I can recommend it.”

“Whose answer do you require?”

“The man who holds it.”

She looked at him. He smiled slightly.

“He may be more willing to answer when the interested parties cease disagreeing.”

“Have they?”

“Most.”

***

Fayd asked the Ecktorian to return.

The visitor’s Viterran was excellent, his accent more evident in the length he gave certain vowels than in any difficulty finding words. He addressed Aveline with polished courtesy and explained that the houses he represented had no wish to burden a carrier for an error of description.

“Then you’ll say so in writing,” Riarhin said.

He glanced at Fayd, amused. “I have.”

“What remains?”

“A question about who accepts the correction.”

“Your house?”

“The house which received the goods.”

“And they want?”

“To be assured that accepting this signature will not oblige them to accept several others.”

Riarhin looked at the papers. One correction was becoming a door through which people feared other claims would enter. She had known that problem before. She also knew it could keep a crew unpaid while men with clean cuffs improved the wording.

“Separate them.”

“That is what we are doing,” Fayd said.

The Ecktorian looked pleased by her impatience. He gave the name of a household in Ecktoria whose representatives would accept the distinction. Another household still needed persuading. In discussing how its answer could be reached, he used the name of the Unburnt as casually as a merchant might name a receiving house.

Riarhin kept her face attentive. She had heard enough about Ecktoria to recognise the name and too little to know what part of it sat in the adjoining room.

“The whole circle?” Fayd asked.

“Those who sent me.”

“Then write that.”

The visitor inclined his head. He withdrew with the revised paper, leaving the sealed packet where it lay.

“An extensive acquaintance,” Riarhin said.

“An improving one.”

“Does it reach the harbour?”

“It reaches people whose wishes do.”

Fayd turned Deirdre’s claim towards her and showed her the office to which the corrected statement would be sent. He named the day after which she could ask for a reply. The practical answer was there at last, small enough to fit in two lines.

***

He did not dismiss her.

Riarhin waited. Outside the window two clerks were crossing the yard, one carrying a bundle too large for the cloth tied around it. The other kept a hand beneath the lower papers and argued with him all the way.

“You visited the Mound’s house,” Fayd said.

“Yes.”

“Did you enjoy it?”

“Parts.”

“Which parts?”

“The young men were glad to be useful. Their families were glad to see them.”

“And the other parts?”

“You must ask a more useful question.”

He looked at her for a moment, then smiled more openly.

“Very well. Garin has been recommended for work carrying instructions beyond the usual royal households. Does he listen before answering?”

“Yes.”

“To women as well as men?”

“To me.”

“Did you need to make that difficult?”

“Not particularly.”

Fayd took up a pen, but made no note.

“And Alard?”

“He prefers a question whose answer he knows.”

“Many men do.”

“He thinks that makes it a better question.”

Fayd set down the pen.

Riarhin had given him impressions of two young men she had met over bread. Neither had sworn her to secrecy. That did not make the exchange feel innocent. She could see how easily Aveline’s invitations might become a second occupation which nobody formally offered her.

“What do you want to know about Deirdre?” she asked.

“I was going to ask who receives her in Theona.”

“There it is.”

“The crown has interests in the islands. A carrier’s family may be able to tell us why a permitted landing becomes disputed.”

“She came to recover her money.”

“And she may.”

“Without giving you her kin?”

“Yes.”

“Then I won’t.”

He accepted the refusal with a slight movement of his head.

“You might tell her I would receive her.”

“I can tell her that.”

It was less than he had asked and more than she had intended to agree. He appeared content with it.

***

At the door he spoke again.

“People have been asking after a northern traveller who came to see Elyas before his arrest.”

Riarhin turned. She kept Bea’s face, the weight of the gown, the position of her left hand against its fold. All of it required attention at once.

“Many people travelled.”

“Some remain in the neighbourhood.”

“Is travel an offence?”

“No.”

“Then why tell me?”

“You may hear questions. Maelis has been distressed by her recent encounter. I would dislike those near her to mistake an inquiry for a danger they must answer quickly.”

Na’ar. Berent. Herself. She could not tell which of them he intended her to imagine first.

“Should she be less distressed?”

“That depends on what she has not told me.”

Riarhin held his gaze. He had let her reach the door before speaking, placing the choice of staying or leaving visibly in her hands.

“I’ll tell Deirdre where to ask for her bond,” she said.

“Please do.”

She left. The clerk outside offered her cloak and asked whether she needed a carriage. She declined because she wanted to walk far enough for the room to stop arranging her thoughts.

***

Maelis read the two lines about the bond and asked what else had been said.

Riarhin told her about the proposed introduction to Deirdre. She did not repeat the assessments of Garin and Alard until Maelis looked up from the paper and waited.

Then she told those too.

“He likes giving people something useful before asking whether they might be useful in return,” Maelis said.

“He didn’t require it.”

“No. He can remember an answer without requiring it.”

Na’ar was at the lower table cleaning mud from his boots. Riarhin went down and told him the warning concerning the northern traveller. He listened without looking up, then set the brush aside.

“Does Berent know?”

“I’ll tell him.”

“Tell him today.”

She sent word through the agreed carrier that she would meet him at the edge of the market. He came on foot, dressed plainly, and stood beside a stall selling repaired harness while she gave the message.

“Have they an order?” he asked.

“Not that he said.”

“Have I been named?”

“Not to me.”

Berent looked at the harness. “I won’t run because somebody wants to see whether I run.”

“Good. Don’t make a demonstration of staying either.”

He nodded. He had something to tell her in return: Mara had received an answer concerning her petition for Elyas’s papers. She was permitted to submit further particulars. Nothing had been supplied.

Riarhin closed her eyes briefly.

“How is she?”

“Angry.”

“And otherwise?”

“She asked whether you were eating.”

Riarhin looked down at the gown beneath her cloak, good cloth paid for with money they needed for other things. “Tell her yes.”

“I thought so.”

The answer had no reproach in it. She heard one anyway.

Chapter Twenty-Four — The Mother She Never Met

Orrel’s reply concerned the melon.

He had accepted her apology in three short lines and added that she had been right about the neighbour’s washing. The frames were moving. If the next crop failed, he intended to blame her with moderation.

Riarhin read the note twice, then put it beside the mirror rather than with the papers concerning Elyas. Maelis saw where she placed it and made no comment.

They were dressing for an evening at which Riarhin had no errand.

She had tried to find one. Margery’s invitation named no useful guest, the queen was engaged elsewhere, and Jaennis intended only to listen to music and see the people she liked. Riarhin could decline. She had been offered no access which refusal would necessarily lose.

Instead she chose the brown gown and asked Vessa whether the cuff could be made less likely to catch.

“Now you’ve worn it, you can tell me where it troubles you,” the dressmaker said. “Much better than imagining it.”

She moved the fastening. Riarhin raised an arm and turned, feeling the cloth settle correctly around the body beneath the likeness.

“There.”

Vessa nodded. “You look as though it belongs to you.”

***

Lyra was leaving the palace when Riarhin arrived.

She came through the lesser gate beside Deirdre, who was arguing about a packet. Lyra held the packet flat against her own palm while the carrier pointed at the writing on its cover. They stopped under the arch to finish the discussion, and Riarhin could hear the anger in Deirdre’s voice without hearing the words.

At last Lyra gave the packet back. The woman took it, touched her wrist in thanks, and went away.

Lyra turned towards Riarhin.

For a moment they stood among people passing in both directions. Then Lyra looked at the gown, the hair, the face, and back at Riarhin’s eyes.

“Aveline,” she said.

Riarhin inclined her head.

“Have we been introduced?”

“Maelis told me to expect you.”

Nothing in the sentence established what Maelis had told her. Lyra came nearer.

“Is it difficult?” she asked quietly.

“Yes.”

“Good to know something is.”

Riarhin accepted the anger. “Have you seen Mara?”

“Yesterday.”

“How was it?”

“We spoke.”

“Did she—”

“Riarhin.”

The name was barely breathed. A man carrying a folded carpet passed behind them, and Lyra waited until he had cleared the arch.

“You needn’t arrange us. I know you want to. We can be unhappy with each other and still go back.”

“I wasn’t—”

“Yes, you were.”

Riarhin let the denial go. She looked at the place where the packet had rested against Lyra’s hand. A pale impression from its cord remained across the skin.

“Can I help with that?”

“The packet? No. It has reached the right person.”

“And you?”

Lyra’s expression changed slightly.

“I’m going home. To eat something I didn’t have to discuss with anyone before choosing it.”

“That sounds pleasant.”

“It may be. You could try it.”

Riarhin glanced towards the lighted windows. Lyra followed the glance and looked back with a tired, small smile.

“Go. You’re expected.”

Riarhin touched her hand. It was an awkward place for such a gesture, and Lyra almost withdrew before recognising what was being offered. She let the contact last a moment.

“Another day,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

Lyra left by the gate. Riarhin watched until the crowd took her, then went inside.

***

Eda was singing.

Maelis had arranged the introduction, but the young woman had chosen the song and brought her own accompanist, a broad man who kept his instrument covered until the lamps were moved far enough away. She saw Riarhin enter and lifted two fingers in greeting without breaking the verse.

The song was the one they had argued over at supper.

Halfway through, it took the northern turn. The suitor escaped the sisters and made off with their horse. Eda gave the stolen animal a voice it had not possessed in Riarhin’s version, which made Catrin laugh so suddenly that she spilled wine on her own wrist.

When it ended, Riarhin went to her.

“You’ve improved it.”

“I’ve made it longer. That is how singers improve things.”

“Does your aunt approve?”

“My aunt believes all songs should end before the next tide.”

Margery called them over to settle whether the younger sister knew about the horse before the last verse. Eda said she did. Catrin said the verse did not support it. Riarhin listened while each demonstrated the line with a different emphasis, then found herself defending an interpretation she had never considered until Catrin objected to it.

Jaennis sat beside Vaelrie on the broader couch. Vaelrie’s hand lay against the dowager’s sleeve, occasionally moving when she leaned forward to hear. Neither seemed interested in settling the argument.

The room was warm. Someone had opened a window just enough to admit air without the night’s chill. Riarhin could smell rain on the stone outside, the wine Catrin had spilled, a little smoke from a candle burning badly at one end of the table.

She wanted the next song.

***

Later, Margery asked her to dance.

There were only four couples and insufficient room for an ambitious figure. They managed by moving the chairs, then abandoning the movement that required the dancers to pass behind them. Riarhin knew the pattern. She had learned it as a child watching Bea practise with another woman in a room smaller than this one.

She followed Margery through the first turn and nearly stepped too far. Bea’s longer appearance tempted her to lengthen her stride. Margery adjusted without comment.

“Have I hurried you?”

“No. I was thinking.”

“Dangerous pastime.”

“So I hear.”

On the next turn they moved easily. Riarhin kept the face with less deliberate effort when she stopped trying to attend to everyone else in the room. Margery’s hand was warm. The gown moved properly around her knees. For several measures she had no use for a doorway beyond the space it gave them to turn.

When the music ended, Margery kept her hand long enough to lead her towards the open window.

“Better?”

“Yes.”

“You looked as though you’d decided to enjoy it.”

“I did enjoy it.”

“I know. There’s a difference.”

Riarhin looked at her. Margery was close enough for the light to show fine lines beside her eyes, and the slight unevenness in her lower lip where she must once have bitten it badly. Bea had looked at people from this distance. Riarhin remembered how easily her mother made it seem that the rest of a room had ceased to require her attention.

She let her own attention remain.

Margery smiled and touched the changed fastening at her cuff.

“This is new.”

“Mended.”

“It suits you.”

“The mending?”

“The ease.”

Riarhin laughed softly. Margery’s hand stayed at her wrist, asking nothing beyond permission to stay there. She allowed it.

***

They went into the smaller room when someone began setting the table for late food.

Margery took the seat by the window and left Riarhin the one facing her. A servant brought cups and asked whether the door should remain open. Margery looked at Riarhin before answering.

“Open,” Riarhin said.

The servant left it so. Voices from the other room continued, softened by the turn in the passage. Riarhin could hear Eda laughing at something Maelis had said.

“Were you always a companion?” Margery asked.

Riarhin looked at her cup.

“No.”

“What came before?”

“A great deal of travelling.”

“Alone?”

“Some of it.”

“Did you like that?”

She could have made Bea answer. A remembered smile, a phrase about freedom, a suggestion that the person asking might change her preference. Her mother had used such answers with visitors when Riarhin was within hearing, and the child had thought them effortless because she could not see the work.

“I liked being able to leave,” she said.

Margery nodded, considering the answer without trying to improve it.

“And now?”

“I’m less certain what I want to leave.”

The words surprised her. She looked up quickly, expecting to find herself exposed by them. Margery merely turned her cup so that its warm side rested against her fingers.

“That happens.”

“Has it happened to you?”

“Yes.”

“Did you stay?”

“Several times. Once too long. Once I left early and spent years arranging reasons not to ask whether I could return.”

“Could you?”

“I never asked.”

They sat with that. The reply had neither instructed nor consoled her. Riarhin thought of Sada’s letters in the chest at Glenruan and the last one she had begun southbound, left unfinished when news overtook them.

Margery reached across the small space between their chairs and took her hand.

“You needn’t tell me.”

“I know.”

She did know, and for a moment wanted to tell her everything precisely because she had not been asked. The wanting made her remember Lyra’s account of the supper. She drew back a little, attending to the room, the cup, the doorway.

Margery released her at once.

“Too much?”

“No. I’m sorry.”

“You needn’t apologise for moving your hand.”

Riarhin looked at her. Margery’s hand lay open beside hers.

She put her hand back where it had been.

***

They did not kiss.

Riarhin wanted to. She discovered that wanting when Margery leaned nearer to speak over a burst of laughter from the other room, and it remained when the noise fell. She could have turned her face the necessary little distance. Bea’s face would have turned with her.

Margery saw the hesitation and gave it somewhere to go by asking whether she wanted food. Riarhin said yes. They returned to the others, took places beside Eda, and joined an argument about how late a supper could be served before it became a different meal.

Vaelrie had fallen asleep against Jaennis’s shoulder. Jaennis continued speaking with the others, one hand resting protectively over the fingers in her lap. When Catrin offered to wake her, she shook her head.

“She’ll miss the pears.”

“I’ll save one.”

It was easy to believe in the affection. Riarhin did believe in it. The knowledge sat beside the conversation with Lyra and would not arrange itself into the simpler thing she wanted.

She ate the pear Margery passed her and stayed until the musician put his instrument away.

***

At her room, the lamp had been left low.

Na’ar had seen her to the door and gone back to Maelis, who was arguing that an evening could not be called late while half the household remained awake. Riarhin unfastened the gown slowly, managing the upper closure Vessa had altered to make it easier for her to reach.

She sat before the mirror and let the face go.

Her own mouth looked younger and less assured. She touched it with two fingers, finding the same skin she had felt while Margery held her hand. The room was quiet enough for her to hear a cart in the next street and a woman calling to somebody at a window.

On the table lay Orrel’s note. She put it away carefully.

Then she drew a sheet towards her and wrote to Sada. She described the room in which she was staying, omitting the part which would endanger anyone who carried the letter. She wrote that she had been dancing and stopped for a long while after it, uncertain what she wanted the sentence to mean.

She did not cross it out.

Before sleeping, she laid the brown gown where it would not crease. Margery had asked her to walk in the garden the next afternoon, if the weather held. There was nothing she needed to learn there.

She intended to go.

Part IV — The Room Narrows

Chapter Twenty-Five — An Answer From Jaennis

The rain held off.

Margery was waiting beside the garden door with a pair of stout shoes in her hand. She had dressed for the walk and then remembered a patch of ground beneath the fruit trees which had swallowed a shoe the previous autumn. The gardener assured her it was firm now. She intended to test his assurance in something she could afford to lose.

“You could have sent to put me off,” Riarhin said.

“I wanted to walk with you.”

“Yes.”

“Was that difficult?”

“A little.”

Margery sat on the low wall to change her shoes. Riarhin held the pair she took off, turning them soles together so that the damp would not mark her sleeve. There was a dark repair across one heel. She had been prepared for another question, something requiring an answer about the north or the man whose name she had supposedly carried, and the simple business of waiting with the shoes left her oddly at a loss.

“I have nowhere I need to be until evening,” Margery said. “Have you?”

There were several places Riarhin believed she ought to be. None would receive her merely because she arrived wanting something. Maelis had promised to send if Jaennis found time for her, and she had left word where she could be reached.

“No.”

“Then we might even go slowly.”

They took the outer path. Beyond the garden wall a man was mending the covering of a cart, standing in its bed and trying to work the end of a needle through leather too thick for it. Riarhin watched him abandon the needle, search among his tools, and return with an awl.

“My sister says I notice everything that is going wrong,” Margery said.

“You saw him too.”

“I meant you watching him. He’ll mend it.”

“He will now.”

Margery laughed and led her towards the fruit trees.

The late pears were wrapped individually against the birds. One had fallen with its cloth still tied around it, and Margery picked it up, examined the bruise, and put it in her pocket. She asked whether the trees at home bore well. Riarhin described the small sour apples at Glenruan, which Iona insisted were better than any grown elsewhere because she had stolen so many of them. The telling grew to include Tavish’s argument that fruit taken from his own garden scarcely counted as a theft and Iona’s answer that he was spoiling it.

“Is she yours?”

“No. My cousin’s daughter.”

“You sound as though you miss her.”

Riarhin pressed a twig aside for them both to pass. Iona would be taller when she saw her again. She had left with a promise to write and sent one cautious account of the road, saying nothing about the execution because Tavish would have heard it before the letter reached him. She had not told the girl that she was frightened.

“I do.”

Margery put her hand through Riarhin’s arm. They walked for a while without trying to make the small admission yield more.

***

The interruption came in a pair of muddy stockings.

A woman stood at the far garden gate arguing with a servant who was quite willing to fetch somebody but could not make her understand that he needed to know whom. She had a child beside her carrying one shoe. The other remained on her foot.

“It isn’t the shoe,” she said as Margery approached. “I can get the shoe.”

“What is it?”

“The wall. They’ve pulled my wall into the ditch.”

Behind her, through the open gate, Riarhin could see two mounted men and a wagon stopped in the lane. One rider wore the queen’s colours. He dismounted when he saw Margery and began an explanation which the woman cut across with a sharp movement of her hand.

“He knows what he’s done.”

The wagon belonged to the men preparing the ground for the army inspection. They had come down a lane marked on their instructions, found a turn narrower than expected, and tried to take it without unloading. The near wheel struck the foot of the woman’s orchard wall. Several yards had fallen. When she went to stop them pulling the wagon free, she lost her shoe in the disturbed ditch.

The rider said the wall had been unsound.

“Then you should have been careful of it.”

Margery asked her name.

“Cecily Orme.”

“And is anybody hurt?”

“The boy’s frightened.”

The child looked fiercely at his mother. Riarhin crouched, holding the pear she had been given on the path.

“Would you like this?”

He considered the bruise.

“What’s happened to it?”

“It fell.”

“Was it them?”

“No.”

He accepted it.

Veralyn came through the garden with Tylan before Margery had finished hearing the rider. Someone had gone to fetch them from the room overlooking the outer ground. The queen wore a dark gown without a mantle, and one of her women followed carrying it, trying to judge a moment when putting it over her shoulders would not interrupt what she was doing.

Riarhin rose. The child moved behind his mother with the pear.

Veralyn listened to the rider, then asked Cecily to show her the turn.

The company followed into the lane. The wagon stood with one wheel at an angle against the fallen stone; a horse, still harnessed, had begun to tremble from the effort of holding the load. Tylan told the driver to chock the rear wheels before he loosened anything. Cecily tried to explain the wall’s line, but Veralyn was looking beyond it at the trees.

“Does your orchard run through to the inspection ground?”

“Yes, Your Grace. But I don’t want—”

“You need your wall put back.”

“And the ground. A wagon over the roots—”

“We’ll keep the heavy loads on the road.” Veralyn looked at Tylan. “The horses can come through from the other end. There is room to turn them without this.”

“Through the orchard?” Cecily asked.

“On the strip beside it. Will you show the marshal?”

Cecily hesitated, then led Tylan to the gap. She pointed with considerable firmness while he bent to hear her. Veralyn accepted her mantle at last.

“Someone write it,” she said. “She oughtn’t to have to come and ask again.”

A clerk found a flat stone on the garden wall and set his board on it. Riarhin stood close enough to read over his shoulder while Margery held down the sheet’s corner against the wind. The queen directed payment for restoring Cecily’s wall and the disturbed ground, including damage caused by the altered horse approach. Then she told Tylan’s man to amend the inspection instructions: the horse companies would enter by the orchard strip rather than the lower lane. Foot and wagons retained their separate approaches.

“The muster order was sealed yesterday,” the clerk said.

“Then this is an addition to it.”

He wrote the day’s date at the head, copied the direction on a second sheet, and read both aloud. Cecily asked that the fallen stone be sorted before carts ran over it. The queen added that too.

“Who receives hers?” the clerk asked.

“I do,” Cecily said.

“Someone who will read it for you, if—”

“I can read.”

He handed it over. His ears had reddened. Margery witnessed the duplicate, and Riarhin watched the queen’s seal pressed beside the new words.

Cecily read her copy through, moving her lips only over the longer words. The child had finished the unbruised half of the pear. He offered his mother the rest, and she took it without looking up.

***

Their walk ended there.

Margery stayed to make sure Cecily had the name of the man who would arrange the payment. Maelis’s message reached Riarhin while the driver and two soldiers were lifting the wagon enough to set the wheel straight. Jaennis could see her before the evening meal.

“You’ll go?” Margery asked.

“Yes.”

“I thought so.”

Riarhin could hear no reproach in it and went looking for one anyway. Margery rescued her good shoes from the servant to whom they had been entrusted and put them on the wall.

“Give me your arm again. I can’t balance in these.”

Riarhin did. For a moment Margery’s fingers tightened against her sleeve, and she wished the message had come later.

***

Jaennis had a torn piece of lace laid across her knee.

It was fine enough that Riarhin could scarcely see where a repair might begin. A little cushion held the pins. Jaennis removed one and caught the broken edge, then glanced towards the chair opposite her.

“Sit down, Aveline.”

There was nobody else in the room. Beyond the inner door someone moved a stool, scraping its feet along the floor.

“I asked to speak privately.”

“And we are speaking privately. I haven’t sent my women out of the house.”

Riarhin sat.

She had practised the opening while walking from the garden. Every version now sounded like a question asked by someone who already knew the answer, and she had too little knowledge to afford that vanity.

“Lyra spoke to you before Elyas was arrested.”

“Yes.”

“The northern communication was entered the next morning.”

“Orrel has been helpful.”

“He told me when it was received.”

Jaennis eased the pin through a loop. “Is he to be asked about that as well?”

“I am asking you.”

“Then leave him out of it.”

Riarhin felt a quick, unwelcome agreement. She had already made one servant explain herself because a stranger wanted to know something. She placed both hands on her lap and let the glamour settle around the stillness.

“Lyra went to your supper intending to say very little. She remembers feeling that she could trust you with more.”

“She could.”

“Could she?”

“I did not promise to conceal a prince’s private approach to people beyond the queen’s reach.”

“She trusted you to hear her.”

“I heard her.”

The lace lay between them, white on Jaennis’s dark skirt. Riarhin wanted to snatch it away. She had no right to be sitting there doing this delicate, unnecessary work while Lyra’s account remained in the room.

“What did you do to her?”

Jaennis set down the pin.

“I helped her feel at ease.”

“With a charm.”

“Yes.”

The answer came without an effort to soften it. Riarhin had expected resistance, enough that she could press against it and feel herself advancing. Instead she had the thing she came for, and Jaennis was watching her decide what to do with it.

“Did she know?”

“No.”

“Did you ask her leave?”

“No.”

“And then you sent what she told you to be examined.”

“I asked that the northern journey be investigated.”

“Elyas was executed.”

“I know.”

“You can say that very easily.”

“I’ve had longer to know it than you have.”

Riarhin leaned forward. Her mother had done it differently, with the smallest change in her shoulders, making the man opposite believe he had finally said something worth hearing. Riarhin felt the change begin in herself. She could lower her voice. She could make Jaennis wish to finish an explanation she had begun. The prospect gave her a moment’s ugly pleasure.

She sat back.

“Did you ask for his death?”

“No.”

“Did you try to prevent it?”

“I asked Veralyn to consider what his marriage had already cost him, and how few claims he had left to make for himself.”

“What did she say?”

“That men came to him nonetheless.”

Riarhin looked towards the inner door. The movement beyond it had stopped.

“They did,” Jaennis said. “You know that.”

“They came to a man who would hear them.”

“And whose word they preferred to their duke’s. They brought him quarrels which had not been judged as they wished. Men who would not march for Harald offered him horses. Some would have followed him if he had asked.”

“He didn’t ask.”

“Was it safe to wait until he did?”

“Yes.”

Jaennis held her gaze. Riarhin heard how quickly she had answered. Elyas had sent Berent north because he believed something was wrong, and men who trusted him would have heard that belief sooner or later. She could imagine him refusing an armed following. She could also imagine him opening his door again the next morning, because someone stood outside it in the rain.

“He could have answered the charge,” she said. “With his own people heard. With time.”

“He was heard.”

“Then you know I mean something else.”

Jaennis lowered her eyes to the lace.

“Veralyn believes she saved a great many lives.”

“Does that comfort you?”

“It ought to matter to you.”

“It does. I would like to know whose lives she counted before she spent his.”

For the first time Jaennis’s hand moved without purpose. She touched the pin cushion, left it where it was, and folded the torn lace carefully over itself.

Outside, a woman called that the lamps were being brought in. Jaennis answered that they could wait.

Riarhin stood. She had wanted an admission she could carry back to Lyra, something that would stop her turning the supper over in her mind and searching for the moment at which she had failed. She had it. The getting had not made the next thing easier.

“Will you tell Lyra what you told me?”

“If she asks.”

“She shouldn’t have to.”

Jaennis rose too. At the door she paused with her hand on the latch.

“Be careful what you encourage people to tell you, Aveline.”

Riarhin passed her without replying. She had Bea’s face well in hand until she reached the passage. There, with nobody close enough to see, she shut her eyes and waited for her own anger to stop dragging at it.

Chapter Twenty-Six — The Paper That Does Not Fit

Lyra had gone to the wrong door.

That was what the woman above the seamstress’s shop told Riarhin when she arrived the following morning. A man had called shortly after breakfast and said the keeper of the military correspondence wanted a discrepancy explained. Lyra had taken her cloak, put two letters into a drawer, and asked the woman to tell a waiting visitor that she would return before noon.

“Which door?” Riarhin asked.

“The west office. I told the other one that too.”

“What other one?”

“The fellow who came for her papers.”

The woman’s fingers were white against the edge of the door. She had been holding it open with her foot, as if permitting the conversation for a moment would commit her to less than inviting Riarhin inside.

“Did he take them?”

“He had an order.”

“What did it say?”

“I didn’t read it.”

“Did you see the seal?”

“He had an order,” she repeated. “There were two men with him.”

Riarhin stepped back. The woman closed the door slowly enough to avoid the appearance of slamming it in her face.

She could still take the news to Lyra. She had imagined doing so all the way there, trying to find a way to say You were made to speak without letting it sound like a pardon bestowed on a child. The sentences remained ready while she walked to the west office. She disliked herself for keeping them.

At the office a boy was arranging stools in a row. He told her to wait, then fetched a man who asked her name and her business and wrote both down before saying that Lyra was held for examination.

“What examination?”

“Of matters laid against her.”

“What matters?”

“You are a relation?”

“No.”

“Then I can’t give you particulars.”

“Can I see her?”

“No visits have been allowed.”

“Has she asked for anything?”

The man lifted his pen again. Riarhin watched the drop gathering at its point.

“I can put your inquiry with the others.”

“How many others?”

He waited. She gave him the address of Maelis’s lodging and made herself leave before he asked her to.

***

Maelis read the folded message twice.

It had been brought by Deirdre’s hired wagoner, who remained downstairs unwilling to leave without a reply. Riarhin found Maelis at her worktable with a clean bowl placed over a stain in the wood and three unopened invitations pushed against it.

“She’s at the wool store beyond the east gate,” Maelis said. “She’s been told to stay there.”

“Who told her?”

“The officer who served this.”

She passed the message across.

Deirdre had written hurriedly under the formal wording copied by someone else. She was required to present herself the following day concerning receipt of a copy of protected military instructions from Lyra at the lesser palace gate, two afternoons earlier. The copy was described as the muster order and its direction for the horse companies’ approach through Cecily Orme’s orchard.

Riarhin read that part again.

The workroom had been arranged for some entirely different morning. A basin stood under the window with two flower heads floating in it; Maelis had been comparing their colours against a length of ribbon and had left the ribbon pinned to a board. Beside it lay the invitations, some opened with a knife and one torn impatiently at the fold. Riarhin looked at these things while the meaning of the charge reached her. The room would have gone on being this room if the letter had been about a dinner. She wanted the flowers taken away and could not have explained whom that would help.

The lesser gate. The packet Deirdre had taken back. Her hand on Lyra’s wrist.

“I saw them,” she said.

Maelis watched her.

“I didn’t see what was in it.”

“Deirdre says it was a claim for freight, returned because she had brought the wrong supporting account. The released bond was to pay it. Lyra marked the place where the account was missing.”

Riarhin put the paper flat on the table.

“The orchard direction was made yesterday.”

“Perhaps she had it before it was issued.”

“No. A wagon brought the wall down yesterday afternoon. I was there. The queen changed the approach while we were standing beside it.”

Maelis drew the paper back towards herself. Her nail followed the description as she reread it.

“Does this say it was the whole order?”

“It says the order with the addition. That addition wasn’t a thing anyone could pass on the day they say she passed it.”

“Then someone has been careless.”

“Someone has written it.”

From downstairs Na’ar called up that the wagoner had another load to collect and wanted to know whether he should go. Maelis asked Riarhin to wait while she composed an answer. She wrote only that she had received the message and would come herself before evening.

“You aren’t telling her?” Riarhin asked.

“In a paper carried by a man whose name I learned this morning? No.”

Riarhin gave her the pen.

***

Margery was receiving a woman who wanted her eldest boy placed in a household.

They were at the window, inspecting a patch on the elbow of his best coat. The boy wore it while they discussed whether the repair was visible enough to matter. He held himself so rigidly that Riarhin could see the muscles working in his jaw.

Margery came away when she saw her.

“Is something wrong?”

“Lyra’s been taken.”

The boy’s mother stopped speaking. Margery led Riarhin into the little room where they had sat after supper and left the door half open.

“What has happened?”

Riarhin told her. She kept it to the charge and the impossible direction, including exactly what she had seen at the gate and what she had not. Margery listened until she had finished, then went to close the door.

“You signed the duplicate,” Riarhin said. “You can tell them when it was made.”

“Yes.”

“Will you come with me?”

“To whom?”

“The queen. You can ask to see her.”

“I can ask to see her about the things she permits me to bring.”

“This is one of her people being held on a lie.”

“And I can attest to what happened in the orchard.”

“Then do it where she’ll hear.”

Margery’s face hardened a little. Riarhin remembered the pressure of her fingers while she changed her shoes. She had come expecting that intimacy to open a door, and now that it did not she wanted to press harder.

“I have a nephew waiting to learn whether he’ll keep his place,” Margery said. “Three of the women you ate with last night depend on appointments I helped them obtain. I won’t walk into the queen’s private room and accuse an officer whose case I haven’t seen of fabricating it.”

“You’ve seen the thing that proves it.”

“I have seen a summons copied into a letter. Let me sign an account of yesterday. Put it before someone who can compare the papers.”

“While Lyra waits.”

“Yes,” Margery said, and the effort of it was plain. “While she waits.”

Riarhin turned towards the window. A thread had caught on the curtain hook and pulled a little ladder in the weave. She fixed her attention on it until the red hair at the edge of her vision ceased to tremble.

“Will you give me the account today?”

“Now, if you sit while I write it.”

She sat.

Margery fetched paper. She wrote carefully, with long pauses to be sure of what she could say. Riarhin corrected the time of the queen’s arrival; Margery considered it, agreed, and changed the line before signing. She included her witnessing of the duplicate direction and the fact that the wall had fallen that afternoon.

When she had finished, she made a second copy.

“For me,” she said. “In case someone wants to know exactly what I gave you.”

The pen scratched over the last few words. Riarhin wanted to tell her that yesterday’s walk had been something she wanted for itself. Saying so now would sound like an argument for doing more.

“Thank you.”

Margery sanded the sheet and tipped the grains back.

“I’ll send word if I’m asked about it.”

Outside, the boy was standing in precisely the same place. His mother smiled hopefully at Margery as the door opened. Riarhin passed them both and went downstairs with the paper held flat against her body.

***

Cecily Orme had found her other shoe.

It stood beside the hearth full of crumpled linen. Her son was trying to pull the linen out when Riarhin arrived, and Cecily told him to leave it alone twice before asking what the visitor wanted.

“The direction the queen gave you yesterday. May I see it?”

“Is somebody saying I asked too much?”

“No.”

“I didn’t ask them to come through my orchard.”

“I know. I was there.”

Cecily studied her. Recognition came slowly without Margery and the garden around them. She took the paper from a box on the shelf and laid it on the table, keeping one hand on it.

Riarhin read the date. The words were as she remembered them, including the separate care required for the fallen stone. The seal was intact.

“May I copy it?”

“What for?”

She could have offered a harmless reason. Cecily had a claim against the queen’s household and a child who needed the income from an orchard; there were several ways to make borrowing the paper sound like helping her. Riarhin brought out Margery’s account instead.

“A woman is being accused of handing these directions to someone before they existed.”

Cecily read the account. She pulled a chair out with her foot and sat.

“Was she here yesterday?”

“No.”

“Then I can’t say anything about her.”

“You can say this was given to you yesterday.”

“Yes.”

“And that the wall came down yesterday.”

“The driver can say that. Half the lane can.”

“I’d like your leave to have a copy witnessed.”

Cecily looked towards the window. Through it they could see the gap in the wall and the stones placed carefully beside the ditch, where somebody had begun sorting the larger ones from the broken pieces.

“Will they stop paying for that?”

“I don’t know.”

The child abandoned the shoe and came to lean against her knee. She smoothed his hair while she considered.

“You don’t take mine away.”

“I won’t.”

“And I want Margery here when it’s copied. She knows what I was promised.”

Riarhin agreed. Cecily put the paper back in its box, closed the lid, and kept the box on the table between her hands.

***

By evening Riarhin had a witnessed copy, Margery’s account, and a reply from the west office stating that further particulars would be received through the appointed clerk.

Maelis returned from the wool store after dark. Deirdre had shown her the freight claim, the missing account, and the places Lyra had marked for correction. She would answer the summons with her wagoner and the keeper of the store, who could say when she had gone there. She had refused Maelis’s suggestion that she spend the night elsewhere.

“She’s done nothing wrong,” Maelis said. “She insists that ought to help.”

Riarhin sat at the table where her unfinished letter to Sada lay beneath a book. There was room to put the new papers beside it if she moved the lamp. She found herself unwilling to do so.

“What does Fayd say?”

“He says the matter is under examination.”

“Did you speak to him?”

“To his clerk. He came back with the answer in Fayd’s own hand.”

“Nothing else?”

“Nothing about Lyra. He asked that Deirdre attend as directed.”

Riarhin folded Margery’s account into the copy of Cecily’s direction. Mara had a locked chest and a reason to distrust anyone coming for papers. She would take these to her in the morning.

From the courtyard came the wagoner’s voice. He had returned for a coin Maelis owed him, and Na’ar was making him stay long enough to eat. Riarhin heard the scrape of a stool and the man asking whether he might bring his boy in from the cart.

Lyra had once made her stay to finish a bowl of soup before going out into the cold. She could recall the place where Lyra’s thumb had rested on the rim, and not what they had been talking about.

She went downstairs to ask whether the west office accepted food.

Chapter Twenty-Seven — The Repaired Stair

The broth came back cold.

Riarhin had carried it in a covered pot with a cloth wrapped around the handles. The man at the west office accepted the bread, counted the pieces, and returned the pot because the place where Lyra was held had its own provision. When Riarhin asked whether she had eaten, he said he could find out. She waited until he returned to his desk and began another task.

“Will you find out now?”

He looked up. “I have sent the question.”

There was no place to put the pot down except the floor. She held it while he entered the bread in a narrow book. Beside Lyra’s name he wrote the number of pieces and the name Riarhin gave him. Aveline Marr had brought her bread. Riarhin wondered whether Lyra would laugh when she heard that, and whether laughing would be something she could still do there.

“Where is she?”

“In the rooms used for examination.”

“Here?”

“The bread will reach her.”

She took the receipt. Outside, a man with a rolled blanket asked whether they accepted bedding. She told him she had not asked. He went in clutching it against his chest, and she waited to see whether he would bring it out again.

***

Mara cleared a space in the chest herself.

It stood in the bedchamber, where Riarhin had never been while Elyas was alive. There were two hooks beside the door and a pale mark beneath one of them where something had rubbed the plaster. The bed was made. One pillow lay flat beneath the cover and the other had been set against the headboard.

Mara checked the dates before putting the papers away.

“You keep a copy.”

“I have one.”

“Not all in the same house.”

“That is why I’ve brought these.”

Mara’s hands stopped on the lid. “I know. I know you know.”

She lowered it and turned the key.

Riarhin wanted to tell her about Jaennis. The conversation needed time, and she had an appointment with Orrel which had taken three messages and the use of an invitation she was no longer sure would be renewed. She gave Mara the outline, including the admission about the charm. Mara listened standing beside the bed.

“Tell Lyra.”

“I can’t get to her.”

“Put it in a letter.”

“They’ll read it.”

“Then they’ll read it.”

Riarhin looked at her. Mara rubbed her thumb over the key’s head, catching it against a notch in the metal.

“She ought to know,” Mara said.

“I’ll find a way.”

“Today?”

Riarhin had no answer she liked. Mara put the key into her pocket and went ahead of her downstairs.

***

Orrel’s melon had been eaten by a squirrel.

He told her this before he would sit down. The creature had entered the frame through the opening made to keep the plants from becoming too damp and hollowed out the best fruit from the underside. Orrel had spent the morning admiring it before he tried to lift it.

“I thought you ought to know,” he said. “You were interested.”

“I was.”

“Were you?”

She met his eyes.

“Yes. I would have liked to hear whether moving the frames worked.”

“They haven’t been moved yet. My neighbour wanted the washing out first, and now she says it will be wet tomorrow.” He pulled a chair towards the table. “Give me what you have.”

Riarhin laid down the copy she had kept. He read the formal description of the charge, Margery’s account, and the direction copied from Cecily’s original. He began at the beginning again, checking the dates against one another. She watched him doing what she needed somebody to do and tried not to lean over him.

“This requires an answer,” he said.

“Can you get one?”

“I can have the discrepancy entered.”

“It has been entered. I was given an acknowledgment.”

“By the office holding her. I mean before the examiner who must consider it.”

He explained the difference while folding the sheets together. The receiving clerk could add material to a case without obtaining an immediate decision about it; the examiner could direct comparison with an original. Orrel could request that comparison. He could not command the man to perform it in front of them.

“Will they alter the charge?” she asked.

“They may say the description in the summons was wrong.”

“Was it?”

“I haven’t seen the copy they claim she passed.”

“If it has the orchard direction?”

“Then I want to know how it got there.”

“And while you find out?”

He looked towards the door. The room they had been given belonged to a household officer absent for the afternoon; a basket of garments waited beside his desk, each with a little scrap attached. Orrel picked up a scrap which had fallen on the floor and replaced it on the sleeve it belonged to.

“I’ll ask that she receive visitors.”

Riarhin felt how much hope she was prepared to fasten to that lesser offer. She thanked him before he could make it more cautious.

He needed a signature from the officer in the rooms beyond the back passage. A servant led them to the door Riarhin had taken by mistake on her earlier visit. She stopped when she saw where they were going, and Orrel glanced back.

“This time you’re with me.”

The service stair at the far end remained screened. The boards which had barred it were gone. A man knelt at the bottom with a shallow box of tools, shaving a strip from the edge of the lowest tread. He moved aside for two servants carrying covered dishes, then returned to his work.

“Is it open?” Riarhin asked him.

“For feet. Nothing with wheels until the upper rail’s fixed.”

“I thought the work was finished.”

“The treads are finished.” He held up the sliver he had removed. “Mostly.”

A maid came down with an empty basket, put her foot exactly where he did not want it, and received a complaint which sounded well rehearsed between them. Riarhin looked past her. The screen concealed the stair from the public doorway even while people used it; somebody approaching from the far passage would see their feet before their faces.

Orrel called her. She followed him into a small waiting room and sat beneath a shelf of rolled cloth, careful not to catch Bea’s apparent height against its edge.

***

The signature took longer than the conversation.

An officer brought their papers back once to ask whether the orchard owner would attend if called. Riarhin said she would have to ask her. He wanted a promise. Orrel told him to write that the woman retained the sealed original and could be approached at her house. Then they waited again.

In the passage the guard changed. Riarhin heard the incoming man’s boots, the exchange of two ordinary words, and the departing guard asking someone whether the supper dishes had come down. They had not. He said he would take his food after them and went away.

Riarhin began to set the movements beside what she had heard on the earlier evening: trays, passage, the interval before a guard appeared. If supper held a man upstairs, if the returning dishes drew another down—

A servant passed the door carrying two clean trenchers and asked the guard for the time.

“Late enough,” he said.

“She wants the little table in her room.”

“Then take it.”

“The carpenter’s at the stair.”

“He’ll move.”

There was no empty interval. The new guard stayed where he was while supper changed around him. The servants’ movement marked somebody’s appetite, and she had nearly made it into a rule governing a different man.

She leaned back. Her feet ached. She had been holding them in the posture that made Bea’s legs seem longer and had forgotten the strain until it sharpened along her calves.

Orrel noticed her shift and offered to fetch a stool. She said she was all right, then changed her mind. When he returned, she put both feet on it and felt absurdly close to tears.

The officer brought the signed request at last. Comparison with the original correspondence would be heard in five days. A visitor’s request had been forwarded but not granted.

“Five days,” Riarhin said.

“That is the first opening.”

“Open something else.”

The officer began an answer. Orrel touched the paper in Riarhin’s hand.

“Take this one. We can ask for sooner while you have it.”

She let him finish the business. On the way out she thanked the carpenter for moving his box so that she could pass without lifting the hem of her gown. He hardly looked up.

***

She wrote Lyra a letter at Maelis’s table.

The first version sounded like testimony. She tore it across after reading it once, then wrote that she had spoken to Jaennis, who acknowledged using a charm to encourage confidence without asking Lyra’s leave. She put down the words as closely as she remembered them. She did not add that this meant Lyra had done nothing wrong. Lyra could decide what to make of it when she had the truth.

Below it she wrote about the melon.

She almost removed that part. It seemed cruel to carry a small, amusing disappointment into the place where Lyra waited to learn whether she would be released. Then she remembered her making jokes about the seamstress’s customers while refusing to give Riarhin their names. Lyra had known other people were in danger that morning too.

Riarhin kept the melon.

Na’ar came to the door while she was drying the ink. He had a little packet under one arm.

“What is that?”

“Maelis’s good scarf. She left it.”

“Where?”

“Under a man’s chair. She says fetching it is work she hired me for.”

“Did he object?”

“He asked if she’d worn it when she came in.”

Riarhin laughed before she had considered whether she wanted to. Na’ar grinned at her, pleased with the effect, and then saw the address on the folded letter.

“I can take it in the morning.”

“I’ll take it.”

“You need sleep.”

“So do you.”

“Yes. She pays me badly for it.”

He set a plate beside her elbow. There was bread and a piece of the meat she had smelled cooking while she wrote. He left without waiting to see whether she ate.

Riarhin turned the plate so that it would not catch the edge of the letter. She took a mouthful and discovered how hungry she was.

Chapter Twenty-Eight — The Army Ready

Cecily stood in her orchard to watch the horses pass.

She had hung the queen’s direction in a little wooden frame beneath the shelter of her doorway. Her son wanted to hold it up for the riders, but she told him the men had orders of their own and needed to watch where they were going. The gap in the wall had been left open until the inspection was finished. Beyond it, sorted stone lay under a cloth.

Riarhin passed her with Margery. They had exchanged greetings at the palace door with the care of people uncertain how much of an earlier conversation remained between them. Now Margery stopped to ask whether the promised payment had arrived.

“Half,” Cecily said. “The rest when they’ve done.”

“Is that enough to have the work started?”

“Yes. The mason came yesterday. He says it will stand longer than the old one.”

She sounded proud despite herself. A horse snorted close to the fence, and her boy leaned towards it until she caught his collar.

Riarhin had brought the paper against Lyra into this house. The payment had come anyway. She was glad of that, and gladder than she wished to be that she could tell herself they had not yet hurt Cecily for helping.

On the strip beside the orchard the companies advanced two abreast, keeping enough distance that a startled animal could be checked without carrying the one behind it into the fence. Their saddles differed in age and repair. The horses were fed. A man with a bandaged wrist rode at the rear of a group, his rein looped through the sound hand, and the officer beside him spoke without making him turn his head.

Margery watched them a moment before taking Riarhin’s arm.

“We’ll be late.”

“For what?”

“The part where they tell us how early everyone else arrived.”

***

A household retainer had stopped the water cart.

Beyond him the inspection ground opened under a pale sky, all the disorder of preparation held just outside the places where guests would stand. Boys carried stools beneath their arms. A groom sat on an overturned bucket stitching a buckle into a strap while the horse it belonged to cropped the grass within reach. From the far end came the rising dust of a company turning together, boots disturbing soil which the morning’s damp had held until so many feet arrived.

The awnings looked splendid from here. Up close Riarhin could see the extra ropes and the men assigned to keep people from tripping over them. A woman had brought her daughter to watch a brother ride past; the girl stood on a box behind the nearest pavilion, angry whenever someone crossed in front of her. Riarhin moved aside before the child had to ask.

His master’s pavilion stood on the edge of the viewing ground, with two fine horses tied behind it and nobody yet seated under the awning. The retainer wanted the cart moved because it obscured the approach. A young officer refused, explaining that it was placed beside the drinking trough according to the arrangement he had been given.

“Then move the trough as well.”

“It weighs more than the cart when it’s full.”

“Empty it.”

The carter stood with his hands tucked into his belt. Behind him, a woman waited with three cups on a board. Riarhin could see from the set of her mouth that she had already been made to put off something of her own.

The officer looked towards the pavilion. “Whose ground is this?”

“My lord’s place has been there since the stakes went in.”

“Whose ground?”

“The queen’s.”

“Then the order placing the water stands.”

The retainer reached for the bridle of the cart horse. Riarhin released Margery’s arm before she had quite decided to move, but the carter stepped between the hand and his horse’s head.

“Don’t turn her across the shaft. You’ll lame her.”

The young officer put two fingers on the retainer’s wrist. His voice dropped. Riarhin could no longer hear all the words, only before your lord comes and ask him yourself. The man pulled free, straightened his sleeve, and went into the pavilion.

The carter rubbed the mare’s neck. The woman with the cups asked whether she could cross now. The officer moved out of her way at once.

Margery drew Riarhin onwards.

“He’s learned a little since last year.”

“You know him?”

“He was at the academy with my nephew. The boy used to apologise to chairs when he struck his knee.”

“He may still.”

“I hope so. Chairs have very little means of redress.”

They found their places.

The officer from the water cart passed their chairs carrying a folded plan. Margery called him over to ask after her nephew. He answered with a politeness which began to wear thin when she asked whether the young man was still borrowing money.

“He has bought a good horse,” he said.

“So everyone keeps telling me.”

“It was worth buying.”

“And was lending him the money worth doing?”

He looked at her. Margery laughed and let him escape, but Tylan intercepted him before he had gone ten paces. The marshal had come out from behind the pavilion with the retainer’s master beside him. Riarhin could hear them now; the breeze had dropped and the ground between the chairs was still empty.

The older lord wanted the officer corrected for laying a hand on his man. He described the movement as though it had been a blow. The officer waited until Tylan asked him, then said exactly where he had touched the wrist and why.

Tylan called the carter over.

It took some time. The man would not leave the mare until someone had promised to hold her, and the boy sent for him tried to hurry the explanation until Tylan raised a hand and let him finish. Riarhin watched the older lord’s irritation deepen while a man with mud on his boots was given the same space to speak.

“Was your horse in danger?” Tylan asked.

“The shaft was. She’d have followed it into the trough if he pulled her round.”

“Had you told him?”

“I’d just begun.”

Tylan thanked him and sent him back. He turned to the lord.

“The water stays. Your man will leave the teams to their drivers.”

“And the officer?”

“Will apologise for the touch.”

The young officer’s face changed. He opened his mouth, stopped, then bowed to the retainer and gave the apology in a voice controlled with some effort.

Riarhin thought the business finished. Tylan put his hand on the officer’s shoulder and kept him there.

“You’ll be apologised to as well. You were carrying out the arrangement I approved.”

The lord stared at him.

“Here?”

“Your complaint was here.”

For a moment Riarhin thought he would refuse. He looked towards Veralyn’s place beneath the awning, still empty, then at the women watching from the chairs. He gave his man a short instruction. The retainer apologised without lifting his eyes.

Tylan dismissed the officer. He kept the lord beside him as they walked away, speaking more quietly now. Riarhin caught only the beginning: that the man’s people were being asked to serve alongside others, that he could not have each company bring a different rule for whose hand might stop a frightened horse.

Margery leaned towards her.

“He’ll remember that.”

“The lord?”

“Both of them.”

Riarhin watched the officer rejoin his company. He was standing straighter than before, though the apology had plainly hurt him.

She remembered Elyas’s account of the horses returned to a village, and the road that was expected in exchange. The retainer’s master arrived before the water cart had moved and crossed to speak with the officer. After a brief exchange he took a cup from the waiting woman, drank, and went to his pavilion. Riarhin watched the carter loosen his shoulders.

***

The queen inspected a saddle with a split girth.

It had been brought forward by a soldier whose captain wanted him punished for letting the leather fail. The soldier said he had reported the weakness two days before, been told it would serve until the inspection, and borrowed another strap because he did not intend to find out what happened when it broke beneath him. He produced the old piece from his saddlebag.

Tylan took it from him. He bent the leather, exposing the pale line at the split, and asked the captain who had examined it. The captain named a man. Tylan sent for him.

Riarhin stood with the women just outside the space cleared around Veralyn. Matylde had been kept at the palace; there was no child to redirect the queen’s attention, no toy horse to retrieve. The green amulet lay against black cloth. When Veralyn turned, the brass frame caught the light and the glass darkened almost to the colour of the gown.

The man who had examined the girth came at a trot. He admitted seeing it. He had promised a replacement and forgotten which saddle needed one after being called away to count newly arrived horses.

“Then you will finish the count with someone who remembers why you’re counting,” Tylan said. “And you will examine the rest before they leave the ground.”

The captain tried to explain that the soldier had spoken insolently.

“Did he ride on the bad strap?” Veralyn asked.

“No, Your Grace.”

“Did he steal the one he borrowed?”

“No.”

“Then leave him enough credit for wishing to stay on his horse.”

There was laughter, checked quickly. The soldier’s face flushed. Veralyn asked how long he had served and whether he intended to remain.

“Another term, if they’ll have me.”

“Will they?” she asked the captain.

“Yes, Your Grace.”

The soldier had a sister apprenticed to a cloth cutter. He sent part of his pay to her master and had nearly finished the sum agreed for her lodging. Another term would allow her to take a place without being indebted to the man for everything she wore and ate. He explained this awkwardly, as though the detail might make his ambition look smaller than the queen expected.

“Bring her work to the household when she’s ready,” Veralyn said. “Let it be judged with the others.”

Riarhin watched him leave. The failed strap hung from Tylan’s hand until an attendant took it away. She had seen captains who made the complaint more dangerous than the thing complained of. Here the man went back to his horse with his place secure and his sister’s future a little less remote.

For a moment the morning was easy to stand in. She could hear the pleasure in the women around her, the relief of an officer whose company had passed well, the steady clink of metal as men led their mounts off the ground. She wanted to let those things tell her that she had mistaken the larger danger. It was an almost bodily wish, like the desire to sit after walking too far.

Margery touched her sleeve.

“She’s asking you.”

Veralyn had turned towards them.

“You have seen a few armies, Aveline. What do you make of this one?”

Riarhin brought her attention back to the face she was wearing.

“They expect to be fed tomorrow.”

“And is that praise?”

“Yes.”

Tylan smiled. The queen seemed pleased with the answer.

***

They ate under a long awning open on two sides.

The wind reached the cups but could not lift the cloth, which had been fastened beneath the table with little wooden pegs. Riarhin sat opposite an older woman discussing the cost of sending her son to Valora. He had written for money after being paid, which she considered a warning sign in a man intended to command others. Margery asked what he had spent it on. The woman said a horse, then admitted that it had been a good horse at an unusually good price.

At the far end of the table two officers unrolled a plan for Tylan. Riarhin could see the marked river bends but not read the small names beside them. One man was talking about Acasian crossings and the difficulty of moving fodder after the local grass had been eaten down. Tylan asked how much had already been purchased. The answer was more than the man beside him expected.

Veralyn listened while taking bread from the dish. An envoy seated near her began speaking of Thepyrgos, of demands for guarantees and the conduct of ships. The queen told him that the harbour question would receive a separate answer. He persisted until she looked directly at him.

“I have not asked the men at those crossings to decide it.”

He bowed his head. The conversation broke into several smaller ones, leaving the plan open beneath Tylan’s hand.

Riarhin waited until the officer moved away.

“Are there not people to be spoken to before those men march?” she asked.

Tylan turned to her. “There are people being spoken to now.”

“And if they don’t agree?”

“Then our men will have what they need.”

She remembered Berent describing his crossing: the orchard, the men cut off beyond it, the need to get them out before the next riders came. Tylan had spent years hearing such accounts with names attached. He could hardly need her to remind him that preparation would not keep all of them alive.

“It looks a great deal to set in motion.”

“It takes longer to prepare men badly,” he said. “You keep having to fetch them back.”

Veralyn laughed softly. Her hand rested for a moment against his wrist, the unconsidered touch Riarhin remembered from the earlier visit. He turned the plan so that she could see the part nearest him.

The envoy tried another objection. A promised delivery had been delayed for want of wagons, he said, and the assurance given to one office did not mean it would reach the men who needed it. Before he had finished, an attendant approached with a message for Tylan. The wagons had arrived that morning. The officer who read the message aloud sounded delighted by the interruption.

“Then we shall spare you that worry,” Veralyn said.

The envoy smiled because there seemed nothing else to do. Riarhin looked at the queen’s hand, still resting near the edge of the plan. A little crust clung to her thumb. She brushed it away.

***

Riarhin left with Margery before the last companies departed.

At the orchard Cecily’s boy was asleep on a folded coat. The mason had come to measure the open gap, standing back whenever a horse passed. Cecily said he could wait until morning if he was frightened of being trodden on. He answered that he was more frightened of her asking why he had not begun.

Margery stopped to laugh with them. Riarhin stayed on the path, looking at the new shoes on the passing horses, the straps checked, the men who expected supper and would receive it.

“Was that why you came today?” Margery asked when they walked on. “To see where they would be sent?”

“I wanted to hear the queen speak.”

“About Lyra?”

“That too.”

“You didn’t ask.”

“No.”

The chance had existed in some form. She could have interrupted over the bread or while Veralyn examined the girth. She had wanted the evidence properly compared first. She had wanted an answer which could not be dismissed before all those people. Both reasons still seemed sound, and neither explained why she had felt so grateful when the wagons arrived.

Margery kept her arm where it was. Riarhin let herself be walked back to the gate.

At her lodging Na’ar gave her a receipt for the letter. The office had accepted it, opened.

“Did you see her?”

“No.”

She unfolded the receipt. Beneath the entry for the letter somebody had added a request from the prisoner for a comb and a woollen wrap, without pins.

Riarhin went upstairs and opened her chest.

Chapter Twenty-Nine — Mara’s Answer

Dawn reached Maelis’s house without reaching the lower room.

Riarhin could see brightness at the top of the window while the table beneath it remained grey. The night’s air had settled close to the floor; when she put her feet down, the cold found every small place where the skin had worn thin inside her shoes. She stayed sitting on the bed until the sounds above told her Maelis was awake. Somewhere in the street a woman argued that a delivery ought to have come yesterday, and a man answered that yesterday was no longer available.

Riarhin would have liked to answer several people that way. She lit the lamp instead and opened her chest.

The comb was bone, with a tooth missing near one end.

Riarhin found it wrapped in a scarf she seldom wore. She had another, newer one, but Lyra had borrowed this one years before and complained about the broken tooth while continuing to use it. A thing Lyra might recognise seemed worth more than its small defect. Riarhin cleaned between the remaining teeth with a thread and set it beside the woollen wrap.

Maelis watched from the doorway.

“They may keep it.”

“Then she’ll ask for another.”

“I meant because it’s sharp enough to hurt someone.”

“Everything is, if you work at it.”

“That is an excellent sentence to leave out of your letter.”

Riarhin wound the thread around her fingers. She had slept badly, waking whenever the face in her dream began to change. There was no need to hold it while she slept alone, yet she kept finding herself trying, as though abandoning the task for a few hours would make it impossible to resume.

“Come here,” Maelis said.

“I have somewhere to go.”

“Yes, and you intend to go looking like a woman whose mouth belongs to someone else.”

She closed the door. Riarhin let the glamour go while Maelis moved the lamp and made her turn towards the window. The relief was immediate enough to embarrass her.

“How long have you been keeping it up at night?”

“Only until I’m alone.”

“Be alone sooner.”

Maelis touched her chin, tilting it by a finger’s breadth. Riarhin resisted, then allowed the movement. The woman smelled faintly of the scent she wore for evenings and the sourer preparation she used at the worktable. She was frowning with real attention.

“Now think of your mother’s mouth before she answered a question she didn’t like.”

“I don’t want to wear that all day.”

“Neither did she, presumably. I’m asking you to remember movement.”

The mouth settled. Riarhin felt her own breath loosen with it.

Maelis sat down uninvited and reached for the wrap. It was thick, plain wool, long enough to cover shoulders and arms.

“No pins,” Riarhin said.

“I can read.” Maelis tested the edge between two fingers. “This catches. There’s a rough place in the binding.”

“I can mend it.”

“Go where you need to go. I’ll mend it. Na’ar will take it when it’s done.”

Riarhin hesitated. Maelis held out a hand for the comb, as impatiently as if she were being paid to prepare someone else’s child for a journey.

“Go.”

***

Mara was writing when Riarhin arrived.

She had put the table near the window and laid three sheets beside a dish of ink. Nelle was beating dust from a rug in the yard. Each blow carried through the open shutter, and Mara stopped writing whenever it fell, waiting for the little vibration in the table to subside.

Riarhin closed the shutter.

“I’d rather have the light,” Mara said.

She opened it again.

“I can ask her to stop.”
