“She needs it dry before evening. Sit down.”

Riarhin sat opposite her. The sheets contained a fair copy of Mara’s petition about Elyas: the dates of his arrest and examination, the request for the depositions on which his sentence rested, the names of people willing to say what he had asked them to do. One paragraph had been crossed through. A fresh version beside it asked that the refusal to provide those depositions be given in writing.

“Who told you to take that out?”

“A man who came yesterday. He said the queen had already heard what she needed.”

“What did he offer?”

Mara looked up.

“He had to offer something,” Riarhin said.

“He said I needn’t keep answering questions about the people who came here. If I withdrew the petition, the household could be left to recover in peace.”

“Did he put that in writing?”

“No. I asked.”

Riarhin took the unused sheet and turned it straight. She wanted to put a plan on it, something with a next step and a person to approach, before Mara asked what she had accomplished since they last spoke.

“The comparison will be heard in three days,” she said. “Orrel has asked for sooner. I saw the queen yesterday.”

“Did you speak to her?”

“Yes.”

“About Elyas?”

“The army.”

Mara laid down her pen.

Riarhin told her about the inspection. She heard herself giving the wrong details: the strap, the carter, the young officer who knew whose order governed the ground. They were things Mara should understand because they made the queen’s support more than fear, but in this room they sounded like excuses for an enjoyable afternoon.

“You had dinner with her,” Mara said.

“With several dozen others.”

“Was it good?”

“Mara.”

“Was it?”

“Yes.”

The rug beating stopped. Outside, Nelle called for the boy to help her lift it off the line.

“I needed to be there,” Riarhin said.

“You wanted to be there.”

“Some of the time.”

“Which part did you need?”

“You asked me to find out what was happening.”

“I asked you to help me make her answer for killing him.”

“And if I walk up to her with a knife, will that help?”

Mara drew back. Riarhin saw the wound before she had finished being angry enough to want it.

“Is that what you think I’ve asked?”

“No.”

“You make me stupid, and then you can keep being careful.”

“I don’t think you’re stupid.”

“Then answer what I said.”

Riarhin looked at the crossed-out paragraph. She had advised Mara to preserve the evidence and avoid giving anyone a fresh reason to act against her. Mara had done the preserving and gone on asking the question. Riarhin had eaten pears with the people who could refuse to answer it. She had also found out what happened to Lyra. Both things occupied the same days, and she wanted Mara to sort them more kindly than she could herself.

“I wanted to stay,” she said. “Yesterday. There was a moment when it seemed easier to believe they knew what they were doing.”

“Perhaps they do.”

Riarhin met her eyes.

“Perhaps she knows exactly what she’s doing,” Mara said. “Does that make it better?”

“No.”

“I don’t need you to dislike every room she goes into. I need you to remember what room I’m in.”

Riarhin reached across the table, then stopped short of touching her.

“I remember.”

“Then help me finish this.”

***

They worked until Nelle brought food.

Mara would not withdraw the petition. Riarhin stopped asking her to consider it and helped distinguish what she could attest from what she had been told. The names of those willing to speak went on a separate sheet, to be produced only when a hearing was granted; she would not send a list of frightened people into an office merely to be told that it had been received.

They made another copy of Cecily’s direction. Mara kept the witnessed one in the chest and put the plain copy with her petition, explaining in a short note that it concerned a second prisoner whose evidence also required examination. Riarhin objected to linking the cases. Mara listened to the objection, considered it, and kept the note separate so that she could decide when to use it.

Berent came through the yard while they were eating.

A man had followed him from the market, he said. He recognised him from the lane near his lodging. The man bought an apple, stopped when Berent stopped, and became interested in a harness-maker’s window when Berent turned to look at him.

“Where is he now?” Riarhin asked.

“Outside, I expect.”

“You brought him here?”

“He has seen me come here before. I wasn’t going to show him how hard I could work to conceal it.”

He took the seat Nelle offered. His confidence irritated Riarhin because it had an argument behind it. She made him eat before she asked how he would send word if he needed them. They agreed on the public yard of a baker whose gate opened onto two streets. Mara already went there for bread. If she failed to arrive by the end of the following afternoon, Berent would come to the house while Riarhin waited elsewhere to hear what he found.

“If I’m merely delayed?” Mara asked.

“Send Nelle.”

“I sometimes go places without a retinue.”

“Then send the baker’s boy. Pay him properly.”

Mara almost smiled. “You should try giving orders without sounding as though the rest of us have recently learned to walk.”

Berent coughed into his cup. Riarhin kicked his boot under the table, gently enough to let him laugh.

The moment passed. They finished arranging where a message could be left, what the message should say, and how long to wait before assuming that silence meant something worse than inconvenience.

***

Na’ar brought news before Riarhin left.

The wrap and comb had been accepted. He had waited until a woman who carried things into the examination rooms returned, and asked her whether Lyra had received them. The woman would say only that she had given the prisoner what was permitted. Then, after he had started to leave, she called him back and handed him a scrap.

Riarhin unfolded it beside the window.

The tooth is still missing. Tell Orrel the squirrel has excellent judgement.

Beneath it Lyra had written that the room was cold in the morning and warm after the kitchen fires had been lit. She wanted clean linen if it could be sent. Her last line was smaller.

I would like to see somebody I know.

Riarhin read the whole scrap aloud. Mara held out her hand and read it again for herself.

“She got your letter.”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

Mara went to the cupboard where she kept the household linen and began choosing pieces without waiting for a request. Riarhin watched her hold two shirts together to judge the fit. One had a repair at the shoulder. She put it back and took the other.

“I’ll go again tomorrow,” Riarhin said.

“To Lyra?”

“Yes.”

“Tell her I’ll come too, when they allow it.”

Riarhin folded the scrap along its existing crease. The paper had been held tightly enough for the edge to soften. She put it inside her sleeve, where she could feel it when she moved.

Chapter Thirty — The Protection Offered

The baker sold the last of the little seed loaves while Riarhin waited.

Each batch changed the yard. People who had stood patiently against the wall pressed forward when the door opened, then dispersed with their purchases held inside their coats. Warm air followed the bread out of the bakehouse and was gone almost at once. A dusting of flour marked the threshold; shoes had carried it halfway across the flags, where yesterday’s rain had turned it into little white rims around the puddles.

Riarhin chose a place which left the gateway clear. It was also the place from which she could see both streets. She noticed the coincidence and stayed there.

She had bought one when she arrived because it gave her a reason to stand in the yard, then a second because Mara liked them and might not have eaten. The second lay in her basket with the linen the west office had accepted in part and returned in part, deciding that a lacing was too long. Riarhin had offered to cut it. The clerk told her the woman who received the clothing had finished for the day.

Now the shadows reached the far wall of the yard. Berent had gone to the manor. Nelle had not come. A boy scraped dough from a board and rolled the scraps into a ball, making a little animal with four clumsy legs. Riarhin watched him flatten its head into something he liked better.

She wanted to leave. There were several ways to reach Mara’s house quickly, and waiting beside a baker’s wall felt like obeying a plan made by people who did not know how much time they were wasting. She had made the plan herself. Berent would return here because she had told him she would remain.

The boy held up the animal.

“Horse,” he said.

“It needs ears.”

He pinched two into being.

Berent came through the gate before the next batch came out of the oven. Riarhin knew from the way he looked for her that Mara had not simply gone somewhere else.

“She was taken this morning.”

“By whom?”

“Men from the Mound. Nelle saw them. There was a woman with them who helped her pack.”

Riarhin set the basket down before she could drop it.

***

Nelle had kept the pot warm until the bottom burned.

The smell reached Riarhin as soon as she entered the kitchen. Nelle stood beside the hearth with a damp cloth in her hand, making no effort to lift the pot away. She kept beginning with the same thing: they had spoken civilly; they had said there was no cause for alarm; Mara would be provided for.

“What did Mara say?” Riarhin asked.

“That she was provided for here.”

“Then?”

“They said the house couldn’t be kept secure while people were coming to answer questions about the petition.”

Berent drew a chair back for her. Nelle ignored it. Riarhin took the cloth from her hand and lifted the pot onto the hearthstone. The cloth was too wet; heat came through at once, biting her palm. She put it down without looking at the skin.

“Did they show an order?”

“Yes. I read it.”

Nelle went to the dresser and took a folded paper from beneath a dish. The direction placed Mara in protected residence while matters connected with her petition were examined. It named the Mound’s Valoran household as responsible for her safety. At the bottom, in a different hand, somebody had entered the date and the number of men in the escort.

“Was she allowed to refuse?”

“She did refuse.”

Riarhin waited.

“One of them said she needn’t go in her walking shoes. There was time to fetch others.”

Nelle sat then. She looked at her own hands as though they belonged to the woman who had carried the shoes down.

“What did she take?”

“A change of clothes. Her comb. The little book. She wouldn’t take a jewel. I tried to put her mother’s pin with the clothes and she gave it back.”

“Did she leave a message?”

“She said to tell you she was going where they sent her. She said I wasn’t to follow.”

“Anything else?”

Nelle shook her head. Then she remembered the chest.

“They asked her to open it.”

Riarhin felt the room narrow around the space where that sentence ended.

“Did she?”

“Yes. She took out two papers herself and gave them over. They looked at the rest.”

“The direction about the orchard?”

“I don’t know which ones. They were folded.”

Riarhin went upstairs with Nelle’s permission and found the chest closed but unlocked. Mara’s petition was gone. So was the note concerning Lyra. The witnessed copy of Cecily’s direction remained beneath the spare household cloth, with Margery’s account inside it. They had looked without taking everything, or Mara had shown them enough to make the rest seem unimportant. Riarhin could not know which.

She took the papers. On the bed the impression of a small case remained in the coverlet, the cloth darker where it had been pressed flat.

Downstairs she told Nelle to go to someone she trusted for the night.

“This house needs looking after.”

“It will stand until morning.”

“And when she comes back?”

Riarhin had an assurance ready. She swallowed it and asked where Nelle could be found.

***

They did not go straight to the estate.

Riarhin disliked every other thing they did first. Berent took Nelle’s account down while it was fresh and kept the paper which named the residence. She went to Maelis, who read it, stopped midway through an objection to being disturbed, and sent Na’ar to find the man who carried provisions to the Mound’s household.

He returned with the man’s sister. The carrier was still at work; she kept his accounts and wanted to know whether a delivery had been refused.

“No,” Maelis said. “We need to know if he took household goods there today. For a woman newly arrived.”

“If you want someone’s things fetched, tell him before he loads.”

“Has he done it already?”

The sister opened a folded list. There was a charge for a box and a covered basket from Mara’s house to the estate, added after the morning load. The address and the name of the person who had received them were written underneath. She had the list because the extra load belonged in a separate account and her brother would otherwise try to charge the household twice for the same journey.

Riarhin asked when he would return.

“Before dark if they don’t keep him waiting again.”

“What kept him?”

“Yesterday? A horse in the loading place. Today I don’t know.”

She closed the list. Maelis paid for the inconvenience of coming and said she would speak to the carrier later if she needed work done. The woman left with an entirely ordinary reason to think them troublesome.

Riarhin stood at the window. The estate she remembered had families in its public rooms, a boy writing to his father, Garin explaining the cattle returned to a woman who had lost them. Mara’s box had passed through a yard where people carried sacks and argued about horses. There would be somewhere to put it. There would be a room.

“Will they let me visit?” she asked.

Maelis leaned back in her chair. “You are asking the wrong woman.”

“Can you get me asked?”

“I can ask.”

“Today.”

Maelis looked at Na’ar. He had remained by the door, listening, with his arms folded loosely over his chest.

“Today,” she agreed.

***

The answer was no.

It came through the servant who ordinarily brought invitations to Maelis, with an apology for not knowing when the restriction might be lifted. Mara had arrived safely. Visits would be arranged when the necessary inquiries were complete.

Riarhin read the message twice, then put it beside the direction Nelle had kept. She had spent the afternoon collecting things which agreed with one another and made none of what they described acceptable.

Berent arrived after sunset. He had gone as far as the road beside the estate, keeping to the public way, and seen more horses than he remembered from the earlier account. He had not tried the gate. A man had come out to watch him pass.

“You weren’t stopped?”

“No.”

“Don’t go again.”

“I can ride a road.”

“You can also teach them which rider to expect.”

He accepted that reluctantly. Na’ar moved the lamp so all three could see the paper.

“What do you want to know?” he asked.

“Where they’ve put her. Who can open it. Whether they’re keeping her there.”

“Those are three questions.”

“I can count.”

“Then don’t send one man to answer all of them.”

Riarhin sat down. The burn on her palm had begun to throb. She put the hand under the table, where nobody would mistake it for an injury deserving attention.

Maelis brought a basin anyway.

“Put it in.”

“It’s nothing.”

“Then it will cost you very little to indulge me.”

The cool water hurt and eased the pain together. Riarhin let her hand rest in it while they divided the questions among the things each of them could plausibly learn.

When she looked up, Maelis was studying her face.

“You’ll need me before you go near the queen.”

“Can you get me near her?”

“I have been thinking.”

“Quickly?”

“Very unkind thoughts. They often come quickly.”

Riarhin almost laughed. The impulse caught in her throat, and she bent over the basin until it passed.

Chapter Thirty-One — The Last Invitation

Maelis arrived downstairs carrying two gowns.

“This one,” she said, laying the darker across the table. “The other makes you look as though you’ve dressed to be remembered.”

“I want an audience.”

“You have one. Late afternoon, after the household petitions. You are to speak to the queen and the marshal about Mara’s comfort.”

Riarhin pushed the chair back.

“Her comfort?”

“That is what I asked about. It was a question to which someone could say yes.”

“And what did you give for it?”

“A promise to have you there on time and to see that you understood what the invitation permitted.”

Maelis placed a pair of gloves on the gown. One had a loose stitch at the wrist. Riarhin picked it up and turned it inside out.

“You didn’t promise I would keep to it.”

“I have met you.”

Na’ar came in behind her with a basket of things she had apparently ordered him to fetch. He set it on the bench and began removing them: thread, a small jar, a packet wrapped against the damp. At the bottom lay a loaf and two onions.

“The onions were mine,” he said when Maelis looked at them.

“We have onions.”

“Those were yours.”

She let him keep them.

Riarhin sat with the glove on her lap. The afternoon now had an edge she could see. She could reach Veralyn while Mara remained close enough to be brought home before night; she could show the papers, name the false charge, ask Tylan to remember the house where he had once been welcomed. She had been building such a meeting in her mind for days, each new piece of evidence making it more exact. None of those imagined meetings included the queen refusing to hear what was plainly there.

“Where is she held?” she asked Na’ar.

“Newer wing. Upper room facing the yard, according to the man who took the box in. He carried it to the stair and a household servant took it from him.”

“So he didn’t see her.”

“No.”

“How does he know which room?”

“He was told to leave the basket below the room with the blue coverlet airing at the window. Same stair serves three doors, he thinks.”

Riarhin put the glove down. A wing, a stair, three doors. She knew less than the shape of the answer made her feel she knew.

“He thinks?”

“He doesn’t count doors for a living.”

“Did you ask anything else?”

“I asked what he wanted for carrying a heavy chest. Then I paid him for his time and left.”

She nodded.

***

Deirdre came before noon.

Her summons had ended in an instruction to remain available. The officer who questioned her had shown her a copied sheet which she had never seen before. She told him so. He asked whether Lyra had enclosed it with the freight claim. She said she had opened every fold of that claim herself and brought the whole of it back for him to examine.

“He wanted me to remember another paper,” she said. “I told him I’d remember a different one if he showed me one I’d ever handled.”

Maelis poured her a drink.

“They let you leave?”

“After making me wait long enough to miss a buyer. I gave them the storekeeper’s name and told them where I’d be. My bond has been paid. My accounts are there to be seen. I am not going to spend the rest of the season sitting beside them.”

She had come to ask Maelis to stop involving her in conversations with people who thought a Theonan relation meant she could arrange an island’s obedience. Riarhin listened while Maelis promised only to make the introductions Deirdre permitted. Then Deirdre noticed the two gowns and the papers spread beside them.

“What have you done now?”

Maelis looked at Riarhin.

“Mara has been taken to the Mound’s estate,” Riarhin said. “I am going to ask for her release.”

“And if they say no?”

“I may need to leave the city with her.”

Deirdre set down the cup without drinking.

“How many?”

The question frightened Riarhin more than a refusal. She had not yet made the count in front of a person who could act on it.

“Four. Possibly five.”

“Including Maelis?”

Riarhin glanced at her.

“Possibly,” Maelis said.

“With horses?”

“If we can.”

“If you bring five riders to my store while men are asking about me, you won’t get as far as the second gate.” Deirdre pushed the gowns aside and rested her forearms on the cleared wood. “I have a carter taking empty packing cases south tomorrow. He lodges tonight at his sister’s place, outside the city. I can send something there which gives you a reason to call. I can’t promise he will carry people he hasn’t agreed to carry.”

“Will you ask him?”

“What will you tell him?”

Riarhin thought about how many truths a carter needed before the weight in his wagon became a choice he had made.

“That the household has prevented Mara from leaving, and we’ve helped her do it.”

“Good. Tell him that before he puts you under anything.”

Maelis asked about passage. Deirdre’s face closed further.

“There are boats. There are people who can ask other people. There is a very great deal of road before either helps you.”

“And at the other end?”

“My sister’s household can receive somebody I send. Until they know who is being sent, they haven’t agreed.”

“Theona,” Maelis said to Riarhin.

Deirdre gave her a sharp look. “My sister. I can’t offer you Theona.”

She described a landing claim still disputed with cousins who had brought a royal officer into it. Her sister’s household used the shore under an arrangement heard locally, and the cousins wanted the crown’s word to displace that hearing. A fugitive prince’s widow was the sort of guest who could cost them the right to put a boat on their own beach.

“What would they require?” Riarhin asked.

“To know she’s coming. No armed following. No letters sent in their name, and no asking their neighbours to take sides while eating at their table.”

“I can agree to that.”

“You can agree for yourself. Ask the widow.”

Deirdre drew the nearer sheet towards her and asked for the carter’s payment in advance. Riarhin counted money onto the table.

***

Nelle found them while Deirdre was leaving.

She had gone back to the manor in the morning to collect more clothes and found a servant waiting with a message. Mara wanted the heavier mantle sent before evening. She would be leaving with part of the household at first light and did not know when another parcel could reach her.

Riarhin read the note. The hand was Mara’s. Beneath the request for the mantle she had added that Nelle was to keep enough money for her own wages and those of the boy. Somebody had underlined the time by which the parcel must arrive.

“Where are they taking her?”

“The servant wouldn’t say. I asked twice.”

“Did he know?”

“He said to send the mantle.”

Riarhin gave the note to Maelis. The votary read it, put it down, and returned to the gown she had chosen.

“You still have this afternoon.”

“Will the queen know?”

“Ask her.”

Riarhin walked to the window. A man was rolling a barrel down the street with the care it required at each uneven stone. She watched him reach the corner, adjust his grip, and vanish beyond it. Outside the city there were roads she knew and roads she had never travelled; putting Mara on one of them would turn a house with three possible doors into a search conducted behind an army.

Berent had not returned. Na’ar went to find him at the baker’s yard, and Riarhin sent the note back with Nelle so that the mantle could be delivered without giving anyone cause to think their message had alarmed more people than intended.

She took out Lyra’s scrap.

The missing tooth. The squirrel. Somebody she knew.

“We need someone at tomorrow’s comparison,” she said.

“Orrel will be there,” Maelis answered.

“Even if I can’t.”

“Ask him.”

“And if they move her too?”

Maelis’s hands stopped over the gown.

“I haven’t heard that they will.”

“Neither have I.”

Riarhin looked at the table where Deirdre had counted the passengers. She had said four, possibly five, and Lyra had not been among them. The omission was already in the practical work of the day, made before she had faced it plainly enough to call it a choice.

She could go to the west office again. She knew the desk, the receiving clerk, the door through which the woman carried parcels. She did not know which room held Lyra or how many locked doors lay between it and the street. She knew more about the place where Mara had been taken. That was true. It was also useful to her, and she disliked how quickly she reached for it.

“Write to Orrel,” Maelis said. “While there is time to send it.”

Riarhin sat. She asked him to attend the comparison with the copy already in his keeping, whether or not Aveline Marr could be present. She asked him to press the request for a visit and to send any answer to the address Lyra had given for her own correspondence, where someone would have to take responsibility for keeping it safe. Then she struck out the last instruction; that room had already been searched. She put Maelis’s address instead and arranged with the landlady to hold anything arriving for them.

It was a small thing to leave in place. She could feel the temptation to call it enough.

She sealed it before she could.

***

Na’ar came back with Berent while Maelis was finishing her face.

Riarhin heard them below. She wanted to go down at once, but Maelis kept her seated and made her finish a sentence about the garden without moving her mother’s mouth too carefully.

“You’re holding every word before you let it out. She had known how to speak for rather longer than that.”

“So have I.”

“Then show me.”

Riarhin tried again. The sentence came easily this time, and with it the memory of Margery changing her shoes. She let that remain while Maelis turned her towards the light.

“Better.”

“Will it hold?”

“You are asking whether you can keep attending. I can’t answer for you.”

Maelis stood back. In the mirror Bea looked composed, a woman prepared to ask a favour and make the asking pleasant. Riarhin had seen her mother look that way with a tooth aching badly enough to keep her awake. She remembered being angry because Bea had smiled at a man after sending her daughter away from the bedside.

She touched the loose stitch in the glove. Maelis had mended it while she spoke to Deirdre.

Downstairs, Berent wanted to come to the palace.

“No,” Riarhin said. “They are already watching you.”

“All the more reason not to leave me standing somewhere with nothing to do.”

“You have something. Wait for me here. If I haven’t returned by dark, go to the place Deirdre named. Don’t come looking for me at the gate.”

He disliked it. She let him say so before repeating the instruction.

Na’ar offered to walk with her. Maelis required him for an errand first; she said it in the tone of a woman reminding a hired guard who paid him, and Riarhin saw the amusement at the edge of his mouth before he turned away. There were still things between those two which belonged to them.

She went alone.

At the street door she adjusted the skirt so that its weight fell evenly. Toothpick lay close against her left calf, where she had fastened it before dressing. It was a poor place to reach from a chair. She had put it there anyway.

Chapter Thirty-Two — Riarhin

Tylan opened the door himself.

The room beyond him smelled of the wax used on the floor and a fire that had burned down without being fed. Late light reached across the carpet as far as the leg of a table, showing every thread there with a clarity the rest of the room lacked. Three cups waited beside a covered jug. Someone had put out chairs for a conversation which had not begun.

Veralyn sat at the window with a paper in her hand. As Riarhin entered, she folded its lower edge against the upper, pressed the crease with a thumbnail, and unfolded it again.

“Aveline.”

The queen did not rise. Tylan took Riarhin’s cloak before she could decide whether to offer it. His sword moved against the chair as he laid the cloak over its back; he steadied the sheath with the same hand that had just held the cloth.

“Will you sit?”

“I would rather stand.”

“As you wish.”

He went to the table and uncovered the water. The small business gave Riarhin time to look at Veralyn without having to explain the looking. Black cloth, a narrow green border at the cuff, the amulet lying where the light from the window could reach it. Beside the queen stood Shadowsteel in its sheath. The chair had been moved a little to accommodate it.

Maelis had said to ask about Mara’s comfort. Riarhin could still begin there, let the pretence carry them into the thing she meant to say. She heard how little patience she had for another opening prepared in the street.

“Mara is being moved tomorrow.”

“Yes,” Veralyn said.

“Where?”

The queen looked down at her folded paper. “The arrangements are Tylan’s.”

Riarhin turned towards him.

“From the estate to a household travelling under military protection,” he said. “Her rooms are prepared. She will have attendants and her own things.”

“Can she leave?”

“No.”

“Then don’t tell me how comfortably she can stay.”

Tylan set the jug down. He had filled two cups, leaving the third empty when Riarhin declined it.

“I asked for the move. The men keeping her now are accustomed to guarding doors. The people receiving her have had women and children in their charge before.”

“You could send her home.”

“I could recommend it. I haven’t.”

Veralyn smoothed the fold in the paper and looked out of the window. Riarhin could see nothing beyond it but the upper branches of a tree, their last leaves moving against pale sky. The queen had been so attentive to the weaver, to the soldier’s torn girth. Riarhin found herself wanting to recover that attention as if Mara’s situation had failed through the manner in which it was presented.

She took the papers from her sleeve.

“Then look at these.”

Tylan cleared a place. He read the charge first, then Margery’s account, then the copy of Cecily Orme’s direction. He set the first sheet beside the third and checked the dates without speaking. Riarhin watched his finger stop at the name of the orchard.

“This is what they say she delivered?”

“Yes.”

“Have you seen the sheet they showed the carrier?”

“No. Deirdre has. She says it was never in the packet she gave Lyra.”

Tylan went to the door.

For a moment Riarhin thought he meant to send her away. Instead he called for the clerk waiting beyond it, gave him the copied charge, and dictated an instruction: the original alleged enclosure was to be kept intact, with the receiving entry and every later addition brought to the comparison. Nobody was to substitute a corrected description for the one which had caused the arrest without retaining both.

“Tomorrow?” the clerk asked.

“Tonight. The comparison is tomorrow. I want the papers ready for it.”

The man glanced towards Veralyn. She was watching Tylan now.

“Do as he says,” she said.

Tylan signed the instruction on the corner of the table and waited until the clerk had gone before closing the door.

“They can’t make the date less inconvenient by copying it again,” he said.

Riarhin put a hand on the remaining papers. She had come prepared to argue against indifference. What he had just done mattered. She could imagine Orrel receiving the preserved original, being able to make the question stick where a corrected heading might otherwise have let it slide. Lyra was still waiting in a room whose location nobody would give her.

“Let me see her.”

Tylan looked to the queen.

“After the examination,” Veralyn said.

“She has asked for someone she knows.”

“Then you may be the person she sees afterwards.”

“Is that a promise?”

The queen set the folded paper on the windowsill.

“It is an answer, Aveline. You have brought the same request in several forms.”

Riarhin could feel the knife against her calf. Her gown concealed it while making reaching it an action that would be seen. She had dressed for a woman who must first be listened to, and now the small inconvenience of the cloth seemed part of a much larger humiliation.

Tylan drew the empty chair farther from the table.

“Sit down before you decide what else you mean to ask.”

“I know what I mean to ask.”

“Then sit while you ask it.”

She nearly obeyed. He had the voice of a man accustomed to being useful in a room where other people were frightened. Berent would have recognised it. Elyas had written about it, making her remember the man who returned stolen horses and expected a road in exchange.

“Mara goes home,” she said. “She can pursue her petition from her own house. You know who comes there. You know who writes to her. You can hear the witnesses she has asked you to hear.”

Veralyn’s gaze moved from the empty chair to Riarhin’s face.

“You have been thinking how to make this acceptable.”

“Yes.”

“To me?”

“To anyone who doesn’t want her hurt.”

“She’s at the Mound’s house. Their men know their duty. Tylan has arranged a safer place for her tomorrow. That is the situation.”

The queen stood at last. She left the paper where it lay and came a few steps towards them, looking at Riarhin with something more like curiosity.

“What will you do with it?”

“I am asking you to change it.”

“I know.”

Tylan turned the nearest cup so its handle faced away from the papers. Riarhin watched his hand because the queen was watching her.

“You have found a way into my rooms,” Veralyn said. “You’ve made friends, asked questions, put people to some trouble. I have heard more about Aveline Marr in the last few days than about women who have been here for years. I would like to see what comes of it.”

She paused, considering Riarhin’s expression.

“Or shall I say Riarhin?”

The name did not break the glamour.

Bea remained in the room. The queen could look at the face as long as she liked; Riarhin could keep it there. For a moment she wanted to do that simply because being expected to surrender it felt like another instruction.

Then she let it go.

The release reached her in pieces: her own voice waiting without correction, the true height of her eyes, the position of a mouth which no longer required remembering. She drew a breath and discovered how shallowly she had been breathing. The gown hung about the body for which Vessa had allowed while fitting the woman everyone else saw.

Tylan’s expression changed. He had known enough not to start, but the sight of her seemed to cost him something the suspicion had not.

Veralyn came closer.

“There you are.”

Riarhin hated the pleasure in it, and the little answering want to be recognised by someone who remembered her before all this began.

“I have been here,” she said.

“Yes. So I see.”

The queen looked towards the chair. Riarhin remained standing. This time Tylan left it alone.

“Elyas sent you?” he asked.

“He sent Berent. I came after the news.”

“Berent should have brought his concerns to me.”

“He brought them where Elyas asked him to.”

“He is a knight. There are obligations—”

“He kept one.”

Tylan accepted the rebuke with a small movement of his head. “Yes. There are sometimes several.”

Riarhin had thought of saying that Elyas left a letter. She wanted to put his handwriting on this table and make them wait while she read the part in which he asked not to be proved right before a grave. But the letter was his voice to her, written because he could not safely say everything elsewhere. Offering it here would give Veralyn another piece to examine while she asked what Riarhin meant to do.

“He knew what you would call his death,” she said instead.

Tylan’s face closed.

“Did he know how many people were making use of him?”

“He knew some. He told me so.”

“He refused to name them when he was asked.”

“Were they guilty of anything?”

“Some had offered men.”

“Which he hadn’t asked for.”

“He kept receiving them.”

There it was again, a door opened for somebody who had come a long way. Riarhin could imagine all the sensible reasons to close it. She could imagine herself telling Elyas to stop letting frightened men make him responsible for their fear. She had offered advice like that to other people and thought herself clear-sighted while doing it.

“You could have kept him alive,” she said.

“I asked him to accept a command away from those households. He would have had work he knew, men who needed him, room to do some good.”

“And leave Mara?”

“She could have joined him.”

“When?”

Tylan looked at her directly. “When the appointment was settled.”

“After he stopped hearing the people you didn’t want heard.”

“After he stopped becoming the man they could use against the settlement.”

Veralyn had begun walking towards the table. At Elyas’s name she had listened; as the answers repeated, her attention wandered to the papers lying beneath Tylan’s hand. She drew Mara’s petition from among them and read the last paragraph.

“She writes plainly,” she said.

“She wants an answer.”

“She has had one.”

Tylan put his palm on the table, stopping the queen before she set the petition back into the pile.

“Veralyn.”

She looked up.

“Let her stay tonight. Let us finish this without a house full of young men discovering how little they understand the woman coming through their gate.”

The room went very still.

Riarhin could hear the slow settling of the unlit fire, a little ash falling through the grate. Tylan knew what she might do. He had put it into words without asking her to threaten anyone, and now she wanted him to go on, to spend the authority she had watched him use at the inspection.

The queen glanced at her.

“Would you have me tell them their oath ends when keeping it becomes difficult?”

“I would have you decide what keeping Mara there is worth.”

“I have.”

Tylan held her gaze. His hand remained on the table long enough that Riarhin thought he might refuse. Then he removed it and stepped aside to let Veralyn put the petition down.

He turned back to Riarhin.

“I will not countermand her.”

“Then why say it?”

“Because she needed to hear it.”

The queen touched his arm as she passed. There was affection in the touch, and a familiarity that made it more difficult to bear than a reward for obedience. Tylan did not lean towards her. He returned the copied direction to Riarhin and kept the receipt for the instruction he had sent.

“The papers will be there tomorrow. That I can do.”

“And Mara?”

“She leaves at first light.”

Outside the window the last light had withdrawn from the carpet. Riarhin thought of the rooms at Maelis’s lodging, Berent waiting with too little to do, Na’ar pretending he was not watching the door. They had a few hours to find a room behind three possible doors. Tomorrow they would have a road, perhaps several, and no means of knowing which one carried Mara.

Lyra’s room was already like that. Riarhin could name the desk where parcels were accepted and the woman who carried them through. She could not reach the place beyond. I would like to see somebody I know. She could hear the line without seeing the paper.

She had thought finding the lie would make a passage open. Tylan was preserving the lie for examination; the room stayed shut.

Veralyn turned towards her. Her hand stayed on the chair beside Shadowsteel.

“You can stay,” Tylan said. “We have until supper. I will give you the whole of it.”

He meant it. She could ask about the people who had offered Elyas men, hear the proposal he had refused, try to find some word that would make them regard Mara as a woman whose life belonged to her. They had let her come close enough for a different sort of attempt too. Two steps to Veralyn. Tylan between the table and the door. The knife where her own preparation had made it hard to reach.

Tylan waited beside the table.

“If I stay, will you stop the transfer?”

“No,” Veralyn said.

Riarhin looked at the papers. She took those she had brought and left Mara’s petition where the queen had placed it.

“Then I am going to her.”

Veralyn’s fingers closed briefly on the chair’s carved edge.

“The men there have their orders.”

“Change them.”

“No.”

“They’ll try to stop me.”

“Yes.”

The answer came readily. Riarhin saw Tylan glance at the queen, and for an instant wondered whether he heard the same readiness. He drew a breath but did not interrupt.

“If you take her,” Veralyn said, “there will be blood. Understand that before you go.”

She had become completely attentive. Riarhin remembered a younger woman’s delight in a story, the question that always came when she had tried to hurry past the difficult part. And what did you do then? She could have answered this one by remaining, made the queen sit through another hour of the arguments she had already found tedious.

Mara would be moved at first light.

Riarhin went to the door.

“Mine too, if they can get it?”

“They will defend the house.”

Tylan reached the door before she did and opened it. For a moment they stood close enough that she could see the tired skin beneath his eyes.

“The horse companies are leaving tomorrow,” he said quietly. “The roads will be busy.”

“Is that a warning?”

“It is something you ought to know.”

“Will you send men after Berent?”

“He hasn’t been ordered into custody.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“No,” Tylan said. “It isn’t.”

He stepped back. She could not tell whether he had given her something he should not have, or merely made her spend another breath asking. The doubt travelled out of the room with her.

The guard outside looked from her to the queen. His hand moved towards his weapon, then stopped. Veralyn told him to let her pass.

She walked through the passage in the gown made for Bea. A servant carrying clean cups stepped out of her way. Farther on, Enna stood beside a basket and stared at her face, then lowered her eyes without calling to anyone.

At the garden door Margery was speaking with another woman. Riarhin might have turned aside. There were other ways out, and a dozen reasons not to add this meeting to the things she was already leaving.

Margery saw her.

For a moment neither moved. Riarhin watched recognition reach the hands before the face, the way Margery looked at her left wrist and then back into her eyes.

“Aveline?”

“Riarhin.”

Margery drew a breath. The woman beside her stepped away, pretending to look for someone in the garden.

“Were you going to tell me?”

“I wanted to.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“No.” Riarhin made herself stay where she was. “I don’t know when I would have.”

Margery looked at the open door behind her. “Is someone following you?”

“They’ve let me leave.”

“I see.”

Riarhin doubted that either of them did.

She wanted to tell Margery about Mara, to make the next thing urgent enough that this one could be postponed without looking like a choice. Instead she said, “I wanted the walk.”

Margery’s mouth tightened.

“I know.”

The answer hurt more than disbelief. Riarhin could offer nothing that would let her keep the kindness without asking more of it.

“Orrel has Lyra’s papers,” she said. “Please don’t withdraw yours.”

“I signed what I saw. That hasn’t changed.”

Riarhin bowed her head and left through the garden.

Beyond the gate she lifted the skirt enough to walk quickly. The gown caught once against the ironwork; she freed it with a hard pull that broke a thread. Nobody called her back.

She had to reach the others before dark.

Part V — The People She Takes

Chapter Thirty-Three — Before They Move Her

Maelis opened the door and let her in.

The passage was crowded with things which had not been there when Riarhin left: a folded blanket, a tied bundle, the plain shoes Maelis wore when she did not expect to be admired. From the room beyond came the smell of bread warming beside the hearth. They had kept food for her, or had been unable to finish their own while they waited.

She had rehearsed telling them on the walk back. Inside the familiar doorway, with the warmth against her face, she found herself wanting to wash her hands first. Something small and finishable before she changed what the house was for.

Her eyes went first to Riarhin’s face, then to the torn thread hanging from the skirt. She caught it between two fingers, broke it off, and closed the door behind them.

“Do I need to send someone back for that gown?”

“No.”

“Good. I dislike asking twice.”

Berent rose from the table. Na’ar remained seated, watching her. She told them what had happened while Maelis unfastened the closure at her neck, an absurdly intimate assistance to receive while repeating the queen’s refusal to release a woman from confinement. The gown loosened. Riarhin stepped out of it into the shirt she had worn beneath and reached for her own clothes.

“She let you walk out?” Berent asked.

“Yes.”

“Knowing what you mean to do?”

“I told you what she said.”

Na’ar drew the gown away from her boots before she trod on it. He folded it badly and put it on the empty chair.

“Then we have until morning.”

They had less. A night in which a household expected to leave early would be full of people carrying things at unusual hours. Berent had seen a covered wagon entering the estate and two packhorses led towards the stables. The main gate had been open while the wagon passed; he could see the bright iron of a new staple where the old latch had been. He had kept riding. Nobody had detained him, though the man who watched from the gateway had called another to look.

Riarhin put on her belt. Remorse hung differently against these clothes, familiar weight in a place which had remained empty for weeks. She checked the knives without drawing them and turned her back to the room while fastening her collar. Her hands had begun to shake now that nobody needed them to resemble Bea’s.

“Tell me the track,” she said.

Berent described what he had seen from the road: fresh wheel marks, a heap of thorn cuttings beside the granary boundary, a cart standing near the neighbour’s fence. The woman with the hens had been taking in washing. There would be very little moon before midnight.

“We go by the drainage cut,” Riarhin said. “Below the track. Once we’re through the little gate, you stay with it. Na’ar comes to the stair with me.”

“No,” Na’ar said.

She looked at him.

“You need someone between the house and the stable yard. Once they shout, men will come from both. A man at your little gate can watch them run past him.”

“We don’t know the passage is narrow enough.”

“You saw it.”

“I saw one side of it.”

“Then I’ll look at the other before I choose where to stand. Berent goes with you.”

He rose with a hand on the table, taking his weight on the sound leg until he was upright.

“No chasing them,” she said.

“They can come to me.”

He took the cup from the table and placed it beside the loaf, making the space between them smaller with his hand. Riarhin hated the little arrangement. It looked possible. She had made too many things look possible by setting one object beside another.

“If you can’t hold it?”

“I come back through the yard. You don’t go looking for a different door.”

She nodded. He moved the cup away.

They needed a cart close enough to carry a person who could not walk. Riarhin said this because it was how she had learned to plan, and disliked the way Berent glanced at her when she did. Deirdre’s carter was waiting well outside the city; bringing his heavy wagon to the estate would announce a visit and make turning on the working track difficult. Na’ar had hired a smaller cart through the lodging’s keeper, paying more than it was worth for leave to return it late. He had said they were moving a chest. The owner had asked to be paid before they began.

“Does he know what you’re moving?”

“No.”

Riarhin ran her thumb over the edge of the table. “Keep enough to replace it.”

“I already did.”

***

Maelis made them eat.

She could enforce very little else. She put bread into their hands and told them the carter’s name was Hobb, his sister’s holding lay beside a limeburner’s disused shed, and the painted blue board at the fork was still there, though Deirdre said it leaned enough to point at the wrong road. The right road was the smaller one, with a broken stone trough just beyond the turn.

“Do you know it?” she asked Berent.

“I can find it.”

“I asked whether you know it.”

“No.”

“Then remember the trough. I am not waiting in every shed south of Valora.”

She had packed. Riarhin saw the case beneath the bench for the first time. It was too small for everything in Maelis’s workroom, too carefully fastened to have been thrown together after Riarhin arrived.

“You’re coming?”

“I am going to the place at which you intend to arrive. We can discuss the distinction when you get there.”

“What happens to this house?”

“It belongs to someone who will find my rent on the table. I have done her enough harm by lodging in it.”

Maelis took one of her rings off, considered it, and put it back on. Her breathing was shallow. Whatever satisfaction she had found in arranging the afternoon had not relieved her fear of its consequences.

Riarhin went to fetch Lyra’s papers. There were three copies now in different hands, and she knew exactly how little that resembled having Lyra out of the rooms where they kept her. She wrapped the copy from Mara’s chest against damp and pushed it into her bag.

Berent watched her.

“And Lyra?”

“Orrel has the comparison tomorrow.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“I know.”

He waited. Na’ar stopped tightening the strap on his forearm.

“I don’t know how to get her out tonight,” Riarhin said. “I am going for Mara.”

Berent lowered his eyes to the table. He had brought a message from Elyas which had reached her while there was still time to stay in Glenruan another day. She wanted to put that old delay between herself and this one, to explain why she could not bear to wait for Mara too. It would make Lyra’s room no warmer.

“I’ll carry these,” she said, touching the bag. “I won’t tell you that saves her.”

Na’ar gave her the last piece of bread. She ate it because he was waiting for her to take it, and because she would need the strength.

***

They left separately.

Riarhin walked behind the cart until the houses thinned, her cloak drawn around the sword. Na’ar led the horse. Berent met them beyond the last bright windows, where the road passed between stubble fields and the wind brought the smell of turned earth. The ground fell by so little that she noticed it through the drainage rather than the horizon: shallow cuts meeting a larger ditch, a low culvert carrying water under the road. There was nowhere here for an army to disappear. Three people could only be patient about when they crossed an open piece of ground.

They left the cart behind a hedge well short of the estate. Berent looped the rein where the horse could reach neither the roadside grass nor the harness with its teeth. Riarhin took a coil of rope from the bed, reconsidered the noise of its buckle, and left the buckle behind.

At the drainage cut they crouched. The little gate was darker than the wall beside it. Above it the thorn cuttings Berent had seen had been spread along the low boundary, filling the easy place where Riarhin had intended to climb.

She felt along the earth for a dry stone and found only cold mud.

Na’ar touched her shoulder. He pointed towards the granary, where a low lean-to roof met the older masonry. Rain had left a dark streak down the boards. The crossing would be higher there but clear of thorns.

They watched first.

A woman came out from the neighbouring holding carrying a lamp. She checked the henhouse, spoke to something inside, and turned towards the estate when a door banged. Riarhin remained with her cheek near the damp bank while the light reached across the field. The woman could have seen them if she had walked to the fence. Instead she went back to her house and shut the door.

Riarhin waited until the light behind the shutter moved away.

“Leave her alone if she comes out,” she whispered.

Na’ar gave a small nod. Berent was looking at the black shape of the house beyond the yard.

Riarhin crossed the track on hands and knees. At the granary she stood, felt for the edge of the lean-to, and tested it with her weight before pulling herself up. A board gave a small complaint. She stopped, listening to footsteps somewhere within the estate, then moved again.

From the roof she could see the yard side of the little gate. A bar lay across two brackets. There was no lock.

She lowered herself until her arms were straight and let go.

Chapter Thirty-Four — The Service Yard

The landing jarred her teeth.

Riarhin bent with it and stayed low beside the granary. The yard smelled of damp grain and the sour water thrown out from a kitchen. Somewhere close a horse scraped its hoof against wood. She could see the stair door in the newer wing, recessed beneath a small roof, with a lantern hanging to one side.

A man stood under it.

He was holding a cup between both hands. His weapon leaned against the doorframe within reach. Riarhin watched him turn his face away from the light to look across the yard. She had expected someone inside the stair door. Finding him outside changed the distance she would have to cross after opening the gate.

She moved to the bar. It lifted easily from the first bracket and caught against the second. She steadied the far end with her right hand, working it free a finger’s width at a time so that it would not fall against the wood. When she had it loose, she lowered it to the ground and opened the gate just enough for Na’ar to enter.

Berent followed. Na’ar pointed once at the connecting passage between the newer wing and the older house. A light moved behind its far arch. He waited for it to pass, then crossed into shadow along the stable wall.

Riarhin touched Berent’s arm and pointed to the man by the stair. Berent nodded. He would come after her, not beside her.

She kept the granary between herself and the lantern as long as she could. At its corner the yard opened. The man had turned towards a sound inside; she crossed while his head was angled away, closing the last few steps too quickly to pretend she belonged there.

He saw her hand first.

She caught his cup with her right forearm, drove it against his chest, and went in under the arm reaching for the weapon. Toothpick was already in her left hand. Her hips carried the cut. She felt the blade enter, withdrew it, and moved with him as he folded so his head would not strike the stone.

The cup broke anyway.

A voice from upstairs asked what had fallen. Riarhin dragged the man into the recess and pressed herself beside him. He moved once against her boot. Berent reached the door, saw the blood, and looked up at the shuttered window.

“Cup,” Riarhin said, making her voice thick and low.

“Clumsy bastard.”

Footsteps moved away overhead. She wiped the knife on the dead man’s coat and found that her right hand had begun to grip his shoulder as if there were something more to be done for him. She released it, then took the small ring of keys fastened at his belt.

Berent tried the latch. The door opened inward.

***

The stair divided halfway up.

A short flight ran towards the public rooms, and the longer one turned under the roof of the newer wing. They had been told three doors. From the landing Riarhin could see four, the nearest narrow enough to be a cupboard. Light showed beneath the farthest.

She paused. There was a woman speaking beyond that door, the words muffled by a hanging or a second partition. Berent came up behind her, leaving enough distance that both could move if someone appeared.

Riarhin laid two fingers against the wood.

“Mara.”

The speaking stopped. A chair scraped.

“Who is there?”

She knew the voice. Her relief arrived before she had checked anything else, strong enough to make her turn the latch at once. It did not move.

“Mara. It’s me.”

A step approached. “Riarhin?”

“Yes.”

“The key’s outside. He carries it. There’s a bolt below the latch.”

The second key turned in the lock. Riarhin felt along the iron plate for the bolt, set low enough to be reached from a chair in the passage and hidden from above by the thick frame. Before she could draw it back, Berent caught her sleeve.

Berent turned at a sound below.

A man stood where the shorter flight joined theirs. He had come from the public rooms carrying a lamp and a crossbow, its stock tucked under one arm so he could use the free hand at a door. For a moment he looked as surprised as Berent. Then he dropped the lamp.

Berent took two steps down towards him.

The bow struck the stair rail as the man tried to bring it up. It discharged with a short, heavy sound. Berent’s leg gave beneath him and he fell against the wall, pulling the man down by his coat. The lamp broke below them. Fire ran through the spilled oil in a wavering line.

Riarhin left the door and went down.

The man was trying to draw a knife with Berent’s hand locked on his wrist. She caught the upper rail, swung past Berent’s shoulder, and drove Toothpick into the space beneath the man’s jaw. He struck at her with the bow. It caught her side hard enough to take her breath. She went closer, turned the blade, and held him until the striking stopped.

From the far door Mara called her name.

“Stay there.”

Berent was looking at his thigh. The bolt had passed through the outer flesh above the knee, leaving its head beyond the cloth on the far side. Blood darkened the leg of his hose and ran onto the tread.

“Can you stand?”

He tried. The sound he made answered her.

Riarhin caught him beneath the arms and pulled him up one step, clear of the oil. He helped with his sound leg, his boot scraping for purchase. Below them the fire found the edge of a hanging.

A shout came from the yard.

***

Mara opened the narrow door beside her room.

It was a linen cupboard. She took a folded sheet, pulled until it tore against a projecting nail, and came to the landing with the long strip gathered in her arms. Riarhin had returned to lift the bolt and let her out; now she could scarcely get a word in as Mara knelt beside Berent.

“Hold this here. Hard.”

Riarhin pressed the folded cloth where she pointed. Mara cut away enough hose to see the wound without moving the shaft, using Berent’s knife because her own small scissors had been taken. Her fingers were steady. She asked him whether he could feel his foot, made him move it, then pushed another pad against the bleeding.

“Don’t pull it out,” she told Riarhin.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“I want you to hear me.”

“I hear you.”

The hanging below had begun to smoke heavily. Someone was trying the lower door. Riarhin heard Na’ar shout from the yard, not words she could distinguish, and a blow against timber.

“We have to move.”

“Then get under his arm.”

Mara wound the cloth to keep the pads in place, securing them around the protruding bolt as well as she could without driving it farther. Berent held the rail while they raised him. Riarhin took most of his weight. He was taller than she remembered when she needed to carry him this way, his mailed shoulder hard against her neck.

The shorter flight was filling with smoke. Mara pointed to the opposite door.

“Laundry stair. They use it when they bring the water up.”

“Where does it come out?”

“Beside the kitchen. I saw them through my window.”

It took them back towards the yard but farther from the little gate. Riarhin could see no useful choice in remaining where they were. She let Mara lead them through the door.

The laundry stair was narrow and clean, worn hollow in the middle of each tread. Berent descended sideways, moving the injured leg only when he had enough support to keep it from striking the wall. Halfway down he began to shake. Mara spoke to him quietly, counting the steps he had left as if the number could be made smaller by sharing it.

At the bottom a young servant stood holding an empty bucket.

He saw the blood and backed away. Riarhin let him go. His bucket struck the wall as he turned, and the noise brought a man through the kitchen door carrying a pole he had seized from beside the oven.

“Behind me,” she said.

Mara pulled Berent into the recess. Riarhin drew the Last Blade into her right hand.

The man thrust down the passage. She slipped the point beside her, struck the shaft away with the right-hand knife, and moved in before he could shorten his grip. Her left hand finished it. His body came against her with the whole weight of a man who had meant to keep standing. She twisted aside and let him fall.

There was no space for the sword here. She stepped over his leg, opened the outer door, and looked into the yard.

Na’ar had pulled a feed trolley across the entrance to the connecting passage. A man lay on the near side of it. Beyond him Riarhin saw another pair of legs moving back, then the flash of a blade as someone reached over the timber.

Na’ar struck the hand away. He had set his sound leg back against the wall; the other stayed close beneath him while he worked over the trolley.

“Go,” he called.

A bell began ringing in the older house.

Chapter Thirty-Five — The Way Through

The yard was larger with Berent in it.

Mara had one of his arms across her shoulders and held the binding against his thigh with her free hand. He could put weight on the sound leg for a step, then had to be carried through the next movement. Every few paces she made him stop. Riarhin wanted to lift him and run, but the angle of the bolt would put its point against her body and its shaft against his, and she could not carry him well enough to make haste an improvement.

She sheathed the knives. Remorse came into her left hand as she moved out from the kitchen wall.

A man crossed from the stables with a sword drawn. He saw the blade in her hand and slowed, spreading his feet. Behind him another man came with a spear. The first was willing to hold her while the second reached her side.

Riarhin gave ground towards the granary, away from Mara’s line of retreat. The swordsman followed. She let his first cut pass, turned her wrist to meet the returning edge, and moved around his forward foot. He recovered well. She had no room to admire it.

The spear came in low. She stepped beyond its point, struck the shaft aside, and closed along it. The swordsman had to check his next blow to avoid his companion. Riarhin used the check, cutting across the spearman’s forearm and passing him before his weapon fell. She turned where the open ground let her turn and brought Remorse back between herself and the sword.

Mara was nearer the gate now. Berent dragged his injured foot, jaw set so hard Riarhin could see it from across the yard.

The swordsman attacked again. She drew him another step, gave his blade a yielding contact which let it travel past her shoulder, then went in beneath his guard. The point entered his coat below the ribs. She withdrew without waiting to see his expression and moved away before he could catch her.

Someone called her name.

Garin stood in the open doorway of the older house.

He had a weapon but had not yet brought it forward. The light behind him made it difficult to see his face. Riarhin knew him by the way he leaned to look past the trolley, trying to understand which people were where before committing himself.

“Stay there,” she said.

“Put it down.”

“Let us go.”

“Mara is to be protected.”

“Ask her from whom.”

He looked towards Mara. She had reached the little gate and was trying to help Berent over the high step. The movement pulled at his wound; he cried out, and she told him to hold her neck as hard as he needed.

Garin moved.

Riarhin saw his decision too late to know what it had been. He came down into the passage behind the trolley while another man pushed in from the side. Na’ar had to turn to meet them. The timber shifted, one wheel climbing a stone, and Riarhin lost sight of Garin behind his shoulder.

She could go back. There were still only a few paces between her and the passage.

From above, a shutter opened.

Na’ar saw it too. He moved against the wall, making the man at the window lean farther out for a shot. The trolley’s position shielded most of him, but it also gave the men behind it something to climb. He struck one down and kicked the wheel back into the hollow it had left.

“Riarhin.”

This time it was Mara. Berent had fallen outside the gate.

***

She reached him on her knees.

His boot had caught the high step when Mara tried to turn him through. He lay half on the track, face white in the scant light, with both hands pressing the bandage where it had slipped. Mara had caught his shoulders before his head struck the ground. She could not raise him alone.

Riarhin put Remorse away. They got him upright between them, and for three steps he managed to hop with their support. At the fourth his strength went again.

“Cart,” Mara said. “Bring it.”

“It’ll turn badly here.”

“Then don’t turn it.”

Riarhin ran.

At the hedge the horse lifted its head sharply when she caught the rein. She soothed it with the voice that came without choosing, released the loop, and led it backwards until she could bring the shafts around. The wheel scraped a stone. From the estate came a shout, the bell still ringing over it, then the crash of wood giving way.

She drove the cart up the working track towards the gate. It was too narrow to turn without taking a wheel into the shallow cut. Mara saw her coming and pulled Berent closer to the hedge to leave the wheels room. Riarhin halted with the cart bed beside him.

Na’ar came through the gate.

He had lost the fastening at one shoulder. Blood ran down his forearm from a cut she could see, and there was another dark patch high on his side. He pushed the gate towards its frame, found the bar lay inside, and turned to face the opening.

“Get him up.”

Riarhin put both hands under Berent’s shoulders. Mara held the injured leg clear of the edge while Na’ar caught his belt with one hand and heaved. Berent screamed once. The sound stopped abruptly when he ran out of breath.

They laid him along the bed. Mara climbed after him and pressed the binding again.

The gate struck Na’ar’s back.

He turned into it, driving it shut against the man on the other side. His boots slipped on the track. Riarhin grabbed the edge with him, but there was no place outside to set the bar, and more weight came against the wood from within.

“Take the horse,” he said.

“I’ll hold it.”

“You can’t get round them with the cart here.”

He was right. To reverse under pressure would bring the wheel across Berent’s dangling foot; to go forward would take them past the gate before there was room to turn. The farm entrance farther along offered enough space, if they could reach it.

Na’ar shoved once more, making the gate’s latch catch for a moment. He used that moment to take up the long pole the wounded spearman had dropped inside, drawing it through the gap before the man beyond recovered.

“Now.”

Riarhin went to the horse’s head.

They moved past the gate at a walk which felt slower than standing. The cart rocked in its ruts; Mara braced her feet against the boards and told Berent not to fight the motion. Behind them the latch tore free of the wood.

Na’ar let the gate swing wide. With the pole across its opening he struck the first man back into the second, buying the space to follow the cart. He walked backwards after them, keeping the point between himself and the men coming through. Riarhin reached the farm entrance and began to turn.

A bolt struck the cart’s side.

The horse shied towards the ditch. She caught its bridle with both hands and brought its head round before the wheel went over the edge. Mara bent across Berent. Na’ar looked up at the granary roof, where a man had climbed out high enough to shoot over those on the track.

He moved towards the hedge to deny the man another clear line. His scarred leg took the sideways weight and folded. He caught himself on the sound one, but the pole dipped as he recovered, and the nearer pursuer stepped around its point.

Riarhin saw the sword enter beneath Na’ar’s raised arm.

He struck the man with the butt of the pole, breaking his forward movement, and stumbled against the hedge. He stayed up. For a moment she believed he had taken the wound in some way he could bear, that his size and stubbornness had made this another injury to be argued over later.

He tried to breathe.

The pole dropped. He caught at the hedge with his free hand and went down onto one knee.

Riarhin left the bridle, drew Toothpick, and ran back.

The nearest man turned towards her. She cut his wrist as he lifted the sword and drove him sideways into the ditch with her shoulder. She barely felt the collision. Na’ar was below her now, his hand finding the front of her coat and gripping it hard enough to pull her close.

“Move it,” he said.

“I will.”

“The cart.”

“I know.”

She tried to get beneath his arm. His weight came over her, crushing her shoulder, and his breath broke against her ear. She could smell the meal they had shared under the iron smell of the wound.

From the cart Mara called that men were coming across the field.

Riarhin looked. Three shapes had left the main approach and were moving towards the farm entrance, cutting off the turn if she stayed. She dragged Na’ar half a pace. His boot caught against the bank.

“Lift,” she said. “Help me.”

His hand loosened on her coat.

She had seen men pretend weakness to make someone come closer. She had seen them rise after wounds which ought to have left them down. She waited for the hard pull, the angry breath, something he could give her to work with.

His eyes were open. They did not follow her when she moved.

She put her hand against his neck, then under his jaw where she could be sure. Behind her the horse stamped, frightened by the running men. Mara called again.

Riarhin lowered Na’ar against the bank.

She could not carry him and drive. She could not lift him onto the cart alone before they reached it. Every answer she tried came back to the same two people on the boards, one holding the other’s blood inside him with a folded sheet.

She left him there.

At the cart she climbed onto the front, caught the reins, and took the horse through the turn. A man reached for the shaft. She struck his hand away with the butt of the whip and drove on. The wheel hit a rut hard enough to throw Berent against Mara, and Riarhin heard her own name used as a command to slow down.

She slowed enough.

The neighbour’s door stood open. The woman with the hens watched from behind it, lamplight on her sleeve. Riarhin saw her looking past the cart to the dark shape against the bank.

Then the hedge hid them both.

Chapter Thirty-Six — One More Passenger

The blue board pointed towards a field.

Riarhin saw it, remembered the trough, and almost took the larger road anyway. She had been looking for the board for so long that finding it felt like permission to stop deciding. Mara said her name from the cart. She turned the horse into the narrower lane.

Behind them, farther back than she could judge by sound, hooves reached the road.

She looked once. Lanterns moved at the fork they had passed before this one, where a patrol waited across the broader way. There were riders coming from the estate. She could distinguish them by their haste and the man at their head with no cloak over his pale shirt. He pointed after the cart.

The patrol’s officer raised an arm.

“Hold there.”

Riarhin turned forward and urged the horse on. The hoofbeats faltered behind them. She waited for the renewed rhythm of pursuit, listening so hard that she nearly missed the darker bulk of the stone trough at the roadside.

Nobody followed them into the lane.

At the limeburner’s shed a shutter opened. Maelis came out carrying a lamp low enough to shield its light from the road. Her first glance went to Riarhin’s face; her second to the bed of the cart.

“Where’s Na’ar?”

Riarhin could not make the name into a sentence.

Mara answered. “He’s dead.”

Maelis lifted the lamp. She looked into the cart again, at the space beside Berent where somebody might have been lying out of sight.

“Bring that here,” Mara said. “I need to see this.”

Maelis obeyed. Riarhin climbed down and found that she had wound the rein around her hand tightly enough to leave pale furrows across the palm. A man took it from her one finger at a time.

“Hobb,” he said. “Is there anyone behind you?”

“There was.”

He listened towards the road. His sister stood at the door in a man’s old coat, holding the latch so that it would not knock against the wall in the wind.

“You said she was leaving a house,” Hobb said to Maelis.

“She has.”

He looked at the blood on the cart. “Get them inside.”

***

Berent had to be lifted on a door.

Hobb and his sister took it off the shed’s inner room while Mara stayed beside him in the cart. Riarhin reached to help and was told to hold the lamp instead. She stood above the boards with the flame shaking in her hand while they moved him, trying not to look at the place where Na’ar’s hand had been on his belt.

They carried him into a room used for storing dried roots and empty sacks. Hobb’s sister had laid blankets over a low platform against the wall. Berent opened his eyes when they put him down and asked whether Mara was there.

“I’m here.”

“Good.”

Then he tried to sit up.

Mara pressed him back by the shoulder. “You can ask again lying down.”

He took a breath and waited until he could speak. “Na’ar?”

Riarhin set the lamp on the shelf.

“He’s dead.”

Berent stared at her. She had said it at last, and it had done nothing to the impossible expectation that he would enter from the yard complaining about their haste. His place in the room remained exact. He would have stood beside the door until he saw who needed lifting.

“Where?” Berent asked.

“Outside the gate.”

“Did you—”

“I couldn’t bring him.”

Mara looked up from the wound. Her hands were red to the wrists. Riarhin could not bear a word intended to absolve her and turned towards Hobb before Mara spoke.

“We need someone who knows more than we do.”

“My sister’s gone to fetch him.”

“Who?”

“A man who took iron out of my shoulder. She’ll bring him if he’s sober enough to ride.”

“And if he isn’t?”

“Then she’ll bring him in something with wheels.”

Hobb went to watch the road. Riarhin sat against the wall where she could see the door and laid her hands palm up on her knees. One was burned, both were cut, and she had no memory of getting the smaller injuries. She rubbed at a dark mark with her thumb until Mara told her to stop.

***

The surgeon arrived smelling of sleep.

His coat had been buttoned wrong in the dark. Hobb’s sister corrected it while he washed, an intimacy of long acquaintance which he accepted without interrupting his questions. She knew where to put his bag and which of its buckles had to be pulled sideways. Riarhin watched the two of them fit an emergency into a room that had held nothing but sacks a few hours before. The roots still hung from the beam above Berent’s head. A little earth fell from them whenever someone struck the wall, and Mara asked for a cloth to be stretched beneath them.

The surgeon looked up, agreed, and waited while it was done. Riarhin had been counting every delay until she saw the dust caught on the cloth instead of the wound.

He had been brought from his bed rather than a drinking table and resented Hobb’s joke when it was repeated to him. He asked whose wound, how long, how much blood. Mara answered. He examined what she had done, nodded once, and told her which things he needed from his bag.

Riarhin went outside when they began. Berent had asked her to stay; she had said she would be just beyond the door. The distinction satisfied nobody, but he needed Mara where she was and Riarhin was taking up space which the man required to work.

In the yard Maelis stood beside the little cart.

She had found the torn fastening from Na’ar’s coat caught in a split board. It was only a scrap of cloth, dark where his blood had dried. She held it between two fingers with an expression Riarhin could not read.

“Did he suffer?”

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

Maelis closed her eyes.

“I could tell you it was quick.”

“Was it?”

“Some of it.”

The votary put the cloth in her pocket. She did it carefully, as though the edges might cut her.

“He had begun telling me what I ought to eat,” she said. “As if that fell within his appointment.”

Riarhin leaned against the cart. The wood beneath her hand was sticky. She took the hand away.

“He did that.”

“He said I talked too much when I was hungry.”

“You do.”

Maelis laughed once. It escaped without pleasure, and she pressed the heel of her hand against her mouth until she could lower it.

Inside, Berent cried out. Riarhin stepped towards the door. Mara told her, through it, to stay where she was.

She stayed.

***

They could not leave at dawn.

The surgeon said Berent had lost enough blood that being shaken over bad roads before he had rested might finish what the bolt had begun. He had removed the iron and dealt with the wound; he could promise neither that it would remain clean nor that the bleeding would stay stopped if they treated his work as permission to hurry.

Hobb looked towards the window. “People will be moving.”

“They already are,” his sister said. “You can’t bring a wounded man into my roots and then complain that he needs a morning.”

The surgeon was paid. Before he left, Mara asked him three questions about what would require them to stop again. Riarhin listened from beside the door, holding each answer because she could not afford to lose any of them. He told Mara to keep doing what she had been doing and seek help if the leg worsened. He gave her a small parcel from his bag and charged for that separately.

When he had gone, Hobb brought the hiring into the room.

“I have agreed to carry packing cases to a forwarding yard. I haven’t agreed to be hanged.”

“You can refuse,” Riarhin said.

“And leave you here with my sister?”

“We’ll find another way.”

“Before or after someone asks why my cart went past the gate with blood on it?”

She had no answer. He pulled a stool to the bedside and addressed Mara.

“Who are you?”

“Mara Everblood. Elyas was my husband.”

He studied her. “They’ll be looking harder for you than for someone who failed to pay a debt.”

“Yes.”

“And you want to go where?”

“Somewhere I can answer a question without asking the queen whether I’m allowed.”

Riarhin watched Hobb’s face. Anger alone would not move him; he had cargo promised and a sister standing within reach of the consequences. He asked how many people knew his name. Maelis answered without leaving Deirdre out. He asked what money remained. Riarhin laid it where he could count it.

They agreed that he would carry them as far as the forwarding yard, south first and then by the westward freight road towards Fairport. He would not take them into the port. They would travel in the open wagon with the cases piled around Berent’s bedding rather than on it, and if stopped he would say whom he had been told he carried. Maelis began to object to the last condition. Mara accepted it before she finished.

“He’s already risking enough.”

Hobb nodded and went out to alter the load.

***

Deirdre came near midday.

She looked into the room, counted the living people, and did not ask where the fifth was. Maelis went outside to speak to her. When they returned, the carrier had agreed to ride ahead and ask about a boat; she still could not promise her sister would receive them, but she could send word on a vessel leaving before they reached the coast.

“There may be no reply in time,” she said.

“Then tell us what you have when we get there,” Riarhin answered.

She gave Deirdre a separate note for Orrel. It asked only that he continue, said that she had had to leave the city, and named no destination. Deirdre read it before she agreed to put it into a messenger’s hands.
