She would not carry an unexamined packet from one accused woman to another man’s office after the morning she had spent answering for Lyra’s.

Riarhin waited while she read. She could feel Mara watching her, and was grateful that nobody said the note would help.

When the carrier had left, Maelis sat on the edge of an empty crate.

“You’ll have to make room for my case.”

“There is room.”

“For me as well.”

Riarhin looked at her. “You don’t have to come because we held a knife to you.”

“I’m aware of that. I have been aware of several opportunities to make your life difficult, and I may yet use one.”

“Then why?”

Maelis glanced at Mara, who had turned away to moisten Berent’s lips. For a moment her expression changed, the fear still present beneath something almost delighted.

“A stolen face. A queen who knows and asks the thief to dinner. A rescue announced to the woman from whom the prisoner is being rescued.” She touched the case with her foot. “My lady is enjoying herself.”

“Na’ar is dead.”

“I know.”

The delight left her face. She looked towards the cart outside, then back at Riarhin.

“I didn’t say I enjoyed every part.”

Riarhin wanted to hate her for the distinction. Maelis met her gaze without pretending it was another reason, a nobler one she had only now discovered.

“And you?” Riarhin asked.

“I have done things which pleased her less. It is a relief to be moving the other way.”

She would say no more. She rose to fetch the case, and Riarhin made room for it beside her own bag.

Chapter Thirty-Seven — The Crossing Agreed

They left in the afternoon.

Hobb’s sister held the horse while they carried Berent out. She had packed food which she expected to be returned as an empty basket and made Hobb repeat that he would write when he reached the forwarding yard. She spoke to him as though the journey were an ordinary one in which he might forget.

Riarhin tried to give her more money. The woman closed her hand over it.

“Keep that for the next person who needs persuading.”

At the road Riarhin looked back. The woman had already gone to scrub the floor. Their borrowed cart stood beside the shed with its shafts raised, cleaned badly enough that she could still see a dark line beneath the near board.

They travelled south until evening among wagons which had left Valora after the inspection. Some carried empty baskets back to the farms. Others bore ironwork, cloth, men who had been paid and were going to spend it. Hobb kept his place among them, neither hanging back nor pushing for room. When Riarhin asked why he stopped at a busy yard, he told her a cart which arrived after everyone else was easier to remember.

Berent remained under a cover against the wind, awake more often than he could speak. Mara sat with him, lifting the edge whenever they halted to make sure the binding had not shifted. Maelis took turns walking so there would be less weight in the bed. Riarhin offered to take her place and was told to rest before she became another person who needed carrying.

That night they slept beneath a roof open at one end. A man in the next wagon snored in sharp, indignant bursts. Riarhin lay with her head on her bag, watching the road through a gap between two cases. Each time a horse passed she sat up far enough to see its rider.

She found herself waiting for Na’ar to tell her to lie down.

***

On the third day Berent woke with a fever.

It was slight enough for Hobb to suggest travelling in the cool before noon. Mara asked him to put his hand against the sick man’s neck, then to listen while she moved the leg enough to look beneath the binding. Berent bit down on his own sleeve. Hobb took his hand away.

They stayed.

The yard keeper gave them a small room for more money than the previous night’s shelter and sent for a woman who dressed wounds among the carters. She knew the surgeon’s sort of work and examined the place without claiming it had been badly done. There was swelling. She wanted to see what it did over the next night.

Riarhin stood in the doorway while Mara helped her. She had begun to count the time they were giving the people behind them: a morning, an afternoon, the distance a mounted man could cover while Berent lay on a pallet. She could feel herself wishing the leg would look better because she needed it to, and went outside before she made that wish into advice.

Maelis found her behind the carts.

“You’re frightening Hobb.”

“Is he going to leave?”

“He is trying to decide whether you intend to make him leave. Stop looking at the road as though it owes you something.”

Riarhin sat on an upturned bucket. From here she could see a boy holding a horse’s foot while his father examined the shoe. The boy’s shoulders trembled with the effort, but he held it until the man was ready to let it go.

“Do you think anyone went back for him?” she asked.

Maelis understood at once.

“The men at the house must have done.”

“Would they bury him?”

“I don’t know.”

Riarhin looked at her hands. She remembered where she had put him, how the bank supported his shoulder, the arm she had left beneath him because moving it would take another moment. She could have moved it. It was a small thing to be unable to stop thinking about when she had left his whole body to strangers.

“I have to tell Tavish.”

“Yes.”

“I don’t know how.”

Maelis crouched beside her, keeping her skirt out of the dirt with one hand.

“Say his name first. You can find the rest afterwards.”

Riarhin fetched a sheet from her bag. She wrote Na’ar and stopped. The boy released the horse’s foot. His father praised him, and she heard that too clearly, each ordinary word arriving as if someone meant her to answer it.

She turned the sheet over. On the other side she wrote Ailsa’s name.

There was no safe carrier waiting to take it north. The name was something she could write while the rest would not come. She traced the final stroke once more, darkening it, and thought of Ailsa seeing a stranger at the door with a letter. She would wipe her hands before taking it. She would read the first lines standing where the light was best, then sit down when she understood.

Riarhin set the pen across the little bottle.

Mara came out of the room, drying her hands. The woman tending Berent had taken her place beside him; through the doorway Riarhin could hear her asking him to drink. Mara waited until he answered, then looked down at the sheet.

“Another letter?” she asked.

“Someone in Ubral.”

Mara waited. Her hands rested on the folded cloth she had been using to support Berent’s leg. She had washed it twice and still been unable to remove all the blood.

“Ailsa,” Riarhin said. “I stayed with her sometimes.”

“Does she know where you are?”

“Valora. I sent a letter before Lyra was taken. Na’ar found a carrier going north.”

Saying what he had done let her speak his name without first crossing the place where he was dead. She wanted to stay there. He had brought the price back, complaining that the man charged more for paper than for something a horse could eat. She had given him the coins and asked nothing about his own letter.

“What did you tell her?” Mara asked.

“Very little.”

“Did she expect more?”

“She asked me to tell her if I couldn’t write.”

“That seems sensible.”

“It was.”

Mara looked at the name on the sheet. Riarhin turned it back to Tavish’s unfinished line and then, ashamed of the movement, turned it over again.

“I meant to go back.”

“You still might.”

She could see the path beside the stream, Ailsa’s door sticking when the wood swelled, the dry patch beneath the eaves where a person could stand while finding the key. She had no key. Ailsa had offered her one and she had said there was no need because somebody was always there. The stupidity of that answer reached her now with the force of fresh news.

“Would you?” she asked Mara.

“To Ubral?”

“If this were over.”

Mara looked towards the place where Berent slept.

“I don’t know what over would be.”

“No.”

“But I’d like to meet her.”

Riarhin took up the pen. Beneath Ailsa’s name she wrote that she was alive, that she was leaving the country with people who needed help, and that she could not say when another letter would follow. Then she stopped making the journey sound like work that might excuse her absence.

I want to see you. I cannot promise when. I should have taken the key.

She read it once. It would have to remain with her until there was a way to send it which did not lead anyone to Ailsa’s door. Mara handed her a clean scrap to lay against the wet writing.

She put the unfinished letter away before the ink dried.

***

Berent was better in the morning.

Better meant that he knew which yard they were in, could drink without Mara raising his head, and objected when the woman who had treated him told him to leave the binding alone. They waited until she was satisfied that the fever was falling. Then Hobb padded the place where the cart’s side pressed against Berent’s bedding, grumbling that he would soon have more household linen than freight.

They turned west with the next company of wagons.

The land changed by degrees. The fields remained worked, but water gathered in the lower cuts and the road crossed longer runs of raised earth between them. Banks divided grazing from ploughland. A farmer stood in a ditch clearing the mouth of a drain while his daughter held the tools above the water, passing one down whenever he reached up. Beyond some banks the reeds thickened enough to hide the surface; beyond others cattle grazed where somebody’s forebears had made dry ground and their children kept making it.

Riarhin watched the work until a wagon in front stopped. Hobb swore. A load had shifted, and the whole line waited while the owner climbed up to set it straight.

Nobody’s eyes were on them. They were simply another cause for impatience if Berent needed to be moved when the road cleared.

At a roadside shrine Mara bought a little oil from the woman who kept the lamps. She gave no account of whom it was for. Riarhin stood outside with the horse and watched her set the cup beside two others, then remain with her hands resting against the shelf.

When she came out, Riarhin asked whether it was for Elyas.

“For him. For Na’ar.”

“Would Na’ar want that?”

“I don’t know what he would want here.”

Mara touched the cart’s side before climbing in.

“I wanted to do it.”

Riarhin took the horse’s head until she was seated. At the next halt she finished the first line of the letter to Tavish.

Na’ar died helping me get Mara out.

She left room beneath it for what she had done with him.

***

They reached the forwarding yard on the seventh evening after the rescue.

Light remained along the tops of the roofs after the ground between them had gone blue. The yard had two entrances, both wide enough for loaded wagons, and the men calling the arrivals made it clear which one belonged to people who knew their business. Hobb took the proper turn without waiting to be directed. For the first time since Valora, Riarhin saw him straighten in his seat.

The air carried a dampness different from the fields. Salt had entered it, faint beneath tar and hot animal hide. A bell sounded beyond the roofs; another answered from farther away, and Berent opened his eyes at the change in the noises around him. Mara told him they had reached the coast before he could ask. He turned his face towards the little strip of sky visible between the cases.

Hobb had expected to be there sooner. He gave the tally to the man receiving the cases and remained while each was checked, refusing to let the urgency of his passengers cost him payment for the journey. Riarhin waited beneath the shed roof beside Berent. He was sleeping with his good hand resting on the outside of the cover, the fingers closing sometimes as if holding a rein.

Deirdre came through the yard carrying a loaf under her arm.

“Still four?” she asked quietly.

“Still four.”

She looked at Berent. “Can he sit in a boat?”

“He can lie in one.”

“The first is small.”

“How small?”

“Come and ask its owner.”

Mara stayed with Berent. Riarhin and Maelis followed Deirdre into a room above the weighing place, where a broad woman in a patched coat sat warming her hands around a cup. She gave her name as Davra and said she had already heard enough to know what she would not do.

“No quay. No carrying you past a clerk who thinks he knows the face of a knight he used to drink with. You go downriver in the small boat to where my vessel lies outside the busiest water. If your man cannot make that change, say it now.”

“We’ll have to see the boat,” Riarhin said.

“Good. Someone who asks.”

Davra knew the Fairport coast and the crossing to Theona. She carried lawful loads when they paid enough and other loads when somebody made the risk worthwhile; she had no intention of disguising four fugitives as an ordinary entry on her cargo account. They would board by a lesser landing away from the port’s main quays. The delay would cost her a tide she had hoped to use elsewhere.

Riarhin asked the price.

Davra named it. Maelis made a sound of real pain.

“That includes the people I must pay to know less than they’ve seen,” Davra said. “It does not buy a fight with a patrol boat.”

“And if one comes?”

“We stop. I haven’t enough men to win the sea from the queen.”

Deirdre put a folded paper on the table. A reply had come on a returning boat from her sister, who would permit them to land and shelter until Berent could be moved. She had named conditions more exact than those Deirdre had suggested in Valora.

No gathering of armed men. No use of the household’s name in appeals to the island moots or Conall. No letters sent from its landing without being shown to the host. If a claim for their surrender came, they would hear it together before anyone gave an answer. The sister would not promise to begin a war between neighbours for people she had never met.

Riarhin read the paper, then pushed it back.

“Mara has to hear this.”

“We’re waiting for her answer,” Deirdre said.

For a moment Riarhin was ashamed of how pleased she had been with herself for remembering. Deirdre took the loaf downstairs. They brought the terms to the place where Mara sat beside her injured friend, and she read them twice before agreeing.

“Tell your sister I will answer for myself,” she said. “I won’t pretend to speak for the people who used to come to Elyas.”

Deirdre nodded. “She’ll like that better.”

***

Hobb left them at the lesser landing the next night.

Riarhin paid what remained due and added the money set aside for the little cart. He said his sister would see it returned if there was anyone willing to come for it. Then he put his hand on Berent’s shoulder and told him he would send an account for every mile he had made them lose.

Berent managed a smile. “Send it to me.”

“I intend to.”

Hobb drove away before they began carrying him down.

The small boat lay against a timber stage set low over the water. Mara went aboard first. Maelis passed the bags, then followed them, too busy keeping her case dry to find words for her discomfort. Riarhin and two of Davra’s people brought Berent between them on the narrow litter the yard’s carpenter had made. They lowered him into the boat with his injured leg supported along the bench.

They had almost finished when a lantern appeared on the bank.

A patrol boat came out from behind the bend, moving slowly enough that Riarhin first saw its reflection, broken by the oars. Three men sat in it. Another stood by the tiller with the lantern held above his shoulder. Davra’s boatman stopped coiling the rope.

“Stay where you are,” he whispered.

There was nowhere useful to go. The water beside the landing was black and cold, the bank open above it. Riarhin could feel Remorse under the wrapping at her feet. Drawing it would change nothing about a bow pointed into a crowded boat.

The patrol came close enough for the lantern to show Mara’s face.

The man at the tiller looked at Berent, then at Riarhin. His gaze paused on her ears. He asked the boatman’s name and which vessel waited for him. The answers satisfied him more quickly than they should have.

A younger man turned towards his officer. “That’s—”

“I can see who’s there.”

The officer shifted the lantern, leaving their boat darker.

“The outer water’s running hard. Keep him flat through the bend.”

He signalled the oarsmen. The patrol moved on towards the other landing, where there was neither a lit window nor a boat waiting to be searched.

Riarhin remained still until the light disappeared.

“What was that?” Mara asked.

“I don’t know.”

She looked back along the shore. The patrol had room to turn. They could reach them before the larger vessel; they could send word ahead of a slow, burdened boat. Instead the sound of the oars grew fainter.

The boatman untied them with shaking hands.

***

Davra received them without a welcome.

She wanted Berent brought aboard first and made everyone stop talking while the men managed the lift. By the time he lay beneath shelter, Mara was grey with fatigue and Riarhin’s side hurt each time she breathed. Maelis put her case down in a place immediately required for something else and endured being told to move it with unusual restraint.

Only when they were clear of the coastal lights did Davra ask about the patrol.

“They saw us,” Riarhin said.

“Did they speak to you?”

“To the boatman.”

“Did they know you?”

“The man at the tiller knew enough to stop the younger one saying it.”

Davra looked astern. There were lights behind them, but none drew nearer.

“You’ll tell me anything else I ought to know.”

“Yes.”

“Before it comes alongside.”

Riarhin went below the small shelter where Mara sat with Berent. Deirdre had remained ashore; her sister’s letter was wrapped with Lyra’s papers in Riarhin’s bag. They had permission to arrive somewhere. She had been treating that as the difficult part until the patrol let them leave.

Mara moved to give her room.

For once Riarhin sat without checking whether she was needed elsewhere first.

Chapter Thirty-Eight — The Shore

There was no room to sleep without touching someone.

Riarhin lay with her shoulder against a folded sail and Maelis’s boot near her knee. Above them a man crossed the deck whenever the vessel changed its motion, his steps so regular that she began anticipating them and woke when he failed to come. Berent slept in pieces, rousing to ask for water or to shift a little before pain stopped him. Mara answered each time until Riarhin took the cup from her.

“Lie down.”

“I am lying down.”

“You’re holding yourself up on your elbow.”

Mara gave a tired laugh and obeyed.

Beyond the shelter the sea moved past in long, dark reaches. Riarhin could see it through the gap beside the sail, lit now and then by something in the wake which disappeared before she could decide whether it had been light at all. She had crossed water with Na’ar before. She had been afraid, been ill, argued with him about where he put his feet. Remembering those things brought him so close that she turned to tell him Mara was finally asleep.

Maelis opened her eyes.

“What?”

“Nothing you need.”

The votary watched her for a moment, then moved the boot out of her way.

***

On the second morning the wind fell.

Davra disliked it more than she had disliked the rougher water of the first night. She paced the available deck, studying the sail and the horizon, and sent a man to check the lashings of a load which had not moved. The work gave him something to do. Riarhin recognised the habit and tried not to repeat it on Berent.

His fever had not returned. He could speak longer now, though he rarely chose to. Once, while Mara slept, he asked whether Riarhin had seen Na’ar die.

“Yes.”

“You’re sure.”

“Yes.”

He shut his eyes. Riarhin waited because there was another question in the way his fingers held the cover.

“I heard you go back.”

“I went back.”

“You couldn’t bring him.”

“No.”

Berent turned his face towards her. “Would you have left me?”

The ship creaked around them. She could offer a kindness he would recognise as such, and neither of them would have to say why he asked.

“I don’t know.”

He looked away. She stayed beside him.

After a while he asked for the cup. She held it while he drank, and when he had finished he put his hand briefly over hers.

***

Mara found the unfinished letter when Riarhin asked her to fetch clean cloth from the bag.

She did not read beyond the first line. Riarhin saw her fold it carefully and lay it on top before bringing the cloth across.

“You can finish it when we’re ashore.”

“I should have finished it already.”

“Why?”

“So that I don’t keep trying to find a better way it happened.”

Mara sat beside her. On deck Maelis was asking a sailor whether the nearest visible land was the island to which they were going. He said it was land and left the rest of her question unanswered because Davra was calling him.

“You came for me,” Mara said.

Riarhin looked down at her hands.

“Yes.”

“I haven’t thanked you.”

“You needn’t.”

“I want to. And I want him alive. I don’t know how to say either without the other.”

Riarhin could hear the water against the hull, slow in the failing wind. She had imagined Mara angry with her for leaving Na’ar, then grateful, then unable to bear her. Each imagined answer put the feeling somewhere she could meet it with one of her own. Mara sat beside her wanting both things, and she could do nothing to arrange them more comfortably.

“I left Lyra,” Riarhin said.

“I know.”

“She asked to see someone.”

Mara took her hand. There were still small dark places under both their nails despite the water they had used.

“We’ll have to find out what happened at the hearing.”

Riarhin nodded. Neither of them said what the hearing must have done.

***

They saw the landing on the third day.

Davra pointed it out after they had passed a busier reach of shore where houses gathered around a quay. A flag hung over one of the buildings there, limp in the morning air. Farther along, a boat lay hauled above the waterline with its hull opened for repair. Two children stood watching the ship, then ran towards a house.

They were ordinary movements. Riarhin had not known how much she needed them until she found herself watching the children instead of the windows beyond them.

The agreed beach was sheltered by a low headland. There was shingle above the tide and darker sand below, with enough water at the edge for the small boat to approach without being dragged. A woman stood on the shore beside two men and a handcart. She raised no flag. When the boatman called a name, she answered it and came down towards the wash.

“Deirdre’s sister?” Riarhin asked.

“Eilis,” the boatman said. “Let me speak first.”

They had brought Berent down before anyone else. He lay along the middle of the boat with his eyes closed, conserving himself for another lifting. Mara knelt beside him. Maelis held her case between her feet and stared at the approaching beach with the concentration of someone afraid of being sick at the moment she most wished to appear competent.

Eilis caught the bow rope and passed it to one of the men.

“Which is the widow?”

Mara raised a hand.

“You’ve heard my terms?”

“Yes.”

“They haven’t changed because you’ve crossed.”

“I understand.”

Eilis looked at Berent and called to the man by the cart to bring the blankets down. She wore a heavy skirt pinned out of the water and had a split in one thumbnail wrapped with thread. Her resemblance to Deirdre showed when she frowned at something she had already been told would be troublesome.

Maelis began an introduction. Eilis stopped her with a hand.

“After he’s out.”

Riarhin stood to help, and the boat shifted beneath her. She put a hand on the gunwale until it settled. There were enough people to lift Berent if she went to the shore side, enough hands to keep the injured leg from striking the edge. For an instant she turned towards the place Na’ar would have taken.

The boatman was waiting for her to move.

She moved.

***

The sand gave under her boots.

They carried Berent above the reach of the wash and set him on the waiting cart. Mara folded a blanket against the side to keep his leg clear of the boards. One of Eilis’s men asked whether she needed a woman who attended the sick; Mara said yes, and he went towards the house without waiting for another instruction.

There were nets drying beyond the strand, a patched roof, a field divided by low banks. Farther inland a little group of people stood beside a gateway, watching without approaching. Eilis saw Riarhin notice them.

“My cousins,” she said. “They’ll want to know whose permission you have.”

“Yours.”

“For my house.”

Riarhin nodded. Eilis had already turned back to make sure the cart’s handles were properly held before anyone lifted them.

Before they were lifted she took a cloth from the cart and gave each of them a piece of oatcake, beginning with Berent, who could not eat his, and ending with Riarhin. Riarhin thanked her and ate. Eilis folded the cloth over what remained and went to help with the cart.

Maelis stepped out of the boat with her case. Water reached the hem she had worked so hard to keep dry. She looked at it, gave a small exhausted laugh, and carried the case up the beach herself.

Riarhin stood beside the emptied boat.

The queen had let her walk out. She had known the place Riarhin meant to go, known its men would defend it, and refused to make a single word of their orders different. At the fork the patrol had held back riders who could have followed them. At the lesser landing another officer had looked directly at their faces and sent his men elsewhere.

Had Tylan asked for mercy? Had one of the officers let them pass on his own? Riarhin looked at the cart, at Berent’s bound leg.

But Veralyn had named the blood before any of them moved. She had given Riarhin room to go and, beyond the house, room to leave.

Riarhin looked at Mara, bending over Berent on the cart. She had wanted this woman out. She had carried that wanting into the queen’s room and laid it where Veralyn could examine it. The queen had let her purchase what she could not obtain by asking.

Had she expected Na’ar? Riarhin could not get past his name.

A wave slid around her boot. She stepped back.

For years she had known how easily she could want someone dead. She had learned to distrust the relief in it, the sudden tidiness of a world from which one person had been removed. Tulgoth’s sons had names; she had not needed to remember every one of them to go on finding the next. She stood with the water seeping into her boot.

Veralyn must die.

She thought of Tylan reaching to take Matylde from her mother’s tired arm. Of the soldier buying his sister a place, the orchard wall rebuilt, the girl at a loom whose work had been accepted.

Lyra was there. The queen’s army was ready. Riarhin had given Veralyn the little green amulet and laughed about the trouble it had cost her; she could remember every fold in the cloth and still not know what she had put into that hand.

From the cart Mara called her name.

Riarhin took the bags the boatman passed up. One belonged to Berent, one to Maelis, and the last held her own things with the unfinished letter and Lyra’s papers wrapped inside. There should have been another. She waited for it for the length of a breath.

Then she carried what they had up the beach.
